# Standard library outline

Status: **partly implemented** per family: each package under `internal/sketch/std` is Ergo source that the checker checks like user code and `ergo build` lowers, with Go bodies in `runtime/v1` where a family needs the host; each family below states its own status, and a family with no package is designed. The prelude's `Console` (`Println`) and the `Sleep` of its live scheduler lower ([runtime.md](runtime.md#host-and-lowering-notes)). `ergo build` lowers `timeout.After` to its `runtime/v1` body, which takes its deadline from the program's `Scheduler` ([runtime.md](runtime.md#timeouts); `TestTimeoutAfterLowers`, `TestTimeoutDeadlineIsTheProgramScheduler`). The four v1 packages `task`, `scope`, `queue` and `slices` (named `slice` until std-align S2a) have Ergo signatures, which the checker checks like user code, bound to Go bodies in `runtime/v1` ([v1 packages](#v1-packages-task-scope-queue-and-slice)); `ergo build` lowers their calls (check-v1 stage D3b; `TestTaskGoAndTaskResults`, `TestErgoRunQueueOperations`, `TestErgoRunSliceWithCapacity`). The context packages `trace` and `log` lower too (stage 4a; [Context values](#v1-packages-task-scope-queue-and-slice)).

The standard library is the "Effect for Go" track, the standard library. Its module map is the contract: each family states the Effect modules it covers, the construct it uses and its tier. Modules are restated in Ergo's idioms, not ported API for API. Effect is prior art for the guarantees, not a dependency (owner, 2026-10-26). Go names are kept wherever Go has one. Definitions are in the [glossary](../../GLOSSARY.md).

**Tiers.**
- **kernel**: a checker or runtime rule the module needs.
- **library**: ordinary Ergo code over the kernel, with no compiler branch keyed on its identity.
- **host**: a host package, the only code that calls Go.

## Module map

| Family | Ticket | Effect counterpart | Construct or package | Tier |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Tasks and supervision | std concurrency | Fiber, `Effect.all`/`forEach` concurrency, `race`/`raceAll`, FiberSet, FiberMap, FiberHandle, Supervisor | `task.Go`, `Task`, `tasks.Transform`, `tasks.Set`, `tasks.Race` | kernel + library |
| Scope and resources | std scope and resources | Scope, acquireRelease, ensuring, onExit, addFinalizer, Layer.scoped, forkScoped | automatic release ([resources.md](resources.md)); `build.Scope` for dynamic registration (README Gap 13) | kernel |
| Schedule, retry and timeout | std schedule, retry and timeout | Schedule, retry, repeat, timeout, Clock.sleep, Duration | `schedule`, `retry.On`, `timeout.After(d) { }`, duration literals | library |
| Channels, queues and streams | std channels, queues and streams | Queue, PubSub, Mailbox, Stream, Sink, Channel | `queue.Queue`, `for … in`, iterators that are resources | kernel + library |
| Schema and codecs | std codecs and I/O | Schema, Arbitrary | `ParseX`/`Wire` by name, `json.Codec` | library + coherence rule |
| Sync and STM | std sync and STM | Ref, SynchronizedRef, Deferred, Semaphore, Latch, TxRef, TxQueue, TxSemaphore | `sync.Ref`, `sync.Atomically() { }`, `sync.Tx` | kernel + library |
| Batching, caching and pools | std batch, cache and pool | Request, RequestResolver, Cache, ScopedCache, Pool, KeyedPool, RcRef, RcMap, ScopedRef | `batch.Resolve` in `context`; [`cache`](#cache): `Get`, `Put`, `NewReleasing` | library |
| Config | std config | Config, ConfigProvider, Redacted | [`config`](#config): `Load`, `Redacted[T]` | library |
| Observability | std trace, log and metric | Tracer, Logger, Metric, LogLevel, References | `with trace.Span(…)`, `log`, `metric` | library on kernel G1/G2 |
| Clock, random and testing | the prelude services | Clock, Random, Console, TestClock, TestServices, TestContext, `it.effect` | prelude capabilities, `testing.VirtualClock` in `context` | kernel seams + library |
| HTTP | std http and rpc | HttpClient, HttpServer, HttpRouter, HttpApi | `func(http.Request) http.Response`, total `catch` | library + host |
| RPC | std rpc | Rpc, RpcGroup, RpcClient, RpcServer | `rpc.Answer` (an http handler, so one listener serves RPC beside other routes), `rpc.Serve`, `rpc.Call`, `rpc.Loopback` (a server in process, so a contract's laws run through the wire), and the server and client `ergo gen rpc` writes from an operation type ([rpc.md](rpc.md)) | library + host |
| HTML | std html | (none; Lustre and Elm prior art) | `html` functions | library |
| Actors | the actor library | cluster Entity (OTP, Gleam and Akka Typed prior art) | `actor.Start`, `actor.Ask` with an `actor.Reply[T, E]` in the message, `actor.Address[M]`, `actor.StartMachine(m, n)`, `actor.StartAgent` and `actor.Call` with a function on a lent `*S` ([actor library note](../research/actor-library.md)) | library: no compiler rule names it |
| Host packages | std host packages | platform FileSystem, Path, Command, Socket; sql | `os`, `fs`, `net` ([net](#net)), `sql` with cancel hooks | host |
| Core data | std core data | Option, Array, Record, HashMap, HashSet, Chunk, Order, Equivalence, Equal, Hash, Data | prelude `Option`, `slices`, `maps`, `cmp` | library + built-in constraints |

Out of scope for now: durable workflows.

## Prelude

The prelude is in scope in every package without an import: `Option`, `Never` (the type with no value; its function `Absurd` is reached by the dot form, `n.Absurd[T]()`; [types.md, Never](types.md#never)), `Range`, `Seq`, `CallSite` ([types.md, Call sites](types.md#call-sites)), `Task`, `Decision`, `Timeout`, `Machine`, `InputMachine`, `Scheduler` (with `AlarmClock`, the result of its `Alarms`: a deadline source only the runtime makes and arms), `Random`, `Console`, `Ambient`, `TestAmbient`, `Unsatisfied`, the failure of a refined type's derived parse function ([types.md, Refinements](types.md#refinements) 4), and the codec's event types `Writer` and `Reader` with its shape failures `WireWrongKind`, `WireMissingField` and `WireUnknownTag` ([types.md, Codecs](types.md#codecs)) (`Clock` is `time.Clock` since std-align S2b; [time](#time)). `Ambient` is the authority to choose the host, which only the entry provides ([Host packages](#host-packages)). `TestAmbient` is the test's ambient, the authority to reach the host in a fresh folder per case, which only the test runner provides; `TestFolder()` names the folder ([rows.md](rows.md#tests-ambient)). The function `Unreachable(reason)` states that a path cannot run: its call has the bottom type, and it raises a defect, never a typed failure ([types.md, Unreachable](types.md#unreachable); owner q29). The function `InForce[R]()` is the providers of the capability row `R` in force at its call, as a `Context[R]` value, for a library that keeps a function with `needs R` to run it later ([rows.md, Context values](rows.md#capability-rows) 10, InForce; Q16). Its functions on slice and Map types, `Append`, `Get`, `Delete` and `Put` (below, Slices and Maps), are in no scope: they are reached only by the dot form, `xs.Append(x)`, so a package may declare its own function of the same name (check-v1 D-D6, D-D8). The same holds for a prelude function on a prelude type, which is that type's operation (196m): `Sleep` is `LiveScheduler`'s, reached as `s.Sleep(d)` on a `LiveScheduler` value `s` or through a context that binds `Scheduler: NewLiveScheduler()`; `NewLiveScheduler` needs `Ambient` ([Host packages](#host-packages) 6). A bare `Sleep(…)` is S104 naming the dot form, and a program may declare its own Scheduler provider with its own `func Sleep(s Instant, d duration)` (checker-fixes prelude-scope S1). `check.IsPredeclared` reports the names the prelude and the universe predeclare, for tools such as bind-c. The capabilities are ordinary interfaces bound by `context` (the decision on the Foreign protocol), so a test replaces any of them.

**Absence.** A lookup by a key the caller holds returns `Option[T]`; any other miss is a named failure. `o.OrFail(f)`, the prelude's `OrFail[T, F](o Option[T], f F) T fails F`, is `o`'s value, or fails with `f` when `o` is `None`: the one call from a miss as an `Option` to a miss as a failure, carrying what the caller knows (`m.Get(k).OrFail(NotFound{Key: k})`). The other way is a catch arm that names the miss and returns `None`. Its laws are `OrFailIsMatch` and `CatchUndoesOrFail`. `F` is a slot row (rows.md, Error rows 8, Lowering), so the failure keeps its Go type until the call fails with it, and a hit builds no error.

## v1 packages: task, scope, queue and slice

Language v1 has four small std packages beside the prelude. A file imports each one it uses; none is ambient (parse-v1 ruling 6). Each pairs ordinary Ergo signatures with a Go body in `runtime/v1`, bound by the identity of the declaration it implements, never by its target text ([runtime.md](runtime.md#host-and-lowering-notes), Extern bodies). The signatures are `extern go` declarations in `internal/sketch/std/task`, `scope`, `queue` and `slice`; the checker checks them, and a program that imports them, with the rules of user code. Their calls lower (check-v1 stage D3b), and their waits take the caller's task (stage 2).

| Package | Signatures | Contract | Go body |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `task` | `func Go[T, E, R](body func() T fails E needs R) Task[T, E] needs R`; the prelude's `Task[T, E]` has `Wait() T fails E` and `Stop()` | [runtime.md, Tasks](runtime.md#tasks) rules 7 to 12 | `Go(s *Scope, span Site, body func(*rt.Task) (T, error)) *Task[T]`; the methods `Wait`, `Stop` and `Move(dest *Scope)`, the return of a Task, which only the lowering calls (task.go) |
| `scope` | `func OnExit[R](body func() needs R) needs R` | [runtime.md, Exit actions](runtime.md#exit-actions) | `OnExit(s *Scope, body func())` (task.go) |
| `queue` | `type Closed {}`; `type Full {}`; `type Queue[T] { Push(v T) fails Closed takes v, Pop() T fails Closed, End(), Values() Seq[T], Poll() Option[T], Offer(v own T) fails Closed \| Full, Cap() int, Len() int }`; `func New[T](capacity int) Queue[T]` | [runtime.md, Queues](runtime.md#queues) | `New(capacity int) *Queue[T]`; the methods `Push(t *rt.Task, v T) error`, `Pop(t *rt.Task) (T, error)`, `Close()`, `Values(t *rt.Task) iter.Seq[T]`, `Poll() (T, bool)`, `Offer(v T) error`, `Cap() int` and `Len() int`; `Closed`, `Full` (queue.go) |
| `slices` | `func WithCapacity[T](n int) []T`, and the functions of [Slices and Maps](#slices-maps-and-cmp) | below | `WithCapacity(n int) []T` (data.go); the rest in collections.go |

**`Values`.** `q.Values()` returns the prelude's `Seq[T]` (lower-v1 Q1; [types.md, Loops and len](types.md#loops-and-len)): `for v in q.Values()` pops each value in turn, waiting like `Pop`, and ends when the queue is closed and empty; a `break` leaves the values not yet popped in the queue. Its Go body returns Go's `iter.Seq[T]`. A `for` over the queue is never an eagerly collected slice. Its doc comment has an `Unbounded:` paragraph (as `actor.Actor.Changes` has): the Seq ends only when another task closes the queue, so lint's `unbounded-collect` reports a `slices.Collect` of it ([tooling](../tooling.md#lint)). Std declares which Go packages it covers in one table, `internal/sketch/stdlib/coverage.go`, which lint's `foreign-where-std` reads; `TestStdCoverageIsTotal` lists every std package there once. The call evaluates its receiver once, where the Seq is made, and returns a Seq that calls the Go `Values` body each time it is iterated, with the task of the code that iterates it, so a stop of the iterating task ends a wait inside the loop, also when the Seq was made in another task (check-v1 stage D3b). For a queue whose element holds a resource, each value popped is owned by the for-in that ranges over the call, so the call is admitted only there ([resources](resources.md) R35, 16a fix r2).

**Hidden arguments.** Some Go bodies need a value that the Ergo signature does not show and the lowering supplies at the call. The Go body names each one by its Go type, which no Ergo type lowers to, so the body's own signature is the binding; there is no attribute in the Ergo source:

1. `*Scope` is the **frame**: the frame scope of the innermost source function at the call ([Exit actions](runtime.md#exit-actions) 5). In an exit action's block, it is the scope of the function that registered the action; the scope itself routes to the action's frame. `task.Go` and `scope.OnExit` take it, and so does every function whose result holds a `Task`, as its hidden destination ([Tasks](runtime.md#tasks) rule 8): its return is `Task.Move(dest)`.
2. `Site` is the **site**: the call's Ergo identity, the calling function, the called declaration and the call's source position, such as `main calls task.Go at main.ergo:12:10`, the form of a call frame in a defect report. `task.Go` names the task by it, which a defect's task chain shows ([G2](runtime.md#g2-fork-tree-defects)).
3. `*rt.Task` is the **task**: the task that runs the call. `Push`, `Pop` and `Values` take it, because a stop of that task ends their wait ([Queues](runtime.md#queues) rule 8). `ContextBound` and `ContextValue`, the bodies of the context reads in `trace` and `log` (below), take it because they read that task's context values.
4. `Sleeper` is the **scheduler**: the `Scheduler` capability of the caller's environment, which is the provider the program bound (`env.Scheduler`). `Sleeper` is a `runtime/v1` interface with the one method `Sleep(*rt.Task, time.Duration)`, so the program's own `Scheduler` interface satisfies it. The program's `Scheduler` also has `Alarms(*rt.Task) *v1.AlarmClock` (the prelude operation `Alarms() AlarmClock`), which `v1.Alarmer` names: a body asserts it on the `Sleeper` to arm deadlines that run no Ergo code, as `HTTPServe` arms its phase alarms, and a Go `Sleeper` of a runtime test without it keeps the body's reference path. Lowering supplies it to `After` and to `HTTPServe` (std http's connection phase timers, E4), whose Ergo signatures need `Scheduler`, so the checker has bound it at the call. A body that takes it and whose signature needs no `Scheduler` is an internal compiler error (`TestStdExternsHaveBodies`). The deadline is time, and time is a capability (Q-S4-7): a test `Scheduler` controls it, as Effect's `TestClock` drives `Effect.timeout` and a Go test injects a clock.
5. `GraphOf` is the **graph**: a function that gives the graph of the machine whose states and signals are the body's type parameters `S` and `E`, or nil ([runtime.md](runtime.md), Machines and actors 15.4; binsize BS7). Lowering supplies it to `MachineSupervise` and `MachineChild`, the starts of an actor: `ergoMachineGraph[S, E]`, a generated function whose type switch gives each machine's static graph. A traced actor calls it to name its arms and timers; an untraced one never does.
6. `ContextPackage` is the **reading package**: the namespace of the package that declares the context-read extern, `std:` and its path (`std:log`; [runtime.md, G1](runtime.md#g1-context-through-with) 3). Lowering supplies it to `ContextBound` and `ContextValue`. Each binding is stored under the pair of its type's declaring package namespace and its protocol key, so a package reads only its own bindings. Std source does not spell a package prefix.
7. `Releaser[T]` is the **result release**: the generated release of a value of the call's result type `T` ([resources](resources.md) R3), a `func(*rt.Task, T)` that runs on the task it receives, or nil when `T` holds no resource (L8 B4; README Gap 47). A body that drops a value a block returned, because a stop or another block's exit won, releases it with this function, masked: `After` (a value the deadline or a stop dropped), `TasksRace` (the losers' values, and every value when the race fails) and `TasksTransform` (the finished results when another call fails). The checker records the release at the call (`Info.ResultDrops`); the lowering passes nil when there is none, and those bodies then keep no value (the plain path, as fast as before).

The hidden parameters come first, in this order, each at most once; a hidden type after an ordinary parameter breaks the convention. The parameters after them stand for the Ergo parameters, one each, in order. The Go results are the Ergo results, then `error` when the Ergo signature has an error row. A function parameter is a Go function under the same convention: the block of `task.Go` is `func(*rt.Task) (T, error)`, which receives the child's task, and the lowering adapts its function value to that shape (the block's providers are captured, never passed). A slice of function parameters is a Go slice of the same function shape: the blocks of `tasks.Race` are `[]func(*rt.Task) (T, error)`, and the lowering adapts each element at the call (`Param.Funcs`). An operation of a type that a std extern returns (`queue.Queue[T]`, the prelude's `Task[T, E]`) is the method of that name on the Go type the extern's body returns (`*Queue[T]`, `*Task[T]`), under the same convention, and the lowering calls that method directly (Types 3).

**Calling policy.** Each body that an extern or an operation binds states how its call waits, in a directive line of its Go doc comment, as `//ergo:pure` states purity (STD-NOPRIV NP4d). `//ergo:wait KIND` names the task the call waits on: `none` (it never waits), `caller` (the caller's task; a stop of it ends the wait), `handle` (the task of the handle it is given), `drain` (an uninterruptible stop and drain), `child` (it starts and joins children of the caller), `member` (the caller joins a clock's domain), `parent` (the caller is the parent of the task it starts), `settle` (the rest of a manual clock's domain parks) or `end` (an actor's runner ends, uninterruptibly). A kind that waits on or starts under the caller's task needs the hidden `*rt.Task`, and `v1.BodyOf` refuses a body that states one without it. `//ergo:release caller` marks the body of a checked `Close` (masked on the caller's task, [resources](resources.md) R9) and `//ergo:release frame` an exit action's registration; `//ergo:bridge TARGET PROVIDER` marks the body that stands for the prelude extern whose Go target is TARGET, erasing its zero-field PROVIDER operand (the host `Sleep` of `LiveScheduler`). A body with no `//ergo:wait` is no binding: no policy is assumed for it, and `TestStdExternsHaveBodies` holds every std extern's body to one. The lowering reads the policy with the signature (`lower/bodycontract.go`); no table of the compiler names a body. *Prior art:* Go's compiler reads the runtime functions it calls from their declarations in the runtime package (`cmd/compile`'s builtin table is generated from them, `mkbuiltin.go`); GHC's primops carry their attributes (`can_fail`, `has_side_effects`) on each primop's declaration in `primops.txt.pp`.

The lowering supplies these values from the caller's frame and the checked declaration:

1. **A function takes the environment if and only if it reads it.** Every lowered function, method, function literal, function value, context builder and package initializer takes a hidden `env *Env` first, except an env-free function; the environment holds one field per capability and the running task (`env.task`, a `*rt.Task`). `main` gets its task from `v1.Main`, and a `task.Go` block runs with a copy of the caller's environment that holds the child task. A pure function (no `fails`, no `needs`, no witnessed type parameter, no `Task` result (a row type parameter changes nothing: `flag.CommandUsage[C, E]` is pure; fu4b), an empty footprint, and only calls of pure functions of any package, of pure std bodies ([Pure bodies](#pure-bodies)), `len` and conversions; lower-v1 stage 5, fu-lower, batch-9 C3) takes no environment and is called with none. Purity is decided with each function's own package's facts, so a std function with an Ergo body (`strings.Cut`) and a function of an imported package are pure when their bodies are, and a caller of one is pure only when the callee is (`TestPureStdCallsKeepFunctionsPure`, `TestPurityAcrossPackages`). So `func Fold(n int) int` lowers to Go's `func Fold(n int) int`. An env-free function meets the same rules with `fails` allowed: whether a function fails decides nothing, since a typed failure is a returned Go error, so a function takes the environment if and only if it, or a function it calls, reads it (a capability, a task, a poll, an opaque call such as a function value's, an adapter or an owned value's release). So an `Atoi` written in Ergo lowers to Go's `func(s string) (int, error)` (ENV-FREE-FALLIBLE, `TestEnvParamFollowsReads`, `TestEnvLoweringsAgree`). Purity stays the narrower fact that tabulation, memo, folding and the facts export read; every pure function is env-free. The lowering before the rule, where only a pure function is env-free, is kept as the reference (`envParamLowering=pure`). Its function value keeps the one Go shape and discards the environment. No source name changes purity; the exception is decided from the Go method that the lowering emits. The host protocols are `Error() string`, `String() string`, `GoString() string` and every canonical method of go vet's stdmethods check (`ReadByte() (byte, error)`, `Seek(int64, int) (int64, error)` and the others). A pure function that lowers to a Go method whose name and Go signature meet a protocol exactly keeps the environment, so its type never joins the protocol by accident. A pure function that lowers to a Go method with a protocol's name and another signature stays pure, and its Go method name gets an underscore (`Seek_`, one more while a field or another function on the type has that name, as a renamed `Error` field does). A plain function, `func String(n int) string` included, is pure by the rule above. Kotlin draws the same line: it passes its hidden continuation only to `suspend` functions.
2. **A frame scope where one is needed.** A function that calls `task.Go`, `scope.OnExit` or a function whose result is a `Task` opens `fs := v1.NewScope(env.task)` and `defer fs.End(&err)` (`fs.End(nil)` when the function cannot fail), and passes `&fs` as the frame. The checker records per call whether the result holds a `Task` (`Info.Calls[x].TaskResult`, from the one owner of Task containment), for direct calls, calls through function values and function literals; the lowering never derives it again. A function whose result is a `Task` takes the caller's scope as a hidden destination after the environment, and its return is `v.Move(dest)`. A result that holds a `Task` inside another type (`Option[Task[T, E]]`) is refused with S109 naming check-v1 stage E, and so is a `Task` as a type argument.
3. **The site** of `task.Go` is `FUNC calls task.Go at FILE:LINE:COL`: the source function that makes the call, as the call table names it, and the position of the call.
4. **The task** of `Push`, `Pop` and `Values` is `env.task`.
5. **A hidden task is bound where the blocking happens, never where a value is made.** A std value that defers a runtime call takes its task from the environment it is called with, and evaluates its receiver and arguments once, where it is made, in source order:
   - The Seq of `q.Values()` calls `Values` with the iterating environment (above).
   - A method value of an operation, `q.Push` or `t.Wait` of a std operation type or `s.Area` of the program's operation type, is a function value of the uniform shape: it takes the caller's environment first, and calls the operation with that environment's task. One lowering writes every operation call, direct or through a method value. The checker still refuses `t.Wait` and `t.Stop` as values (a Task rule, [runtime.md, Tasks](runtime.md#tasks)), and `task.Go` and `scope.OnExit` as values: a generic function is only called or instantiated, and an explicit instantiation used as a value is S109 naming check-v1 stage E.
   - The block of `task.Go` is bound once, where `task.Go` is called: `let mut f = one; task.Go(f); f = two` runs `one`.
6. **A block with no value.** A type argument that stands for no value ([types.md, Generics](types.md#generics) 6) lowers to `struct{}`: `task.Go() { work() }` is a `*v1.Task[struct{}]`, and `t.Wait()` discards the `struct{}`. A declared generic function whose `T` is no value has its `func(…) T` parameters adapted to return `struct{}{}`, and its `T` result discarded. A `T` that is no value in any other position, such as the result of a nested function type or a type argument of a declared generic type, is refused with S109 naming check-v1 stage E.

`timeout.After` lowers to `v1.After(site, task, scheduler, release, d, body)` (lower-v1 stage 4, Q-S4-7). Its deadline is a second child of the caller's task that calls `Sleep(d)` on the scheduler argument; `rt.AfterSpan` races it against the body and drains both before it returns. So the deadline is the time the program's `Scheduler` gives: a test `Scheduler` decides when it passes, and the timeout test needs no real sleep. `v1.After` arms no host timer of its own. Its failure is `v1.Expired`, the `runtime/v1` struct that `timeout.Expired` binds (Types 4 below). A std extern that is neither an intrinsic nor a `runtime/v1` body is an internal compiler error, because `TestStdExternsHaveBodies` proves that none exists. `v1.BodyOf` (runtime/v1/files.go, compiler only) reads a body's shape from the embedded source, as the lowering reads it; `TestBodiesFollowTheHiddenConvention` pins each body's shape, and the test of `internal/sketch/std` ties each extern and operation to its body. *Prior art:* Gleam's `@external(erlang, "m", "f")` and ReScript's `external f: t = "f"` bind a host function by name with the signature the source states and pass nothing hidden; Go's own `iter.Seq` carries its stop through `yield`; Effect passes its fiber context implicitly through the runtime; Kotlin passes a hidden `Continuation` as the last parameter of a `suspend` function, the closest relative of the hidden task here.

**Types.** Each Ergo type in a std signature has one Go type, which the body writes and the lowering must use:

1. A basic type is its Go type (`int` is `int`), a type parameter keeps its name, and `[]T` is a Go slice.
2. A row parameter (`E`, `R` in `task.Go[T, E, R]`) has no Go type parameter: rows are erased (rows.md), so the body is `Go[T any]`, and `Task[T, E]` is `*Task[T]`. The type parameters left after erasure are the Go ones, with the same names in order and the same constraint (none or `any` is `any`, `comparable` is `comparable`, `ordered` is `runtime/v1`'s `ordered`, whose terms are Go's `cmp.Ordered`'s and the lowering's generated set's), so the checker admits a type argument exactly when the Go call compiles: `slices.WithCapacity[[]int](3)` is valid on both sides, and so is `slices.Sort` of a slice of a type parameter `T ordered`. An operation takes the type parameters of its type (`Queue[T]`, `Task[T, E]`). An error row is the last Go result `error`; a capability row is captured by the lowered block, never passed.
3. A runtime-backed operation type (`queue.Queue[T]`, the prelude's `Task[T, E]`, `http.Exchange`) declares an unexported operation. The ordinary visibility rule seals it: only a type of its package implements it ([grammar.md](grammar.md#visibility), rule 2). Its declaration names the runtime's concrete Go type (`extern go ergo.local/prototype/runtime/v1.Queue type Queue[T] { … }`; [The body contract](#the-body-contract)), in `runtime/v1` or the leaf that declares it (`extern go ergo.local/prototype/runtime/v1/http.Exchange type Exchange { … }`; binsize BS1), and the lowering binds it to a pointer to that type (`*v1.Queue[T]`, `*v1.Exchange`; the prelude's `Task[T, E]` is `*v1.Task[T]`) and emits no interface. Backend bindings do not decide source assignability. An operation type with only exported operations (`Console`, `os.Process`) admits a program provider. A sealed type implemented in Ergo (`http.Streaming`) still lowers as a program interface. A type defined over a std operation type and a pointer to one remain S108 (types.md, Defined types 1 and Operation types).
4. A record type whose declaration names a `runtime/v1` struct, `extern go ergo.local/prototype/runtime/v1.Closed type Closed {}`, is that struct, with the same fields: `queue.Closed` is `v1.Closed`. The lowering never generates a struct for it, because `Push` and `Pop` return `v1.Closed{}`, and a `catch` arm `case queue.Closed:` must match that value. An error type's Go text is its Ergo name: `v1.Closed{}.Error()` is `queue.Closed`. The declaration is the binding (STD-NOPRIV NP4c), never a table keyed by the record's name, so two packages may each declare a `NotFound` and name two Go types (`v1.NotFound`, `v1.ExecNotFound`). A record whose declaration names no Go type, such as `strings.Halves`, is a declaration of the program. The checker holds the declaration to the Go type: the package is trusted, the Go type is an exported type of `runtime/v1` with the declaration's type parameters, a record's struct has its fields in order, and an operation type that names one is sealed (`TestRuntimeTypeDeclarations`). `TestStdRecordTable` holds no Go type to two declarations, and every `runtime/v1` struct with an `Error` method to a std record, so a `catch` arm can name each failure a body returns; lower `TestStdRecordsKeyedByPackage` lowers two std packages that each declare `NotFound` and catches each in its own arm. Extern bodies need no table: an extern names its Go body in its target (`v1.StringsSplit`), and Go needs that name to be unique in `runtime/v1`, so a body name carries its package (`Strings`, `Filepath`, `FS`).
5. The prelude's `Option[T]` as the result of an operation of a std type is Go's comma-ok `(T, bool)`: `queue.Queue[T]`'s `Poll() Option[T]` is `Poll() (T, bool)` (std-align S3d). `Option` is a union the program emits, so no `runtime/v1` body can name it; the lowering calls the method and adapts its results in place to `Some{Value: v}` when the bool is true and to `None{}` when it is false, through the helper that also lowers the prelude's `Get` (`optionReturn`), so a later change of `Option`'s Go form changes both. The `None` path allocates nothing (`TestQueuePollNoneAllocatesNothing`). A `(T, bool)` body for any other Ergo result is an internal compiler error. *Prior art:* Go's `v, ok := m[k]` and `v, ok := <-ch`; ReScript lowers `None` to JavaScript's `undefined` and `Some(v)` to `v` with no box, which Go has no general form of; Gleam lowers its `Option` as an ordinary custom type (on Erlang the atom `none` and the tuple `{some, V}`), and its Erlang externals return that form themselves.
6. A defined type over a basic type, a refinement too, whose declaration names a `runtime/v1` defined type over the same Go basic type is that Go type: `os.Code` is `v1.Code`, `type Code int` in Go, so `v1.Exit`'s field and the program's `os.Code` values are one Go type. The runtime type may be an alias of the basic type too: `fs.Text` names `v1.MappedText`, `= string`, so a text is the Go string of its Mapped, and the borrow rule (types.md, Borrows 8) is the rule of every type whose declaration names `MappedText`, never of the path `fs` (STD-COPY-SAME). The predicate stays Ergo's: the checker checks constants and `ParseCode` checks a computed `int`, as for any refinement. A binding's host type takes no `where` (Go checks nothing on it); a declaration that names a runtime type does, because Ergo checks it (`syntax.IsRuntimeTarget`).

The std test compares each Ergo type parameter, parameter and result type with its body's Go one under these rules (`TestEachExternHasItsGoBody`), and ties each record of a v1 package to its Go struct and text (`TestStdTypesBindRuntimeTypes`, through `v1.StructFields`). A record that names no Go type and that no extern of its package returns or fails with (`task.Done`, which `task.ExitOf`'s Ergo body builds) is the program's declaration, as a union of the package is: no Go body can make one, so no `catch` arm or `match` waits for a runtime value of it.

**Slices and Maps.** `slices.WithCapacity[T](n)` is an empty slice with room for `n` elements; it makes no element, and a negative `n` is a defect, as Go's `make` panics. Its body is pure ([Pure bodies](#pure-bodies); FUSION), so `slices.Transform`, which calls it, is pure over a pure function. There is no `slices.Repeat`: not every type has a zero value (parse-v1 ruling 7). `slices.AppendAll(xs, ys)` is `xs` with the elements of `ys` at the end, one bulk append: it is `extern go append` at the shape `[T](xs []T, ys []T) []T`, which any module can declare (OPT4b), and the value rule treats it as `Append` (in place for `xs = AppendAll(xs, ys)` on one place or a dead `xs`, a clipped `xs` elsewhere; `ys` is read, and copied only when its elements hold a Map or slice; `TestBuiltinsAgree`). The prelude's `xs.Append(x)` returns the slice with `x` at the end, `m.Get(k)` returns `Option[V]`, `m.Delete(k)` returns the Map without `k` (check-v1 D-D6), and `m.Put(k, v)` returns the Map with `k` set to `v`, an entry added or replaced (item 13, L2). They are prelude functions, `func Append[T](xs []T, x T) []T`, `func Get[K comparable, V](m Map[K, V], k K) Option[V]`, `func Delete[K comparable, V](m Map[K, V], k K) Map[K, V]` and `func Put[K comparable, V](m Map[K, V], k K, v V) Map[K, V]`, reached by the dot form of a function on a type ([types.md, Methods](types.md#methods)) from a value whose type is exactly an unnamed slice or Map type; the bare call `Append(xs, x)` is S104 naming `xs.Append(x)`. `Append` takes one element. A defined type over a slice reaches `Append` after assignment ([types.md, Assignability](types.md#assignability) clause 2): `let raw []int = xs`, then `raw.Append(x)`; a defined Map uses `let raw Map[string, int] = m`, then `raw.Get(k)`, `raw.Delete(k)` or `raw.Put(k, v)` (check-v1 D-D8). `Append`, `Delete` and `Put` have Go bodies in data.go. `Get` builds the program's own `Option`, so its body is lowered in the program, as the comma-ok read `v1.Get` is. All four are pure ([Pure bodies](#pure-bodies) 5). A slice and a Map are values ([types.md, Bindings](types.md#bindings); owner q26, 2026-11-08): `xs.Append(x)`, `m.Delete(k)` and `m.Put(k, v)` leave `xs` and `m` unchanged. The lowering writes in place for `xs = xs.Append(x)`, `m = m.Delete(k)` and `m = m.Put(k, v)` on one place, and for a source that is dead after the call; elsewhere `Append` appends to a clipped slice, so Go's `append` makes a new array, and `Delete` and `Put` write a copy of the Map. `Put` is the index write `m[k] = v` as an expression: it needs no `let mut` root, so a pure callback such as `sync.Ref`'s `Update` returns `entries.Put(k, v)`. `Put` copies its value `v` only as any argument is copied (a kept read of a `let mut` root that is written later, [types.md, Bindings](types.md#bindings) 2): a value inside a Map has no write path (a Map read is `Get`, S108 on `m[k]`), so a value `Put` stores is never written through the Map, and `Put` needs no copy of `v` where `Append`'s element needs one. *Prior art:* Go `make([]T, 0, n)` and `append`; Elixir's `Map.put/3`, Gleam's `dict.insert` and ReScript's `Belt.Map.set` return a new map, as `Put` does, and BEAM's maps share the unchanged part where Ergo writes in place on a rebinding; Gleam's `list` module and Elixir's `Enum` keep collection functions out of the prelude.

**Context values: trace and log.** Two packages make and read the context values of [runtime.md, G1](runtime.md#g1-context-through-with) (stage 4a). They are the first std packages with Ergo bodies; the lowering emits those bodies as it emits a user package's.

| Package | Exported declarations | Key | Go body of the read |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `trace` | `func Span(name string) span`, the value `with trace.Span("checkout") { … }` binds; `func Current() Option[string]`, the innermost span's name or `None` | (`trace`, `span`) | `ContextBound(t *rt.Task, pkg ContextPackage, key string) bool` and `ContextValue(t *rt.Task, pkg ContextPackage, key string) string` (context.go), through unexported externs |
| `log` | `func Annotate(key string, text string) annotation`; `func Annotation(key string) Option[string]` | (`log`, the annotation's key) | the same |

Each value's type (`span`, `annotation`) is an unexported record with the protocol's unexported functions `contextKey` and `contextText` on it, so it binds by the rule every type uses and the packages export only the four declarations above. A read has empty rows and is effectful ([rows.md, Footprints](rows.md#footprints)). The rest of the trace and log APIs is the standard library. The lowering opens and ends a region with `Scope.OpenContext(pkg, key, text string) *Context` and `Context.End()` (context.go), which only the lowering calls. *Prior art:* OpenTelemetry's `Tracer.start_as_current_span` and Elixir's `Logger.metadata` set a value for the running process; Java's `ScopedValue` scopes it to a block, as `with` does.

## strings and strconv

Std `strings` and `strconv` work on text (wave std-1, unit G5a). They are pure functions: no operation type and no capability. Each export is an `extern go` of a `runtime/v1` body in `text.go` that calls the Go function of the same name, under the convention of [v1 packages](#v1-packages-task-scope-queue-and-slice): no hidden argument, a `runtime/v1` Go name with the std package as prefix (`StringsSplit`, `StrconvAtoi`), because Go needs each name of `runtime/v1` to be unique (Types 4), and each states `//ergo:wait none` and no release route ([Hidden arguments](#v1-packages-task-scope-queue-and-slice), Calling policy). `TestStdTextRuns` runs each function.

| Package | Signatures |
| --- | --- |
| `strings` | `Split(s, sep string) []string`, `SplitN(s, sep string, n int) []string`, `Join(xs []string, sep string) string`, `HasPrefix`, `HasSuffix`, `Contains(s, x string) bool`, `ContainsRune(s string, r rune) bool`, `EqualFold(s, t string) bool`, `TrimSpace(s string) string`, `TrimPrefix`, `TrimSuffix(s, x string) string`, `TrimLeft`, `TrimRight(s, cutset string) string`, `Fields(s string) []string`, `Repeat(s string, n int) string`, `ReplaceAll(s, old, repl string) string`, `ToLower`, `ToUpper(s string) string`, `Index`, `LastIndex`, `Count(s, sub string) int`, `IndexByte(s string, c byte) int`, `Slice(s string, i int, j int) string`, `Lines(s string) Seq[string]`; `type Halves { Before string, After string }`, `func Cut(s, sep string) Option[Halves]`; `type Builder`, `NewBuilder() Builder`, `WriteString(b *Builder, s string)`, `WriteByte(b *Builder, c byte)`, `Grow(b *Builder, n int)`, `Len(b Builder) int`, `String(b Builder) string` (rule 10); `WriteBytes(b *Builder, bs []byte)`, `WriteFrom(b *Builder, start int, n int)`, `Bytes(s string) []byte`, `Hint[W, T]() int`, `Borrow(hint int) []byte`, `Give(out []byte, hint int) []byte`, `FromBytes(b []byte) string` (rules 11, 12) |
| `strconv` | `type Malformed { Text string }`, `type OutOfRange { Text string }`; `Itoa(n int) string`, `Atoi(s string) int fails Malformed \| OutOfRange`, `ParseInt(s string, base int, bits int) int64 fails Malformed \| OutOfRange`, `ParseFloat(s string) float64 fails Malformed \| OutOfRange`, `ParseBool(s string) bool fails Malformed`, `FormatBool(b bool) string`, `FormatFloat(f float64, fmt byte, prec int) string`, `AppendInt(out []byte, n int64, base int) []byte`, `AppendUint(out []byte, n uint64, base int) []byte`, `AppendFloat(out []byte, f float64, fmt byte, prec int) []byte` (appenders: `t.out = AppendInt(t.out, n, 10)` appends in place, as std json's writer does; any package may), `Quote(s string) string`, `Unquote(s string) string fails Malformed` |
| `utf8` | `RuneCount(s string) int`; `type Decoded { Rune rune, Size int }`, `DecodeRuneInString(s string) Decoded`; `RuneText(r rune) string`, `AppendRune(p []byte, r rune) []byte`, `String(b []byte) string`, `ValidString(s string) bool`, `ValidRune(r rune) bool`, `RuneLen(r rune) int` |
| `strings`, `strconv` (std-text-runes) | `strings.ToValidUTF8(s string, replacement string) string`; `strconv.FormatInt(n int64, base int) string` |
| `strings`, `utf8` (SQ5 strings-gaps) | `strings.IndexAny`, `strings.LastIndexAny(s string, chars string) int`; `strings.TrimFunc[R](s string, cut func(r rune) bool needs R) string needs R`; `strings.FieldsFunc[R](s string, sep func(r rune) bool needs R) []string needs R`; `utf8.IsDigit`, `utf8.IsLetter`, `utf8.IsSpace(r rune) bool` |
| `strings` (std-strings-pad) | `strings.Compare(a string, b string) int`; `strings.PadLeft(s string, width int, pad rune) string`; `strings.PadRight(s string, width int, pad rune) string` |

1. Lengths and indexes are bytes, as `len(s)` is. Ergo has no substring syntax yet (`s[i..j]` is an index by a `Range`, S108), so `strings.Slice(s, i, j)` is Go's `s[i:j]`. A bound outside `0..len(s)`, or an `i` after `j`, is a defect, as an index out of range is; so is a negative `n` in `Repeat`.
2. `Cut` is Ergo over `Index` and `Slice`: `Some` of the text before and after the first `sep`, or `None` when `s` has no `sep`. A `sep` at index 0 gives an empty `Before`.
3. Go's one `*NumError` is two failures (std-align S1c): `Malformed` for a text the function cannot read (Go's `ErrSyntax`) and `OutOfRange` for a number that does not fit its type (Go's `ErrRange`), so a caller tells "not a number" from "too big" with a `match`. `Atoi`, `ParseInt` and `ParseFloat` fail either; `ParseBool` and `Unquote` fail `Malformed`. `ParseInt` takes Go's base rules (0 reads the prefix `0b`, `0o`, `0x` or `0` and allows `_`); a base outside 0 and 2..36, or a bit size outside 0..64, is a defect, as a negative `Repeat` count is. Both records are `runtime/v1` structs (`v1.Malformed`, `v1.OutOfRange`; Types 4), with the texts `strconv.Malformed{Text:…}` and `strconv.OutOfRange{Text:…}`.
4. Each call is transparent in footprints: every body in `text.go` is marked pure ([Pure bodies](#pure-bodies)).
5. `slices.SortFunc` and `SortStableFunc` (follow-up G5c) are in [Slices, maps and cmp](#slices-maps-and-cmp).
6. `Lines` is Go 1.24's `strings.Lines`: each line keeps its line feed. It is Ergo over `IndexByte` and `Slice`, because a pure Go body cannot import `iter`; `IndexByte` is Go's, which inlines, so a line costs no call into `Index`'s switch on the length of its sub (std-speed-1). `io.Input`'s `Lines` uses it too. `SplitN` with `n == 0` gives an empty slice where Go gives nil. `utf8.RuneCount` counts each byte of an invalid sequence as one rune, as Go does.
7. The algebra of `strings`, `strconv` and `utf8` is in the [Std laws](#std-laws) ledger: split and join, count and split, cut, index, case and trim idempotence, repeat and replace lengths, lines, and the strconv round trips. Each law runs in place on its own source (`go run ./scripts/ergotests -std`), drawing texts from the law's generators; the laws over Ergo bodies (`Cut`, `Lines`) run in lower `TestStdTextRuns2`. Two of the plan's laws are false and are stated in a true form: "ReplaceAll removes old" fails on `ReplaceAll("aabb", "ab", "")`, which is `"ab"`, so the law is `ReplaceAllLength`; "a text equals its case maps under folding" fails on `"İ"`, whose lower case `"i"` does not fold to it, so the law is `EqualFoldIsSymmetric`.
8. **Runes** (std-text-runes). `utf8.DecodeRuneInString(s)` is the first rune of `s` and its size, Go's two results as the record `Decoded`: an invalid byte is `Decoded{Rune: '\uFFFD', Size: 1}` and an empty text `Size` 0, so a loop that moves on by `Size` reads every byte once and ends (law `DecodeMovesOn`). `RuneText(r)` is Go's `string(r)`, `"\uFFFD"` for a rune that is not valid (a surrogate, a negative rune, one past U+10FFFF); `ValidRune`, `RuneLen` (-1 for an invalid rune), `ValidString` and `AppendRune` are Go's. `AppendRune` takes and gives Go's `[]byte`; Ergo has no conversion between `[]byte` and `string`, so `String(b)` is the text of a buffer an appender wrote, Go's `string(b)` (GX9: a Recorder's SendBytes body; GX10: a codec pair's appended form). `strings.ToValidUTF8(s, r)` replaces each run of invalid bytes with `r`, once per run, as Go's does. `strconv.FormatInt(n, base)` writes `n` in a base 2 to 36 with Go's digits; a base outside that is a defect, as Go's panics. The laws `DecodeInvertsRuneText`, `InvalidRuneTextIsReplacement`, `DecodeMovesOn`, `ToValidUTF8IsValid` and `ParseIntInvertsFormatInt` run in place on their own source (`ergotests -std`); `TestStdRunesMatchGo` holds each body to Go's function on 10,000 texts with every rune length, U+FFFD and invalid bytes (a lone continuation byte, cut 2-, 3- and 4-byte sequences, an overlong form, a surrogate's bytes, `0xff`, one past U+10FFFF) and runes of every class; lower `TestStdRunesRuns` runs each function from an Ergo program.
9. **Padding and order** (std-strings-pad). `strings.PadLeft(s, width, pad)` is `s` after copies of the rune `pad`, so that it is `width` runes wide, and `PadRight` puts them after `s`; a text as wide as `width` or wider, and a negative width, pad nothing. Width counts runes as Go's `fmt` width does, so `PadRight(s, 8, ' ')` is `fmt.Sprintf("%-8s", s)` and `PadLeft(s, 8, ' ')` is `"%8s"`, for a multi-byte rune and an invalid byte (one rune each) too. A `pad` that is not a valid rune is written U+FFFD, as `utf8.RuneText` gives it. Interpolation has no width (`"\(x)"` takes no `%-8s`): that is a grammar change. `strings.Compare(a, b)` is Go's: 0, -1 or +1 by bytes. The laws `PadWidth`, `PadKeepsText` and `CompareIsAntisymmetric` run in place on their own source (`ergotests -std`); `PadLeft` and `PadRight` are Ergo bodies, so the law runs the code a program runs. Effect's `String.padStart` and `padEnd` count UTF-16 code units, and Gleam's `string.pad_start` counts graphemes; Ergo counts runes, as Go's `fmt` does.
10. **Trims and the builder** (STD-sh). `strings.TrimLeft(s, cutset)` and `TrimRight` are Go's: `cutset` is a set of runes, not a prefix or suffix (`TrimPrefix` and `TrimSuffix` remove one). `strings.Builder` builds a text by appending to one buffer, as Go's `strings.Builder`: `let mut b = strings.NewBuilder()`, `b.Grow(n)`, `b.WriteString(s)`, `b.WriteByte(c)`, `b.Len()`, `b.String()`. Its one field is unexported, so a program builds it with `NewBuilder` only (types.md, Zero values 6). The writes take the builder by borrow and lower to Go's `append` on the buffer, in place (types.md, Bindings 2, the hand-over of a pure std body), so a loop of writes allocates what Go's Builder does; `Grow` makes room as Go's does, and a negative count is a defect, as Go panics. The writes return nothing: Go's `WriteString` and `WriteByte` return an error that is always nil. Go's Builder panics when a copy of it is written after a write (its copy check); here a Builder is a value (types.md, Bindings 1), so a copy is its own builder and a write to one never reaches the other: the rule holds by construction, with no check at run time and no diagnostic. `String` on a builder that nothing reads after the call shares its bytes, as Go's does through `unsafe.String`: its receiver is consumed (types.md, Bindings 2, consumed arguments), and its body calls the reviewed owned form `StringsBuilderTextOwned` (internal/residue/testdata/unsafe.txt). A builder read again after `String` is copied first, so the text and the builder never share a byte that a write can reach; no Builder operation writes below the buffer's length. The laws `TrimLeftRightKeepEnds` and `BuilderConcatenates` run in lower `TestStdTextRuns2`; runtime/v1 `TestStdRunesMatchGo` holds the trims and the builder's bodies to Go's `strings.TrimLeft`, `TrimRight` and `strings.Builder` on 10,000 seeded cases. Effect's `String.trimStart` and `trimEnd` remove white space only; a text builder in Effect is an array of parts and `join`, which is the workaround this type replaces.
11. **Bytes and the builder's own text** (SB2). `strings.Bytes(s)` is the bytes of `s`, a copy, as Go's `[]byte(s)`; `strings.FromBytes(bs)` is the text of `bs`, as Go's `string(bs)`. `FromBytes`'s parameter is owned (types.md, Bindings 2, consumed arguments): its body calls the reviewed owned form `StringsBuilderTextOwned`, so a `[]byte` that nothing reads after the call becomes the text with no copy, where Go's `string(bs)` always copies, and one read again, or shared with a binding read again, is copied first; a later write to the bytes never reaches the text (law `BytesRoundTrip`, which writes through the binding it read and through one that shared it). `b.WriteBytes(bs)` adds the bytes of `bs`, as Go's `Builder.Write`. `b.WriteFrom(start, n)` adds `n` bytes copied from `b`'s own text at `start` on, as an LZ77 decoder copies a match: byte `k` of the `n` is the byte at `start+k` of the text after the call, so a run that reaches past the end repeats what the call wrote (`WriteFrom(b.Len()-1, n)` repeats the last byte); law `WriteFromRepeats` holds it to a loop of single bytes. Go's Builder has no such write: a Go program copies from its own `[]byte`, with one `append` for a run it holds and a byte loop for one that overlaps. `WriteFrom` does the first in one compare and one `append`, and copies an overlapping run in passes that each copy all the run written so far, so a run of `n` bytes at distance `d` takes about log2(n/d)+1 appends. A negative `n`, a start outside the text, or a start at its end with `n > 0` is a defect, with Go's slice or index error. runtime/v1 `TestBuilderAppendFromCounts` counts user-space instructions on the thread (never time): a held run at most 1.1 times hand Go's `append` (2026-11-13: 1.03) and an overlapping run of 60 bytes at distance 3 at most 0.5 times hand Go's byte loop (0.43). The two laws run in lower `TestStdTextRuns2`. Receipt (corpus-wave ST, SB2-request.md): an LZ77 decode of generated tokens, 137 user instructions per token against hand Go's 130 (1.06×; the rest is the Builder record kept in memory across its inlined writes), 0.86× its bytes allocated (the text is the buffer, where Go's `string(out)` copies), and 5.1× fewer instructions than the 64 KiB ring that a program kept before `WriteFrom` (lzpack).
12. **A writer's borrowed buffer** (NOPRIV-JSON). A writer that builds a text in a `[]byte` (a codec's provider, std json's `text`, a module's own) takes the size hint of its writer type and value type, `let hint = strings.Hint[W, T]()`, borrows an empty buffer with `strings.Borrow(hint)`, appends to it, takes the text with `strings.FromBytes(b)`, a copy since `Give` reads the buffer after (rule 11), and gives the buffer back in place, `t.out = strings.Give(t.out, hint)`; nothing reads or writes the buffer after. The bodies (`runtime/v1/buffer.go`) keep the buffers in `sync.Pool`s of arrays of the powers of two from 1 KiB to 4 MiB, as Go's encoding/json keeps its `encodeState`, so a value encoded again allocates its text and no buffer. The hint is the length of the pair's last text (NOPRIV-HINT): `Borrow` takes an array of the least size that holds it and `Give` puts an array back in the pool of the largest size it holds, so a large text reuses its buffer, and an encode after a GC emptied the pools (two GCs empty a `sync.Pool`) allocates one buffer of at most twice its size instead of regrowing a small one by append. A number `Hint` never gave is no hint. Go's encoding/json has one pool for every type, and its encode after a GC regrows from a small buffer. `Borrow`'s result is new memory and `Give` is an appender (it gives its operand), so the value rules write to the buffer in place and copy nothing (types.md, Bindings 2). Every module borrows from the same pool: std json's `Encode` does, and so does `examples/cborpack`'s CBOR codec, whose receipt counts the same allocations as std json's. Go's own pool is not reachable from Ergo (a `sync.Pool` holds `any`); Effect has no buffer pool (a text is built by `join`).
13. **A short quote stays on the stack** (B1-Q). `strconv.Quote`'s `runtime/v1` body is Go's own `Quote` written out, `string(strconv.AppendQuote(make([]byte, 0, 3*len(s)/2), s))`, which costs 78 in Go's inliner: a body that called `strconv.Quote` cost 84, past the budget of 80, so the call stayed out of line and its result escaped. Inlined into its caller, a quote whose buffer is 32 bytes or less (a text of up to 21 bytes with no escapes) that does not leave the caller is built on the stack, as in hand Go (`TestStrconvQuoteStaysOnStack`; the CAP-bytes probe's allocations per byte equal its control's).
14. **Rune sets and classes** (SQ5 strings-gaps; dray's gap G8). `strings.IndexAny(s, chars)` and `LastIndexAny` are the byte offsets of the first and last rune of `s` that `chars` holds, or -1; `TrimFunc(s, cut)` is `s` without its leading and trailing runes `cut` accepts, and `FieldsFunc(s, sep)` the runs of `s` between runes `sep` accepts, none when it accepts all. Each reads `s` as a `for` over it does: a byte that begins no valid rune is U+FFFD, which `chars` holds when it has U+FFFD or such a byte (law `IndexAnyFinds`, in lower `TestStdTextRuns2`). The function argument may need any row, which the call then needs, and may be called on a rune more than once, as Go's. `utf8.IsDigit`, `IsLetter` and `IsSpace` are Go's `unicode` classes: a digit is class Nd (so `'٣'` is one), a letter category L, and a space `'\t'`, `'\n'`, `'\v'`, `'\f'`, `'\r'`, `' '`, U+0085, U+00A0 and class Z. All six call Go's functions through `import go`, as a user binds them, and the check-time evaluator has a native of each (eval `TestEveryPureStdExternHasANative`; the two that take a function call it through the evaluator, rune by rune as Go does). An Ergo body of `TrimFunc` counted 3.296x Go's and one of `FieldsFunc` 1.044x (batch-24 SQ5 receipt), so the Go bodies stay; lower `TestStdMatchesGo` holds TrimFunc, FieldsFunc and the classes to Go's on seeded texts and runes, invalid ones included. Effect's `String` has no rune sets (its predicates are JavaScript's `/\d/` and friends, by UTF-16 unit); Gleam's `string` has `trim` only, and Elixir's `String.split/3` takes a pattern.

*Prior art:* Gleam's `gleam/string` and ReScript's `Js.String2` bind host text functions as direct externals with no wrapper; Elixir's `String` and `Integer.parse/1` (`{n, rest} | :error`) are plain BEAM calls. Ergo binds Go's `strings` and `strconv` the same way, and gives `Atoi` a typed failure in its row in place of Go's `error`.

## Slices, maps and cmp

Std `slices`, `maps` and `cmp` (std-align S2a) follow Go's packages of the same names for names, argument order and meaning, and Effect's `Array` and `Order` where Go has no function. The std package `slice` is now `slices`, with no alias. `TestStdSlicesRuns` runs each function; the laws are in `slices/laws.ergo`, `maps/maps.ergo` and `cmp/cmp.ergo`, with their rows in [Std laws](#std-laws).

| Package | Signatures | Go body |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `slices` | `WithCapacity[T](n int) []T`; `AppendAll[T](xs []T, ys []T) []T` (an extern of Go's `append`, `append(xs, ys...)`); `Sort[T ordered](xs []T) []T`; `SortFunc[T](xs []T, compare func(a T, b T) int) []T`; `SortStableFunc` (the same signature); `Contains[T comparable](xs []T, x T) bool`; `Index[T comparable](xs []T, x T) int`; `Reverse[T](xs []T) []T`; `Equal[T comparable](a []T, b []T) bool` | `runtime/v1` collections.go and data.go |
| `slices` | `IndexFunc[T, E, R](xs []T, f func(x T) bool fails E needs R) int fails E needs R`; `Max[T ordered](xs []T) Option[T]`, `Min`; `Last[T](xs []T) Option[T]`; `type Ascending[T ordered] { xs []T }`; `Ascend[T ordered](xs []T) Ascending[T]`; `Items[T ordered](a Ascending[T]) []T`; `BinarySearch[T ordered](a Ascending[T], x T) Option[int]`; `Collect[T](s Seq[T]) []T`; `Sorted[T ordered](s Seq[T]) []T`; `Values[T](xs []T) Seq[T]`; `Transform[T, U, E, R](xs []T, f func(x T) U fails E needs R) []U fails E needs R`; `Filter[T, E, R](xs []T, keep func(x T) bool fails E needs R) []T fails E needs R`; `Fold[T, A, E, R](xs []T, init A, f func(acc A, x T) A fails E needs R) A fails E needs R`; `TransformFilter[T, U, E, R](xs []T, f func(x T) U fails E needs R, keep func(x U) bool fails E needs R) []U fails E needs R` and `FilterTransform[T, U, E, R](xs []T, keep func(x T) bool fails E needs R, f func(x T) U fails E needs R) []U fails E needs R` (the one loops of the fusion laws, [types.md](types.md#laws) Laws 14) | Ergo |
| `slices` | `Clone[T](xs []T) []T`; `Concat[T](a []T, b []T) []T`; `Insert[T](xs []T, i int, ys []T) []T`; `Delete[T](xs []T, i int, j int) []T`; `Repeat[T](xs []T, n int) []T`; `Slice[T](xs []T, i int, j int) []T` | `runtime/v1` collections.go |
| `slices` | `Len[T ordered](a Ascending[T]) int`; `At[T ordered](a Ascending[T], i int) T` | Ergo |
| `maps` | `Keys[K comparable, V](m Map[K, V]) Seq[K]`; `Values[K comparable, V](m Map[K, V]) Seq[V]`; `GetOr[K comparable, V](m Map[K, V], k K, def V) V`; `Update[K comparable, V, E, R](m Map[K, V], k K, f func(old Option[V]) V fails E needs R) Map[K, V] fails E needs R` | Ergo |
| `maps` | `Clone[K comparable, V](m Map[K, V]) Map[K, V]` | `runtime/v1` collections.go |
| `slices` | `FilterMap[T, U, E, R](xs []T, f func(x T) Option[U] fails E needs R) []U fails E needs R`; `Find`, `Any`, `All` (`accept func(x T) bool`, results `Option[T]`, `bool`, `bool`); `Partition[T, E, R](xs []T, keep …) ([]T, []T)`; `Dedupe[T comparable](xs []T) []T`; `Chunks[T](xs []T, n int) [][]T`; `Windows[T](xs []T, n int) [][]T`; `GroupBy[T, K comparable, E, R](xs []T, key func(x T) K …) Map[K, []T]`; `FlatMap[T, U, E, R](xs []T, f func(x T) []U …) []U`; `Zip[A, B](a []A, b []B) [](A, B)` | Ergo |
| `maps` | `Merge[K comparable, V](a Map[K, V], b Map[K, V]) Map[K, V]`; `Filter[K comparable, V, E, R](m Map[K, V], keep func(k K, v V) bool …) Map[K, V]`; `TransformValues[K comparable, V, U, E, R](m Map[K, V], f func(v V) U …) Map[K, U]`; `Entries[K ordered, V](m Map[K, V]) [](K, V)` | Ergo |
| `set` | `type Set[K comparable]` (unexported fields); `New`, `Of(xs []K)`, `Add`, `Remove`, `Has`, `Len`, `Values(s) Seq[K]`, `Sorted[K ordered](s) []K`, `Union`, `Intersection`, `Difference`, `IsSubset`, `Equal` | Ergo |
| `cmp` | `Compare[T ordered](a T, b T) int`; `Less[T ordered](a T, b T) bool`; `Max`, `Min[T ordered](a T, b T) T` (Go's builtin `max` and `min`: for floats a NaN gives NaN, `Max(-0, +0)` is +0 and `Min` is -0) | Ergo (Compare, Less); `runtime/v1` collections.go (Max, Min) |
| `cmp` | `type Order[T] { Compare(a T, b T) int }` with laws `Reflexive`, `Antisymmetric`, `Transitive`; `type Natural[T ordered] {}`, an `Order[T]` by `Compare`; `type Reverse[T any, O Order[T]] { Of O }`, an `Order[T]`; `Then(first int, next int) int` | Ergo |
| `slices` | `SortBy[T any, O cmp.Order[T]](xs []T, by O) []T`; `IsSortedBy[T any, O cmp.Order[T]](xs []T, by O) bool` | Ergo |

1. **A new slice, never a changed one.** `Sort`, `SortFunc`, `SortStableFunc` and `Reverse` return a new slice and leave their argument unchanged, where Go's change it in place: a `let` is immutable, and aliasing is visible (plan owner question 3; law `SortLeavesItsArgument`). The copy is made only where it is needed: where no binding reads the argument after the call (a fresh slice, a local read no more, an owned parameter), `Sort` and `Reverse` sort or reverse its own array in place, as Go's do, through their runtime/v1 owned forms `SlicesSortOwned` and `SlicesReverseOwned` (types.md, Bindings 2, consumed arguments); a live argument, or one a second binding shares, is copied first, where Go's call silently changes every alias. `Sorted` is `Sort(Collect(s))`, so the slice it collects is sorted in place by the same rule (SB4). Receipt (SB4, generated slices of 64 ints, per element): `Reverse` of a dead slice no longer copies: 16 to 8 bytes allocated per element (the 8 is the slice itself, which `WithCapacity` puts on the heap where hand Go's `make` stays on the stack) and 62.4 to 50.2 user instructions per element, against hand Go's in-place `slices.Reverse` at 41.3. `SortFunc` and `SortStableFunc` still copy: their runtime/v1 bodies take a function, and the owned form is for a pure body (a follow-up for the lowering; an Ergo body over an in-place extern removed the copy but cost 300 to 384 instructions per element, through a second closure around `compare`).
2. **Option, never a panic or a second result.** `Max` and `Min` of an empty slice are `None`, where Go's panic. `BinarySearch` is `Some` of the index of the first `x`, or `None`, where Go returns `(int, bool)`. Each is an Ergo body over a `runtime/v1` body that the Ergo body calls only where Go's answer is defined.
3. **Callbacks.** `Transform`, `Filter`, `Fold` and `IndexFunc` take a function with a row and pass the row on, as `task.Go` does: an effectful or failing callback is allowed, and its first failure ends the call. `Transform` is Effect's `Array.map`: `Map` is the prelude's type, which no package may redeclare, so the name is the one types.md, Generics, uses. The comparator of `SortFunc` and `SortStableFunc` has an empty row, as Go's has no error: the lowering adapts the Ergo function value to Go's `func(a, b T) int` (`lower/std.go`, `adaptPlainFunc`), and its error, which a value with no error row never returns, is dropped. A comparator must be a strict weak order, as Go's `SortFunc` needs; a comparator that is not one gives an unspecified order and no defect, as in Go.
4. **Order.** `ordered` is Ergo's constraint: the integer, float and string types, `duration`, and the types defined over them; its Go form is `runtime/v1`'s `ordered`, whose terms are Go's `cmp.Ordered`'s. `cmp.Compare` is a total order on floats too: a NaN is less than every other value and equal to a NaN, and `Sort` puts a NaN first, as Go does. std's `cmp.Compare` and `cmp.Less` are Ergo bodies, and lower's `TestStdMatchesGo` holds them to Go's.
5. **Map order.** `maps.Keys` and `maps.Values` are Seqs in an unspecified order, as a `for` over a Map is ([types.md, Loops and len](types.md#loops-and-len) 1). `slices.Sorted(maps.Keys(m))` gives a fixed order, which a golden test needs.
6. **Purity.** The Go bodies of `Sort`, `Contains`, `Index`, `Reverse`, `Equal`, `Compare`, `Less` and the halves of `Max`, `Min` and `BinarySearch` are marked pure ([Pure bodies](#pure-bodies)). `SortFunc` and `SortStableFunc` take a function, so they are not, and neither is the body behind `Sorted`, which sorts the slice it has just collected in place.
7. **Laws and runners.** `TestStdSlicesLawsRun` (lower) restates each law as an Ergo property test and runs it with `--seed 1`. Under it, `TestStdSlicesLaws` (runtime/v1) checks the laws that a Go body decides over 10,000 seeded cases each, on `[]int` and `[]string` with empty, one-element and duplicate-heavy slices, the extremes of `int`, multi-byte UTF-8 and invalid bytes.
8. **A sorted slice is a type** (std-strengths SS5). `BinarySearch` takes an `Ascending[T]`, not Go's `[]T`. Go's `slices.BinarySearch(x []E, target E) (int, bool)` needs a sorted `x` and says so in prose: an unsorted `x` gives a wrong answer and no diagnostic, and so does Effect's `Array` search with an `Order`. `Ascending`'s one field is unexported, so only `slices` builds one ([types.md, Zero values](types.md#zero-values-and-definite-initialisation) 6): `Ascend(xs)` is a sorted copy (Go's `slices.Sort` on a clone), and outside `slices` a literal, an empty literal, a zero value (`let a slices.Ascending[int]`) and a literal of a defined type over it are S114, a `[]int` where an `Ascending[int]` is expected is S108, and a property parameter of the type is S108 (no generator builds it). A conversion to a defined type over it and back makes no new value, so it is allowed. `Items(a)` is a copy: a slice element is assignable and aliasing is visible, so a shared slice would let a caller unsort `a`. The departure from Go's signature is decided by NORTH_STAR "Strong abstractions with zero-cost implementations" under the 2026-11-08 std rule (std-strengths O2). The laws `AscendOrders`, `AscendPermutes`, `AscendLeavesItsArgument`, `ItemsIsACopy` and `BinarySearchFindsTheFirst` state it; `TestAscendingIsTheOnlyWayIn` (lower) and the check fixture `negative/ascending-is-the-only-way-in.ergo` hold the refusals, and `TestStdAscendingMatchesGo` (lower) holds `BinarySearch(Ascend(xs), x)` to Go's `slices.BinarySearch` on 10,000 seeded cases.

9. **Copies and views** (std-slices-maps). `Clone` is Go's `slices.Clone` (Effect's `Array.copy`): a write to the copy never reaches `xs`. `Concat(a, b)` is Go's `slices.Concat` (Effect's `Array.appendAll`): a new array, so an `Append` to `a` with room after its elements never changes the result (law `ConcatIsIsolated`); Ergo has no variadic parameter, so it takes two slices. `Insert(xs, i, ys)` and `Delete(xs, i, j)` are Go's `slices.Insert` and `slices.Delete` (Effect's `Array.insertAt` and `Array.remove`, which take one element) in a new array: Go's write `xs`'s array when it has room (Delete shifts it and zeroes its tail), these never do (laws `InsertPlaces`, `DeleteUndoesInsert`); a bound outside `0..len(xs)` is a defect. `Repeat(xs, n)` is Go's `slices.Repeat`: `xs` n times over; Effect's `Array.replicate(x, n)` is `Repeat([]T{x}, n)`; a negative `n` is a defect, as Go's panics. `Slice(xs, i, j)` is Go's full slice expression `xs[i:j:j]` (Effect's `Array.slice`): it shares the array of `xs` but has no room after `j`, so an `Append` to it makes a new array and never writes `xs[j]` (law `SliceHasNoRoom`); Go's `xs[i:j]` keeps the room, and an `append` to it writes `xs`. A bound outside `0..len(xs)` or an `i` after `j` is a defect. `Len(a)` and `At(a, i)` read an `Ascending` with no copy, where `Items` copies; `At` out of range is a defect, as an index is.
10. **Map copies and upserts.** `maps.Using[K, V, H](by)` is the empty Map whose provider is `by`, the way to a Map whose provider has no default, such as a seeded hash ([types.md, Maps](types.md#maps) 3; Rust's `HashMap::with_hasher`); it is the prelude's `MakeMap`, as `MakeSlice` is the empty slice. `maps.Clone` is Go's `maps.Clone`; its body restates it (a loop into a new map), because `maps` is not in `PureImports`. `maps.GetOr(m, k, def)` is the value of `k`, or `def`: Effect's `HashMap.get` with `Option.getOrElse`, Go's comma-ok read. `maps.Update(m, k, f)` sets `k` to `f` of its value now, `Some` or `None` (Effect's `HashMap.modifyAt`); it returns the result and leaves the caller's `m` unchanged, as the prelude's `Delete` does, since Maps are values ([types.md, Bindings](types.md#bindings) 1, q26), so a program writes `m = maps.Update(m, k, f)`. `m` is an owned parameter ([types.md, Bindings](types.md#bindings) 2): that assignment hands `m` over and writes it in place, as the prelude's `p = p.Delete(k)` does and as hand Go writes `m[k] = f(m[k])`, and a call whose `m` stays live copies it at the call. When `f` is a pure literal or a named pure package function, the lowering's general inlining rule ([runtime.md, I1](runtime.md#i1-interruption-points) 2, Inlined calls) writes `Update`'s body at the call with `f` called directly, by checked facts and not by `Update`'s name (OPT6). This is a lowering choice, not a promise; `-ldflags=-X=ergo.local/prototype/internal/sketch/lower.inlineLowering=call` calls `Update`. `TestValueRuleAgrees` and `TestInlineAgrees` hold both lowerings to the same model. `maps.GetOr` and a `match m.Get(k)` read with Go's comma-ok form, and the prelude's `Delete` and an index write change an owned Map in place. `f` may fail and need capabilities, as `Transform`'s callback may; its failure ends `Update` and leaves `m` as it was. The laws `CloneIsIndependent`, `UpdateSeesTheOldValue` and `GetOrDefaults` state them. `TestStdSlicesMoreMatchGo` (runtime/v1) holds `Clone`, `Concat`, `Repeat`, `Slice` and `maps.Clone` to Go's functions on 10,000 seeded cases, with arguments that have room after their elements; `TestStdSlicesMoreRuns` (lower) runs each function.
11. **Sequence functions** (std-spike D1). `FilterMap`, `Find`, `Any`, `All`, `Partition`, `GroupBy` and `FlatMap` take a function with a row and pass it on, as `Transform` does (rule 3); `Find`, `Any` and `All` stop at the first element that decides them. `FilterMap` is one pass where `Filter` then `Transform` is two (law `FilterMapIsFilterThenMap`). `Partition` puts the kept first, as Gleam's `list.partition`, where Effect's puts the refused first (law `PartitionIsFilterTwice`). `Dedupe` keeps each element's first occurrence (Effect's `dedupe`; law `DedupeKeepsFirst`); Go's `slices.Compact` drops only neighbours, which is `Dedupe` of a sorted slice. `Chunks(xs, n)` is Go's `slices.Chunk` collected, the last chunk holding what remains, and an `n` under one is one, as Effect's `chunksOf` (Go's panics; law `ChunksConcat`); `Windows(xs, n)` is each run of `n` neighbours, none when `n` is under one or `xs` is shorter (Gleam's `list.window`; law `WindowsSlide`). Every chunk and window is a new slice. `GroupBy` keeps each group in the order of `xs` in a Map (law `GroupByPartitions`). `Zip` stops at the shorter slice, as Effect's `zip` (law `ZipLen`); `FlatMap` joins in order (law `FlatMapIsConcatOfTransform`).
12. **Map combinators** (std-spike D1). `maps.Merge(a, b)` is `a` with `b`'s keys over it, `b` winning, as Effect's `HashMap.union` and Go's `maps.Copy` into a clone (law `MergeRightWins`); `Filter` and `TransformValues` return a new Map (law `FilterAndTransformKeepKeys`); `Entries` is the pairs in ascending key order, so a Map reads in a fixed order with one call (law `EntriesAreSorted`).
13. **Set** (std-spike D1). Std `set` is Effect's `HashSet` over Go's map: `Set[K]` is a value, so `Add` and `Remove` return a new Set and a copy never sees a later change. `Union`, `Intersection`, `Difference` and `IsSubset` are the algebra of sets (law `SetAlgebra`); `Of` holds each element once (law `AddHasRemoveHasNot`); `Values` is a Seq in no order that stops (law `SetValuesStops`), and `Sorted` reads it in one. Runners: lower `TestStdSlicesMoreLawsRun`, `TestStdMapsMoreLawsRun`, `TestStdSetLawsRun`.
14. **An order is a provider** (CONSTRAINTS C3, 2026-11-18). `cmp.Order[T]` is an order on `T` as a value: `Compare(a, b)` is negative when `a` is before `b`, positive when after, 0 when neither. A program declares an order as a provider type, `type ByPriority {} implements cmp.Order[Entry]` with `func Compare(_ ByPriority, a Entry, b Entry) int`, so a type has as many orders as the program declares, an order may hold fields (`ByColumn { Name bool }`), and a program orders a type it does not own (`cmp.Natural[int]`). A call names its order: `slices.SortBy(entries, ByPriority)`; nothing is looked up. `cmp.Then(first, next)` orders a record by its fields, the first field first: `cmp.Then(cmp.Compare(a.Priority, b.Priority), cmp.Compare(a.Name, b.Name))`. Each provider in a program runs `Order`'s three laws in `ergo test`; the generic providers `Natural` and `Reverse` run them through the top-level laws `NaturalIntIsAnOrder`, `NaturalStringIsAnOrder`, `NaturalFloatIsAnOrder` (a NaN included) and `ReverseOfNaturalIsAnOrder`, and `Then` is a monoid with identity 0 (`ThenIsAssociative`, `ThenOfZeroIsNext`, `ThenWithZeroIsFirst`), so a chain of any length over orders is an order. `SortBy` is an Ergo body: a median-of-three quicksort with an insertion sort under 13 elements, on a copy of `xs`; it does not keep the order of equal elements (`SortStableBy` is a follow-up). Under an order that breaks the laws it still ends, reads only inside `xs` and returns the same elements (`TestSortByAgrees`, provider `Chaos`). Laws `SortByIsSorted`, `SortByIsAPermutation` and `SortByOfNaturalIsSort` hold it; `TestSortByAgrees` (lower) runs seeded slices and generated orders against an insertion sort, under the known-witness lowering and the reference. *Prior art:* Go's `slices.SortFunc` and `cmp.Or`; Effect's `Order<A>`, `Order.reverse` and `Order.combine`; Gleam's `list.sort(xs, by:)` and `order.break_tie`; Rust's `sort_by` and `Ordering::then`. Not taken from Effect: `Order.struct` over an object of orders (`Then` writes it), dual signatures, and the runtime `Equal` and `Hash` traits (Ergo generates `==` from the type).

*Prior art:* Go's `slices`, `maps` and `cmp`, whose names these keep; Effect's `Array.map`, `filter`, `reduce`, `sort` with an `Order`, `findFirst` and `head` returning `Option`; Gleam's `gleam/list` (`map`, `filter`, `fold`, `sort` with a comparator, `first` returning `Result`) binds Erlang's `lists:sort` as an external, as `slices.Sort` binds Go's; Elixir's `Enum.map`, `Enum.sort_by` (stable) and `Enum.max` (raises on empty, unlike `Max` here); ReScript's `Belt.Array` returns new arrays and `option` for a missing element.

## time

Std `time` (std-align S2b) follows Go's `time` package for names and meaning, and Effect's `Clock` and `TestClock` for the capability and the test provider. `TestTimeRuns` (lower) runs each function; the laws are in `time/clock.ergo`, `time/laws.ergo` and `time/wire.ergo`, with their rows in [Std laws](#std-laws).

| Name | Signature | Go body |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `Time` | record with unexported fields: Unix seconds, nanoseconds, and a monotonic reading when a clock made it | none |
| `Unix`, `UnixSeconds`, `Nanosecond` | `Unix(sec int64, nsec int64) Time` (nsec carried into the seconds, as Go's); `UnixSeconds(t Time) int64` (Go's `t.Unix()`); `Nanosecond(t Time) int64` | Ergo |
| `NewTime` | `NewTime(sec int64, nsec int64) Time`: `Unix` kept in the years 1 to 9999 (a `sec` outside them wraps by their span; `nsec` wraps into its second). It is `Time`'s constructor, which a generator draws a `Time` with ([runtime.md, Tests](runtime.md#tests) 6), so a law or property may take a `Time` or a record that holds one | Ergo |
| `Add`, `Sub`, `Before`, `After`, `Equal` | `Add(t Time, d duration) Time`; `Sub(t Time, u Time) duration` (saturated, as Go's); `Before`, `After`, `Equal(t Time, u Time) bool` | Ergo (Go's `Time.Sub` step for step, NP4f-17) |
| `Layout` | `RFC3339 \| RFC3339Nano \| DateTime \| DateOnly \| TimeOnly \| Custom{Text}`; `LayoutText(l Layout) string` | Ergo |
| `Format`, `Parse` | `Format(t Time, layout Layout) string`; `Parse(layout Layout, value string) Time fails ParseError`; a year outside 0 to 9999 is ISO 8601's expanded year ([time](#time) 10) | `TimeFormat`, `TimeParse` (timetext.go) |
| `ParseTime`, `Wire` | `Time`'s by-name codec pair in its append form ([types.md, Codecs](types.md#codecs) 1; GX10): `ParseTime(b []byte) Time fails ParseError` is `Parse(RFC3339, utf8.String(b))`; `Wire(t Time, into []byte) []byte` is `into` with `Format(t, RFC3339Nano)`'s bytes added (law `WireIsFormat`, and the pair's derived `TimeRoundTrips` over the instants `NewTime` makes). So a `Time` encodes as its RFC 3339 text in UTC with nanoseconds, as Go's `MarshalJSON` writes it, without its monotonic reading | Ergo over `TimeAppendUTC` (timeappend.go: Go's `AppendFormat`, for the years 0 to 9999) and `Format` |
| `Compare`, `Min`, `Max` | `Compare(t Time, u Time) int` (Go's `t.Compare(u)`: -1, 0, +1); `Min`, `Max(t Time, u Time) Time` (`t` when they are the same instant) | Ergo |
| `UnixMilli`, `Milliseconds` | `UnixMilli(ms int64) Time` (Go's `time.UnixMilli`); `Milliseconds(t Time) int64` (Go's `t.UnixMilli()`, rounded down) | Ergo |
| `Truncate`, `Round` | `Truncate(t Time, d duration) Time`, `Round(t Time, d duration) Time`: Go's, multiples of `d` since the zero time (January 1 of year 1), no monotonic reading | `TimeTruncate`, `TimeRound` (timetext.go) |
| `Interval` | half-open `[Start, End)`: `NewInterval(start Time, end Time) Interval fails Reversed`; `Start`, `End(i Interval) Time`; `Length(i Interval) duration`; `Contains(i Interval, t Time) bool`; `Overlaps(a Interval, b Interval) bool`; `Intersect(a Interval, b Interval) Option[Interval]` | Ergo |
| `ParseDuration`, `DurationText` | `ParseDuration(s string) duration fails ParseError`; `DurationText(d duration) string` | `TimeParseDuration`, `TimeDurationText` (timetext.go) |
| `DurationISO`, `ParseDurationISO` | `DurationISO(d duration) string` (`PT1H30M`); `ParseDurationISO(s string) duration fails ParseError` | `TimeDurationISO`, `TimeParseDurationISO` (timetext.go) |
| `Times`, `Divide`, `Count` | `Times(d duration, n int) duration` (saturated); `Divide(d duration, n int) duration`; `Count(d duration, unit duration) int` | `TimeTimes`, `TimeDivide`, `TimeCount` (timetext.go) |
| `Month`, `Weekday` | unions `January \| … \| December` and `Sunday \| … \| Saturday`; `MonthNumber(m Month) int` (1 to 12); `MonthOf(n int) Option[Month]`; `WeekdayNumber(w Weekday) int` (Sunday 0, as Go's) | Ergo |
| `Civil`, `Calendar`, `Date` | `Civil { Year, Month Month, Day, Hour, Minute, Second, Nanosecond, Weekday Weekday, YearDay }`; `Calendar(t Time) Civil`; `Date(year int, month Month, day int, hour int, min int, sec int, nsec int) Time` | Ergo |
| `Clock` | `Clock { Now() Time, Elapsed(t Time) duration }` with the laws `NowNeverFalls` and `ElapsedIsNowSub`; `Since(t Time) duration needs Clock`, which is `Clock.Elapsed(t)`. A provider gives `Elapsed` as `Now().Sub(t)` or by a cheaper read of the same clock (std-speed-1) | none |
| `Host` | the host's `Clock`: `NewHost() Host needs Ambient`; `Now(_ Host) Time`; `Elapsed(h Host, t Time) duration`, which reads only the monotonic clock when `t` has a monotonic reading, as Go's `time.Since` does | `TimeHostNow`, `TimeHostMono` (time.go) |
| `Manual` | a test `Clock` and `Scheduler`: `NewManual(sec int64) Manual`; `Now`, `Elapsed`, `Sleep(m Manual, d duration)`, `Alarms(m Manual) AlarmClock`, `Advance(m Manual, d duration)`, `Sleepers(m Manual) int` (sleepers and armed alarms), `Asleep[T, E](m Manual, t Task[T, E]) bool` | `Timeline` over `rt.Virtual` (time.go) |

1. **Clock moved.** The prelude's `Clock { Now() int64 }` had no unit and no law. The prelude cannot name a std type, so `Clock` is `time.Clock` with `Now() time.Time` (owner question 1, its fallback). `Scheduler` and `LiveScheduler` stay in the prelude; `LiveScheduler` is a host provider under `Ambient`, made only by `NewLiveScheduler()` ([Host packages](#host-packages) 6).
2. **Monotonic reading.** A Time that a clock read carries a monotonic reading, as Go's does. `Sub`, `Before`, `After` and `Equal` use it when both times have one, so `NowNeverFalls` holds on the host when the wall clock is set back. `Format` and `Parse` use the wall reading only. `==` compares the monotonic reading too, as Go's `==` does: compare instants with `Equal`.
3. **UTC only.** `Format` writes in UTC, and `Parse` reads a zone abbreviation against UTC (`time.ParseInLocation` with `time.UTC`). Neither reads the host's zone, so their bodies are pure ([Pure bodies](#pure-bodies)), and the text of a program does not depend on the host. A program that needs a zone reads it through Go's `time` (`import go time`, `time.LoadLocation`), and carries Go's zone data in its binary with `import go time/tzdata` in the package of `main` ([Build-time assets](#build-time-assets) 5).
4. **Layouts are a union.** The named layouts are the variants of `Layout`, and `Custom{Text}` holds any other Go layout. The union is the design, not a stand-in for package-level constants: a `match` over a Layout covers every layout, and a named layout cannot be misspelled, where Go's layouts are strings (W6 in [Std strengths over Effect](#std-strengths-over-effect)).
5. **ParseError.** `Parse` fails `ParseError { Layout, Value, Message }`, with Go's message. A std record names its own Go type (Types 4), so a `time.Malformed` would be its own Go type; the name is `ParseError` because the record carries the layout and Go's message, which `strconv.Malformed` does not.
