6. **Manual.** `Asleep(m, t)` says whether the task `t`, or a task it started, sleeps on `m`, so only `m`'s `Advance` wakes it; a task that ended sleeps on nothing (FK4-g2-g1; runtime/v1 `TestTimeAsleepSeesBelow`). Its time moves only by `Advance`; a `Sleep` returns when `Advance` reaches its deadline, and a stop of the sleeping task ends the sleep and removes it. Copies of a Manual share one timeline. Bind one value for both capabilities: `context { time.Clock: m, Scheduler: m }`. `NewManual` takes Unix seconds, not a Time, because a function whose first parameter is a Time must not reach state (`TestStdHostProvidersHaveAuthority`). `TestManualClockModel` (runtime/v1) checks it against a reference model over 500 seeds. **Advance settles** (fua-b; batch-13 MB2 G2). `NewManual` makes the calling task the first member of the Manual's domain, the same domain a `testing.VirtualClock` has: that task, and every task started below a member from then on, actors and their timers, works and children included. A clock that a member of a domain makes joins that domain, and its maker stays a member: one domain, which every clock its members made shares, and with several auto clocks armed the one whose earliest deadline is nearest its own now moves (P3-9; until then a second clock took its maker out of the first one's domain, so a `testing.VirtualClock` made before an unused `time.Manual` never moved and the run deadlocked; `TestAutoClockMovesBesideAnotherClock`). `Advance(m, d)` first waits until every member but its caller waits on a wait the clock sees (a `Sleep` on it, a queue's `Push` and `Pop`, a `Task`'s `Wait` and `Stop`, `timeout.After`, and an actor's inbox, `Send`, `Ask`, `State`, `Changes`, `ExitOf`, `Host.Get`, `Stop` and `Drain`). Then it moves the time to the earliest deadline due by now+d, wakes every sleeper of that instant, and waits again, one instant at a time. So when `Advance` returns, each timer due by then has fired at its own instant, and every task it woke, and every task those woke, has run to its next wait: an actor has stepped its timer's signal, committed and published the state. A driver reads the result at once, with no loop on `Sleepers` or `State`. A member that runs on, or waits on anything else (the host's input, a file, a lock), keeps `Advance` waiting until that wait ends, as Go's `synctest.Wait` does; those host waits are the only waits of a member that `Advance` does not see. A stop of the caller ends the wait. **One `Advance` at a time** (batch-13 round 2, B1): a call of `Advance` on a Manual while another task's `Advance` of it runs is a defect in its caller ("time.Manual: Advance while another task's Advance of this clock is running"), and the running one goes on. The running `Advance` waits for every other member to park, and a caller of `Advance` is not parked, so a second member's call can only come while the first runs: waiting for it would wait forever (each for the other), and letting both move the time would leave the sum of the moves undefined (two `Advance(1s)` from one instant both target the same time). Go's `synctest.Wait` makes the same call: two goroutines of one bubble that call it at once panic. `TestManualAdvanceSettles` (runtime/v1) holds this against a reference timed fold, for generated machines with timers, a ticking task and a `Changes` watcher, beside generated member tasks that wait on an actor in each of its waits (`Stop`, `Drain`, `Ask`, `ExitOf`, `State`, a full `Send`) while its step waits on the caller of `Advance`; `TestManualAdvanceOneAtATime` holds the one-at-a-time rule. *Prior art:* Effect's `TestClock.adjust` runs the fibers that each passed sleep woke before it returns; Go's `testing/synctest` `Wait` returns when every goroutine of the bubble is durably blocked; Tokio's paused time moves only when the runtime is idle.
7. **Laws and runners.** `TestStdTimeLawsRun` (lower) restates `FormatParseRoundTrip`, `SubAddInverse` and `Timeline.AdvanceMovesRead` as Ergo property tests. `TestTimeLaws` (runtime/v1) runs `NowNeverFalls` on the host clock and checks the round trip against Go's own `time` ([time](#time) 10). The sleep law `SleepAdvancesNow` needs a task beside the Manual. `task` imports `time` already (its law `StopIsIdempotent` sleeps on a Manual), so `time` cannot import `task` without a cycle (`TestStdImportsAreAcyclic`): the law is in `task`, with its row under `task`, and `TestStdTimeLawsRun` restates it. `TestManualClockModel` (runtime/v1) holds it too, against a reference model.

8. **Durations as text and numbers** (std-duration; `time/duration.ergo`). The language's `duration` takes `+`, `-`, ordering and `==` ([types.md, Operators](types.md#operators) 4); these functions give the rest of Go's `Duration`. `ParseDuration(s)` is Go's `time.ParseDuration`: an optional sign, then numbers with optional fractions, each with a unit (`ns`, `us`, `µs`, `μs`, `ms`, `s`, `m`, `h`), or `0`; any other text, or one past the range, fails `ParseError` with an empty `Layout`, the text as `Value` and Go's message (`time: missing unit in duration "12"`). `DurationText(d)` is Go's `Duration.String` (`1h2m3.5s`), which `ParseDuration` reads back (law `DurationTextRoundTrips`). `Times(d, n)` is `d` n times over, **saturated** at the range of a duration where Go's `d * time.Duration(n)` wraps, as `Sub` saturates (law `TimesKeepsTheSign`); Effect's `Duration.times` is unbounded. `Divide(d, n)` is Go's `d / time.Duration(n)`, toward zero, and `Count(d, unit)` is Go's `int(d / unit)` (`Count(d, 1ms)` is `d.Milliseconds()`); the one quotient past the range, the smallest duration over -1, saturates, and a zero divisor is a defect, as Go's division panics (laws `DivideInvertsTimes`, `CountIsWholeUnits`). So `time.Times(1ms, n)` is a duration of `n` milliseconds, where `duration(n)` of an `int` is refused (a conversion is only from `int64`). runtime/v1 `TestStdDurationMatchesGo` holds each body to Go's function on 10,000 seeded texts and durations (valid and invalid texts, fractions, every unit, missing and unknown units, the extremes) and `Times` to the exact product clamped; the laws run in place on their own source (`ergotests -std`); lower `TestStdDurationRuns` runs each function and `flag.Duration`. `DurationISO` and `ParseDurationISO` (std-spike T5) are ISO 8601's duration of time, as Temporal writes it: `PT1H30M`, `-PT0.5S`, zero `PT0S`; hours are never folded into days (a day of a zone is not always 24 hours: a count of days is a `civil.Period`, [civil](#civil) 17). The parse reads an optional sign, `PT`, then `H`, `M`, `S` in that order, each once, a fraction (`.` or `,`, up to nine digits) on the seconds only, and refuses a date part, so `P1M`, a month, is never a minute. runtime/v1 `TestTimeDurationISOMatchesModel` holds the text to a big-integer model on 20,000 seeded durations; law `DurationISORoundTrips` (every duration, the smallest included). Go has no ISO duration text; Java's `Duration.toString` writes `PT8H6M12.345S`, as here.
9. **The calendar** (std-time-civil; `time/civil.ergo`). In UTC, as every Time is (3). `t.Calendar()` is one `Civil` record of Go's `t.UTC().Date()`, `Clock()`, `Nanosecond()`, `Weekday()` and `YearDay()`, where Go has five methods; a record is one call and needs no tuples. `time.Date(year, month, day, hour, min, sec, nsec)` is Go's `time.Date(…, time.UTC)`, with Go's normalisation: a field outside its range carries into the next larger one, so day 0 is the last day of the month before, hour 24 the next day and nsec -1 the nanosecond before; the result has no monotonic reading. `Month` and `Weekday` are unions, where Go's are integers, so a `match` over a month or a weekday is total and a 13th month cannot be written; `MonthNumber`, `MonthOf(n) Option[Month]` and `WeekdayNumber` give Go's numbers. The arithmetic is the proleptic Gregorian calendar of Go's `time` (Howard Hinnant's `days_from_civil` and `civil_from_days`), in Ergo with no host call, so it lowers to integer arithmetic. The laws `DateRoundTrip`, `WeekdayStep`, `DateNormalises`, `YearDayCounts` and `MonthOfInvertsNumber` run in `TestStdTimeLawsRun`; lower `TestTimeCivilMatchesGo` holds `Calendar` and `Date` to Go's on 20,000 seeded instants and dates over years -9999 to 9999, with fields far out of range and the leap-day and era edges. Effect's `DateTime.getPart` and `DateTime.make` read parts by a string key; Gleam's `gleam/time/calendar` has `Date { year, month: Month, day }` with a `Month` type, as here.
10. **One text domain** (std-spike T0). `Format` and `Parse` agree on every instant a `Time` holds. In the years 0 to 9999 the text is Go's. Outside them, the layout's year (`2006`) is ISO 8601's expanded year, a sign and at least six digits (`+010000-01-01T00:00:00Z`, `-000001-12-31T23:59:59Z`), as Temporal and RFC 9557 write it; Go writes `10000` and `-0001`, which Go's `Parse` refuses, so before this unit a program reached a text it could not read back with `Add`. `Parse` reads the expanded year only in the form `Format` writes: six digits, or more with no leading zero, and never a year 0 to 9999 (`+002027` is a `ParseError`), so each instant has one text. The bodies move the instant by whole 400-year cycles (146,097 days, a whole number of weeks, so the calendar and the weekday repeat) into the years 2000 to 2399, let Go write or read the text there, and put the real year in its place; this holds for every `int64` second, where Go's own year arithmetic overflows. A two-digit year (`06`) is the year modulo 100. The law `FormatParseRoundTrip` draws every Unix second and every nanosecond; runtime/v1 `TestTimeLaws` holds the text to Go's in the years 0 to 9999, the round trip in two more layouts (the year in the middle, the year twice) and the refusal of each non-canonical year text, on 20,000 seeded seconds and the `int64` extremes.
11. **Instants as numbers, order, rounding and intervals** (std-spike T0). `UnixMilli` and `Milliseconds` are Go's `time.UnixMilli` and `t.UnixMilli()` (law `UnixMilliInverts`: the milliseconds read back, and an instant before 1970 rounds down, not toward zero). `Compare`, `Min` and `Max` order instants as `Before` does (law `CompareOrders`). `Truncate` and `Round` are Go's (laws `TruncateIsFloor`, `RoundIsNearest`). `Interval` is a half-open range of instants that only `NewInterval` builds, so its start is never after its end (`Reversed` otherwise; an interval is empty when they are the same instant); intervals that only touch do not overlap, and `Intersect` is `None` for them (laws `IntervalContainsStart`, `OverlapsIffIntersect`). Effect's `DateTime` has no interval type; Temporal and jiff have none either; Go's `time` has none.

*Prior art:* Go's `time` (monotonic readings in `time.Time`, `testing/synctest`'s fake clock); Effect's `Clock` service and `TestClock.adjust`; Gleam's `gleam/time` keeps instants as seconds and nanoseconds in UTC and formats RFC 3339 only; Elixir's `DateTime` is UTC-first and its `System.monotonic_time` is separate from wall time; ReScript binds JavaScript's `Date`.

## ratelimit

Std `ratelimit` (STD-SURVEY ST1) limits how often a key may do something. It ports the purpose of Effect's `RateLimiter` (`effect/persistence`): a server, a client of a paid API or a job runner asks "may this key spend N tokens now?", and is told yes with what is left, or no with exactly how long to wait. Go's convention is `golang.org/x/time/rate` (`AllowN(now, n)`, `WaitN`, a burst and an interval per token). The contract is in `ratelimit`; `ratelimit/memory` keeps each key's state in the process. A provider over a shared store (Redis, a database) is a module that runs the same laws.

| Name | Signature |
| --- | --- |
| `Rule` | `TokenBucket{Capacity int, Every duration} \| FixedWindow{Limit int, Window duration} \| SlidingWindow{Limit int, Window duration}`; `LimitOf(rule Rule) int`; `Check(rule Rule) Rule fails BadRule` |
| `Limiter` | `Limiter { TakeAt(key string, cost int, now time.Time) Grant fails Limited \| BadCost }` with the laws `RefusalChangesNothing`, `RetryAfterIsExact` and `CostBelowOneIsBad` |
| results | `Grant { Remaining int }`; `Limited { RetryAfter duration }`; `BadCost { Cost int, Limit int }`; `BadRule { Reason string }` |
| `Take`, `Wait` | `Take(l Limiter, key string, cost int) Grant fails Limited \| BadCost needs time.Clock`; `Wait(l Limiter, key string, cost int) Grant fails BadCost needs time.Clock \| Scheduler` |
| `memory` | `memory.New(rule ratelimit.Rule) memory.Limiter fails ratelimit.BadRule`; `memory.TakeAt` |

1. **The time is an argument.** An operation carries no capability ([types.md, Capabilities](types.md#capabilities) 4), so `TakeAt` takes the time, as Go's `AllowN(now, n)` does; `Take` and `Wait` read it from the context's `time.Clock`. So a law states a provider's rule over explicit times, with no clock at all.
2. **The algorithms.** `TokenBucket` holds `Capacity` tokens and gives one back each `Every` (bursts of `Capacity`); `memory` keeps it as one time per key, the generic cell rate algorithm's arrival time (GCRA), whose decisions are those of a bucket of whole tokens (law `AgreesWithTheModel` compares the two). `FixedWindow` grants `Limit` tokens per `Window`, a window starting at the first take after the last one ended (Effect's fixed window). `SlidingWindow` grants at most `Limit` tokens in every span of `Window`, however placed: the exact rule, for one entry per grant in the last `Window` (Effect has no sliding window; a counter that weights the previous window is cheaper and wrong at the edges).
3. **Exact refusals.** A refused take changes nothing, and its `RetryAfter`, above zero, is exact: one nanosecond earlier the same take is still refused, at it the take is granted, if no other take of the key comes between (laws `RefusalChangesNothing`, `RetryAfterIsExact`). A cost below one or above the rule's limit fails `BadCost`, which no wait would grant (law `CostBelowOneIsBad`); `Check` refuses a limit below one or a time of zero or less (law `CheckRefusesBadRules`).
4. **Late times.** Tasks may read the clock in one order and take in the other. `memory` decides a take at a time before its key's last grant as one at that grant's time, and counts its `RetryAfter` from the caller's own time (law `LateTakeCountsAsTheLastGrant`), so the rule holds under any interleaving.
5. **Wait** sleeps each `RetryAfter` on the context's `Scheduler` and takes again (law `WaitSleepsTheRetryAfter`, on `time.Manual`). It promises no order among waiters: x/time/rate's `WaitN` reserves a future token, which needs a provider that can hold reservations; Effect's `onExceeded: "delay"` sleeps and retries, as here.
6. **A handle.** A `memory.Limiter` is a synchronised handle: every copy is the same limiter, its keys are in one `sync.Ref`, and each take is one atomic step of its key.
7. **Not taken from Effect**, each a workaround (W1, W4, W5, W14, W18): the `RateLimiter` service tag and its `layer`/`layerStoreMemory` (a provider is a value bound by `context` or passed); `onExceeded` as an option string (two functions, `Take` and `Wait`); `RateLimitExceeded` with a `_tag` (a typed failure in the row); `Duration.Input` strings; `RateLimiterStore`'s persistence coupling (each provider owns its state).
8. **Laws and runners.** The laws are beside their declarations, in `ratelimit.ergo` and `memory/memory.ergo`. Lower `TestStdRatelimitLawsRun` restates them as Ergo tests: the member laws on a memory limiter of each algorithm, small limits and times drawn; `AgreesWithTheModel` 80 drawn takes and waits (by drawn times, by the last `RetryAfter`, and by one nanosecond less) on two keys against a fold of every grant before them.
9. **Receipt** (`benchmarks/ratelimit`, `go run ./scripts/opcount -control -rounds 5 benchmarks/ratelimit/KIND`): instructions and allocations per take against a hand Go limiter with `time.Time` under a mutex (`testdata/control`) and the Go floor with `int64` nanoseconds and a ring per key (`testdata/floor`).

*Prior art:* Effect's `RateLimiter` (token bucket and fixed window, `onExceeded`); Go's `golang.org/x/time/rate` (token bucket with float tokens, `AllowN(now, n)`, `ReserveN`); `throttled` and Redis' `redis-cell` (GCRA); Cloudflare's sliding-window counter (an estimate). Gleam, ReScript and Elixir have no limiter in their standard library: Elixir programs use `Hammer` or an ETS counter owned by a process, Gleam programs an actor holding a dictionary, each serialising the takes as the `sync.Ref` does here.
## cache

Std `cache` (std-spike C1) remembers the value of a lookup per key, with a capacity, a time to live and an idle time read from `time.Clock`, and runs one lookup per key when many tasks ask at once. It follows Effect's `Cache` (capacity, `timeToLive`, one pending lookup per key) and `ScopedCache` (a finalizer for each entry that leaves), and Go's `golang.org/x/sync/singleflight` for the lookup given to each call.

| Name | Signature |
| --- | --- |
| `Config` | `Config { Capacity int, Life duration, Idle duration }`; `0s` is no expiry |
| `New`, `NewReleasing` | `New[K comparable, V](config Config) Cache[K, V] fails BadConfig`; `NewReleasing[K comparable, V](config Config, release escaping func(k K, v V)) Cache[K, V] fails BadConfig` (`BadConfig { Reason }`) |
| `Get` | `Get[K comparable, V, E, R](c Cache[K, V], k K, lookup func(k K) V fails E needs R) V fails E needs time.Clock \| R` |
| reads | `Peek[K comparable, V](c Cache[K, V], k K) Option[V] needs time.Clock`; `StatsOf(c Cache[K, V]) Stats` (`Stats { Hits, Misses, Size int }`) |
| writes | `Put(c Cache[K, V], k K, v V) needs time.Clock`; `Invalidate(c Cache[K, V], k K) bool`; `InvalidateAll(c Cache[K, V])`; `Prune(c Cache[K, V]) needs time.Clock` |

1. **A handle.** A `Cache` is a synchronised handle: every copy is the same cache, and tasks share it ([Sync and STM](#sync-and-stm) 1). `New` refuses a capacity under one and a negative time (`BadConfig`; law `NewRefusesBadConfig`).
2. **Capacity.** A cache holds at most `Capacity` entries; when a lookup or a `Put` would pass it, the entry read least recently leaves (law `ModelAgrees`).
3. **Expiry.** An entry lives `Life` after its lookup ended and `Idle` after its last read, by the context's `time.Clock`; a `Get` never returns a value past either, and runs the lookup again. An expired entry leaves when a `Get` of its key finds it, when it is evicted, or at `Prune` (law `ModelAgrees`, under `time.Manual`). A cache whose `Life` and `Idle` are both 0 never expires an entry, so it reads no clock: its operations keep `time.Clock` in their rows, and do not call it.
4. **One lookup per key.** While one `Get` runs the lookup of a key (a lookup in flight), every other `Get` of that key waits for it, then reads the entry it left. The lookup's failure `E` is the failure of the `Get` that ran it, and is not kept, so a waiting `Get` runs the lookup again; if the task that runs it stops, `scope.OnExit` ends the lookup and the next waiting `Get` runs its own. A lookup that gives its value is kept by one update of the cache's state; only a failed or stopped one takes a second, which drops its slot. A `Put` or an `Invalidate` of the key while the lookup runs wins: the lookup's value goes to its caller and is not kept (law `OneLookupAtOnce`; law `FailureIsNotKept`).
5. **Release.** `NewReleasing`'s `release` runs once for each entry that leaves, evicted, expired, replaced by a `Put` or invalidated, never for an entry the cache holds, and outside the cache's state, so it may use the cache (law `ModelAgrees`). `V` is a value the cache copies to each `Get`, so a resource (one with `Close()`) is refused by resources R44; an entry that owns a resource keeps a handle and closes it in `release`.
6. **Counts.** `StatsOf` counts each `Get` once: a hit when it returned an entry's value, a miss when it ran the lookup; `Size` counts the entries held, expired ones not yet dropped included (law `ModelAgrees`).
7. **Not taken from Effect**, each a workaround (W1, W4, W5, W14): the lookup bound at construction as an `Effect` (`Get` takes it, so its rows are the caller's); a failed lookup's `Exit` kept until its time to live; `Duration.Input` strings.
8. **Laws and runners.** The laws are beside their declarations in `cache.ergo`. Lower `TestStdCacheLawsRun` restates them as Ergo tests; `ModelAgrees` runs sixty drawn operations against a reference model, a list of entries from the least recently read, under `time.Manual`. `examples/lru`'s explored test searches the schedules of three `Get`s of one key (`ergo test --explore`).
9. **Storage.** The entries are cells of one slice, which a map from key to cell index finds, so a hit is one map read and a write of its cell in place. An evicted entry's cell goes on a free list for the next miss. The recency order is a list of (cell, stamp) marks; a mark whose stamp is no longer its cell's is stale and skipped, and stale marks are dropped when they outnumber the live ones. On the text lowering with OWN1's Ref take-over, the `BenchmarkStdCache` workload counts 8.98M user-space instructions per op, down from 10.17M with a map of slots, against 3.38M for the hand Go control (std-spike CACHE-1L, measured; no speed result is claimed). Most of the rest is the generic code, the boxed `found` and `loader` values of `step`, and the `scope.OnExit` frame of each miss: these belong to the IR families, flat unions and the OnExit cost work, not to this package. A waiter queue made only when a second `Get` waits was tried and saves nothing here: the exit action's captured state costs the allocation that the queue did.

*Prior art:* Effect's `Cache` and `ScopedCache`; Go's `singleflight.Group.Do` and `hashicorp/golang-lru`'s `expirable.LRU`; Caffeine's `LoadingCache` (one load per key, `expireAfterWrite`, `expireAfterAccess`, a removal listener). Gleam, ReScript and Elixir have no cache in their standard library: Elixir programs use `Cachex` or an ETS table owned by a process, and Gleam programs an actor holding a dictionary, each a process that serialises the lookups as the `sync.Ref` does here.

## civil

Std `civil` (std-spike T1) is calendar values with no zone: a date, a time of day, a date with a time of day, a period of calendar units and a span of dates, with the calendar arithmetic over them, in pure Ergo (no Go body). Go's name for zone-less values is `civil` (`cloud.google.com/go/civil`); Temporal calls them `PlainDate`, `PlainTime` and `PlainDateTime`. The intent of Effect's `DateTime` calendar half and of date-fns and day.js is the same: build a real date, move it by calendar units, find the start and end of its week or month, count the units between two dates, and write and read it as text. The calendar is ISO 8601's proleptic Gregorian calendar, years 1 to 9999, with no leap seconds. `civil` imports `time` (`Month`, `Weekday`, `Time`, `duration`); `time` does not import `civil`. Zones are package `tz` (std-spike T2).

| Name | Signature |
| --- | --- |
| `Date` | record with one unexported field, the day number from 1970-01-01 (`int32`); `MinDate()` is 0001-01-01, `MaxDate()` is 9999-12-31 |
| `NewDate`, `FromUnixDays`, `UnixDays`, `DateOf` | `NewDate(year int, month time.Month, day int) Date fails NoSuchDate`; `FromUnixDays(n int64) Date` (saturated); `UnixDays(d Date) int64`; `DateOf(t time.Time) Date` (UTC, saturated) |
| fields | `Year`, `Day`, `YearDay`, `Quarter(d Date) int`; `Month(d Date) time.Month`; `Weekday(d Date) time.Weekday`; `ISOWeek(d Date) Week` (`Week { Year, Week }`); `DaysIn(year int, month time.Month) int`; `IsLeap(year int) bool` |
| order | `Compare(a Date, b Date) int`; `Before`, `After`; `Min`, `Max`; `Clamp(d Date, lo Date, hi Date) Date` |
| arithmetic | `AddDays`, `AddWeeks`, `AddMonths`, `AddYears(d Date, n int) Date`; `Period { Years, Months, Weeks, Days int }`; `Add(d Date, p Period) Date`; `DaysBetween(from Date, to Date) int`; `Between(from Date, to Date, largest Unit) Period` |
| periods | `Unit = Days \| Weeks{First time.Weekday} \| Months \| Quarters \| Years`; `StartOf`, `EndOf(d Date, u Unit) Date`; `Next`, `Previous(d Date, w time.Weekday) Date` |
| times of day | `TimeOfDay` (nanoseconds from midnight, unexported); `NewTimeOfDay(hour, minute, second, nanosecond int) TimeOfDay fails NoSuchTime`; `Midnight()`; `Hour`, `Minute`, `Second`, `Nanosecond(t TimeOfDay) int`; `SinceMidnight(t TimeOfDay) duration` |
| date and time | `DateTime { Date Date, Time TimeOfDay }`; `DateTimeOf(t time.Time) DateTime` and `Instant(dt DateTime) time.Time` (UTC); `AddDuration(dt DateTime, d duration) DateTime`; `Sub(a DateTime, b DateTime) duration` |
| spans | `Span` (both ends included, unexported); `NewSpan(from Date, to Date) Span fails Reversed`; `From`, `To(s Span) Date`; `Len(s Span) int`; `Contains(s Span, d Date) bool`; `Overlaps(a Span, b Span) bool`; `Intersect(a Span, b Span) Option[Span]`; `Dates(s Span) Seq[Date]`; `Split(s Span, u Unit) Seq[Span]` |
| text | `Text(d Date) string` (`2027-02-28`); `ParseISODate(s string) Date fails ParseError` (`ParseError { Text, At, Reason }`) |
| format values | `Field = Literal{Text} \| YearNumber{Digits YearDigits} \| MonthNumber{Pad} \| MonthName{Width} \| DayOfMonth{Pad} \| DayOfYear{Pad} \| WeekdayName{Width} \| ISOWeekYear \| ISOWeekNumber{Pad} \| Hour24{Pad} \| Hour12{Pad} \| MinuteNumber{Pad} \| SecondNumber{Pad} \| Fraction{Digits int, Trim bool} \| Meridiem`; `Pad = ZeroPad \| SpacePad \| NoPad`; `Width = Full \| Short`; `YearDigits = AllDigits \| FourDigits \| TwoDigits{Pivot int}`; `Names { Months, MonthsShort, Weekdays, WeekdaysShort []string, AM, PM string }`; `English() Names` |
| format builders | `DateFormat`, `DateTimeFormat` (unexported fields); `Pattern(text string) DateTimeFormat fails BadPattern` (LDML), `FromLayout(text string)` (Go's layout), `Fields(fields []Field, names Names)`, `Named(f, names)`; the `Date…` forms `DatePattern`, `DateLayout`, `DateFields`, `DateNamed` give a `DateFormat`; `ISODate()`, `ISODateTime()`; `FormatFields`, `DateFormatFields`; `BadPattern { Text, At, Hint }` |
| format and parse | `FormatDate(d Date, f DateFormat) string`; `ParseDate(f DateFormat, s string) Date fails ParseError`; `FormatDateTime(dt DateTime, f DateTimeFormat) string`; `ParseDateTime(f DateTimeFormat, s string) DateTime fails ParseError`; `AppendDate(d Date, f DateFormat, into []byte) []byte`, `AppendDateTime(dt DateTime, f DateTimeFormat, into []byte) []byte`, `AppendWithZone(dt DateTime, z ZoneParts, f ZonedFormat, into []byte) []byte`, which append the text to `into` as Go's `AppendFormat` (law `AppendIsFormat`) |
| zoned formats | `ZonedFormat` (unexported fields); `ZonedPattern`, `ZonedLayout`, `ZonedFields`, `ZonedFormatFields`; the fields `OffsetField { Colon, Z bool }`, `ZoneID`, `ZoneAbbreviation`; `ZoneParts { Offset int, Abbreviation, Zone string }`; `FormatWithZone(dt DateTime, z ZoneParts, f ZonedFormat) string`; `ZonedText { Wall DateTime, Offset Option[int], Abbreviation, Zone Option[string] }`; `ParseWithZone(f ZonedFormat, s string) ZonedText fails ParseError`: tz's door ([tz](#tz) 11) |
| relative text | `Approx { Count int, Unit RelUnit, Past bool }`; `RelUnit = Now \| Seconds \| Minutes \| Hours \| Days \| Months \| Years`; `Distance(from time.Time, to time.Time) Approx`; `DistanceDates(from Date, to Date) Approx`; `Words { Now, Today string, Ago, In Affix, Second, Minute, Hour, Day, Month, Year Plural }` (`Affix { Before, After }`, `Plural { One, Other }`); `EnglishWords() Words`; `Phrase(a Approx, w Words) string`; `DurationWords(d duration, w Words) string` |
| durations and Period text | `DurationParts { Days, Hours, Minutes, Seconds, Nanoseconds int }`; `Parts(d duration) DurationParts`; `PeriodText(p Period) string` (`P1Y2M3W4D`); `ParsePeriod(s string) Period fails ParseError` |

1. **A Date is a day number.** It lowers to a Go struct of one `int32` (4 bytes; Go's `time.Time` and `civil.Date` are 24), so `==`, `Compare`, `DaysBetween`, `AddDays` and `Weekday` are one or two integer operations, and a Date is a `Map` key. Reading the year, month or day costs one conversion (Howard Hinnant's `civil_from_days`, about 20 integer operations), which `BenchmarkStdCivil` (lower) measures against Go's `t.Date()`. Gleam, Elixir and Go keep a date as year, month and day; the day number is the one place civil leaves them, because the uses in the examples sort, range and subtract dates more often than they read fields.
2. **Authority.** Only civil builds a `Date`, a `TimeOfDay` or a `Span`. `NewDate` fails `NoSuchDate` for 30 February, month 13 cannot be written (`time.Month` is a union), and `NewSpan` fails `Reversed` when `to` is before `from`, where Go's `time.Date` normalises silently and Effect's `makeUnsafe` throws. A property draws a date from integers through `FromUnixDays`, which every day number reaches.
3. **Saturation.** Arithmetic past 0001-01-01 or 9999-12-31 gives `MinDate()` or `MaxDate()`, as `time.Sub` and `time.Times` saturate (law `ArithmeticSaturates`). Temporal throws a `RangeError`; a JavaScript `Date` becomes `Invalid Date`.
4. **Clamping.** `AddMonths`, `AddYears` and the months of `Add` clamp the day to the new month's last day: 31 January plus one month is 28 or 29 February, and 29 February plus one year is 28 February (laws `AddMonthsClamps`, `AddMonthsRetracts`, `AddMonthsAdds`). Effect, date-fns, day.js and Temporal clamp the same way; Go's `AddDate` gives 3 March, and a program that wants that writes `AddDays`. `Add(d, p)` moves by `p`'s years and months as one count of months with one clamp, then by its weeks and days (Temporal's order).
5. **Between has one meaning.** `Between(from, to, largest)` counts whole units from `from` with `Add`'s clamping, so `Add(from, Between(from, to, u)) == to` for every unit (law `BetweenInvertsAdd`), and every part has the sign of `to - from`. `Years` gives years, months and days; `Months` and `Quarters` give months and days; `Weeks` gives weeks and days; `Days` gives days. So 31 January to 28 February is 0 months and 28 days; date-fns's `differenceInMonths` says 1 month by a special case for the last day of a month, which breaks the inverse.
6. **Units are a union.** `StartOf(d, civil.Weeks{First: time.Monday})` names the day a week starts on, where Effect's `startOf(self, "week", { weekStartsOn })` takes a string and an option bag with a default of Sunday, and ISO's week starts on Monday. `EndOf` is the period's last day, inclusive. A week that would start before 0001-01-01 starts on `MinDate()`, and one that would end after 9999-12-31 ends on `MaxDate()` (law `StartOfBounds`). `ISOWeek` is ISO 8601's week: weeks start on Monday and week 1 holds the year's first Thursday, so 1 January 2027 is in week 53 of 2026 (law `ISOWeekIsThursdays`).
7. **Spans** hold both ends, as a date picker's range and a report's do; `time.Interval` (instants) is half open. `Dates(s)` yields each date from `From`; `Split(s, u)` yields the part of `s` in each period of `u`, the calendar buckets of a report (laws `SpanDaysCount`, `SplitTiles`, `SpansOverlapIffIntersect`, and the Seq rule's `SpanDatesStops`, `SpanSplitStops`).
8. **Text.** `Text(d)` is ISO 8601's `YYYY-MM-DD`, Go's `time.DateOnly`; `ParseISODate` reads exactly ten bytes of ASCII digits and two dashes and a day that exists, so `2027-02-30`, `2027-2-28`, a sign and trailing text are `ParseError`s, never another day (law `TextRoundTrips`). Typed formats and patterns are std-spike T4.
9. **Times of day.** A `TimeOfDay` is 00:00 to 23:59:59.999999999; `23:59:60` is not one. `DateTime` arithmetic is on a clock with no zone, so every day is 24 hours; `DateTimeOf` and `Instant` read and write an instant in UTC (laws `TimeOfDayRoundTrips`, `InstantInvertsDateTimeOf`). The law `CivilAgreesWithCalendar` holds civil's calendar equal to `time.Calendar`, field by field.
10. **Laws and runners.** Each law is beside its declaration (LAWS BESIDE THEIR CODE). Lower `TestStdCivilLawsRun` restates them as Ergo tests (the probe is generated from the source laws). Lower `TestStdCivilMatchesModel` holds civil to a Go reference model on 20,000 seeded operations: Go's `time` for a date's fields, its ISO week and its text, a clamp for months, and walks for `Between`, `StartOf`, `EndOf`, `Next` and `Previous`, with the calendar's ends, leap days and ISO week borders among the inputs. Business days are not in civil: a workweek and a holiday predicate wait for an example that needs them (owner, 2026-11-15).
11. **A format is a value** (std-spike T4). A `DateFormat` or `DateTimeFormat` is a list of `Field` values, built once and read by one `match` per field; `FormatDate` and `ParseDate` read the same value, so the round trip is a law over drawn formats (`FormatParseRoundTrips`; every draw builds, so every draw reaches the parse). The builder checks what Go's layout leaves to run time: a `DateFormat` holds no time of day (`BadPattern` names the hour), a 12-hour clock holds its AM or PM, a number whose width varies is never followed by one that may start with a digit, a trimmed fraction is never followed by a literal dot, and `Names` read one way (12 months and 7 weekdays in each width, distinct, none a prefix of another). Go's `Format` reads its layout on each call (F7); Effect formats through `Intl.DateTimeFormat` option bags; date-fns and day.js read token strings on each call.
12. **Two text front ends, one value.** `Pattern` reads Unicode LDML letters (date-fns 2 and later, Luxon, Java, Swift; owner question 1): `y`, `yyyy`, `M` to `MMMM`, `d`, `dd`, `D`, `DDD`, `E` to `EEEE`, `YYYY` with `w`, `H`, `h`, `a`, `m`, `s`, `S` to `SSSSSSSSS`, quoted text. Its traps are refused by name with a hint: `YYYY` without a week ("YYYY is the ISO week year; write yyyy"), `D` beside `M` ("D is the day of the year; write d"), `yy` (a two-digit year needs an explicit pivot, `TwoDigits{Pivot}` through `Fields`; F6, N3), and, in `Pattern` and `FromLayout`, the offset and zone letters and elements, which `ZonedPattern` and `ZonedLayout` read (a `ZonedFormat`, [tz](#tz) 11). `FromLayout` reads Go's reference layout as Go's `nextStdChunk` does (`2006`, `01`, `Jan`, `_2`, `002`, `15`, `03`, `PM`, `.000`, `.999`, …) and refuses `06`, `pm`, `,999` and the zone elements. Law `PatternAgreesWithLayout` holds the two to one text.
13. **Parse is strict.** `ParseDate` reads the whole text, field by field, with no normalisation: 30 February, a weekday or ISO week that is not the date's, a padding the format does not write (`05` for `M`, `5` for `MM`), and trailing text are each a `ParseError` naming the byte and the field (law `ParseIsStrict`: a byte more is refused, and a byte less when the last field has a fixed width). Names match exactly (Go's matches month names in any case). A date comes from year, month and day, or year and day of the year, or ISO week year, week and weekday; every other field read must agree with it. Departure from the plan's `ParseError { At, Field, Got }`: T1's `ParseError { Text, At, Reason }` is kept, its `Reason` naming the field.
14. **A constant format is checked at check time** by the general rule, with no rule keyed on `Pattern`: the builders are Ergo with no Go body, so check-time evaluation runs a package initializer that builds a format (`let stamp = civil.Pattern("YYYY-MM-dd") catch { … Unreachable(…) }`), and `ergo check` of a package with a law reports the defect at the initializer's line. A package with no law finds it at its first test.
15. **Speed.** `FormatDateTime` appends each field straight into one buffer, from slices of constants or names, with no string per field: one allocation, the string, as Go's `Format`; `AppendDate`, `AppendDateTime` and `AppendWithZone` append into the caller's buffer, as Go's `AppendFormat` (std-spike T4c). Lower `TestStdCivilFormatMatchesGo` holds `FromLayout` and `Pattern` formats to Go's `Format` of the same UTC value on 5,000 drawn layouts, and `ParseDateTime` of that text to Go's `time.Parse`; `internal/sketch/lower.BenchmarkStdCivilFormat` measures both against Go (F7). English names only; no CLDR, plural rules, other calendars or localised digits (owner question 3).
16. **Relative text** (std-spike T5). `Distance(from, to)` is how far `to` is from `from` as an `Approx { Count, Unit, Past }`, by date-fns's `formatDistanceStrict` rule: the unit is the largest whose threshold the distance reaches (60 s, 60 min, 24 h, 30 days, 365 days), the count is the distance in it rounded half up, and 12 months is 1 year; under half a second is `Now`. Its units are date-fns's: no weeks. It rounds within the unit the threshold chose, so 59.5 minutes is "60 minutes", as date-fns writes it; day.js's fuzzy table ("a few seconds", 45 s to "a minute") and `formatDistance`'s "about" and "almost" have no one-sentence rule and no law. `DistanceDates` counts dates, not 24-hour days: the day before is `1 Days` at any hour, and the same date is `0 Days`. `Phrase(a, w)` writes an `Approx` in a `Words` value (`EnglishWords()`: "just now", "today", "3 days ago", "in 1 hour"), so the number and the words are apart: a UI matches the `Approx`, a locale is data, and the clock stays in the caller's row (the caller passes both instants; under `time.Manual` the text is fixed). Laws `DistanceIsMonotone`, `DistanceMirrors`, `DistanceDatesCountsDates`; lower `TestStdCivilMatchesModel` draws both against the rule in exact rationals with the English phrase, at each threshold, a nanosecond either side and at the halves. Effect has no relative text; Gleam and Elixir's std have none (Elixir's comes from `Timex`); Temporal's `Duration.round` gives the count and leaves the words to `Intl.RelativeTimeFormat`, as here to `Words`. `DurationWords(d, w)` (std-spike G-TT-2) is a length, not a distance: every hour, minute and second part of `d` that is not zero, largest first, in `w`'s unit words ("1 hour 25 minutes", "2 hours 5 seconds"), as date-fns's `formatDuration` writes one; hours are its largest unit, as in Go's `Duration.String`, so a day is "24 hours"; a part of a second is dropped, so under a second is "0 seconds", and a negative duration is "-" before its size's words (law `DurationWordsReadsBack`). `time.DurationText` stays Go's `1h25m0s`, the text `ParseDuration` reads.
17. **Durations in parts and Period text.** `Parts(d)` is a duration in days of 24 hours, hours, minutes, seconds and nanoseconds, each with the duration's sign (Effect's `Duration.parts`; law `PartsAddUp`). `PeriodText(p)` writes a `Period` as ISO 8601's `P1Y2M3W4D` with each field's own sign, as Java's `Period.toString` does (`P1Y-2M`; a zero Period is `P0D`), and `ParsePeriod` reads it back with an optional sign for every field; every Period, up to each field's range, round-trips (law `PeriodTextRoundTrips`). A Period text has no time part: `PT1M` is a minute, `time.ParseDurationISO`'s, and `P1M` a month, never a minute (law `PeriodAndDurationTextsApart`).

*Prior art:* Temporal (`PlainDate`, `PlainDateTime`; `overflow: "constrain"`; `largestUnit` for `until` and `since`); jiff's `civil::Date` (Rust); `cloud.google.com/go/civil` (`Date`, `Time`, `DateTime` as year-month-day records); Effect's `DateTime` (`add` with clamping, `startOf` and `endOf` by string unit); date-fns (`addMonths`, `differenceInMonths`, `startOfWeek` with `weekStartsOn`); Gleam's `gleam/time/calendar` (`Date { year, month: Month, day }`); Elixir's `Date` (`Date.add`, `Date.shift/2` with clamping since 1.17, `Date.beginning_of_week/2` with a start day); ReScript binds JavaScript's `Date`. Gleam and Elixir compile a date to a tuple or record of integers; none of the compile-to-host languages stores a day number.

## tz

Std `tz` (std-spike T2) is time zones: a zone is a value, the zone database is a capability, and a wall time a zone skips or shows twice is a union the caller matches. `tz` imports `time` and `civil`. Zones come from std's own copy of the IANA database (`tz/iana`, the default) or, by an explicit choice, from the host's zone files (`tz/host`); a zone is read from TZif bytes (RFC 8536) by a reader written in Ergo. Go's name is `Location`; tz says zone, the word of the IANA database, Temporal, Effect, Elixir and jiff.

| Name | Signature |
| --- | --- |
| `Zone` | opaque (unexported fields: the change table, the offsets, the rule after the table); `UTC()`; `FixedOffset(seconds int) Zone fails BadOffset`; `FromTZif(name string, data string) Zone fails BadData`; `Name(z Zone) string` |
| offsets | `Offset { Seconds int, DST bool, Abbreviation string }`; `OffsetAt(z Zone, t time.Time) Offset`; `Civil(z Zone, t time.Time) civil.DateTime` |
| wall to instant | `Resolved = Unique{At} \| Gap{Before, After, Start} \| Overlap{Earlier, Later}`; `Resolve(z Zone, dt civil.DateTime) Resolved`; `Compatible(r Resolved) time.Time`; `CompatibleShift(z Zone, dt civil.DateTime) (time.Time, duration)`; `StartOfDay(z Zone, d civil.Date) time.Time` |
| changes | `Transition { At time.Time, Before Offset, After Offset }`; `Transitions(z Zone, from time.Time, to time.Time) Seq[Transition]` |
| database | operation type `Zones { Load(name string) Zone fails UnknownZone }` with law `LoadIsStable` |
| local zone | operation type `Local { Zone() Zone fails UnknownZone }` with law `LocalIsStable`; `FromTZ(zones Zones, set bool, value string, name string, data string) Zone fails UnknownZone` (the TZ rule) |
| zoned time | `Zoned { At time.Time, Zone Zone }`; `Wall(z Zoned) civil.DateTime`; `Add(z Zoned, p civil.Period) Zoned`; `AddDuration(z Zoned, d duration) Zoned` |
| zoned text | `FormatZoned(z Zoned, f civil.ZonedFormat) string`; `ParseZoned(f civil.ZonedFormat, s string) Zoned fails civil.ParseError \| UnknownZone needs Zones`; `ParseInstant(f civil.ZonedFormat, s string) time.Time fails civil.ParseError`; `Text(z Zoned) string` (RFC 9557); `ParseText(s string) Zoned fails civil.ParseError \| UnknownZone needs Zones` |
| `tz/iana` | `New() Zones` (provider of `tz.Zones`); `Names() []string`; `Release() string`; `TZif(name string) string` |
| `tz/host` | `New() Zones needs Ambient` (provider of `tz.Zones`); `NewLocal(zones tz.Zones) Local needs Ambient` and `Named { Name string, Zones tz.Zones }` (providers of `tz.Local`) |

1. **Resolved is the design.** `Resolve` gives every instant at which the zone shows a wall time. A caller who must refuse a time that does not exist matches `Gap` and fails with its own error; a scheduler takes `Compatible`, or `CompatibleShift`, the same instant with the gap it moved the wall time by (`0s` for a wall time the zone shows), which makes no `Resolved` and so allocates nothing (law `CompatibleShiftIsCompatible`); `Compatible` is ECMA-262's and Temporal's default (`Gap.After`, the wall time moved forward by the gap; `Overlap.Earlier`). `Gap.Before` is the wall time read with the offset after the change and `Gap.After` with the offset before it; `Gap.Start` is the change. Go's `time.Date` gives the instant one gap earlier in a gap (2027-03-14T02:30 in Toronto is 06:30Z by Go, 07:30Z by `Compatible`; F1) and its documentation does not promise either. Effect takes a `disambiguation` string with a default; Elixir's `DateTime.from_naive/3` returns `{:gap, a, b}` and `{:ambiguous, a, b}`, the same shape; jiff's `AmbiguousOffset` has `Unambiguous`, `Gap` and `Fold` (laws `ResolveInvertsCivil`, `GapIsNeverShown`, `OverlapIsOrdered`).
2. **Our own zone database** (owner, 2026-11-15: "just make our own time zone host then."). `tz/iana` is one IANA release, compiled to TZif and written into `runtime/tzdata/zones.bin` (358,181 bytes for 2026c, 598 zones) by `go run ./scripts/tzdata`, whose `-check` holds the file to its source: by default the `zoneinfo.zip` of the Go toolchain, which Go's `lib/time/update.bash` builds from the release its `DATA=` line names with zic's flags, and every zone must load with Go's reader before anything is written. A program carries the copy only when it calls into `tz/iana` (the copy's index has no package-level initializer, so Go's linker drops it otherwise). So a name means the same rules on every host, the valid names are the database's (`posixrules` and `localtime` are not), and a host with no zone files loses nothing. `Load` of a name the copy does not hold is `UnknownZone`, never UTC (laws `EveryNameLoads`, `LoadRefusesOtherNames`). `tz/host` reads the host's zone files instead (a `$ZONEINFO` directory, then the directories of Go's own search): two hosts can then disagree, and a non-zone file of the directory loads when it is TZif; a name with a `..` element, an absolute path or a backslash is `UnknownZone` without a read. Go's `time/tzdata` (Build-time assets 5) is not used by tz.
3. **The database is a capability.** `Load` is in the `needs tz.Zones` row; the program's context chooses `iana.New()` or `host.New()`, and a missing provider is a compile error, not UTC. Elixir's `UTCOnlyTimeZoneDatabase` errors at run time; Effect's `CurrentTimeZone` is a layer.
4. **One reader, held to Go's.** `FromTZif` reads version 1, 2 and 3 TZif as Go's `LoadLocationFromTZData` does: the 64-bit data when there is some, leap second records skipped, the offset before the first change chosen as Go's `lookupFirstZone` chooses it, and the footer's POSIX TZ string read and applied as Go's `tzset` applies it, in the UTC year of the instant. `TestStdTzMatchesGo` (lower) holds the reader to Go's over every zone of the copy: the offset and the wall time (`Civil`) each side of every change from 1900 to 2100 (found by a 6-hour scan and a bisection of Go's offsets), the wall times 15 minutes apart for 3 hours each side of each change, `StartOfDay` of each change's day and the next, seeded instants from year 1 to 9999, seeded wall times and a seeded `Transitions` window per zone: about 1.9 million cases, in eight parts (`TestStdTzMatchesGoAll0` to `7`, each under the shard target) that run in the gate's full tier alone. `TestStdTzMatchesGo` itself is the sample every other run takes: the same cases for every zone in three seeded windows of two years, one in each third of 1900 to 2100 (about 84,000 cases; `ERGO_TZ_SEED` replays a seed, which a failure prints with its command). Its reference reads Go's offsets only, never `Time.ZoneBounds`, which in go1.27 ends a period at 31 December of a leap year (F4; fixed at Go tip, receipts §5). The property trusts Go's reading of the IANA rules; it makes no claim about the rules.
5. **Resolve reads the window's changes.** Every offset of a zone lies between its least and most offsets, so an instant that shows a wall time `u` (read as UTC) lies in `[u - most, u - least]`; `Resolve` walks the zone's changes in that window one at a time, each the least candidate after the last (the table's, then the rule's for each UTC year it reaches), and tests, for each stretch between two changes, the one instant the stretch's offset gives. Two or more are an `Overlap` of the first and last, one is `Unique`, none is a `Gap` at the change whose forward jump holds `u`. It keeps the first and last instant, not a list, so a call allocates its result alone: the box of the interface `Resolved` lowers to (std unions are not flat, lower flatunion.go rule 1, and a `Gap` is 72 bytes, over rule 4). Lower `TestStdTzResolveAllocs` counts it (`testing.AllocsPerRun`, at most one per call) on each wall time of a gap, an overlap and a plain hour of Toronto's table and rule and of a fixed zone; T2's lists allocated three per call in a window with a change.
6. **StartOfDay** is the first instant of a date: midnight, or the change when the zone skips midnight on that date, or, when the zone skips the whole date (Pacific/Apia, 30 December 2011), the instant the date would have begun at (law `StartOfDayIsFirst`).
7. **Transitions** yields each change of a zone's offset, abbreviation or daylight saving flag in a range, reading the rule a year at a time; it is a Seq that stops (laws `TransitionsChange`, `ZoneTransitionsStops`). Go's API cannot enumerate changes exactly (F4); Temporal has `getTimeZoneTransition`.
8. **Laws and runners.** The laws are beside their declarations and draw zones from std's copy and instants from 1900 to 2100 (`draw.ergo`; their two Go bodies are the laws' own, since `tz` cannot import `tz/iana`). Lower `TestStdTzLawsRun` and `TestStdTzIanaLawsRun` restate them (the probes are generated from the source laws); `runtime/v1.TestTzHostZone` holds `tz/host`'s body to its name rule over a `$ZONEINFO` directory. The law helper `lawZones` is the laws' provider of `Zones` over that copy; `tz.Local`'s member law runs over `host.Named` (lower `TestStdTzLawsRun`).
9. **The local zone is a capability too** (std-spike T3). A program that shows local time has `tz.Local` in its row; `tz/host`'s `NewLocal(zones)` reads the host (`needs Ambient`), and `Named` is one name, for a test or a program that keeps its zone in its configuration. The provider holds the database it loads names from, since an operation writes no capability row (types.md, Capabilities 4): `tz.Local: host.NewLocal(iana.New())` lets the host choose the name and std's copy the rules. The host's rule is `FromTZ`: `TZ` unset is the zone `/etc/localtime` names (the path after its link's `zoneinfo/`; a copied file is read as TZif and named `Local`; no file is UTC, as Go's); `TZ` empty or `:` is UTC; otherwise the name after a leading `:`, loaded from the database, and a name it does not hold (a POSIX rule such as `EST5EDT4`, a path) is `UnknownZone`, never UTC. Go's `time.Local` gives UTC for each, silently (N2); jiff errors as here. `runtime/v1.TestTzLocalBodies` holds the two bodies (`TzEnv`, `TzHostLocal`) to that contract with `TZ` set, empty and unset in process, since they read the environment at each call; law `FromTZReadsTheName` holds the rule.
10. **Zoned** is an instant seen in a zone. `Add` moves the wall date by a `civil.Period` and keeps the wall time of day, then takes the `Compatible` instant (a skipped wall time moves forward by the gap), as Temporal's `ZonedDateTime.add` and Effect's `DateTime.add` on a zoned value; `AddDuration` is exact time. Law `ZonedAddKeepsWall` holds `Add` where the zone shows the wall time once.
11. **Zoned text.** A `civil.ZonedFormat` is a civil format that may also hold `OffsetField{Colon, Z}`, `ZoneAbbreviation` and `ZoneID` (built by `civil.ZonedPattern`, LDML's `XX XXX xx xxx Z ZZZZZ z VV`; `civil.ZonedLayout`, Go's `-0700 -07:00 Z0700 Z07:00 MST`; or `civil.ZonedFields`); civil writes those fields from `civil.ZoneParts` and reads them back as text (`civil.ParseWithZone`), and tz gives them meaning. `FormatZoned` writes the zone's offset (with its seconds when it has them, which Go's `-07:00` drops), abbreviation and name at the instant. `ParseZoned` loads a zone name from the context's database and refuses an offset that is not the zone's at that time (Temporal's `offset: "reject"`) and an abbreviation that is not its; an offset alone is a fixed zone; an abbreviation alone names no one offset and is refused. `ParseInstant` reads a text with an offset as an instant, with no database. Formats refuse at build what drops data: Go's `-07` and `Z07`, LDML's `X` and `x` (no minutes). Laws `ZonedFormatRoundTrips`, `FormatZonedWritesTheZone`.
12. **RFC 9557.** `Text` writes `2027-03-14T03:30:00-04:00[America/Toronto]`: RFC 3339's date and time, a fraction when the instant has one, the offset, and the zone's name in brackets; a fixed zone's name is its offset (`[+05:30]`), as RFC 9557 writes an offset zone. `ParseText` reads it back through `ParseZoned`, so a text whose offset is not its zone's is refused (laws `TextRoundTrips`, `ParseTextChecksOffset`). Gleam's `gleam/time` writes RFC 3339 alone; Elixir's `DateTime.to_iso8601` writes the offset and no zone name; Temporal and jiff write and check RFC 9557.

*Prior art:* Temporal (`ZonedDateTime`, `disambiguation: "compatible"`, `getTimeZoneTransition`); jiff (`tz::TimeZone`, `AmbiguousOffset`, the bundled `jiff-tzdb` as an option, an unloadable `TZ` an error); Go's `time` (`Location`, `LoadLocationFromTZData`, host first, `time/tzdata`); Effect's `DateTime` (`makeZoned` with `disambiguation`, `CurrentTimeZone`); Elixir's `DateTime` with a pluggable `Calendar.TimeZoneDatabase` (the `tzdata` and `tz` libraries) and `{:gap, …}`, `{:ambiguous, …}`; Gleam's `gleam/time` has no zones (a local offset only); ReScript binds JavaScript's `Intl`. Lowering paths: Elixir's `tz` library compiles the IANA rules into Elixir functions at build time, `tzdata` reads a release at run time; Go embeds a zip and reads it with its own TZif reader; tz embeds TZif bytes and reads them with an Ergo reader that lowers to plain Go.

## kv

Std `kv` (STD-SURVEY ST3) keeps text values under text keys. It ports the purpose of Effect's `KeyValueStore` (`unstable/persistence`): a program keeps settings, sessions, bookmarks or a cache of answers under keys, in memory for a test and in a file, or a shared store, for a run that must outlive the process. Go's convention is a map under a mutex, or an append-only log replayed at start (Bitcask, a write-ahead log). The contract is the operation type `Store`; the providers live in packages named after where they keep the entries.

| Name | Signature |
| --- | --- |
| `Store` | `Store { Get(key string) Option[string] fails Failed; Put(key string, value string) fails Failed; Delete(key string) bool fails Failed; Scan(prefix string) Seq[Entry] fails Failed }` with the laws `GetAfterPut`, `PutKeepsOthers`, `DeleteIsAbsent`, `ScanIsSorted` and `ScanStops` |
| results | `Entry { Key string, Value string }`; `Failed { Reason string }` |
| `Of` | `Of[T wire](s Store) Values[T]`; `Get[T wire](v Values[T], key string) Option[T] fails Failed \| json.Malformed \| wired[T] needs wired[T]`; `Put[T wire](v Values[T], key string, value T) fails Failed needs wired[T]`; `Delete[T wire](v Values[T], key string) bool fails Failed` |
| `memory` | `memory.New() memory.Store` |
| `file` | `file.Open(files fs.Files, writes fs.Writes, path string) file.Store fails kv.Failed`; `file.Compact(s file.Store) fails kv.Failed` |

1. **The contract.** `Get` gives `None` for a key that holds no value, so a missing key is an answer, not a failure; `Delete` says whether the key held one. `Failed` is the store's own failure, such as the host's error for a write. `Scan(prefix)` yields the entries whose key starts with `prefix`, by key in byte order, each once, as they are when the `Seq` runs (laws `ScanIsSorted`, `ScanStops`).
2. **`memory`** keeps the entries in one `sync.Ref` of a map, so every copy of a `memory.Store` is the same store and each operation is one atomic step. A read runs inside the Ref's step, so the Ref never hands the map out and a later put changes it in place. `Scan` copies the entries under its prefix in one step and sorts them.
3. **`file`** keeps a log, one line per write: `put<TAB>"key"<TAB>"value"` or `del<TAB>"key"`, each text quoted by `strconv.Quote`, which escapes every tab and line feed. A put or a delete is one `fs.Writes.AppendFile` of its line, then a change of a `memory` index that every read answers from: a `Get` never reads the file, and a write the log did not keep changes nothing. `Open` replays the log into the index. `Compact` rewrites the log as one put per key with `WriteFile`, which writes a new file and renames it over the log, so a crash leaves the old log or the new one.
4. **A cut append.** A crash inside an append can leave a part of the last line, with no line feed. `Open` drops it and writes the log back without it, so the next append starts a line; a whole line that is not a record is a broken log, and `Open` fails naming it (laws `FileIsMemory`, `OpenRefusesABrokenLine`). One process opens a log at a time; two tasks that put one key at once may find the other's value after a reopen, since the log keeps the order of the appends and the index the order of the puts, and `Compact` runs while no other task writes.
5. **The file system is a value.** A provider's operation cannot need a capability its contract does not write ([rows.md, Context](rows.md#context) 2), so `file.Store` holds the `fs.Files` and `fs.Writes` it is given, as a Go type holds an `fs.FS`: a program gives `Open` its `fs.Host`, a test an `fstest.Memory`.
6. **Typed values.** `Of[T]` views a store as values of one wire type, each kept as its JSON text (Effect's `forSchema`): `Get` fails as `json.Decode` fails when the text is not a `T`'s (law `OfRoundTrips`).
7. **Not taken from Effect**, each a workaround (W1, W4, W5, W7, W14): the `KeyValueStore` service tag and its `layerMemory`/`layerFileSystem` (a provider is a value bound by `context` or passed); `SchemaStore`'s `Schema` class (a derived codec); `KeyValueStoreError` with a `_tag` (a typed failure in the row); `has`, `isEmpty`, `size` and `modify` (a `Get`, a `Scan`, or a `Get` and a `Put`); the `Uint8Array` forms (a Go string holds bytes). Not built yet: an expiry per key (TTL), which needs the time as an argument of each operation, as `ratelimit.Limiter.TakeAt` takes it.
8. **Laws and runners.** The laws are beside their declarations, in `kv.ergo`, `memory/memory.ergo` and `file/file.ergo`. Lower `TestStdKvLawsRun` restates them as Ergo tests: the member laws on a memory store and on a file store over an `fstest.Memory`; `FileIsMemory` a generated script of puts, deletes, compactions and reopens on four keys against a memory store, then a cut append, a reopen and one more put.
9. **Receipt** (`benchmarks/kv`, `go run ./scripts/opcount -control -rounds 5 benchmarks/kv/KIND`): instructions and allocations per operation against a hand Go store with a map under a mutex and an append-only log (`testdata/control`) and the Go floor, a bare map and an append to a buffer (`testdata/floor`).

*Prior art:* Effect's `KeyValueStore` (`layerMemory`, `layerFileSystem` with a file per key, `forSchema`); Bitcask and Go's `bbolt`, `pebble` and `badger` (an append-only log or a B-tree, a cut tail dropped at open); Bun's `bun:sqlite` and `Bun.redis` (drivers, as modules here). Gleam, ReScript and Elixir have no key-value store in their standard library: Elixir keeps one in an ETS table or DETS (a file), owned by a process; Gleam programs use `gleam_erlang`'s ETS bindings.

## jobs

Std `jobs` (STD-SURVEY ST2) is a queue of jobs that workers lease and finish or fail. It ports the purpose of Effect's `PersistedQueue` (`unstable/persistence`): a program hands work to workers that may crash, and needs each job run at least once, retried by a policy, and given up as a dead letter when the policy stops. Go's convention is a ring of ready jobs and a map of leases under a mutex, or a store with a lease column (River, Asynq). The contract is the operation type `Queue[J]`; its rules are one pure function, `Apply`, that both providers run.

| Name | Signature |
| --- | --- |
| `Queue` | `Queue[J] { EnqueueAt(job J, at time.Time) int fails Failed; ReserveAt(now time.Time) Option[Lease[J]] fails Failed; AckAt(t Ticket, now time.Time) fails Unknown \| Failed; FailAt(t Ticket, why string, now time.Time) fails Unknown \| Failed; DeadLetters() []Dead[J] fails Failed; Count() Counts fails Failed }` with the laws `EnqueueAddsOneWaiting`, `OneLeasePerJob` and `NoTicketIsUnknown` |
| `Policy` | `Policy { Retry schedule.Schedule, Lease duration }`; `Check(p Policy) Policy fails BadPolicy` |
| results | `Ticket { ID int, Attempt int }`; `Lease[J] { Ticket Ticket, Job J, Until time.Time }`; `Dead[J] { ID int, Job J, Attempts int, Why string }`; `Counts { Waiting int, Leased int, Done int, Dead int }`; `Unknown { Ticket Ticket }`; `Failed { Reason string }`; `BadPolicy { Reason string }` |
| the rules | `Op[J]` = `Enqueue[J]{Job, At} \| Reserve{Now} \| Ack{Ticket, Now} \| Fail{Ticket, Why, Now}`; `Outcome[J]` = `Enqueued{ID} \| Reserved[J]{Lease} \| Settled \| NotLeased{Ticket}`; `State[J]`, `Empty[J]() State[J]`, `Apply[J](p Policy, s State[J], op Op[J]) (Outcome[J], State[J], bool)`, `CountOf`, `DeadOf` |
| `memory` | `memory.New[J](p jobs.Policy) memory.Queue[J] fails jobs.BadPolicy` |
| `file` | `file.Open[J](files fs.Files, writes fs.Writes, path string, p jobs.Policy, codec journal.Codec[J]) file.Queue[J] fails jobs.Failed`; `file.Text`, the codec of jobs that are text |

1. **The time is an argument.** An operation carries no capability ([types.md, Capabilities](types.md#capabilities) 4), so each operation takes the time, as `ratelimit.Limiter.TakeAt` does; a law states the rules over explicit times, with no clock.
2. **A job's life.** `EnqueueAt` adds a job ready at `at`. `ReserveAt(now)` leases the ready job with the earliest ready time, the lowest ID first among equal times (law `EarliestFirst`), until `now` plus the policy's `Lease`, and gives its `Ticket`, the ID and the attempt from 1. `AckAt` ends the lease as done; `FailAt` as a failure, after which the policy's `Retry` decides with the attempt and the time since the first attempt began (`schedule.Decide`): `Continue{After}` makes the job ready `After` later, `Stop` makes it a dead letter with its attempts and the last reason (law `AttemptsFollowTheSchedule`).
3. **At least once.** A lease that is neither acked nor failed by its end is a failure at its end, which the next `ReserveAt` applies: the job is leased again with the next attempt, and an ack or a fail of the old ticket, or of any ticket at or after its lease's end, is `Unknown` (law `LeaseEndIsAFailure`). So a worker that crashes loses no job, and a job may run twice: a worker acks only what it finished.
4. **None lost.** Every job enqueued is waiting, leased, done or dead after each operation, no job holds two live leases, and a lease gives the job enqueued under its ID (laws `NoneLost` over generated scripts, `OneLeasePerJob`, `EnqueueAddsOneWaiting`).
5. **`memory`** keeps the `State` in one `sync.Ref`, and each operation is one atomic `Apply`: workers that reserve at once each get another job. Waiting jobs are a slice in (ready time, ID) order with a head index, so an enqueue at the latest time is an append and a reserve moves the head; leases are a second such slice whose slots an ack leaves behind, skipped when their time comes.
6. **`file`** keeps a log of the operations that changed the queue, one tab-separated line each (`enq`, `res`, `ack`, `fail`, a job as the quoted text its codec gives), appended before the caller sees the outcome. `Open` replays them with `Apply`, a pure function, so it reaches the state the queue had (property `FileIsMemory` against a memory queue, over reopens and a cut append). A reserve that found nothing and an `Unknown` ticket are not kept. A cut last line is dropped at `Open`, and a whole line that is not an operation fails it (law `OpenRefusesABrokenLine`). Each operation holds the queue's lock (a `queue.Queue` of one slot) while it applies and appends; a failed append leaves the state ahead of the log, so the queue fails every later operation until it is opened again. The log is not compacted yet.
7. **Not taken from Effect**, each a workaround (W1, W4, W5, W7, W14): the `PersistedQueue` service tag and its store layers (a provider is a value bound by `context` or passed); its `Schema` for the element (a `journal.Codec` for the file); `PersistedQueueError` with a `_tag` (typed failures in the row); `take` as a scoped `Effect` that acks when its scope closes (an explicit `AckAt` or `FailAt` with the ticket). Not built: a workflow engine or a cluster.
8. **Laws and runners.** The laws are beside their declarations: the contract's in `Queue`, the rules' beside `Apply` in `jobs.ergo`, the file queue's in `file/file.ergo`. Lower `TestStdJobsLawsRun` restates them as Ergo tests: the member laws on a memory queue and a file queue over an `fstest.Memory`, each with a lease of 10ms and two retries.
9. **Receipt** (`benchmarks/jobs`, `go run ./scripts/opcount -control -rounds 5 benchmarks/jobs/KIND`): instructions and allocations per job (an enqueue, a reserve and an ack) against a hand Go queue with a heap of ready jobs and a map of leases under a mutex (`testdata/control`) and the Go floor, a ring of ready jobs and a ring of lease ends (`testdata/floor`).

*Prior art:* Effect's `PersistedQueue` (offer, take with a lease, at-least-once redelivery with an attempt count); Go's River and Asynq (a job table or a Redis list with a lease time, retries by a backoff, dead jobs kept); Oban for Elixir (a Postgres table, `attempt`, `max_attempts`, `discarded`); Amazon SQS (a visibility timeout, a receive count, a dead-letter queue). Gleam, ReScript and Elixir have no job queue in their standard library: Elixir programs use Oban, or a `GenServer` with a queue; Gleam programs use `gleam_otp` actors.

## session

Std `session` (MCP-AUTH-AGENT AU2) keeps who is signed in. A client holds a `Token`, 32 bytes of `rand.Secure` in hex; a store keeps only its `Digest`, the SHA-256 of the token, with the session's `Record`. Its sources are yielded/auth's `StatefulSessionPersistence` (`establish`, `verify`, `rotate`, `revoke`, `revokeAll`, a digest in the store, one winner of a rotation, a security revision) and better-auth's renewal; Effect's pin has no session module. Go's convention is a map of token digests under a mutex (scs, gorilla/sessions), each request one SHA-256 and one map read.

| Name | Signature |
| --- | --- |
| `Store` | `Store { Establish(d Digest, subject string, revision int, now time.Time) fails Conflict \| Failed; Verify(d Digest, now time.Time) Verdict fails Failed; Rotate(old Digest, new Digest, now time.Time) Record fails Conflict \| Failed; Revoke(d Digest) bool fails Failed; RevokeAll(subject string, revision int) fails Failed }` with the laws `VerifyAfterEstablish`, `RevokedNeverVerifies`, `RotateMovesTheSession` and `RevisionEndsEverySession` |
| `Policy` | `NewPolicy(renewAfter duration, idle duration, maxAge duration) Policy fails BadPolicy`, for `0 < renewAfter < idle <= maxAge` |
| tokens | `Token`, `TokenOf(text string) Token`, `Text(t Token) string`; `Digest`, `DigestOf(t Token) Digest`, `DigestText`, `ParseDigest(text string) Digest fails BadDigest` |
| verdicts | `Record { Subject string, Revision int, Created time.Time, Renewed time.Time }`; `Verdict` = `Valid{Record} \| Renew{Record} \| Invalid`; `Judge(p Policy, r Record, floor int, now time.Time) Verdict` |
| the rules | `Op` = `EstablishOp \| RotateOp \| RevokeOp \| RevokeAllOp` (each named for the Store operation it records); `Outcome` = `Kept \| Moved{Record} \| Refused \| Ended{Held}`; `State`, `Empty() State`, `Apply(p Policy, s State, op Op) (Outcome, State, bool)`, `VerifyIn(p Policy, s State, d Digest, now time.Time) Verdict` |
| calls | `Start(s Store, subject string, revision int) Token fails Conflict \| Failed needs rand.Secure \| time.Clock`; `Check(s Store, t Token) Verdict fails Failed needs time.Clock`; `Refresh(s Store, t Token) Token fails Conflict \| Failed needs rand.Secure \| time.Clock`; `End(s Store, t Token) bool fails Failed` |
| signed | `Key { ID string, Secret string }`, `NewKeyring(current Key, older []Key, generation int) Keyring fails BadKeyring`; `Sign(k Keyring, subject string, expires time.Time) string`; `Open(k Keyring, token string, now time.Time) Claims fails Tampered \| Expired \| Retired` |
| `memory` | `memory.New(p session.Policy) memory.Store` |
| `file` | `file.Open(files fs.Files, writes fs.Writes, path string, p session.Policy) file.Store fails session.Failed` |

1. **The store never holds a token.** `Establish` takes a `Digest`, so no provider can keep the token; `Check` looks a token up by its digest (law `StartChecksValid` in `memory`: the token's digest given as a token finds nothing), and the file's log holds the digest in hex (law `LogHoldsNoToken`).
2. **The time is an argument** of each operation, as in `ratelimit` and `jobs`; `Start`, `Check` and `Refresh` read `time.Clock` and `rand.Secure` from the context.
3. **A session's life.** `Judge` gives `Invalid` at or after `maxAge` since `Created`, at or after `idle` since `Renewed`, or when the record's revision is below the subject's floor; else `Renew` at or after `renewAfter` since `Renewed`, else `Valid`. A server that gets `Renew` calls `Refresh`, which rotates the session to a new token and sets `Renewed`; `Created` stays, so renewal never passes `maxAge` (law `RenewalStaysUnderMaxAge` over generated gaps).
4. **One winner.** `Rotate` moves a live session from the old digest to the new one in one step; the old verifies `Invalid` after it, and a second rotation of it is a `Conflict` (law `RotateMovesTheSession`). In `memory`, two tasks that rotate one session at once get one record and one `Conflict` (law `RotateHasOneWinner`, run under `ergo test --explore dfs` too).
5. **Revocation.** `Revoke` ends one session (law `RevokedNeverVerifies`). `RevokeAll(subject, revision)` raises the subject's floor: every session below the revision ends, also one established later below it (law `RevisionEndsEverySession`). A floor never falls.
6. **`memory`** keeps the `State` in one `sync.Ref`; each operation is one atomic `Apply`. **`file`** keeps a log of the changes (`est`, `rot`, `rev`, `all`, tab-separated, a subject quoted), appended under a lock of one `queue.Queue` slot before the caller sees the outcome, and replays it with `Apply` at `Open` (property `FileIsMemory` against a memory store, over reopens and a cut append). A cut last line is dropped; a whole line that is not a change fails `Open` (law `OpenRefusesABrokenLine`). A failed append breaks the store until it is opened again. The log is not compacted yet.
7. **Signed tokens** are the stateless form: `KID.GEN.SUBJECT.SECONDS.MAC`, the subject in hex and the MAC an HMAC-SHA-256 of the fields before it, checked with `hmac.Equal`. `Open` is `Tampered` when no key of the keyring signed the token as it is (law `SignedTamperFails`: one byte changed), `Expired` at or after its expiry, `Retired` below the keyring's generation (law `GenerationBumpEndsTokens`). A keyring keeps older keys so tokens signed before a key change still open (law `OldKeyStillVerifies`). No store can revoke a signed token before its expiry or its generation's end.
8. **Not taken**, each a workaround (W1, W4, W5, W7, W14): yielded's service tags and layers per feature (an operation type a provider implements, passed or bound by `context`); tagged errors (`Conflict`, `Failed`, `Tampered` in the row); a schema for the record (a declared type). Not built yet: cookies, CSRF and the guard (AU5), a signed cookie cache, the state-assisted mode.
9. **Laws and runners.** The laws are beside their declarations: the contract's in `Store`, the rules' and the signed tokens' in `session.ergo`, the providers' in `memory/memory.ergo` and `file/file.ergo`. Lower `TestStdSessionLawsRun` restates them as Ergo tests: the member laws on a memory store and a file store over an `fstest.Memory`.
10. **Receipt** (`benchmarks/session`, `go run ./scripts/opcount -control -rounds 5 benchmarks/session/verify`): instructions and allocations of one `Verify` on a `memory` store of 1024 sessions against hand Go (a map of digests to records under a mutex, two time compares) and the Go floor (one SHA-256 into a fixed array, one map read, no lock).

*Prior art:* yielded/auth (stateful, stateless and state-assisted sessions; `renewAfter < idleTimeout <= maxAge`; a digest in the store; a compare-and-set rotation; a security revision); better-auth (7 days, renewed after 1 day, a cookie cache); Go's scs and gorilla/sessions (a store interface over a token, a map or a table); Phoenix's `Phoenix.Token` and `mix phx.gen.auth` for Elixir (a signed token with a max age; a `users_tokens` table whose rows a log-out deletes, a token sent by email kept as its SHA-256); Gleam's `wisp` (signed cookies with a secret key base); ReScript has none of its own.

## proof and totp

Std `proof` and `totp` (MCP-AUTH-AGENT AU3) are second factors: a one-time code sent to an address, and the codes of an authenticator app. Their source is yielded/auth's proofs (scoped, expiring, single use, attempt limits, delivery services) and its TOTP; Effect's pin has neither. Go's convention is a table of hashed codes with an expiry and an attempt count (`mix phx.gen.auth`'s `users_tokens`, Ory Kratos), and pquerna/otp for TOTP.

| Name | Signature |
| --- | --- |
| `proof.Store` | `Store { Issue(id string, d Digest, expires time.Time, attempts int) fails Conflict \| Failed; Redeem(id string, d Digest, now time.Time) fails Unknown \| Expired \| Spent \| TooManyAttempts \| Wrong \| Failed; Claim(key string, until time.Time, now time.Time) fails Spent \| Failed }` with the laws `RedeemOnce`, `ExpiredFails`, `AttemptsAreBounded`, `WrongPurposeFails` and `ClaimOnce` |
| `proof.Delivery` | `Delivery { Send(dest string, purpose string, code string) fails Undelivered }`, the application's mail or SMS; `Recorder`, `NewRecorder()`, `Outbox(r Recorder) []Sent` for tests |
| digests | `Digest`, `DigestOf(purpose string, code string) Digest`, `DigestText`, `ParseDigest(text string) Digest fails BadDigest` |
| the rules | `Op` = `IssueOp \| RedeemOp \| ClaimOp` (each named for the Store operation it records); `Outcome`; `State`, `Empty() State`, `Apply(s State, op Op) (Outcome, State, bool)` |
| calls | `SendCode(s Store, d Delivery, dest string, purpose string, ttl duration, attempts int) Challenge fails Conflict \| Failed \| Undelivered needs rand.Secure \| time.Clock`; `RedeemCode(s Store, c Challenge, purpose string, code string) fails … needs time.Clock`; `VerifyCode(s Store, p totp.Params, subject string, secret string, code string) fails Wrong \| Spent \| Failed needs time.Clock` |
| `proof/memory`, `proof/file` | `memory.New() memory.Store`; `file.Open(files fs.Files, writes fs.Writes, path string) file.Store fails proof.Failed` |
| `totp` | `NewParams(digits int, period duration, window int) Params fails BadParams`, `Default() Params`, `Period`, `Window`, `StepOf(p Params, t time.Time) int64`, `CodeOf(p Params, secret string, step int64) string`, `Code(p Params, secret string, t time.Time) string`, `Verify(p Params, secret string, code string, now time.Time) Option[int64]`, `type Key`, `KeyOf(secret string) Key`, `VerifyKey(p Params, k Key, code string, now time.Time) Option[int64]`, `VerifyWork(p Params, k Key, code string, now time.Time) (Option[int64], int)`, `URI(p Params, issuer string, account string, secret string) string` |

**totp**

1. **A code** is RFC 6238's: the HMAC-SHA-1 of the step, the whole periods since 1970 rounded down (law `StepsAreWholePeriods`), as eight big-endian bytes; RFC 4226's dynamic truncation at the offset the last byte's low four bits give, to 31 bits; the digits with leading zeros (law `MatchesRFC6238`: the RFC's SHA-1 vectors).
2. **The window.** `Verify` reads the code's number once (a code that is not `digits` decimal digits matches nothing), keys the secret once, compares the number with each step's from `window` before now to `window` after, every step whether or not one matched, and is the step that matched (law `VerifyOfCode`). Its work does not depend on the code: `VerifyWork` is `VerifyKey` and the steps it hashed, and law `SameWorkForEveryCode` holds them equal for a code of the window's first step, of its last and of none, so an early exit on a match fails a law, counted, never timed; a step further away does not verify (law `OutsideWindowFails`). `NewParams` refuses a window outside 0 to 2 periods, 6 to 8 digits, and a period that is not whole seconds (law `NewParamsBoundsTheWindow`): a wider window lets one guess match more codes. `Default` is six digits, 30 seconds and one period on each side, what authenticator apps make.
   A server that checks many codes of one secret keys it once: `KeyOf(secret)` keeps the two HMAC-SHA-1 states after the secret's pads, as `hmac.Key` makes them but as bytes (`runtime/v1/digest.HotpKeyState`), never the secret, and `VerifyKey(p, k, …)` is `Verify` under it (`Verify` is `VerifyKey(p, KeyOf(secret), …)`, so `Verify`'s laws hold it). A step's number is one Go body (`runtime/v1/digest.HotpNumber`): the two states resumed where they lie (no copy), the eight bytes of the step and the tag on the stack, the truncation read from the tag, no allocation (TOTP-FLOOR).
3. **`URI`** is the `otpauth://totp/` text an app reads from a QR code: the secret in base32 with no padding, the issuer and the account escaped (law `URIOfTheRFCSecret`). The package is pure: that a period's code is used once is `proof.VerifyCode`'s.

**proof**

1. **A proof is bound to its purpose.** A store keeps the `Digest` of a code, the SHA-256 of the quoted purpose and the code, never the code (law `LogHoldsNoCode` on the file's log; `SentCodeRedeems`: the code as an ID finds nothing); a code of another purpose is `Wrong` and spends an attempt (law `WrongPurposeFails`).
2. **Single use, expiring, bounded.** `Redeem` with the code's digest before the expiry spends the proof; a second is `Spent` (law `RedeemOnce`); at or after its expiry it is `Expired` (law `ExpiredFails`); each wrong code is `Wrong` with the attempts left, and after the last the right code is `TooManyAttempts` (law `AttemptsAreBounded`). Of two redeems at once one wins (law `RedeemHasOneWinner` in `memory`, run under `ergo test --explore dfs` too).
3. **Claims.** `Claim(key, until, now)` marks a key used until `until`: a second claim before then is `Spent` (law `ClaimOnce`). `VerifyCode` claims a TOTP period's step for the subject until the window no longer reaches it, so a code verifies once, also from the next period (law `StepIsUsedOnce`).
4. **Providers.** `memory` keeps the `State` in one `sync.Ref`; each operation is one atomic `Apply`. `file` keeps a log of the changes (`iss`, `red`, `clm`, tab-separated, an ID quoted), a wrong code included as it spends an attempt, appended under a lock of one `queue.Queue` slot, and replays it with `Apply` at `Open` (property `FileIsMemory` against a memory store, over reopens, a cut append and an expiry's nanoseconds). A whole line that is not a change fails `Open` (law `OpenRefusesABrokenLine`). The log is not compacted yet.
5. **`SendCode`** draws an ID of 16 bytes and a code of six digits from `rand.Secure`, each of the million codes equally likely, issues the proof, then sends the code with the `Delivery` (law `SentCodeRedeems` with a `Recorder`).
6. **Not taken**, each a workaround (W1, W4, W5, W14): yielded's service tags per store and delivery (operation types a provider implements); tagged errors (`Expired`, `Spent`, `TooManyAttempts`, `Wrong` in the row). Not built: rate limits on sending (`ratelimit` is the caller's), recovery codes, passkeys (AU8).
7. **Laws and runners.** The laws are beside their declarations: the contract's in `Store`, the providers' in `memory/memory.ergo` and `file/file.ergo`, totp's in `totp.ergo`. Lower `TestStdProofLawsRun` restates them as Ergo tests, the member laws on a memory store and a file store over an `fstest.Memory`.
8. **Receipt** (`benchmarks/totp`, `go run ./scripts/opcount -control -rounds 5 benchmarks/totp/verify`): instructions and allocations of one `totp.VerifyKey` under a secret keyed once (as the floor keys once), with the default parameters against hand Go (a new `hmac.New` a step, a formatted code) and the Go floor (one keyed HMAC `Reset` a step into a fixed array, a numeric compare, no allocation).

*Prior art:* RFC 6238 and RFC 4226; pquerna/otp for Go (`totp.Validate` with a skew of one period); yielded/auth's proofs and TOTP; better-auth's two-factor plugin (TOTP, OTP by mail, backup codes); Elixir's NimbleTOTP (`verification_code`, `valid?` with `since:` to refuse a reused period) and `mix phx.gen.auth` (hashed email tokens with a validity); Gleam and ReScript have none in their standard libraries.
