## rand

Std `rand` (std-align S2c) follows Go's `math/rand/v2` for names and meaning, and Effect's `Random` for the capability and the seeded provider. The capability is the prelude's `Random`. `TestRandRuns` (lower) runs each function; the laws are in the prelude, `rand/source.ergo` and `rand/laws.ergo`, with their rows in [Std laws](#std-laws).

| Name | Signature | Go body |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `Random` (prelude) | `Random { Uint64() uint64, IntN(n int) int, Float64() float64 }` with the laws `IntNInRange` and `Float64InUnit` | none |
| `PCG`, `Seeded` | the seeded provider: `Seeded(seed uint64) PCG`; `Uint64(p PCG) uint64`, `IntN(p PCG, n int) int`, `Float64(p PCG) float64` | `Stream` (rand.go): Go's `*rand.Rand` over `rand.NewPCG(seed, RandSeedHi)`, behind a lock |
| `Host` | the host's provider: `NewHost() Host needs Ambient`; `Uint64`, `IntN`, `Float64` | `RandHostUint64`, `RandHostIntN`, `RandHostFloat64`: Go's global functions (ChaCha8) |
| `Shuffle`, `Perm`, `Choice` | `Shuffle[T](xs []T) []T needs Random`; `Perm(n int) []int needs Random`; `Choice[T](xs []T) Option[T] needs Random` | Ergo over `Random.IntN` |

1. **Random changed.** The prelude's `Random { Int63() int64 }` becomes Go `math/rand/v2`'s three draws. No program called `Int63` (measured: no `.ergo` file outside the prelude). An `IntN(n)` with `n` of zero or less is a defect, as Go's `IntN` panics.
2. **Seeded replays.** `Seeded(seed)` draws Go's stream of `rand.New(rand.NewPCG(seed, v1.RandSeedHi))`, call for call. It needs no `Ambient`, because it reads no host state: a test or a replay binds it, `context { Random: rand.Seeded(7) }`. `TestSeededRandModel` (runtime/v1) checks it against Go's generator over 1,000 seeds.
3. **One stream per provider.** The state is a runtime/v1 `Stream` that the `PCG` record points to, so every copy of a `PCG` shares one stream: `let b = a` draws on from where `a` is, and does not replay it. Two `Seeded` of one seed replay one stream (`SeedReplays`). The lock keeps the stream whole when tasks draw from one provider at once.
4. **Shuffle and Perm draw as Go's do.** `Shuffle` is Go's Fisher-Yates loop, from the last element down to the second, each swapped with the element at `IntN(i+1)`, so under `Seeded(seed)` it gives Go's `r.Shuffle` order. `Perm(n)` shuffles the identity, as Go's `r.Perm` does. `Shuffle` returns a new slice and leaves its argument, as `slices.Sort` does. `Choice` of an empty slice is `None`.
5. **Laws and runners.** `TestRandLaws` (runtime/v1/rand) runs `IntNInRange` for each `n` in 1..1,000 and 1,000 seeded larger `n`, and `Float64InUnit`, on `Stream` and on the host. `TestStdRandLawsRun` (lower) restates `SeedReplays`, `PermPermutes` and `ShufflePermutes` as Ergo property tests.

*Prior art:* Go's `math/rand/v2` (`PCG`, `ChaCha8`, `IntN` that panics on a non-positive `n`, `Shuffle` and `Perm`); Effect's `Random` service with `Random.make(seed)`, `nextIntBetween`, `shuffle` and `choice`; Gleam's `gleam/int.random` and `list.shuffle` read the host's generator with no seed; Elixir's `:rand` keeps a seeded state per process (`:rand.seed/2`) and `Enum.shuffle` draws from it; ReScript binds JavaScript's `Math.random`, which has no seed.

## filepath

Std `filepath` works on file paths by their text alone (std-align S3a). It follows Go's `path/filepath` for its names, arguments and results, and Effect's `Path` service (`join`, `basename`, `dirname`, `extname`, `normalize`, `relative`, `isAbsolute`) for what it covers. It has no operation type and no capability: no function reads the file system or the working directory. Base, Dir, Clean, IsAbs and Split call Go's `path/filepath` through `import go`, as a user binds it (NP4f); `Split` builds the record `Parts` from Go's two results, one scan. Join, Ext and Rel are `extern go` of pure `runtime/v1` bodies in `path.go` until their `import go` form counts within 1.02 of them (`std-go-share.txt` names why). The laws import `strings` for `strings.HasSuffix`; one Go body has one Ergo extern in the std (`TestStdBindsNoForeignBody`).

| Declaration | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `Join(parts []string) string` | The non-empty parts with a separator between each pair, cleaned; `""` when every part is empty. Ergo has no variadic parameters, so the parts are one slice. |
| `Base(path string) string`, `Dir(path string) string`, `Ext(path string) string` | The last element; the path without it, cleaned; the extension of the last element from its last dot. |
| `Clean(path string) string`, `IsAbs(path string) bool` | Go's lexical cleaning; whether the path begins with a separator. |
| `type Parts { Dir string, File string }`; `Split(path string) Parts` | The path cut after its last separator: `Dir + File` is the path. Go returns the two strings; Ergo returns one record. |
| `type NotRelative { Base string, Target string }`; `Rel(base string, target string) string fails NotRelative` | A path relative to `base` that names `target`. Go's `Rel` returns an `error`; Ergo's fails `NotRelative` in its row. |
| `AbsFrom(dir string, path string) string` | `path` made absolute against the working directory `dir`, by its text: `Clean(path)` when it is absolute, else `Join(dir, path)`. It is Go's `Abs` with the working directory a parameter (CAP-FS2), so it reads no host state; a program passes `os.Process.Getwd()`. Law `AbsFromIsAbsolute`. |
| `type BadPattern { Pattern string }`; `Match(pattern string, name string) bool fails BadPattern` | Go's `Match`: `*` is any run of characters but `/`, `?` one character but `/`, `[...]` a class, `\` escapes. A malformed pattern fails `BadPattern`, Go's `ErrBadPattern`. Law `MatchItself`. |

1. The separator is `/`. Go's targets for Ergo are Linux and darwin, where no path has a volume name; Windows is not a target.
2. Every body is pure ([Pure bodies](#pure-bodies)), so a call of one is transparent in a footprint, and a function that only calls `filepath` has an empty footprint. `Abs`, `EvalSymlinks`, `Glob`, `Walk` and `WalkDir` are not in this package: they read the host. `AbsFrom` is `Abs` with the working directory given; `fs.Resolve`, `fs.Glob` and `fs.Walk` are the others (fs 24). `v1.PureImportsExcept` lists them, and `BodyOf` refuses a pure body that names one.
3. `NotRelative` is the `runtime/v1` struct `v1.NotRelative` ([Types](#v1-packages-task-scope-queue-and-slice) 4). Its text is `filepath.NotRelative{Base:… Target:…}`, the generated text of a record.
4. The laws (Std laws below) are the algebra: `Clean` is idempotent and keeps the root, `Split` rebuilds its path, `Dir` and `Base` join back, `Base` has no separator, `Ext` ends `Base` and is the extension of `Base`, and `Join` of `base` and `Rel(base, target)` is `target`. Each runs in place on its own source (`ergotests -std`); `TestFilepathMatchesGo` holds each body to `path/filepath` on the same paths; `TestFilepathRuns` (lower) runs each function.

*Prior art:* Gleam's `filepath` package and Elixir's `Path` module are lexical and read no host state, except Elixir's `Path.expand/1`, which reads the working directory, as Go's `Abs` does; Effect's `Path` is a service, so a test can replace it, which a lexical function never needs. Ergo keeps Go's names and binds Go's functions directly, as Gleam's externals bind Erlang's `filename`.

## math and bits

Std `math` and `bits` (std-text-runes) are Go's `math` for `float64` and Go's `math/bits`. They follow Go's names, arguments and special cases, and Effect's `Number` module for what it covers (`Number.round`, `Number.remainder`; JavaScript's `Math` behind it). Each export is an `extern go` of a pure `runtime/v1` body in `numbers.go` that calls Go's function of the same name, each marked `//ergo:wait none`, with no release route.

| Package | Signatures |
| --- | --- |
| `math` | `Abs`, `Ceil`, `Floor`, `Trunc`, `Round`, `Sqrt(f float64) float64`; `Pow(x float64, y float64) float64`, `Mod(x float64, y float64) float64`; `FMA(x float64, y float64, z float64) float64` (x*y + z rounded once; [types](types.md#operators) Operators 5); `Log`, `Exp(f float64) float64`; `Sin`, `Cos`, `Tan`, `Asin`, `Acos`, `Atan`, `Cbrt`, `Log2`, `Log10(x float64) float64`, `Atan2(y float64, x float64) float64`, `Hypot(p float64, q float64) float64` (rule 6); `Inf(sign int) float64`, `NaN() float64`, `IsNaN(f float64) bool`, `IsInf(f float64, sign int) bool`; `MaxInt64() int64`, `MinInt64() int64`, `MaxInt() int`, `MinInt() int`; `ToInt64(f float64) int64 fails OutOfRange` and the same `ToT` for every integer type `T` (rule 5), `type OutOfRange { Value float64 }` |
| `bits` | `OnesCount(x uint) int`, `OnesCount64(x uint64) int`, `LeadingZeros`, `TrailingZeros`, `Len(x uint) int` and their `…64(x uint64) int` forms; `RotateLeft32(x uint32, k int) uint32`, `RotateLeft64(x uint64, k int) uint64`; `Add64(x, y, carry uint64) (uint64, uint64)`, `Sub64(x, y, borrow uint64) (uint64, uint64)`, `Mul64(x, y uint64) (uint64, uint64)`, `Div64(hi, lo, y uint64) (uint64, uint64)` (std-spike D2) |

1. Go's constants are functions (`math.MaxInt64()`), because a std package `let` has no Go name in a program's build yet; each is an Ergo body of one literal, so the call is pure and inlines. Ergo's targets have a 64-bit `int`.
2. A negative `k` rotates right, as in Go; `LeadingZeros(0)` and `TrailingZeros(0)` are 64 and `Len(0)` is 0. Go compiles `bits` to one instruction where the target has it (POPCNT, LZCNT, TZCNT, ROL), and a body that only calls it inlines into the caller, so `RotateLeft32` costs what Go's costs.
3. `math/bits` joins `v1.PureImports` for these bodies, so a user `extern go math/bits.…` is allowed too (one list, [Pure bodies](#pure-bodies) 2; [grammar.md](grammar.md#declarations), Extern).
4. The laws (Std laws below): `Floor` and `Ceil` bracket a finite `f` at most 1 apart, `Trunc` moves toward zero and is idempotent, `Abs` is never negative; a rotation by `-k` undoes one by `k` (32 and 64 bits) and keeps the ones, and `Len64(x) + LeadingZeros64(x)` is 64; `Sub64` with the borrow undoes `Add64` with the carry in 128 bits, and `Div64` of a `Mul64` product plus a remainder under `y` gives the factor and the remainder back (`SubUndoesAdd64`, `DivUndoesMul64`). `Div64`'s `y` must be above `hi`, as Go's: else the quotient does not fit and the call is a defect, as an integer division by zero is ([runtime.md](runtime.md), Defects). Each runs in place on its own source (`ergotests -std`); `TestStdRunesMatchGo` holds each body to Go's function on floats of every class (integral, halves, `-0`, the infinities, NaN, random bit patterns) and unsigned ints of every width, bit for bit; lower `TestStdRunesRuns` runs each function from an Ergo program.
5. The checked conversions `ToInt`, `ToInt8`, `ToInt16`, `ToInt32`, `ToInt64`, `ToUint`, `ToUint8`, `ToUint16`, `ToUint32`, `ToUint64` and `ToUintptr` cover every integer type that a float converts to ([types.md](types.md#conversions), Conversions 4; `byte` is `ToUint8` and `rune` is `ToInt32`). `ToT(f)` is `f` toward zero when that fits `T`, and otherwise fails with the one failure `OutOfRange{Value: f}`: for NaN, an infinity, or a float whose truncation is outside `T`'s range, where `T(f)` saturates (or gives 0 for NaN). Each body guards one conversion with the open interval in which the truncation fits. `ToInt`, `ToInt64`, `ToUint`, `ToUint64` and `ToUintptr` are Ergo (NP4f-16), converting through `int64(f)` or `uint64(f)`, whose helper's compare is the guard's own; lower `TestStdMatchesGo` holds each to the old Go body at every bound and the floats next to it. The narrower six are runtime/v1 bodies (`runtime/v1.MathToT`): in Ergo they pay the 64-bit helper's compare inside the guard. Ergo has no integer constraint for one generic function, so each width is a function of its own, with the same failure. Each has one law, `ToTFailsOnlyOutside`: it fails exactly for NaN, for `f` at or past `T`'s greatest value plus one, and for a truncation below `T`'s least value, and otherwise gives `Trunc(f)`, which is `T(f)`. `ergotests -std` (the narrow six, in place) and lower `TestStdLawsRun` (the wide five) run the eleven laws over floats at every bound of the conversion and the floats next to them. Go has no checked form: a caller compares by hand, and the comparison `f < math.MaxInt64` is itself wrong, since `float64(math.MaxInt64)` is 2^63. Effect has none, since JavaScript numbers are floats. Rust has `i64::try_from` only for integers, and for floats a hand range check.
6. The trig and logarithm functions (SQ1) are Go's own, called through `import go math` with no wrapper frame, so a call is Go's call: the same bits as a Go program on the same host, Go's special cases (`Sin(±Inf)` is NaN, `Atan2(±0, -0)` is ±Pi, `Hypot(±Inf, NaN)` is +Inf), and Go's assembly or software routine per architecture. Across hosts the bits may differ where Go's routine does (an architecture with its own assembly, or a fused multiply-add inside a software routine on arm64); the property MathIsGo (lower `TestStdMatchesGo`, the `math.Sin` … `math.Hypot` rows) holds the build to Go on the host it runs on, on edge and seeded inputs. The laws hold bit for bit on every host: `Sin`, `Tan`, `Asin`, `Atan` and `Cbrt` are odd and `Cos` even, `Acos` lies in [0, Pi] and `Atan` in [-Pi/2, Pi/2], `Atan` and `Log10` never fall as their argument rises, `Atan2(y, x)` is `Atan(y/x)` for a finite x > 0, `Hypot` reads neither the signs nor the order of its sides, `Cbrt` of an integer's cube is the integer and `Log2` of a power of two its exponent (stdlib `TestStdMathTrigLaws`, in process). Effect's `Number` has none of them; JavaScript's `Math.sin` … `Math.log10` promise no bits either.

*Prior art:* Gleam's `gleam/float` binds Erlang's `math` functions (`floor`, `ceiling`, `truncate`) as externals and `gleam/int` gives bitwise operations over BEAM instructions; ReScript's `Math` binds JavaScript's `Math` directly, with `Math.clz32` the only bit count; Elixir's `Bitwise` is BEAM instructions and `:math` is Erlang's. Ergo binds Go's functions with no wrapper, so Go's compiler sees its own intrinsics.

## Keyed digests

Std `sha512`, `sha1`, `hmac` and `uuid`, and `rand.Secure` (std-spike D3): the digests, message authentication, secure random bytes and ids a service needs, over Go's `crypto` packages.

| Signatures | Go body |
| --- | --- |
| `sha512.Sum(s string) string` (64 raw bytes); `sha1.Sum(s string) string` (20 raw bytes) | `crypto/sha512`, `crypto/sha1` (`runtime/v1/digest/keyed.go`) |
| `type hmac.Hash = SHA256 \| SHA512 \| SHA1`; `hmac.Size(h Hash) int`; `hmac.Sum(h Hash, key string, message string) string`; `hmac.Equal(a string, b string) bool` | `crypto/hmac` |
| `type hmac.Keyed`; `hmac.Key(h Hash, key string) Keyed`; `hmac.Tag(k Keyed, message string) string` | the hash's `MarshalBinary` states (`runtime/v1/digest/keyed.go`) |
| `type rand.Secure { Bytes(n int) string }`; `rand.NewSecureHost() SecureHost needs Ambient`; `rand.NewSeededSecure(seed uint64) SeededSecure` | `crypto/rand`; a PCG stream |
| `type uuid.UUID` (two `uint64`s); `type uuid.Malformed { Text string, At int }`; `Nil`, `V4() UUID needs rand.Secure`; `type uuid.Generator`, `NewGenerator() Generator`, `V7(g Generator) UUID needs rand.Secure \| time.Clock`; `Version`, `UnixMilli`, `Compare`, `Text`, `Parse(s string) UUID fails Malformed` | Ergo |

1. **Bytes in strings.** As `sha256`'s, each digest and tag is raw bytes in a string; `hex.Encode` writes the usual text. `sha1` has known collisions: it is for formats that name it (Git ids, WebSocket, TOTP), never a signature.
2. **HMAC.** `hmac.Sum(h, key, message)` is Go's `hmac.New(h's New, key)` over `message`, `Size(h)` bytes (law `SumHasSize`); it gives RFC 4231's tags for SHA-256 and SHA-512 and RFC 2202's for SHA-1 (law `MatchesRFC4231`). `hmac.Equal` answers as `==` does (law `EqualIsEquality`) in a time that depends only on the lengths: check a received tag with it, never `==`.
   A key that signs many messages is made ready once: `hmac.Key(h, key)` hashes the key's two pads (RFC 2104) and keeps the two hash states, never the key, and `hmac.Tag(k, message)` resumes them, so each tag costs its message alone, as Go's `Reset` of a reused `hmac.New` does; it is `Sum(h, key, message)` (law `TagIsSum`, keys longer than a block included). A `Keyed` is a value: two tasks may tag with one at once.
3. **Secure is not Random.** `Random` (the prelude's) is fast and replayable, so its draws can be predicted; `rand.Secure` is for keys, tokens and ids. `SecureHost` reads Go's `crypto/rand` and needs `Ambient`, as every host provider does; `SeededSecure` replays a PCG stream for tests (law `SeededSecureReplays`) and is never secure. A draw of `n` bytes has `n` bytes (law `Secure.BytesHasLength`); an `n` under zero is a defect.
4. **UUIDs (RFC 9562).** `V4` is 122 bits from `rand.Secure` with version 4 and the RFC's variant (law `V4HasVersionAndVariant`). `g.V7()` is the Unix millisecond from `time.Clock` in the first 48 bits, a 12-bit counter, then 62 bits from `rand.Secure` (RFC 9562 §6.2, method 1). The counter starts at a random value under 2048 in each new millisecond and counts up within it; when it is spent, or the clock reads back, the id takes the generator's last millisecond, plus one when the counter is spent. So every id one `Generator` makes sorts after the one before, by `Compare` and by `Text`, in one millisecond too (laws `V7IsOrderedInProcess` and `V7IsOrderedByTime`, under `time.NewManual`). A `Generator` holds its last millisecond and counter in one `atomic.Int64`, so tasks that share it share its order; make one per process. Ids of two generators or two processes are ordered only across milliseconds. `Text` is the 36-byte lower-case form; `Parse` reads it in either case and refuses every other form at the byte that breaks it (laws `TextRoundTrips`, `ParseRefusesOtherForms`). Runner of the hmac and uuid laws: lower `TestStdKeyedLawsRun`.

*Prior art and not taken.* Go's `crypto/hmac` takes a `func() hash.Hash`; Ergo takes a union, so the set of hashes is closed and matched. Effect has no hmac, secure random or uuid; its `Random` would make a guessable token, which is why `Secure` is a separate operation type. Gleam's `gleam/crypto` has `hmac`, `secure_compare` and `strong_random_bytes`; Elixir uses `:crypto.mac/4`, `Plug.Crypto.secure_compare/2` and `:crypto.strong_rand_bytes/1`, and `Ecto.UUID` for v4. Go's std has no uuid (`github.com/google/uuid` reads `crypto/rand` and `time.Now` directly); Ergo's reads them through the row, so ids replay in tests. The counter's state is a value the caller makes and passes (`NewGenerator`), not a process global as in `google/uuid`: Ergo has no package state, and a test gets a fresh order per generator. Method 3 (a random increment) is not taken: method 1's counter gives 2048 or more ids per millisecond with no extra draw.

## decimal

Std `decimal` (std-spike D2) is exact decimal numbers for money and quantities: Effect's `BigDecimal`, with a fixed 128-bit coefficient (38 digits) in place of a big integer, written in Ergo over `bits`' 128-bit steps.

| Signatures | Go body |
| --- | --- |
| `type Decimal` (unexported fields: sign, a 128-bit coefficient, scale); `type Overflow {}`, `type DivideByZero {}`, `type Malformed { Text string, At int }`; `MaxScale() int` (38); `Zero() Decimal`; `FromInt(n int64) Decimal`; `New(units int64, scale int) Decimal fails Overflow`; `Scale`, `Sign`, `Neg`, `Abs`; `Add`, `Sub`, `Mul(a, b Decimal) Decimal fails Overflow`; `Cmp(a, b Decimal) int`, `Equal(a, b Decimal) bool` | Ergo |
| `type RoundingMode = HalfUp \| HalfEven \| Down \| Up \| Floor \| Ceil`; `Round(d Decimal, scale int, mode RoundingMode) Decimal fails Overflow`; `Div(a, b Decimal, scale int, mode RoundingMode) Decimal fails DivideByZero \| Overflow` | Ergo |
| `Parse(s string) Decimal fails Malformed \| Overflow`; `Text(d Decimal) string` | Ergo |

1. **A value.** A `Decimal` is a sign, a coefficient under 2¹²⁸ and a scale from 0 to 38: `New(1250, 2)` is 12.50. It holds no pointer, so a copy is a copy and `==` compares coefficient and scale, as `Text` writes them: 1.5 and 1.50 are `!=`, and `Equal` and `Cmp` compare the numbers. This is Java `BigDecimal`'s `equals` trap (Python's `Decimal` compares numbers). Ergo cannot yet declare a type that refuses `==` (every record of equatable fields takes it, [types.md](types.md) Operators 1), so `==` stays and is documented; unit CK-NOEQ would let `Decimal` refuse it and make a caller choose `Equal` or the written form. A zero is never negative.
2. **Exact or a typed failure.** `Add`, `Sub` and `Mul` are exact, at the larger scale and at the sum of the scales; a result whose coefficient needs more than 128 bits, or whose scale passes 38, fails with `Overflow`, never wraps or rounds; so does an operand of `Add` or `Sub` whose coefficient, moved to the larger scale, needs more than 128 bits (law `RingLaws`, `CmpIsSubSign`).
3. **Rounding is named.** `Round` and `Div` take the result's scale and a `RoundingMode`: `HalfUp` (a tie away from zero, Java's `HALF_UP`), `HalfEven` (a tie to the even neighbour), `Down` (toward zero), `Up` (away from zero), `Floor` and `Ceil`. Each lands on the neighbour it names, within one unit of the last place and within half of it for the half modes (law `RoundIsNearest`); `HalfUp` and `HalfEven` differ only at a tie (law `HalfUpDiffersFromHalfEvenOnlyAtTies`). `Round` to a larger scale is exact. `Div` by a zero fails with `DivideByZero`; it multiplies the dividend's coefficient by `10^(scale + b's scale - a's scale)` before it divides, and a product over 128 bits is `Overflow` (law `DivUndoesMul`).
4. **Text.** `Text` is plain digits with a point and `Scale(d)` digits after it ("-0.035", "12.50"); `Parse` reads exactly that form: an optional `-`, digits, and an optional point with digits after it. Signs other than `-`, exponents, spaces and separators are `Malformed` with the byte where the form breaks (law `TextRoundTrips`).
5. **Checked against `big.Rat`.** lower `TestStdDecimalMatchesRat` runs 20,000 seeded operations (parse, add, sub, mul, round and div in every mode, near the 128-bit bound, and divisions whose divisor passes 128 bits while the dividend is near half of it) and holds each result to Go's `math/big.Rat` and an exact rounding of it; a failure prints the operation and both results. Runner of the laws: lower `TestStdDecimalLawsRun`.

*Prior art and not taken.* Effect's `BigDecimal` is a `bigint` and a scale, normalized for equality; its divide rounds to a fixed precision, and `unsafeDivide` throws. Ergo keeps the scale the user wrote (money is "12.50"), fails with a typed `DivideByZero` in place of an `Option` or a throw (W5, W10), and is data-first (W3). It takes no `Equal`/`Hash` instance (W8): `==` is the representation, `Equal` the number. A big-integer coefficient and exponent forms ("1e5") are not taken: 38 digits cover money and quantities, and a fixed size keeps a `Decimal` a plain value with no allocation. Go has no decimal in its std; `shopspring/decimal` is a `*big.Int` and an exponent; Java's `BigDecimal` and C#'s 96-bit `decimal` are the models for named rounding modes and a fixed coefficient. Gleam and Elixir's std have none (Elixir's comes from the `Decimal` package, a big integer).

## cron

Std `cron` (std-spike D4) is cron expressions over `civil` and `tz`: Effect's `Cron`, promoted from `examples/cron`'s schedule package, written in Ergo alone.

| Signatures | Go body |
| --- | --- |
| `type Expr { Minute Field, Hour Field, Dom Field, Month Field, Dow Field }`; `type Field` (unexported bits); `Has(f Field, v int) bool`, `IsStar(f Field) bool`; `Parse(text string) Expr fails FieldCount \| BadValue \| OutOfRange \| BadStep \| BadRange`; `Format(e Expr) string` | Ergo |
| `Horizon() int` (28); `Matches(e Expr, dt civil.DateTime) bool`; `NextWall`, `PreviousWall(e Expr, dt civil.DateTime) Option[civil.DateTime]` | Ergo |
| `Next`, `Previous(e Expr, z tz.Zone, t time.Time) Option[time.Time]` | Ergo |

1. **The expression.** Five fields, Vixie cron's: minute (0-59), hour (0-23), day of the month (1-31), month (1-12 or `jan`-`dec`), day of the week (0-7 or `sun`-`sat`; 7 is Sunday, law `WeekdaySevenIsSunday`). A field is a list of parts; a part is `*`, a value or a range, each with an optional step. The macros `@yearly`, `@annually`, `@monthly`, `@weekly`, `@daily`, `@midnight` and `@hourly` stand for their fields (law `MacrosAreTheirFields`). A step counts from its part's first value: `3-59/15` is 3, 18, 33 and 48, and `5/20` is 5, 25 and 45 (law `StepsCountFromTheStart`). When neither day field is `*` (or `*/s`), a day matches when either does; else both must (law `EitherDayMatches`). `Parse` fails with the field and the text it refuses; `Format` writes the canonical text, which `Parse` reads back (law `FormatParseRoundTrips`).
2. **Wall times.** `Matches` is whether e fires at the minute of a wall time. `NextWall` and `PreviousWall` are the first wall minute after, and the last before, at which e fires: they skip whole months, days and hours that cannot match, and agree with a walk of every minute (laws `NextWallIsFirstMatch`, `PreviousWallIsLastMatch`). A walk reads a date's fields from its day number once and then carries them from day to day and month to month, so it never converts a day number to a date at each step (law `WalksKeepTheCalendar`, 400 days and 30 months each way). `BenchmarkStdCron`'s 5,120 `Next`s count 27.3M user-space instructions against 70.3M for the hand Go evaluator, down from 63.4M, and make no allocation each (77 allocations per run, all in parsing and loading the zone, against the hand Go's 44; std-spike CRON-1, measured). An expression that does not fire within `Horizon()` years (28: a 29th of February comes back on each weekday within it) never fires, as `0 0 30 2 *`: `None`.
3. **Instants in a zone.** `Next(e, z, t)` is the first instant after t at which e fires in z: a wall minute fires at `tz.Compatible(tz.Resolve(z, w))`, which `Next` takes as `tz.CompatibleShift(z, w)` with no allocation, so a minute the zone skips fires once, a gap later (a job at 02:30 runs at 03:30 on the day New York skips 02:00 to 03:00), and a minute the zone shows twice fires once, at its first showing (laws `GapFiresOnce`, `OverlapFiresOnce`). It agrees with a walk of every wall minute from a day before t to two days after (law `NextIsFirstFiring`, in UTC, a fixed offset, New York and Lord Howe's half-hour change, half the time near a change). `Previous` is the last firing before t, and the next firing after it is at or after t (law `PreviousUndoesNext`). Runner of the laws: lower `TestStdCronLawsRun`.
4. **A schedule.** `schedule.Cron(e, z, start)` continues at each firing ([Schedule](#schedule)).

*Prior art and not taken.* Effect's `Cron` holds the five fields as sets plus seconds and a zone, `Cron.next` walks the same way, and `Schedule.cron` builds on it; `parseUnsafe` (a throw) and `Either` results are not taken: `Parse` has a typed failure row (W5, W10), and the zone is an argument of `Next`, not a field, so one `Expr` serves every zone. A seconds field is not taken: Vixie cron, crontab and Kubernetes have five fields. Go's std has no cron; `robfig/cron` runs a job at a gap minute's next real instant and twice in a repeated hour unless the caller works around it; Vixie cron and cronie fire a fixed-time job once on each kind of change, as this package does, and its rule is ECMA-262/Temporal's `compatible`. Gleam and Elixir's std have none (Elixir's `Quantum` and `Crontab` libraries parse the same fields).

## Encodings and digests

Std `hex`, `base64`, `sha256` and `crc32` (G3 std-digest) are Go's `encoding/hex`, `encoding/base64`, `crypto/sha256` and `hash/crc32` over Ergo's `string`, which holds bytes ([types.md, Strings](types.md) 1), so they need no byte-slice type. They match Effect's `Encoding` module (`encodeHex`, `decodeHex`, `encodeBase64`, `encodeBase64Url` and their decoders) for behaviour; Effect has no hash module. `sha256.Sum` and `crc32.Checksum` call Go's `Sum256` and `ChecksumIEEE` through `import go`, as a user binds them (NP4f), on a view of the string's bytes with no copy ([interop.md, Foreign facts](interop.md) 8). Every other extern binds a pure `runtime/v1` body in `digest.go` of the `runtime/v1/digest` leaf, each marked `//ergo:wait none`, with no release route.

| Package | Signatures |
| --- | --- |
| `hex` | `Encode(s string) string`; `Decode(s string) string fails Malformed`; `type Malformed { Offset int }` |
| `base64` | `type Encoding = StdEncoding \| URLEncoding`; `Encode(e Encoding, s string) string`; `Decode(e Encoding, s string) string fails Malformed`; `type Malformed { Offset int }` |
| `sha256` | `Sum(s string) string` (32 raw bytes) |
| `crc32` | `Checksum(s string) uint32`; `Update(crc uint32, s string) uint32` (IEEE polynomial) |

1. `hex.Encode` writes lower-case digits; `Decode` reads either case. Its `Malformed.Offset` is the index of the first byte that is not a hex digit, or the length for an odd-length text of digits (Go's `InvalidByteError` and `ErrLength`).
2. `base64` pads with `=` in both alphabets, as Go's `StdEncoding` and `URLEncoding`. The alphabet is a union, so `Encode` and `Decode` are Ergo bodies that match it to a private extern each, where Go has two `*Encoding` values and Effect two function names. `Decode` fails `Malformed` at Go's `CorruptInputError` offset for unpadded text or a byte outside the alphabet, and skips CR and LF as Go does.
3. `sha256.Sum` is the digest's 32 raw bytes, as Go's `Sum256` and as Gleam's `gleam/crypto` and Elixir's `:crypto.hash/2` give it; `hex.Encode(sha256.Sum(s))` is its usual text.
4. `crc32.Update(crc, s)` carries a checksum over more bytes, as Go's `Update` with `IEEETable`, so a text read in parts has the checksum of the whole.
5. The four Go packages join `v1.PureImports`, so a user `extern go encoding/hex.…` is allowed too ([Pure bodies](#pure-bodies) 2), and they leave `v1.PureUnlisted` (a table IG2 deleted). `hex.Malformed` and `base64.Malformed` are std records bound to `runtime/v1`'s `HexMalformed` and `Base64Malformed`.
6. The laws (Std laws below): `Decode` inverts `Encode` (hex, and base64 in both alphabets), hex doubles the length, base64's length is `(len(s) + 2) / 3 * 4`, every SHA-256 digest is 32 bytes, and `Update` chains (`Update(Checksum(a), b) == Checksum(a + b)`, `Update(0, s) == Checksum(s)`). Each runs in place on its own source (`ergotests -std`); lower `TestStdDigestRuns` holds every function, called from an Ergo program, to Go's packages on seeded bytes, the FIPS 180-4 example inputs and malformed texts, with the `Malformed` offset a `catch` reads.

*Prior art:* Gleam's `gleam/bit_array` (`base16_encode`, `base64_encode`, `base64_url_encode`) and `gleam/crypto.hash` work on `BitArray`s over Erlang's `binary` and `crypto`; Elixir's `Base.encode16/1`, `Base.url_encode64/1` and `:crypto.hash/2` work on binaries, which are Elixir's strings, as Ergo's strings are bytes; ReScript binds JavaScript's `btoa` and has no digest. Ergo binds Go's functions, so each body is one conversion and Go's call.

## semver

Std `semver` (SQ4) reads semantic versions and npm's version ranges as node-semver 7 reads them: strict, without `loose` or `includePrerelease`. Effect has no counterpart; Bun's `Bun.semver` (`satisfies`, `order`) and Go's `golang.org/x/mod/semver` (`Compare` over `vX.Y.Z`) are the controls. It is plain Ergo over the bytes of its text, so check-time evaluation runs it.

| Package | Signatures |
| --- | --- |
| `semver` | `type Version { Major int, Minor int, Patch int, Pre []string, Build []string }`; `Parse(text string) Version fails Malformed`; `Format(v Version) string`; `Compare(a Version, b Version) int`; `type Op = Equal \| Less \| AtMost \| Greater \| AtLeast`; `type Comparator { Op Op, Version Version }`; `type Range { Sets [][]Comparator }`; `ParseRange(text string) Range fails Malformed`; `FormatRange(r Range) string`; `Satisfies(r Range, v Version) bool`; `Holds(c Comparator, v Version) bool`; `type Malformed { Text string }` |

1. `Parse` is `semver.valid`: white space around the text is dropped and one leading `v` is allowed; MAJOR, MINOR and PATCH are numbers with no leading zero up to 2^53-1 (JavaScript's `MAX_SAFE_INTEGER`); the pre-release after `-` and the build after `+` are dot-separated identifiers of `[0-9A-Za-z-]`, and a numeric pre-release identifier has no leading zero. A text over 256 bytes is `Malformed`. `Format` writes the version without its build, as node's `version`.
2. `Compare` is SemVer 2.0's precedence, node's `compare` and x/mod's `Compare` on full versions: MAJOR, MINOR, PATCH, then a pre-release before none, then pre-release identifiers in turn (numbers by value, before words; words by their bytes; a shorter list first). A numeric identifier compares by its digits, with no bound, so it agrees with node's `Number` compare below 2^53. Build metadata never changes the order.
3. `ParseRange` is `new Range`: sets joined by `||`, each a hyphen range `A - B` or white-space-separated comparators; `~`, `~>`, `^`, x-ranges (`1.x`, `1.2.*`, `*`), partial versions and hyphen ranges desugar to plain comparators exactly as node-semver's `replaceTilde`, `replaceCaret`, `replaceXRange`, `replaceStars` and `hyphenReplace` (`^0.0.3` is `>=0.0.3 <0.0.4-0`). A set holding the null comparator `<0.0.0-0` is that comparator alone, and null sets are dropped when another set remains. `FormatRange` is `semver.validRange`'s text, `*` for every version.
4. `Satisfies` is `semver.satisfies`: some set whose comparators all hold, and a version with a pre-release only when a comparator of that set names a pre-release of the same MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH, so `^1.2.3` holds for no `1.3.0-beta`.
5. The property SemverIsNode (eval `TestSemverIsNode`) replays `internal/sketch/eval/testdata/semver/node.jsonl`: answers node-semver 7.8.5 gave for `valid`, `validRange`, `satisfies` and `compare` on versions and ranges that `record.ts` beside it generates from the grammar with near misses. Node never runs at test time.
6. The laws (Std laws below): `Compare` is antisymmetric and transitive and ignores build metadata, `Parse` inverts `Format`, and a range of one comparator holds for a release version exactly when the comparator does. lower `TestStdSemverLaws` runs them in the evaluator.

*Prior art:* Elixir's `Version` (`parse`, `compare`, `match?` with `~>` requirements) is the closest std module; Rust's `semver` crate holds Cargo's ranges, where a bare version means `^`; Gleam and ReScript ship none, and their package managers parse Hex and npm ranges in the tool. Ergo's follows npm's grammar because its package lane (PKG N1) reads npm's registry.

## csv

Std `csv` (std-csv U4) is Go's `encoding/csv` with its defaults, over a text: a comma between fields, RFC 4180 quotes (a quote inside a quoted field written twice), CR LF at a line's end read as LF, `LazyQuotes` off, and every record as wide as the first. Its bodies in `csv.go` of the `runtime/v1/csv` leaf run Go's reader on a `strings.Reader` and Go's writer on a `strings.Builder`, so they are pure ([Pure bodies](#pure-bodies)). Effect has no CSV module.

| Name | Signature | Go body |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `ParseError` | `ParseError { Line int, Column int, Reason string, Message string }`: Go's `*csv.ParseError`, with `Err`'s text as `Reason` and the whole text as `Message` | `CSVParseError` |
| `ReadAll` | `ReadAll(text string) [][]string fails ParseError` | `CSVReadAll` |
| `Rows`, `Row` | `Rows(text string) Seq[Row]`; `Row = Parsed { Fields []string, At []Position } \| Malformed { Error ParseError }` | Ergo over `CSVScan` |
| `Position` | `Position { Line int, Column int }`: where a field starts, Go's `Reader.FieldPos` | `CSVPosition` |
| `Format` | `Format(rows [][]string) string` | `CSVFormat` |

1. **ReadAll** is Go's `csv.NewReader(strings.NewReader(text)).ReadAll()`, failing `ParseError` in the row, with Go's line, column and texts (`record on line 2: wrong number of fields`). A text with no record is no rows.
2. **Rows** gives each record as `Parsed` and, when the text breaks the format, the records before the error and then one `Malformed` with ReadAll's error. A `Seq` cannot fail, so the error is a row of the union, which a `match` must cover, where Go's `Read` returns an error beside a record. Rows is lazy and reads the text once (fs3): `CSVScan` runs Go's `Reader.Read` loop on the text and hands each record to the loop over the `Seq` when it asks for one, so a loop that stops (a `break`, or a `yield` that returns false) reads no further record, and the records before a break and the error come from that one pass. `TestCSVScanStopsTheRead` (runtime/v1) holds that a stop leaves the rest of the text unread. Each `Parsed` carries `At`, where each of its fields starts (fu6c, G-exup-6): Go's `Reader.FieldPos` for every field, a line and a byte column from 1, as `ParseError` has, and a quoted field starts at its opening quote. The purpose is an error that names the input: a program that finds a bad value in a field reports `file:line:column`, as the reader's own errors do, and a record's line differs from its count of records when a quoted field spans lines. Go gives the position on demand from the reader; here the record carries it, because a `Seq` hands on values, not the reader. Rust's `csv` gives one `Position` per record (byte, line, record); Python's `csv.reader` gives `line_num`, the line the reader has read to. Effect has no CSV module. `ReadAll` stays Go's `[][]string`, and a program that needs positions reads `Rows`.
3. **Format** is Go's `csv.Writer` with a line feed after each row: a field is quoted when it holds a comma, a quote, a CR or a LF, starts with a space, or is `\.`. One change from Go: a row of one empty field is written `""`, where Go writes an empty line that its reader skips, so every row with a field reads back. A row of no field is an empty line, which ReadAll skips.
4. **Laws.** `RoundTrip` (rows ReadAll can give read back from Format's text), `LinesCount` (Format writes one line per row and one per line feed in a field), `FormatIsStable` (Format of what ReadAll read from Format's text is the text) `RowsFollowReadAll` (Rows gives ReadAll's rows, or ends in one Malformed with its error) `RowsKeepTheRecordsBefore` (the records before a break are Parsed, in order), `PositionsPointAtFields` (one position per field, in order, at the field's text or its opening quote) and `RowsStops` (Rows stops when yield returns false; [Std laws](#std-laws) 5), over fields spiced with commas, quotes, CR and LF. Lower `TestStdCSVLawsRun` runs them; runtime/v1 `TestStdCSVMatchesGo` holds the bodies to Go's reader (its records, errors and `FieldPos`) and writer on 20,000 seeded texts and 20,000 seeded row sets.
5. `encoding/csv` is in `v1.PureImportHostMembers` with no host member, not in `PureImports`: a pure body's file imports neither `io` nor `os`, so the reader and writer can only be given a string, while a user extern of `encoding/csv` could hand them `os.Stdin`.

*Prior art:* Go's `encoding/csv`; Elixir's `NimbleCSV` (a library, RFC 4180, a parser per separator); Gleam has no std CSV (`gsv` is a package); ReScript programs bind a JavaScript library (`papaparse`). Python's `csv` module writes `""` for a lone empty field for the same reason as `Format`.

## json

`Optional[T] = Absent | Present[T]` selects an absent or present record key.
`Nullable[T] = Null | NonNull[T]` selects null or the bare value. A Nullable
key is required. `Optional[Nullable[T]]` keeps all three states. `Option`
is unchanged. These are ordinary closed unions (exhaustive `match`), with
a codec representation selected in lowering, not a checker exception or
field annotation. The field's type after instantiation selects the form: a
field typed `T` in `Box[T]` omits its key when `T` is `json.Optional[int]`,
also inside a generic function, where the codec witness of `T` carries the
field form. `Optional` outside a record field keeps its tagged form;
there is no absent root or array element. Failure rows remain conservative
structural rows, including the union's possible shape failures.

`Writer.Null` writes a null event. `Reader.TakeNull` consumes null and
returns true, or leaves the next value and its path unchanged on false.
Both the JSON reader and codec's event replay support this event.

Effect Schema's intent is to distinguish absent keys from explicit null.
Ergo does not take schema classes, field annotations or runtime tag checks:
the field type and exhaustive unions express those states. ReScript uses
optional record fields and `Nullable.t`; Gleam separates `optional_field`
from `optional`; Go's `omitempty` and default Elixir maps have only one
empty-value policy, so a separate wrapper is needed for a third state.

`Value = Null | Boolean | Num | Str | Array | Object` is an untyped JSON
value (G4): a field the program passes on without reading its shape. It is
a wire type, alone or as a record field, and a program reads it with an
exhaustive `match`. `Object` is `[]Member` (`Member { Key, Value }`), so it
keeps the order of the members. `Num` keeps the spelling of a number;
`ParseNum` (which fails `Malformed` on a text that is not one JSON number),
`IntNumber` and `Num.Wire()` are the only ways in and out, so a `Num` is
always a valid number. The codec writes each kind as the bare JSON value,
not as a tagged record. The law `ValueRoundTrips`: `Encode(Decode(text))`
is `text` without insignificant white space, with strings in the encoder's
escaping. **Rule for a repeated key:** `Value` keeps every member, a
repeated key included, in order. It does not interpret keys, so it passes
on what it read; the row `JsonValueRepeatedKey` holds this. `Writer.Number`
writes a spelling as it is; `Reader.Peek` gives the next value's kind
(`WireKind`, a prelude union, the end of a list or object included) and
`Reader.Number` reads a spelling. Codec's `Replay` reads a tag as the member
`"_tag"` (`Key` leaves it, `String` reads it). Prior art: Go's
`json.RawMessage` keeps the bytes, unread; ReScript's `JSON.t` and Gleam's
`Dynamic` are trees that the host's parser builds, and they drop number
spellings; Elixir Jason keeps member order only with `objects: :ordered_objects`
and takes the last of a repeated key.

Std `json` (S-json) writes and reads the derived codec's events ([types.md, Codecs](types.md#codecs)) as JSON text (RFC 8259). It is the format Effect's `Schema.parseJson` gives a schema, without a schema value: the codec is the type's own.

| Name | Signature | Go body |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `Codec` | `Codec[T wire] { MaxBytes int, MaxDepth int }` | Ergo |
| `Encode` | `Encode[T wire](c Codec[T], v T) string` | Ergo over `strings.Hint`, `Borrow`, `Give`, `FromBytes` |
| `EncodeWith` | `EncodeWith[T wire, U, R](c Codec[T], v T, use func(text []byte) U needs R) U needs R \| wired[T]`: lends `use` v's text in the borrowed buffer, taken back when `use` returns (http SuccessJSON, GX9) | Ergo over `strings.Hint`, `Borrow`, `Give` |
| `Decode` | `Decode[T wire](c Codec[T], text string) T fails Malformed \| wired[T]` | Ergo over `Open`, `decode[T]` |
| `Indent`, `EncodeIndent` | `Indent(text string, prefix string, indent string) string fails Malformed` validates one JSON value using `Open`'s default limits. It starts each added line with `prefix` and one `indent` per nesting level, adds one space after `:`, and keeps empty arrays and objects on one line. It drops leading and interior layout whitespace and preserves trailing whitespace, as Go's `encoding/json.Indent` does. `EncodeIndent[T wire](c Codec[T], v T, prefix string, indent string) string needs wired[T]` applies the same layout to `Encode`'s output. Use only JSON whitespace in `prefix` and `indent` to keep the output valid JSON. | Ergo over `Open` and `Encode` |
| `Malformed` | `Malformed { Offset int, Reason string }` | `JSONMalformed` |
| `Text`, `NewText`, `NewHTMLText` | a sealed `Writer` with `Null()` and `Written() string` | Ergo: the provider `text { comma bool, html bool, out []byte }` over the value appenders `JSONAppendQuoted`, `JSONAppendInt`, `JSONAppendUint`, `JSONAppendBool`, `JSONAppendFloat` and Go's `append` |
| `Open`, `Reader` | `Open(text string, maxBytes int, maxDepth int) Reader fails Malformed`; `Reader` is a `Reader` | Ergo over `JSONOpen` and `Cursor` |
| `Raw`, `RawOf` | `Raw { text string }`, the text of one JSON value as read; `RawOf(text string) Raw fails Malformed`; `r.String() string`; by-name pair `Wire`/`ParseRaw` over `Value` | Ergo over `Cursor.SkipText` (`(*Cursor).SkipText`) |
| `NewStream`, `Stream` | `NewStream(src io.Source, maxBytes int, maxDepth int) Stream`; `Stream` is a `Reader` | Ergo, none (rule 7) |
| `Settle` | `Settle(r Stream) fails Malformed \| io.ReadFailed` | Ergo |
| `NewSequenceStream`, `Window` | `NewSequenceStream(src io.Source, maxBytes int, maxDepth int) Stream`; `Window(r Stream) int` | Ergo |
| `Elements`, `NewElements`, `Next` | `NewElements[T](r Stream) Elements[T]`; `e.Next() Option[T] fails Malformed \| io.ReadFailed \| WireWrongKind \| wired[T]` | Ergo over `Stream`, `decode[T]`, `Settle` |
| `DecodeStream` | `DecodeStream[T wire](c Codec[T], src io.Source) T fails Malformed \| io.ReadFailed \| wired[T]` | Ergo over `NewStream`, `decode[T]`, `Settle` |

1. **Form** (owner q81). A record is an object whose keys are its field names with the leading capitals lowered (`Port` as `port`, `ID` as `id`, `HTTPStatus` as `httpStatus`; types.md, Codecs 2); a union value an object whose `"_tag"` member names its variant (Effect's form), so `Option` is `{"_tag":"Some","value":…}`; a `Map` an object in key order; a slice or tuple an array. An integer is its exact digits (the Effect RPC profile's ±(2^53−1) rule is the R units'). A float is written as `encoding/json` writes it (JavaScript's `Number` form), and NaN and ±Inf, which JSON has no numbers for, as the strings `"NaN"`, `"Infinity"`, `"-Infinity"`, which `Float` reads back (protobuf's JSON mapping), so `Encode` cannot fail.
2. **Strings.** `"`, `\` and the control bytes are escaped (`\n`, `\r`, `\t`, else `\u00XX`), U+2028 and U+2029 too; no HTML escaping (`encoding/json/v2`'s default), unless the Text is `NewHTMLText`'s (`<`, `>`, `&` as `\u003c`…, `encoding/json`'s default). A byte of invalid UTF-8 is the lone surrogate `\udcXX` of the byte (Python's surrogateescape, PEP 383), which `Decode` reads back as the byte, so every Ergo string, which holds bytes, round-trips; a valid UTF-8 string is plain JSON. Read strings are copied out of the text. A member's key is compared in place and copied only when a decoded value keeps it (a `Map`'s key, an `Object` member's): `Reader.Key` gives a window of the text, a record's decode compares it with no allocation, and the derived `Map` and `Object` decodes copy the key they keep (runtime/v1 `WireKeyKept`), and a `Num` holds its own copy of its digits, so no value `Decode` gives keeps the text alive (JSON-CURSOR-ALLOC). A decode keeps its lists' and objects' frames in its `Cursor` up to 8 deep.
3. **Two phases.** `Open` checks the whole text: one value with white space around it, at most `MaxBytes` bytes (no limit when not positive) and `MaxDepth` levels of lists and objects (10000, `encoding/json/v2`'s, when not positive), else `Malformed` with the offset and the rule. Then `decode[T]` builds the value and fails only on its shape, with the member's path (`units[0].replicas`). Unknown keys are skipped and of two members with one key the last wins (`encoding/json`). `Decode` gives the two phases' result in one pass over the text (JSON-VALUE-DECODE): its `Cursor` checks the grammar of each token, separator and skipped value as it takes it, and after the value or a shape failure it settles: a text the reads took whole with no break is JSON, and any other text gets `Open`'s own answer, so a break is `Malformed` at `Open`'s offset with its reason and outranks the shape failure it caused (law `DecodeIsOpenThenDecode`; runtime/v1 `TestJSONOnePassAgreesWithOpen` holds it read for read on damaged texts). `Open` stays the two-phase reader. Prior art: Go's `encoding/json` `Unmarshal` scans the whole text first (`checkValid`), then decodes, the two-pass form this replaces; `encoding/json/v2`, Rust's serde_json and simdjson's on-demand API check as they decode; Elixir's Jason and Gleam's `gleam/json` build the tree in the one pass that checks it.
4. **Direct writer** (LR3). `Text` is a sealed operation type whose one provider is the Ergo record `text` (the comma state, the HTML flag and the buffer); `NewText` moves a `text` into a provider cell ([types.md](types.md#interface-assignability) 6). An `encode(v, t)` whose `t` is a `Text` is a cell writer: its codec functions are made for `text` (`func(env, w text, v T) text`), each event is a call of `text`'s function, and each straight run of events is rewritten by `Text`'s proved laws with unfold by definition ([types.md](types.md#laws) 14), so a record's opening brace and first key are one constant append, `{"host":`, and every later key with its comma another (`lower/wiretext.go`; serde's monomorphisation over the writer, Jason's pre-escaped keys). The rule reads checked facts and the std ledger, never json's name (`TestLoweringKeysOnNoStdName`); a codec witness carries the direct function, so `Encode`, generic over `T`, takes it. The Writer path stays the reference: `-X …/lower.wireLowering=writer` lowers every encode through the `Writer` interface, and an encode whose writer is typed `Writer` takes it in every build. `runtime/v1` keeps only the value appenders (`[]byte` in, `[]byte` out, pure), the pool of buffers `Encode` takes and gives back, and the reader. **Direct reader** (JVD-DIRECT). A `decode[T](r)` whose `r` is a `Reader` or a `Stream` (a record that provides the prelude's `Reader` by its own functions) reads through codec functions over that record's type, calling its functions for each token, not the `Reader` interface and its adapter; a decode generic over `T` takes it through its codec witness, so `Decode` and `DecodeStream` do. A decode from a value of the prelude's `Reader` type takes the interface path, the reference, and the lower probe test `JsonDirectReadAgreesWithReader` holds the two equal on valid and broken texts, whole and cut. `ParseNum`, which `Num`'s by-name pair calls for every number a decode reads, takes a text that is one number spelled exactly with no `Cursor` (law `ParseNumIsOpened`: it gives what reading the text by `Open` gives). Prior art: serde's derived `Deserialize` is monomorphised over the `Deserializer`, so serde_json reads with no dynamic call per token; Go's `encoding/json` decodes through one concrete `decodeState`.
5. **Laws.** `RoundTrips`, `FastAgreesWithWriter` (the direct path writes what the Writer path writes, in a `Text` and an HTML `Text`, inside events too), `HTMLEscaped`, `InvalidBytesEscaped`, `LineSeparatorsEscaped`, `TruncatedFails`, `DepthLimits` and `StringBytesIsString` (the Writer's `StringBytes`, GX10, writes what `String` writes for the string its bytes spell), `StringBytesReadsString` (the Reader's `StringBytes(into)`, GX12, adds to `into` the bytes `String` reads, escapes and all, with no allocation: runtime/v1 `TestCursorStringBytesAllocatesNothing`); lower `TestStdJSONLawsRun` runs them, with `JsonFastRefinedPair` for a refined field and a by-name pair, which std cannot declare, and `JsonTimePair` for `time.Time`, a pair of the append form (types.md, Codecs 1): the encode lends `time.Wire` a buffer on its stack and writes the string event from its bytes, on the direct path and the Writer path alike.
6. **Text's parts** (LR3a, LR3). `text`'s package-private functions are the parts of each event (owner q111): `sep` (the comma, when one is due), `raw` (text, state unchanged), `setComma`/`commaDue` (the one state), `plainBody` (a string that needs no escaping, between quotes) and a body per value (`stringBody`, `intBody`, …), each without a comma. An event is its body, which unfold by definition reads, so no law restates it. `Text`'s member laws are the facts a body does not give: the comma's text once the state is set (`SepAfterSet`, through `commaText`), the joins of text (`RawJoins`, `PlainIsRaw`, `SetCommaLast`) and the state's moves past a body (`SetCommaSinks`, `SetCommaSinksString`, …), and `SetCommaSets`, a post law. Each rewrite law is proved by a symbolic run (`internal/sketch/std/ergo.laws`, written by `go run ./scripts/stdlaws`), since `out` is a symbol and the appenders are uninterpreted; `SetCommaSets` is tested. Together they give the keyed form `{"a":1,"b":"x"}` as constant text and value bodies.
7. **Stream** (corpus-wave SJ1). `NewStream(src, maxBytes, maxDepth)` is a `Reader` over an `io.Source` ([io](#io) 10), written in Ergo with no Go body and nothing a user module could not write (STD-NOPRIV): a tokenizer that takes the next chunk only when a token runs past the window, keeps the part of a token cut by a chunk edge, and drops each chunk it has passed, so it holds one chunk and the token it is in. Its reads follow `Cursor`'s rules token by token: the path, `Tag`'s look-ahead for `"_tag"` anywhere among the members (the members it passes stay queued), a number taken even when it does not parse as the kind asked for. The derived `decode[T]` runs on it unchanged. **The weaker guarantee:** a Stream cannot check the whole text before the first read, since the text has not all arrived; it checks each byte as it passes, by `Open`'s rules, and after the first break reads on as if the value ended there (`Peek` is `WireEnd`, a read fails `WireWrongKind`). Code that acts on values it read from a Stream may have acted on a text that is not JSON. `Settle(r)` reads the rest and fails with the break `Open` reports for the text, at its offset with its reason: `MaxBytes` before every other rule (after another break it counts the rest of the text, up to `MaxBytes` bytes and no further), and `io.ReadFailed` when a read failed, since the text is then not all there. `DecodeStream` settles before it gives a value or a failure (a failed decode settles first, so a break explains the shape failure it caused), so its result is `Decode`'s for the same text; `Decode` stays the two-phase default. The laws, run by lower `TestJSONStreamAgrees` over texts cut every one to eight bytes: `StreamAgreesWithDecode` (spelled docs, with a piece spliced in, under a nesting limit; read as a `Value` and as a fleet), `StreamAgreesOnPieces` (texts made of pieces, mostly not JSON, under small byte and nesting limits), `StreamRoundTrips`, `StreamFindsTagAnywhere` and `StreamSkipKeepsPlace` (`Skip` passes one list element wherever the chunk edges fall in it, so a later shape failure's path names the element). Prior art: Go's `json.Decoder` (a buffered `io.Reader`, `Token`, no up-front validation: it reports a syntax error where it meets it), `encoding/json/v2`'s `jsontext.Decoder`, Jackson's streaming `JsonParser`, and Elixir's Jaxon and Gleam's `gleam/json` over a full string; none of them settles a typed decode against the whole text, which is what keeps `DecodeStream` equal to `Decode`.
8. **Elements** (corpus-wave SJ2). `NewElements[T](r)` reads the values of a Stream one at a time, each with `T`'s derived codec: the elements of the array a `NewStream` text holds, or each value of a `NewSequenceStream` text, a sequence of JSON values separated by white space (newline-delimited JSON is one; Go's `json.Decoder` reads the same; no value is an empty sequence; `maxBytes` limits the whole text, `maxDepth` each value). It is a pull value: `e.Next()` gives `Some` element, or `None` after the last once the rest of the text is settled, and fails with the text's break, a failed read, or the element's shape failure (settled first, as `DecodeStream`). A stream of values that can fail has no `Seq` form yet (a `Seq` has no failure row; questions.md Q11), so `Next` is the form any library can write today, and a failing `Seq`, when the language has one, is a loop over it. An element `Next` gives was read from text that keeps `Open`'s rules up to its end; a break after it is told by a later `Next`, so a consumer that acts on element 1 and then meets `Malformed` has acted on part of a text that is not JSON (json 7). Only one element is built at a time and the Stream keeps one window: `Window(r)`, the most bytes it held at once, is at most a chunk and the token cut by its edge, however many elements the text holds (law `ElementsHoldOneWindow`, the growth guard; the counted receipt is `examples/events`). A token is a slice of the window, not a copy: the window keeps a token cut by its edge whole, and a string value or a number's spelling a read gives the caller is copied once, so a value the caller keeps does not keep the window (`TestStreamValuesKeepNoWindow`, cmd/ergo, bounds the memory of 10,000 kept values each read from a chunk of its own); a `Map`'s keys are copied (json 2). Each Reader operation is one change of the state, never a `Get`, and the stacks and the token queue keep their room (SJ2s). No operation reads the source inside that change: a token that runs past the window starves, the operation gives its place back (the token's start, the queue's next token, the innermost frame), and the operation reads the next chunk in a change of its own and runs again. So each operation's function has an empty footprint and its change runs unlocked while the program is alone (runtime.md, REF-ALONE; lower `TestStreamOperationsRunAlone`); only the chunk reads lock (JSON-STREAM-STATE, 2026-11-18: `events stream` 7,344 to 6,945 instructions per event). `TestEventsStreamCounts` (cmd/ergo) bounds `events stream` at 1.6 times the hand Go Decoder's instructions per event and 2.1 allocations per event (2026-11-12: 1.47 and 2.07; the rest is the lowering's, corpus-wave ST/sj2-speed-gaps.md). Laws, run by `TestJSONStreamAgrees`: `ElementsEqualWholeDecode` and `ElementsAgreeOnPieces` (Next's elements and last answer are `Decode`'s reading of the text into a slice: the same elements, or the same failure at the end), `ElementsRoundTrip`, `ValuesRoundTrip` and `ValuesStopAtBreak` (a sequence cut short gives the values written whole before the cut, then `Malformed` inside the cut value). Prior art: Go's `json.Decoder.More`/`Decode` over an array's elements, Jackson's `MappingIterator`, Rust serde_json's `StreamDeserializer` (a sequence of values, an iterator of `Result`), Elixir's Jaxon streams.

9. **Loose reader** (wf10 JL; BAML findings §4 rows 1 and 5). `DecodeLoose[T](c, text) Read[T] fails Malformed | wired[T]` reads text that is nearly JSON, as a model writes it, and reports every repair: `Read[T]{Value T, Fixes []Fix}`, `Fix` the closed union `Surrounded | Fenced | TrailingComma | UnquotedKey | SingleQuoted | Comment | Closed | Chose`, each with the span of the original text it names, `Start` to `End` (`FixesNameTheirText`). The repairs are syntax only: the value found in prose (`Surrounded`) or a markdown fence (`Fenced`), a comma before a closing bracket or at a cut end (`TrailingComma`), a bare key (`UnquotedKey`), a string in single quotes (`SingleQuoted`), a `//` or `/* */` comment (`Comment`), and the lists, objects and string the text's end leaves open (`Closed{Depth, String}`). A text with several values reads the first that decodes as `T`, in text order, and `Chose{Index, Of}` names it. No value changes its kind (`"12"` stays a string, `True` is not a boolean, a bare value is not a string), no reading is scored, and a field that does not decode fails as `Decode`'s does (BAML findings §4 rows 2 to 4). Text is read by `Decode` first, in its one pass (wf10 L6): on JSON, `DecodeLoose` is `Decode` with no fix (`StrictTextsAgree`), and a text read with no fix is one `Decode` reads the same (`NoFixMeansStrict`). Only a text `Decode` finds `Malformed` is repaired, in one pass (`RepairIsOnePass`: the pass's count of the bytes it moved over is the text's length; a law over the private scanner, so its ledger row waits for laws and proofs, and the mutant `json-loose-rescan` is killed by `NoiseIsReported`, which counts the values `Chose` names, and refuted by the law at check time on a user copy) that splices the repairs into candidate texts (a value with no repair inside is a slice of the text, not a copy), and each candidate is read by the same `Open` and `decode[T]`, so there is one tokenizer and one decoder. `MaxBytes` bounds the text before any repair and `MaxDepth` the repaired value (`BoundsHoldWhenRepaired`); a text with no value that repairs to JSON fails `Open`'s `Malformed` for the text itself. *Decided (wf10 JL, orchestrator):* the unit's spec asked for a provider of the prelude's `Reader` that repairs as it reads; since every repair is syntax, repairing the text and reading it with `Open` gives the same values with no second tokenizer (one with `Tag`'s lookahead, `Skip` and `Path`). Lists and objects are found in prose, scalars are not (a scalar is read only when the whole text is one). Laws (`TestStdJSONLooseLawsRun`): `StrictTextsAgree`, `NoiseIsReported` (a record's text in prose, a fence, with a trailing comma, bare or single-quoted keys and a single-quoted value, comments, cut at its last brace, before or after another object, reads back with exactly the fix kinds of its noise, its strings holding fences, comments, quotes and braces untouched), `NoFixMeansStrict`, `RepairIsIdempotent`, `FixesNameTheirText`, `CutStringsClose` (a string cut anywhere, inside an escape too, closes to a prefix), `BoundsHoldWhenRepaired`. The same test runs 21 inputs of BAML's `jsonish` tests (`internal/sketch/lower/testdata/loose-baml-cases.json`), each `passing` (the value BAML reads), `different` or `unsupported` on purpose, with the reason. Receipt (`benchmarks/jsonloose`, instructions per read): on a strict text `DecodeLoose` costs 7,940 to `Decode`'s 7,907 (1.004x); a reply of a sentence and a fenced record costs 16,200, against 12,200 for hand Go that cuts the fence and calls `encoding/json` and 11,100 for the floor that finds the braces and calls it (1.33x the control). Path to the floor: the candidate is validated by `Open` after the repair pass read it, and the scan's marks, stack and fixes grow from empty; a repair pass that validates as it goes and hands `decode[T]` its cursor would read the value once. *Sources:* BAML's `jsonish` parser (`engine/baml-lib/jsonish/src/jsonish/parser/entry.rs`: strict `serde_json` first, then markdown blocks, then every object in the text, then the fixing state machine) and its `Flag`s, which Ergo returns to the caller as `Fix`es instead of scoring with them; JSON5 for the key and quote syntax. *Prior art:* Effect's `Schema.parseJson` is strict, as Go's `encoding/json` is; Python's `json_repair` and JavaScript's `jsonrepair` repair text and report nothing; Instructor (Python, Elixir's `instructor_ex`) validates strict JSON and asks the model again; Gleam and ReScript have no repairing reader.
10. **Raw** (wf10 JSON-RAW). `Raw` keeps the text of one JSON value without building it, as Go's `json.RawMessage` does: an MCP or JSON-RPC `id`, a tool's arguments. A decode on json's `Reader` reads it by its own read (types.md, Codecs 11): `Cursor.SkipText` passes the value once and gives the span, white space inside it as written and around it cut. An encode on json's `Text` writes it by its own write (Codecs 10) as it is, with U+2028 and U+2029 escaped and, in an HTML `Text`, `<`, `>` and `&` (they occur only inside strings), as `encoding/json` escapes a `RawMessage`. Every other format sees its `Value` (`Wire`, `ParseRaw`). **Ownership: borrow.** A `Raw` is a substring of the input, no copy; strings never change, so that is safe, and the cost is retention: a `Raw` keeps its whole input alive, so one that outlives a large input is copied (`strings.Clone(r.String())`). Go's `Unmarshal` copies a `RawMessage`'s bytes, and `Marshal` compacts it; `Raw` neither copies nor compacts (white space inside is kept). Laws `RawReadsOneValue` (Codecs 11's sentinel law), `RawRoundTrips` and `RawIsValue`.
*Prior art:* Go's `encoding/json` (reflection, a cached encoder per type, `checkValid` before decoding) and `encoding/json/v2` (`jsontext` tokens, depth 10000, no HTML escaping, duplicate names refused); serde_json's format-specialised `Serializer` the derive calls; Elixir's `Jason.Encoder` derive with pre-escaped keys; Gleam's `gleam/json` (encoders and decoders written by hand as values); ReScript's `JSON.stringify` and `rescript-schema`.

## jsonschema

Package `jsonschema` is the one JSON Schema dialect of std and of the generated packages (wf10 JS0): what `ergo gen schema`, `ergo gen api` (an operation's arguments) and `ergo gen mcp` write, what std `mcp` checks a tool's arguments against, and what std `llm` asks a model to answer in. It moved out of module `mcp` (`modules/mcp/schema.ergo`, AP3), which now calls it.

| Declaration | What |
| --- | --- |
| `Schema` | A JSON schema whose read keywords have their kinds and whose every `$ref` names a schema of the document. Its one field is unexported, so only `Of` and `Parse` make one. |
| `Of(v json.Value) Schema fails Invalid`, `Parse(text string) Schema fails Invalid` | The checked constructors. `Invalid{Path, Reason}`: `Path` is the JSON pointer of the wrong part. |
| `Value(s) json.Value`, `Text(s) string` | The document, as a value and as JSON text. |
| `Conforms(s Schema, v json.Value) bool` | Whether `v` is an instance of `s`. |
| `Encoded`, `Encode(s) Encoded`, `TextOf(e) string`, `SchemaOf(e) Schema`, `NewEncoded(keys []string) Encoded` | A schema with its JSON text, written once (wf10 L7): what `llm.ToolSpec` carries, so a provider splices the text into each request with no encode. Its fields are unexported, so only `Encode` makes one from a checked `Schema`; `NewEncoded` is an object schema of required string keys, the constructor a law's generator calls (a `Request` with tools). |

1. **Keywords** (JSON Schema 2020-12, its validation vocabulary). `Conforms` reads `type` (a name or a list of names), `properties`, `required`, `additionalProperties`, `items`, `prefixItems` (which also fixes the length, as the generator writes tuples), `minItems`, `maxItems`, `anyOf`, `const` (any value; numbers by their spelling), `$defs` and `$ref`. Every other keyword is ignored, as JSON Schema ignores an unknown one, so a schema that uses more is read as looser than it is. `Of` refuses a read keyword with a value of another kind (`KeywordsAreChecked`) and a `type` that names no JSON Schema type.
2. **Integers by value.** An integer is a number with no fraction, whatever its spelling: `2`, `2.0` and `0.2e1` are integers and `1.5` is not (`IntegerHasNoFraction`; 2020-12 §6.1.1). The check reads the spelling's digits and exponent, with no float, so a large integer is not rounded.
3. **References stay in the document.** A `$ref` is `#` or `#/$defs/NAME` (a JSON pointer token, `~0` and `~1` read back) and names a schema of the same document; any other `$ref`, a URL above all, is `Invalid` at its path (`RefsAreLocal`). So checking a value reads nothing but the schema: no network, no file, no capability in any row. A `$ref` may name the schema it is in, so a recursive type has a schema (`RefsRecurse`); a chain of references that never steps into a member or an item decides nothing, and the value does not fit.
4. **Generated schemas.** `ergo gen schema [--check] DIR.TYPE` ([api.md, ergo gen schema](api.md#ergo-gen-schema)) writes `DIR/<pkg>schema/<type>.ergo`: `TYPE() jsonschema.Schema`, parsed once, and the law `TYPEValuesFitTheSchema`, which holds that the derived codec's JSON of every value fits it. The mapper is the hub's (`internal/rpcgen`): a record's fields by their wire keys, required but a `json.Optional` field's; a union `anyOf` its variants, each with its `"_tag"` as a `const`; `json.Nullable[T]` `anyOf` T and null. `TestGenSchemaFits` (`internal/rpcgen`) checks the generated package in process, with the mutants `schema-optional-required` and `schema-union-untagged`.
5. **Encoded** (wf10 L7). `Encode` writes a schema's text once, and `TextOf` reads it back with no copy: a tool spec's schema reaches a vendor's request as the text spliced in, never re-encoded per request (`EncodedIsTheSchema`). No function takes text to an `Encoded` but through `Parse`, so a provider can trust it is a schema.
6. **Laws.** `EncodedIsTheSchema`, `NewEncodedRequiresItsKeys`, `RequiredKeyIsRequired`, `PropertiesAreChecked`, `IntegerHasNoFraction`, `AnyOfIsOneOfItsSchemas`, `ConstIsOneValue`, `PrefixItemsFixTheLength`, `ItemsAreEachChecked`, `RefsAreLocal`, `RefsRecurse`, `KeywordsAreChecked`, in [Std laws](#std-laws); `TestStdJSONSchemaLawsRun` restates them over the exported API.

*Sources:* JSON Schema 2020-12 (Core §8.2 `$defs` and `$ref`, Validation §6); module `mcp`'s AP3 subset; the MCP specification's tool `inputSchema` (an object schema) and the model APIs' structured output (OpenAI's strict `json_schema`, Anthropic's tool `input_schema`), which read the same subset; BAML's findings (ASK Done 3) for one dialect across `mcp`, `llm` and the Api. *Prior art:* Effect's `JSONSchema.make` writes a schema from a `Schema` and checks values by the `Schema`, not by the JSON Schema; zod's `toJSONSchema` and pydantic's `model_json_schema` write one from a type, as `ergo gen schema` does from the codec; ReScript's `rescript-schema` derives one from its schema values; Elixir's `ex_json_schema` validates against a resolved schema and follows remote `$ref`s through a configured resolver, which Ergo refuses so that a check has no capability; Gleam has no JSON Schema package in its standard library. *Decided (wf10 JS0):* `const` takes any value (the AP3 subset took a string only); an integer is decided by value (AP3 decided by spelling); `minItems` and `maxItems` are read because the hub writes them for arrays. Reverse by a narrower `Of`.

## xml

Std `xml` is an XML 1.0 codec for Ergo's derived codecs, in plain Ergo (`internal/sketch/std/xml`). It uses nothing a module could not use: its two `extern go append` lines bind Go's builtin, and it plugs into derived codecs through the open codec mechanism (types.md, Codecs 10), as any format's package may (docs/decisions.md, 2026-11-20: xml moved back into std; std has no privileges). It covers Go's `encoding/xml`.

| Name | Signature | What it does |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `Codec` | `Codec[T wire] { Root string, MaxBytes int, MaxDepth int }` | The root element's name and the limits of a read (0: none). |
| `Encode` | `Encode[T wire](c Codec[T], v T) string needs wired[T]` | Writes `v` as the element `c.Root`. |
| `Decode` | `Decode[T wire](c Codec[T], text string) T fails Malformed \| wired[T] needs wired[T]` | Checks the whole text first (`Check`), so no value is built from a text that is not well formed, then reads the value from the checked text. It fails `Malformed`, or with the derived decoder's failures on the value's shape, each with the value's path. |
| `Check` | `Check(text string, maxDepth int) string fails Malformed` | The root element's name of a well-formed document. |
| `Malformed` | `Malformed { Offset int, Line int, Column int, Reason string }`; `At(text, offset, reason) Malformed` | Where a document breaks and why. |
| `Attribute` | `Attribute { Value string }` | A field written as an attribute of its record's element. |
| `Parser`, `Token` | `NewParser(text, maxDepth)`, `Next`; `Token = StartElement \| EndElement \| CharData \| Comment \| ProcInst \| EndDocument` | The pull reader: one token at a time, keeping only the names of the elements open. |
| `Text`, `Cursor` | sealed operation types | `Text` is the prelude's `Writer` with private parts; its one provider is a cell that `encode` writes by direct calls. `Cursor` is the prelude's `Reader` sealed; its one provider is a cell that `decode[T]` borrows. |

1. **The form.** A value is the content of an element. A record's fields are child elements named by their keys, a list's values are `<item>` elements, a union value is its variant's record with `<_tag>Name</_tag>` first (an `Option` too), a null is `<null/>`, and a `Map` key that is not an XML name is `<_ key="…">`. A character XML cannot hold is written as U+FFFD. A document type declaration is refused, so no entity can be declared and expanded.
2. **Attributes.** A field of type `Attribute` is an attribute of its record's element (`type Product { Sku xml.Attribute, … }` writes `<item sku="…">`) when the record's element has no content before it; elsewhere it is the element's text. Every other format sees its `Value`: JSON writes `"sku": "…"`. `Attribute`'s write is `WireWrite`, the write of its own type that any format's package may declare (types.md, Codecs 10), so the record says which field is an attribute and nothing infers it. The reader gives an element's attributes back as its first keys, so an `Attribute` field reads an attribute or an element alike.
3. **Speed.** `Text` is a sealed operation type with one provider, so the build calls it directly and joins a record's constant tags by the law `Text.RawJoins`; its buffer is borrowed from std `strings` with `T`'s size hint, as std json's is.
4. **Laws.** `RoundTrips`, `OutputIsWellFormed`, `ControlCharacterIsRejectedWhereItIs`, `TruncationIsRejectedInsideTheText`, `AttributesRoundTrip` and `AttributesAreInTheStartTag`, over records, unions, options, lists, maps, tuples, every integer width and floats ([Std laws](#std-laws)); `Text.RawJoins` is proved (`ergo.laws`).

*Prior art:* Go's `encoding/xml` (struct tags, `xml:",attr"`); Rust's quick-xml with serde (`@attr` renames); here the record's field type says what is an attribute, and every format reads the same record.

## mcp

Package `mcp` is a server of the Model Context Protocol: JSON-RPC 2.0 messages, one per line, over a `Transport` (AP2; moved from `modules/mcp` into std in wf10 MCP0, so a program's module requires nothing for it). A program gives its tools as values and binds a transport in its `cmd/<name>/main.ergo`: `mcp/stdio` for a client that starts the program, `mcp/memory` in tests. `ergo gen mcp` ([api.md, ergo gen mcp](api.md#ergo-gen-mcp)) writes an operation type's tools over it.

| Declaration | What |
| --- | --- |
| `Transport` | The operation type of a session: `Messages() Seq[string]`, the client's messages in order, and `Send(message)`; its law `StopsWhenAsked`. |
| `Tool`, `Server` | A tool: its name, description, the JSON schema of its arguments (`Input`) and `Call(arguments) json.Value fails ToolError`. A server: its name, version and tools, in the order `tools/list` gives them. |
| `ToolError`, `UnknownTool` | A tool that ran and failed (the model reads its `Message`); a call of a tool the server does not have. |
| `Call`, `List`, `Handle`, `Line`, `Serve` | One call by name; the listing; one message to an `Answer` (`Answered \| Refused \| Silent`); the answer's line; a whole session over a transport. |
| `Conforms(schema, v)`, `Field(v, key)` | Whether `v` fits a tool's input schema, by std [`jsonschema`](#jsonschema); a member of a JSON object. |
| `mcp/memory`: `Transport`, `New(input)`, `Sent(t)` | A session in memory: the client's messages given at the start, what the server sent kept for the test. |
| `mcp/stdio`: `Transport`, `New() needs Ambient` | Standard input and output, one message per line; only `main` makes it. |

1. **The protocol.** The server answers `initialize` (the protocol version `2025-06-18`, the `tools` capability, the server's name and version), `ping`, `tools/list` and `tools/call`. A message with no `id` is a notification and gets no answer (`CallAnswersOnce`).
2. **Two kinds of failure.** A request the server cannot take is a JSON-RPC error: a message that is not JSON (`-32700`, id `null`), a message with no method (`-32600`), an unknown method (`-32601`), a call with no name, of an unknown tool (`UnknownToolIsATypedError`) or with arguments outside the tool's input schema (`-32602`; `ArgumentsOutsideTheSchemaAreRefused`: the tool never runs). A tool that ran and failed (`ToolError`) is a result whose `isError` is `true`, which the model reads. A tool's value goes back as one text block holding its JSON.
3. **Transports.** `Transport.StopsWhenAsked`: a break out of `Messages` ends the read. It runs for `memory.Transport`, and for `stdio.Transport` under stdio's named context `overText`: a Transport over `io.InOver` and `io.HostOver`, so no process's input or output is read or written (`TestStdMCPLawsRun` runs `ergo test` on std `mcp/stdio`).
4. **Laws.** `ListNamesEveryTool`, `UnknownToolIsATypedError`, `ArgumentsOutsideTheSchemaAreRefused`, `Transport.StopsWhenAsked` and memory's `CallAnswersOnce`, each beside its declaration; `TestStdMCPLawsRun` runs them with a client's whole session.

*Sources:* the MCP specification (2025-06-18: lifecycle, tools, the stdio transport); JSON-RPC 2.0.

## lint

Std `lint` (wf10 LINT4) writes a rule pack in Ergo: the program `ergo lint` starts for the rules of a namespace and talks to over the rule-pack process protocol v1 ([custom-lint.md](../specs/custom-lint.md)). It is the Ergo side of the Go SDK `ergo.local/prototype/lint` and reads and writes the same encoded form, fact schema `ergo.lint.facts` 2. Effect has no lint module. Its purpose here is that a team writes its own style guide in the language it lints, with rows and exhaustive `match` over the facts, where Effect's own rule sets (Effect-TS/tsgo, effect-oxlint) are TypeScript callbacks over syntax.

| Declaration | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `Snapshot`, `TypeDecl`, `Context`, `ContextEntry`, `Callable`, `Footprint`, `Call`, `With`, `Binding`, `Import`, `Span`, `Source` | The facts, as records. A list is empty where the Go SDK's JSON leaves the key out; an absent place is `Option[Span]`, a call of a function value has `Callee` `None`. |
| `Family`, `TypeKind`, `CallableKind`, `CallKind`, `ImportKind`, `Severity`, `OptionType` | Each kind is a union, where the Go SDK has string constants: a rule's `match` on one covers every kind or does not check. `ParseX` and `Wire` are each union's name pair, side by side, so each has its derived round-trip law `XRoundTrips` ([types.md, Codecs](types.md#codecs) 1). |
| `Rule { Name, Version, Description, DefaultSeverity, Requires, Options, Check }`, `Pack`, `Preset`, `RuleConfig`, `OptionSpec` | A pack. `Check func(s Snapshot, options json.Value) []Finding fails RuleFailed` gives the findings in the order it found them. |
| `Finding`, `Related`, `Suggestion`, `Edit` | What a rule reports; the runner gives each finding the rule's identity and its configured severity. |
| `OptionsOf[T wire](options json.Value) T fails RuleFailed` | A rule's options as the record `T` whose fields are the options, read by the derived codec. |
| `FunctionNamed`, `TypeNamed`, `CallableOf`, `TypeIdentity`, `Has` | The declaration lookups a configuration names (`"PKG.Name"`); they read declarations, never a use's spelling. |
| `DecodeSnapshot`, `EncodeSnapshot` | A snapshot from and to its text, in the Go SDK's form. |
| `ReadFrame`, `WriteFrame`, `Frame` | The frame `ERGO-LINT <version> <length>\n` and its payload. |
| `Serve(p Pack) fails ServeFailed \| io.ReadFailed needs io.Stdin \| io.Stdout`, `Answer(p, input) string fails ServeFailed`, `Refusal(version)`, `Reason(f)` | One request answered: `Answer` is `Serve` without the streams. |
| `Manifest(p Pack, executable string) string` | The manifest `ergo lint` reads without starting the pack: the document the Go SDK's `Pack.Manifest` writes. |
