It fails with the typed failure that ended the Set, at this `Wait` and at each later one (errgroup's sticky error), and raises the defects that no `Wait` or `Stop` raised yet, with that failure beside them (every reason survives, G2 4). It is an interruption point of the owner: a stop raises the interruption, and the frame's end drains the Set. Under `LogFailures`, `Wait` never fails, though its row names `E`.
15. **Stop.** `Stop` closes admission, stops every member at once and waits until each has ended, its exit actions included (`StopEndsEveryMember`). It raises the members' defects and drops their typed failures (laws R3, R6). The frame's end does the same and joins the defects to the frame's exit.
16. **Closed.** `Go` on a Set that admits no more tasks (after `FailFast` ended it, after `Stop`) fails with `tasks.Closed` and starts nothing, as `queue.Push` fails `queue.Closed`: a normal end of the protocol, not a stop of the caller.
17. **Virtual time.** `Wait` and `Stop` park the owner where the VirtualClock sees it ([Testing](#testing) 2), and a member's end wakes it in the same step, so a Set of sleeping members runs in virtual time.

`TestSetModel` (runtime/v1) runs 400 generated cases under each policy on a VirtualClock against a discrete-event model: the result of each `Go`, `Len`, `Wait` and `Stop`, the members' runs, the logged failures, the frame's exit and its end instant; a stopped member's exit action sleeps and may panic, so a defect after the Set ended is kept. `TestSetNoRestartAfterClose` (runtime/v1) opens, with an unexported test hook (`closeGap`), the window between `Stop` closing admission and stopping a member, makes the member defect in it, and wants no second run under `Restart`. `TestSetRuns` (lower) builds and runs a program that uses each policy and returns a Set. *Not taken from Effect or Go, and why:* `FiberSet.make` returns an `Effect` in a `Scope` (W4: a `Scope` service in the context) where Ergo's frame owns the Set by the Task rule; `FiberSet.run` and `FiberSet.runtime` are two ways to add a fiber because a fiber is a value Effect must fork (W14), where `Go` takes the body; `FiberMap` and `FiberHandle` (keyed sets) are not built; errgroup's `WithContext` threads a cancel context by hand, which Ergo's task tree does by construction; errgroup's `SetLimit` is `tasks.Transform`'s limit; Effect's `Supervisor` is an observer of fibers, which `ergo ps` (G3) is to be. *Prior art:* Elixir's `Task.Supervisor` (`start_child` per task, `restart: :temporary` by default) and `DynamicSupervisor` (`max_restarts` within `max_seconds`); Gleam OTP's `static_supervisor` with `one_for_one` and an intensity; Kotlin's `supervisorScope` (a child's failure does not cancel its siblings, LogFailures) beside `coroutineScope` (FailFast); Trio's nursery. On the BEAM, a supervisor is a process that links its children, so a crash reaches it as a message; Ergo's Set is a frame-owned object and a member's end reaches it as a call in the member's own task.

Restart policy (`models/supervise/group.ergo`):

| Declaration | Meaning | Go body |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `type Strategy = OneForOne \| AllForOne \| RestForOne`, `func Restarted(s Strategy, failed int, j int) bool` | Which members of a group a crash restarts, members numbered in start order. | none |
| `type Intensity { Max int, Period duration }`, `func Allows(i Intensity, past []duration, now duration) bool`, `func Recent(i Intensity, past []duration, now duration) []duration` | A restart budget over time and its window. | none |
| `type Supervision { Strategy Strategy, Intensity Intensity, Backoff schedule.Schedule }`, `type Verdict = RestartAfter \| GiveUp`, `func Decide(s Supervision, past []duration, now duration) Verdict` | How a group answers a crash. | none |
| `law StrategiesNest(failed int, j int)`, `law WindowForgets(gaps []int, k int, p int)`, `law RecentKeepsTheVerdict(gaps []int, k int, p int, later int)`, `law DecideWaitsTheBackoff(gaps []int, k int, p int)` | The laws ([Std laws](#std-laws)). | none: they run on their own source (`go run ./scripts/ergotests -killer law:NAME internal/sketch/std/tasks`) |

18. **Strategies.** `OneForOne` restarts the member that crashed; `AllForOne` every member; `RestForOne` the crashed member and every member started after it (a later member may depend on an earlier one, never the reverse). So each restarts the crashed member, and what `OneForOne` restarts `RestForOne` restarts, and what `RestForOne` restarts `AllForOne` restarts (`StrategiesNest`). A group restart stops the running members the strategy names other than the crashed one, last started first, then starts each named member that has not finished, in start order, as a new incarnation; an exit of an incarnation a restart already replaced is dropped. The model's machines `GroupOneForOne`, `GroupAllForOne` and `GroupRestForOne` prove this order, the window and the drop in their scope, and its mutant machines (`WindowCountsForever`, `AllForOneSkipsSibling`, `RestForOneRestartsEarlier`, `StaleExitRestarts`, `StartsReversed`) are each refuted by a shortest run.
19. **Window.** An instant is a duration since the group began. A restart at `now` is allowed while fewer than `Max` restarts lie in the Period that ends at `now` (after `now - Period`, at or before `now`): OTP's `MaxR` in `MaxT`, so a burst gives up and a slow trickle never does (`WindowForgets`). `Recent` keeps only the instants in that window, which is all `Allows` reads now and later (`RecentKeepsTheVerdict`), so a supervisor that runs for days holds at most `Max` instants. A `Max` of 0 allows no restart.
20. **Decide.** `GiveUp` when the window allows no more; else the `Backoff` schedule's decision for as many runs as the restarts in the window plus one, at the time since the first of them: `RestartAfter{Wait}` its delay, or `GiveUp` when the schedule stops. So the backoff grows with the restarts in the window and starts again from its first delay after a quiet Period (`DecideWaitsTheBackoff`). `Decide` is pure: the caller keeps the instants and the clock.

*Prior art:* Erlang/OTP's supervisor (`one_for_one`, `one_for_all`, `rest_for_one`; `intensity` and `period`, a sliding window of restart times; children stopped in the reverse of start order, started in order; a `transient` child that ended normally is not restarted); Elixir's `Supervisor` with `:max_restarts` and `:max_seconds`; Gleam OTP's `static_supervisor` (`OneForOne`, `OneForAll`, `RestForOne`, an intensity and a period); Akka's `BackoffSupervisor` and Effect's `Schedule.exponential` for the delay. OTP restarts at once and has no backoff; Ergo's window counts restarts as OTP's does, and the backoff is a `schedule.Schedule` the caller chooses.

## Actors

The actor library, the Actor projection. Std `actor` runs a machine live ([runtime.md, Machines and actors](runtime.md#machines-and-actors) 11). Effect module: none in the pin; the behaviour follows effect-machine's `Machine.spawn` and its actor ref. Go convention: a goroutine that owns the state, with a channel and a `switch`. Its laws are in [Std laws](#std-laws) (runner `TestStdActorLawsRun`).

1. `actor.StartMachine(m, capacity, sink)` starts the actor and returns at once. Its row is the step's `needs R` and `Scheduler`, so a test binds a `time.Manual` and controls the timers; the row holds the work's `R` too. Each entry's `machines.Work` runs as a child of the actor's task (runtime.md, Machines and actors 11 rule 11 and 12).
2. `a.Send(e) fails Stopped` queues `e`. `a.Ask(e) Decision[S, O] fails Stopped | F` waits for the step's decision or failure. The asker matches the `Decision` exhaustively, a `Reject` included.
3. `a.State() S` never fails. `a.Stop() fails F` returns the failure that ended the actor, if one did. `a.Dropped() int` counts the stale timer and work signals. `actor.Changes(a, room) Seq[Change[S]]` gives every committed state as `Changed{State}`, holding `room` states at most: a commit that finds it full drops the unread states, and the loop reads `Overflowed{Missed}` before that commit's state, a typed gap an exhaustive `match` handles, never an unbounded buffer (F-GP4c-1; owner, 2026-11-15). `a.LatestChanges() Seq[S]` is the loop for a reader that wants the current state, not the history: a reader slower than the actor gets the latest state and skips the ones between, so it holds one state (Effect's `SubscriptionRef.changes` through a sliding buffer of one). `WaitFor` and `SendAndWait` read `Changes(a, 256)` and judge the states after an `Overflowed`. `a.Version() int` counts the states it committed after its first, so a reader that read `Version` and then `State` knows a later commit by a larger one (FK2-g2). `a.Held() int` counts the states its loops hold unread, the counter a growth guard reads; the property `TestActorObserveAgreesWithFold` holds a lagging `LatestChanges` loop to its start and the final state, and to one held state.
4. Observing and ending (MU12; runtime.md, Machines and actors 13). `a.Drain() fails F` closes admission, steps every queued signal and ends; it returns as `Stop` does. `actor.WaitFor(a, pred) S fails Stopped` and `actor.SendAndWait(a, e, pred) S fails Stopped` wait for the first state `pred` accepts, from the state at the call (for `SendAndWait`, the state before `e`). `actor.ExitOf(a) Exit[S, O, F]` waits for the end: `Finished{State, Output} | Halted{State} | Failed{Error} | Defected{Reason}`, where `Failed` holds the step's typed failure that ended it (q47). `actor.Latest(a) Option[D]` is the last step's decision. `actor.Select(changes, f) Seq[T]` (`T comparable`) and `actor.SelectBy(changes, f, same)` give `f` of each state with consecutive equal values dropped. They are std functions, not `Actor` members: the Go body never names the program's `Option`, `Decision` or `Exit` (one rule, the opaque runtime), and `WaitFor`, `SendAndWait` and `Select` are loops over `Changes`. Laws: `ExitIsShared`, `SelectOncePerChange`, `SelectStops`, `SelectByStops` ([Std laws](#std-laws)).
5. Children (MU5; runtime.md, Machines and actors 14). `actor.Child(send, m, capacity, toParent)`, called in a state's work with that work's `send`, starts `m` as an actor owned by the work's entry and returns it. Its `Emit` and `Done` outputs come back through `toParent` as signals of that entry; one that arrives after the entry ended is dropped. The entry's children stop, the last started first, before its timer and work stop and before the next entry opens, and when the parent ends; they are kept by identity, so two children of one machine are two children. A child's defect ends the parent. Its `send` is an `entry func(e P)` (types.md, Function values 6): the send the runner gave the work's `Start`, or one passed on to a parameter written `entry`; any other value is S108. Effect intent: effect-machine's `self.spawn` and `ActorHost` give a state scoped child actors; Ergo takes the scope as the entry (no registry id, no generation check by hand, no host object), and the host's laws are properties of `TestActorChildrenAgreeWithTree`.
6. Sources and `Can` (MU10; runtime.md, Machines and actors 15). `actor.Feed(a, source) fails Stopped` gives `a` the values of a `Seq[E]` as signals, in order, on a task `a` owns: the source ends when `a` ends, and when `Stop`, `Drain` or `ExitOf` returns no source runs. `machines.Can(step, s, e)` is whether the pure step takes `e` in `s` (not `Ignore`, not `Reject`); `actor.Can(a, step, e)` is that in the actor's state, and `false` once the actor has ended, with no step run. Effect intent: effect-machine's `.background` and `can`; Ergo takes a `Seq` the runner owns instead of a handler with the actor's `self`, and a pure step by type instead of effectful guards. Laws: `FeedStepsInOrder`, `CanIsTheStep` ([Std laws](#std-laws)).
7. Hosts (mu-host; runtime.md, Machines and actors 16). `actor.NewHost[A]()`, made in a step, is an empty `actor.Host[A]` that the entry the step enters owns; that entry's work fills it once with `actor.ChildIn(send, host, m, capacity, toParent)`, and `host.Get() A fails Stopped` is the child, waits while the host is empty, and fails `Stopped` once the entry ended. The entry is published after its work filled its hosts, waited or ended. Effect intent: effect-machine's `ActorHost` names a lazily started child of a state scope; Ergo drops its identity string, its acquire-that-starts and its hand-written generation check (the entry's end closes the host). `Host` has no member law: its answers depend on an entry, which only the runner opens (`TestActorHostAgreesWithModel` holds them against the model `models/host`).
8. Durable actors (MU9; runtime.md, Machines and actors 17). `actor.StartDurable(m, capacity, sink, recover, save)` starts from the state `recover` gives and runs `save(prev, next, sig)` for each commit before anyone sees it; a failure of `save` is a defect. Std `journal` gives the contract `Journal[S, E]` and `Codec[T]`, with laws, and `journal.Start(m, j, every, capacity, sink)`, which replays the journal with `m`'s pure step and keeps each commit; `journal/memory` and `journal/file` are its providers. The checker refuses a durable machine whose step is not pure (S108), except that the step may make hosts with `actor.NewHost` (DH1): a host the recover's replay makes is unbound until the recovered actor's first entry binds it, or closes it when the first state does not hold it. Laws: `AppendAddsOne`, `SnapshotDropsSignals`, `RoundTrips`, `RecoveryIsTheFold`, `DroppedIsNotJournaled` ([Std laws](#std-laws)).
9. Supervised actors (MU7; runtime.md, Machines and actors 18). `actor.Supervise(m, capacity, sink, policy tasks.Restart)` restarts the actor after a defect while fewer than `policy.Limit` restarts were made, by the restart rule of a `tasks.Set` (Tasks 13), each time in `m`'s initial state; `actor.SuperviseDurable(m, capacity, sink, policy, recover, save)` restarts in the state `recover` gives. A crash answers the `Ask` in the step and the queued ones `Stopped` and drops queued `Send`s; later ones go to the new incarnation. `Done`, `Stop`, `Drain` and a typed failure end it for good; the defect after the budget ends it (`Defected`). `actor.Restarts(a)` counts the restarts. Laws `RestartIsFresh`, `RestartRecovers`. A crash runs the open entry's exit action (item 9a) before the restart. *Effect intent:* effect-machine's `Supervision.restart`, OTP's restart intensity and Akka's `onFailure(restart)` keep an actor alive across a bug from a known state within a budget; Ergo takes that, and not a supervisor tree of its own, or a mailbox kept across the restart (Akka). The budget is a lifetime `Limit`; it does not use the window or backoff of `tasks.Decide`.
9a. Exit actions (FK9-g1; runtime.md, Machines and actors 12a). A `Machine`'s `Exit` gives each state's exit action, `Some(func() needs W)` or `None`. Each entry the actor opened (the first state, an `Enter`, an `Emit`, a restart) runs its state's action once when it ends, with the state it holds then (after its last `Stay`): at the next `Enter` or `Emit`, and at `Done`, a failure, a defect, `Stop`, `Drain`, a crash and the end of a child's owner entry. It runs on the actor's task, masked, after the entry's children, timer and work ended and before the next entry opens; `Stop` and `Drain` return after it, and its defect is the actor's. `machines.Start` and the durable starts keep it; a fold runs none. The Go body gets it as two functions, whether a state has an action and the action's run, so a machine with none pays one call per entry end. Effect intent: effect-machine has no exit action, and a finalizer of a state's task reads the state the task captured; Ergo takes XState's `exit` with the state at the transition, and runs it at `Stop` too, as `scope.OnExit` runs on every path. Laws: `ExitSeesLastState`, `ExitRunsOncePerEntry` ([Std laws](#std-laws)).
9b. Watch, end actions and registry (BEAM-RUNTIME SUPERVISE-2; Watch and Registry are plain Ergo over `ExitOf`, `OnEnd` and `Ended`). `actor.Watch(watcher, a, toSignal) fails Stopped` sends `watcher` the signal `toSignal(ExitOf(a))` once `a` has ended, and also when `a` had ended before the call: a monitor whose report is a typed signal in the watcher's inbox. The wait runs on a task the watcher owns, as a `Feed` source does, so it ends with the watcher; it fails `Stopped` when the watcher has ended or its stop was requested. `actor.Registry[K, S, E, D, F]` (`K comparable`) names actors of one type by a typed key; a program makes one with `actor.NewRegistry` and passes it (no ambient registry, no string names spelled twice). `actor.Register(r, k, a) fails Taken | Stopped` names `a` by `k`, failing `Taken` while another actor holds `k` and `Stopped` when `a` has ended; `actor.Lookup(r, k)` is the actor or `None`. `actor.OnEnd(a, f) fails Stopped` runs `f` once when `a` ends, masked, on `a`'s runner, after its sources stopped and before `Stop`, `Drain` or `ExitOf` of `a` returns, in the order the calls came; it fails `Stopped` once `a`'s end has taken its actions, and `f` then never runs. An actor leaves the registry when it ends: its entry is removed by an end action, numbered by its `Register` so it never removes a later entry, and a registry runs no task of its own. `Lookup` never waits: an entry whose actor has `Ended` is dropped there, and `Register` looks its key up first, so once `ExitOf(a)` has returned, `Lookup` of its key is `None` and the key is free, and `a`'s own step may look itself up. `actor.Ended(a) bool` is whether `a` has ended: true exactly when `ExitOf(a)` would not wait, and once true it stays true; it never waits, so a step may ask it of its own actor (an actor's end as a value, without `ExitOf`'s wait). Laws: `WatchSeesTheEnd`, `RegistryDropsTheEnded`, `LookupFromOwnStep`, `EndedIsMonotone`, `EndedAgreesWithExit`, `OnEndRunsOnce`. *Prior art:* Erlang's `monitor` and its `'DOWN'` message (sent at once for a dead process); Erlang's `register` and Elixir's `Registry`, which unregister a process when it exits; Gleam OTP names a process by a typed `Subject` and has no global registry; Akka Typed's `watch` with `Terminated`/`watchWith`, which `toSignal` follows (the report is a signal of the watcher's own type); Akka Typed's `PostStop` and a GenServer's `terminate/2`, which `OnEnd` follows from outside the actor, with no trap of exits needed to run it.
10. Not implemented yet: `actor.Start` with a handler, `actor.Ask` with an `actor.Reply`, `actor.Address`, `actor.StartAgent` and the resource rules (runtime.md, Machines and actors 6 and 7).

*Prior art:* Erlang `gen_statem` run by its process; Gleam OTP `actor` with a typed `Subject`; Akka Typed timers; XState `createActor`.

## Sync and STM

Std sync and STM.

1. `sync.Ref`, `Latch`, `Semaphore` and `Deferred`. A blocking wait is an interruption point. All four are built: `Ref` has a `runtime/v1` body; `Semaphore`, `Latch` and `Deferred` are Ergo over std `queue` and `Ref`, with no body of their own (STORES SB1), so a module could write them the same way.
   - **Semaphore.** `NewSemaphore(n)` holds `n` permits (`n < 1` is a defect); `s.With() { … }` runs its block while it holds one permit and gives the block's value or failure. Every copy of a `Semaphore` is the same semaphore. The permits are a `queue.Queue` of capacity `n`: a take is a `Push` (it waits while the queue is full, and the waiting takers are served in the order they began), and the release is a `Poll`, registered as an exit action before the take, so the permit goes back on every exit of the block: a value, a failure, a defect or a stop; a stop while it waits takes no permit. `s.Available()` and `s.Size()` read it. Laws: `NeverMoreThanN` (four tasks of fifty blocks each never have more than `n` blocks inside), `ReleaseOnFailure`, `ReleaseOnInterrupt`. Go control: a buffered channel of capacity `n` (`sem <- struct{}{}`, `defer func() { <-sem }()`), the shape the body has.
   - **Latch.** A one-shot gate: `NewLatch()` is closed; `l.Await()` waits until it is open; `l.Open()` opens it for every waiter, now and later, and is true only for the call that opened it; `l.IsOpen()`. `Await` is a `Pop` on a queue no one pushes to, and `Open` ends that queue (Go's `close(ch)`). Laws: `LatchOpensOnce`, `OpenReleasesWaiters`.
   - **Deferred.** `NewDeferred[T]()` is empty; `d.Complete(v)` stores `v` when none is stored and is true then, false otherwise; `d.Wait()` waits for the value; `d.Peek()` is it or `None`. A `Ref[Option[T]]` and a `Latch`. Laws: `DeferredSetsOnce`, `WaitSeesTheValue`.
   - *Not taken from Effect* (Effect 4 `Semaphore.ts`, `Latch.ts`, `Deferred.ts`): `make` returning an `Effect` (W14) and the `makeUnsafe` twin, which exist because a JavaScript value cannot run an effect at construction; `withPermits(n)` as a curried wrapper of an `Effect` value (W2, W3) — `With` takes the block; weighted takes (`take(n)`, `withPermits(n)`, `resize`, `releaseAll`) — no user needs them yet; they can be added over the same queue with a second queue of capacity 1 as a gate, so two takers of several permits never each hold part of what they need (`x/sync/semaphore`'s first-in-first-out rule); `withPermitsIfAvailable`, which needs a take that does not wait, which `queue` has not; a `Latch` that closes again (`close`, `release` without opening) — a gate that reopens is a `Ref` of a `Latch`, and one-shot is the shape `close(ch)` gives Go; `Deferred`'s `Exit` (failure and interruption as completions) — a row has no value form, so a producer that hands over a failure puts it in `T` as a case of a union, and a waiter's stop is its own task's, not the `Deferred`'s.
   - **Ref.** `type Ref[T] { Get() T, Set(v T), Update(f func(v T) T) }`, `func NewRef[T](v T) Ref[T]` and `func Modify[T, R](r Ref[T], f func(v T) (R, T)) R`. A `Ref` is one cell: every copy of it, in a field, an argument, a capture or a task, is the same cell, so it is a synchronised handle that tasks share ([runtime.md, Tasks](runtime.md#tasks) 6). Each operation is atomic: no other operation of the `Ref` runs between `Update`'s or `Modify`'s read and its store, so Updates from several tasks at once are never lost (`UpdateIsAtomic`). `Update` and `Modify` call their function exactly once, never retried (`Ref.UpdateCallsOnce`); the function has an empty row, so it needs no capability while the `Ref` is held (a wait on a handle it captured is not refused yet: follow-up REF-WAIT). `Modify` returns the first element of its function's result and stores the second, as Effect's `Ref.modify` does. No operation is an interruption point: none waits beyond the length of one operation.
   - **A Ref owns its value** (V6; owner q46, 2026-11-09). No binding outside the `Ref` sees a later write to its value, and no value the `Ref` holds is one a binding outside sees written ([types.md, Bindings](types.md#bindings)). A function literal written at the call of `Update` or `Modify` takes the held value over: its parameter is an unshared local, so its body writes the value in place (`u[k] = v`, `ws.Append(x)`, an owned parameter) and a change costs what it changes, not a copy of the value. The `Ref` keeps a flag: its value may be reached from outside (`NewRef`, `Set` and `Get` set it, and so does an `Update` or `Modify` whose function is not such a literal, since that function may keep its argument). Before it hands a value so marked to a literal, the `Ref` copies it, under its lock, once; the lowering proves each literal's results unshared (else the stored value is marked too). So a value `Get` returned never changes, and two tasks never write one value. `fstest.Memory` and `io.Recorder` change their `Ref` only through such literals: a file write costs O(its path), an append O(its path), and a `Print` O(1) amortized (`lower.BenchmarkRefOwn`).
   - **Poison.** A defect in the function of an `Update` or `Modify` poisons the `Ref`: the defect goes on to the caller as it is, with its own stack, and every later `Get`, `Set`, `Update` and `Modify` of that `Ref` raises a defect that names the poisoning ("sync.Ref is poisoned: …"). The `Ref` does not recover the first defect to raise it again: only a task's boundary records a defect (runtime.md, defect ownership). The function may have written part of the value in place, so no task, and no later test, may see that value (owner q46: this replaces "a defect leaves the `Ref` usable, with the value it held before"). Prior art: Rust's `std::sync::Mutex`, which a panic in a holder of its guard poisons, and whose later `lock` returns a `PoisonError`, not the first panic. Ergo differs: v1 has no `into_inner` or `clear_poison` escape, since a value half written has no meaning a program could use; a test that wants a `Ref` after a defect makes a new one.
   - **A stop is not a defect** (REF-MASK, 2026-11-16). The function of an `Update` or `Modify` runs masked on the caller's task: a stop asked while it runs (by another task, or by the function itself) is observed at the caller's next interruption point after the operation, so the `Ref` holds the function's whole result and is not poisoned. Only a defect poisons. Prior art: Effect's `Ref.update` and `Ref.modify` are atomic and never interrupted part way, since their function is pure. The mask costs about 30 instructions per operation (`runtime/v1.BenchmarkModifyOwned`).
   - **Go body.** `*v1.Ref[T]`, a value guarded by a `sync.Mutex`, with the methods `Get() T`, `Set(v T)` and `Update(t *rt.Task, f func(v T) T)`; `NewRef(v T) *Ref[T]` and `RefModify(t *rt.Task, r *Ref[T], f func(v T) struct{ F0 R; F1 T }) R`, whose function returns the Go form of the tuple `(R, T)`; `RefUpdateOwned(t, r, f, clone, fresh)` and `RefModifyOwned(t, r, f, clone, fresh)`, where `t` is the caller's task, which the function runs masked on, the calls of an owned literal, with the copy function of `T` and the lowering's proof that the results are unshared (sync.go). `-ldflags=-X=ergo.local/prototype/internal/sketch/lower.valueLowering=copy` lowers every call to the plain body, the reference `TestValueRuleAgrees` holds the owned calls to. Its control is a hand-written Go value guarded by a `sync.Mutex`; `atomic.Pointer` is a second control for reads (`runtime/v1.BenchmarkRef`).
   - *Workarounds not copied.* Effect's `Ref.make` returns an `Effect<Ref<A>>` and each operation is an `Effect` value (W14) to `yield*` in `Effect.gen` (W1); `Ref.update(ref, f)` and `ref.pipe(Ref.update(f))` are one dual API (W2, W3); `SynchronizedRef` exists because `Ref.update` cannot run an effect. Ergo's `Ref` is a value whose operations are plain calls, and its function's empty row is the checked form of "pure update".
2. `sync.TxRef[T]`, `sync.Atomically() { … }` and `sync.Retry()`. `Load` and `Store` need the `sync.Tx` capability, which only `Atomically` provides.
3. A transaction can re-run, so its body is a replayable parameter ([types.md](types.md#function-values)): its footprint holds only `sync.Tx`, and I/O inside a transaction is an error.
4. `Retry` rolls back, registers the read set and waits, interruptibly, until a `TxRef` it read changes. No lock-freedom or starvation-freedom is claimed.

*Prior art:* Haskell STM, ZIO STM, Effect `TxRef`; Go offers mutexes only. For `Ref`: Effect's `Ref` (an atomic reference with a pure update) and `SynchronizedRef`; Go's `sync.Mutex` and `sync/atomic`; Rust's `Mutex<T>`, which lends `&mut T` to the guard's holder and is poisoned by its panic; Swift's copy on write, which tests uniqueness by a reference count where the `Ref` keeps a flag set by a static proof; Haskell's `IORef` and `atomicModifyIORef`; Elixir's `Agent`, whose update runs in the agent's process; Gleam and ReScript have no shared cell in their std (Gleam's state lives in an actor process, ReScript's in a JavaScript `ref`, which a single thread makes safe).

## Atomic counters and flags

1. `atomic.NewInt64(value)` and `atomic.NewBool(value)` give sealed shared handles. Every copy names the same cell. `Load`, `Store`, `Swap` and `CompareAndSwap` follow Go's `sync/atomic`; `Int64.Add(delta)` returns the new value, with Go int64 wraparound. A matching compare-and-swap succeeds even if the new value equals the old. A mismatching one does not store. There is no writer authority, scheduler requirement, task wait or unsafe implementation.
2. Each operation is sequentially consistent: it has one point in the shared total order, respecting real-time order. A `Load` followed by `Store` is two operations, not a transaction. Use `sync.Ref` for an arbitrary value and a compound update. The source laws cover initial values, stores, exchanges and increments through `tasks.Set`. `runtime/v1.TestAtomicLinearizableHistory` checks a seeded reference history and real task-set increments: unique returned tickets, the final count and every nonoverlapping invocation/return pair; concurrent flag claims have one winner. A pure machine explorer cannot enumerate Go instruction interleavings, so this check uses the actual native boundary.
3. Effect `Ref.ts` solves shared mutable state. Ergo does not copy `Ref.modify`'s result/state tuple, dual-call functions or `pipe`: Go-named scalar operations, ordinary statements and laws state the contract. The compiler maps handles to pointers over Go's typed atomics, never copies their native structs, and keeps the ordinary hidden-task operation ABI for source protocol assignment. The hand-Go controls are `sync/atomic.Int64` and `sync/atomic.Bool`. Benchmarks wait for the quiet-host window; no speed result is claimed.

## Pacing

Std `pace` (RT-SPIKE PACE1) is the one runtime lever a program sees: when Go's collector runs. Its intent is that of Go's `GOGC`, `GOMEMLIMIT` and `runtime.GC`, set by the program that knows its workload. Effect has no counterpart: a JavaScript host owns its collector.

1. **The contract.** `pace.Pacing { Edge() }`. A loop calls `pace.Pacing.Edge()` where one unit of its work ends (a frame, a turn, a batch), so the call is in its row (`needs pace.Pacing`). Std ui's `Run` calls it after each event it handles, as a user's loop does: no rule names `pace` or `ui` (STD HAS NO PRIVILEGES).
2. **The providers.** `pace.Default` (an edge does nothing; Go's pace with the runtime's heap floor, `rt.Pace`); `pace.NewPercent(n)` (Go's `GOGC=n`, set once by `debug.SetGCPercent`; an edge does nothing); `pace.NewAtEdges(limit)` (`debug.SetGCPercent(-1)` and `debug.SetMemoryLimit(limit)`; an edge runs `runtime.GC()` once the heap has grown by `limit/4` since the last collection); `pace.NewCounting()`, the test provider, which counts edges (`c.Edges()`). `NewPercent` and `NewAtEdges` call Go, so the site that builds one is charged `go.Foreign` (README Gap 17): only `main` chooses them, and a test binds `Default` or `Counting`. Every provider is plain Ergo over `import go runtime` and `import go runtime/debug`, with no `runtime/v1` body; on an unpatched Go toolchain each is the same program.
3. **AtEdges reads the heap now and then.** Go's heap size costs a stop of the world to read (`runtime.ReadMemStats`, about 17,000 instructions at `GOMAXPROCS=4`, measured; `runtime/metrics.Read` fills a slice in place, which the value rule for slices at the Go boundary does not allow). So an edge is an atomic count down, and a read sets the next one by `pace.NextRead(grown, edges, room)`: half the room left at the growth rate seen, from 1 to `pace.MaxSpan` (256) edges. Only the edge that ends the count reads, so edges on several tasks never read at once.
4. **Laws.** `Pacing.EdgeKeepsValues` (what a program built before an edge reads the same after it), `DefaultReachesNothing` (a law, which holds no capability, binds `Default`), `CountingCountsEachEdge`, `NextReadLeavesRoom`; `ui.RunPacesEachEvent` holds ui's loop to one edge per event. Go's collector never changes a value, so no provider changes a program's output: cmd/ergo `TestDashGuiAgreesWithControl` plays each seeded script under all three providers (`dash-gui play -pace default|percent|edges`) against the reference model.
5. **Held stretches** (PACE2). `Pacing` also has `Hold()` and `Release()`, and code calls `pace.Held() { … }`, which pairs them and releases however the block ends (`scope.OnExit`). Inside a held stretch no edge collects: an edge that is due owes its collection instead, and the release that closes the last open hold runs it, once. The count of open holds is shared by every copy of the provider, so holds on several tasks, or nested, compose: only the last release collects. Go's memory limit still collects inside a hold, as it does inside a unit of work; `Percent` and `Default` hold nothing, since their edges never collect. `pace.Holds` is the count and the debt (`Open`, `Close`, `Owe`, `Depth`) that `AtEdges` and `Counting` keep and a user's provider may keep too; `Counting` counts the edges marked inside a hold (`HeldEdges`) and the releases that ran an owed collection (`Deferred`). A collection that a closing release runs may overlap a hold another task opens at that moment. The uses: `ui.Run` holds from a pointer's Down to its Up (a drag, the host's capture), and releases as it returns when the host closes mid-drag; `dash-gui play -hold N` holds each `run` of at most N frames as one request. Laws: `Pacing.HoldKeepsValues`; `HoldsOweTheLastClose` (a run of opens, closes and due edges against a model: a count and a flag); `HeldDefersToTheLastRelease` (edges in nested `Held` blocks are held, one collection is deferred to the outer end, and a failing block releases too); `ui.RunHoldsADrag`; dash-gui `PlayPacesEachFrame` counts held edges and deferred collections; cmd/ergo `dashPacesTheCollector` reads Go's trace of a 4,000-frame run held whole: exactly one forced cycle, the release's. Prior art: .NET's `GC.TryStartNoGCRegion` and `EndNoGCRegion` (one region per process, not nested, and it fails when the budget is exceeded, where a hold leaves the memory limit in charge); Unity's `GarbageCollector.GCMode = Disabled` around a level load; Erlang has no need (a process's heap is its own).
6. **What it is not.** The collector's mode is process-wide in Go, so `Percent` and `AtEdges` are one choice, in `main`. `Edge` is an ordinary contract, so a part of the program may bind its own `Pacing` for its subtree (`with context { pace.Pacing: pace.Default } { … }` around latency-sensitive code makes its edges do nothing). A server with many requests in flight has no quiet edge (levers.md §5), so std http's `Serve` does not call `Edge`.
7. **Receipt** (PACE1, measured 2026-11-17 at load 56 to 87, user instructions per frame, the slope from 2,000 to 12,000 frames of `dash-gui play -seed 3`, `GOMAXPROCS=1`, median of 5, spread under 1.3%): `-pace default` 134,844 (base 134,841); `-pace edges` 131,800; `-pace percent` (400) 139,095; `GOGC=off`, the collector floor, 124,541. So the collector costs 10,300 instructions per frame by default and 7,260 at edges (0.70), and `GOGC=400` costs more than the runtime's own heap floor (1.42). The hand Go control (`cmd/ergo/testdata/dash-gui/control`) is 43,874 with or without `GOGC=off`: it allocates almost nothing per frame. dash-gui's frame is 3.0 times the control at edges; the gap is the queues and the allocations, not the collector (a symbol profile at edges: `selectgo`, locks and `closechan` lead; allocation 13%, marking 6%). Peak RSS: 59 MB default, 76 MB edges, 81 MB percent; the control 3.7 MB. dash-gui keeps 16 MB live in its rings, so a collection at an edge still marks them; the frame model of levers.md (8.4 MB of boxes per frame, 1 MB live) is where the edge pays most (0.44).
8. **Receipt of holds** (PACE2, measured 2026-11-17 at load 70 to 90, same counts and conditions, `dash-gui play -seed 3`). A script of requests, 250 against 1,500 lines of `run 8`: `-pace default` 138,140; `-pace edges` 132,205; `-pace edges -hold 8` (each request held) 132,786; `GOGC=off` 125,557; the hand Go control, the Go-runtime floor, 43,923. So a held request costs 581 instructions per frame over plain edges (0.44%: a `Hold` and `Release` per 8 frames and the collection moved to the request's end; 16 forced cycles against 17), and the collector part is 7,229 against the default's 12,583 (0.57). One run held whole (`run 12000` against `run 2000`, `-hold 12000`): 135,502, between edges (131,814) and default (137,527), since inside a long hold only Go's memory limit collects, inside units of work: a hold is for a request or a gesture, not for playback. Edges with holds are 3.02 times the floor; the gap is PACE1's (queues and allocation), not the collector.
9. **A request loop and the memory bound** (GC-EDGE). A server loop that serves one request at a time has a quiet edge after each request, as a frame loop has: `examples/kvstore`'s session marks `pace.Pacing.Edge()` after it answers each line, and its `main` chooses `-pace default|percent|edges` (`pace.NewAtEdges(256 MiB)`), as dash-gui's does. A server with many requests in flight has no quiet edge (6); it keeps `Percent` or the memory limit. Two promises are checked:
   - *No unit skipped, none split.* A loop marks exactly one edge for each unit of work: dash-gui's law `PlayPacesEachFrame` counts frames, kvstore's test `SessionPacesEachRequest` (memory) counts one edge for each line, whether the line answers one line, several, none or a refusal. Edges never change output: kvstore's case `paced` answers the `session` case's lines the same under `edges`.
   - *The memory bound holds.* Under `AtEdges(limit)` no collection starts with the heap past the limit, inside a unit of work and inside a held stretch too, where no edge collects: Go's memory limit (`debug.SetMemoryLimit`) collects first. cmd/ergo `dashPacesTheCollector` reads Go's trace of 4,000 frames held whole (about 360 MB allocated, no edge collecting) and checks every cycle's starting heap against dash-gui's limit, with a sixteenth for the bytes allocated while a cycle starts (mutant `pace-atedges-sets-no-limit`).
   The hand Go control of kvstore (`cmd/ergo/testdata/kvstore/control`) takes the same `-pace` arms through the same public calls (`debug.SetGCPercent`, `debug.SetMemoryLimit`, `runtime.GC` at an edge by `NextRead`'s rule), so a receipt compares Ergo and Go at one pacing; its floor is `GOGC=off`. Receipt: pending (the batch run; GC-EDGE's request names the command).
10. **Composed providers** (PROV-COMPOSE). A part of the program tweaks the pacing in force rather than only replace it, with no new syntax: a wrapper is a generic record over the provider it refines, and the binder that holds the outer provider (`main`, by the layout rule) binds the wrapper for a subtree with an inner `with`. `pace.NewEvery(outer, n)` passes one edge in `n` to `outer` (a loop of small units paces every `n`th; its count is shared by every copy, so tasks count together); `pace.NewQuiet(outer)` passes no edge and every hold and release (a stretch that must not collect at its own edges inside a part that does); `pace.NewRaised(outer, percent)` runs each held stretch of `outer` with Go's percent at least `percent`. Go has one GC percent for the process, so raises on several tasks combine by `pace.Raises`: while any raise is open the percent in force is the largest open one, and only the release that closes the last one puts back the percent `NewRaised` found (a count and a combine, as `Holds` is for holds). A wrapper changes when the collector runs, never a value: the operation type's laws run under the named contexts of `pace/tests.ergo`, wrappers stacked up to three deep over `Default` and `Counting`, and `TestStdLawsRun` asserts them under two such stacks.
   - *Laws.* `EveryOneIsParent` (identity: every edge, hold and release reaches the parent, as if called directly), `EveryPassesOneInN`, `EveryNests` (`Every(a, Every(b, p))` observes as `Every(a*b, p)`, edge for edge), `QuietForwardsHolds`, `RaisesFollowModel` (a run of raises and lowers against a multiset of open percents).
   - *The convention for a lever's package.* Its contract (an operation type with laws, the first being that the lever changes cost and never values), a reference provider, a counting provider, and wrappers as generic records whose constructor takes the outer provider first. A lever consulted at an edge (a frame, a request, a batch) is a capability in the context, as pacing is; one consulted for each item (a hash, a compare, a byte scan) is a type-argument provider (`Map[K, V, H]`), which is pure, has no poll and is static (PROVIDER-LEVERS §0.3).
   - *A subtree that main does not call directly.* The subtree's package declares an operation type that includes the lever's (`type Pacing { pace.Pacing }`), needs it at its entry and rebinds `pace.Pacing` from it inside; `main` binds both, so the slot shows in the package's row.
   - *Not yet.* A leaf that refines the provider in force without holding it (a wrapper over `InForce[pace.Pacing]()`, Effect's `FiberRef.locallyWith`) waits for IR-INFORCE, which lowers `InForce` of a concrete row in a plain function. `rt.Pace`, the runtime's heap floor that no `main` chooses, becomes a provider in PROV-FLOOR. A wrapper is a static call where the provider is a type argument; through the context it is the capability call, which CTX-COPIES makes static.

## Arenas

1. `arena.Of[T]` is a growable store of values of one type, and `arena.Ref[T]` names one value in it. `New[T]()` is an empty store. `a.Put(v)` stores `v` and returns its Ref. `a.Get(r)` is the value, `a.Set(r, v)` replaces it, `a.Update(r, func(v *T) { … })` changes it in place through a borrow, and `a.Free(r)` removes it and returns it. `Has(a, r)` reports whether `r` names a value, `Len(a)` counts the values and `Cap(a)` counts the slots, in use or free. `Null[T]()` is the Ref that never names a value, the "no link" of a linked structure. Refs compare with `==`. A store is a value, as a slice is: change it through a `let mut` root or a borrow.
2. **Use after free is detected, by generations (decided).** A Ref holds its slot's index and the slot's generation. `Free` advances the slot's generation and puts the slot on the free list; `Put` reuses the slot freed last, with the new generation. So a Ref kept after `Free` is stale: `Get`, `Set`, `Update` and `Free` fail with `arena.Stale` on it, also after `Put` reuses its slot, and `Has` is false. `Stale` is in the error row, so a caller must `catch` it or pass it on; a caller whose Refs are live by an invariant of its own writes `Unreachable` in that `catch`, as `examples/lru` does. Only `arena` builds a Ref (its fields are unexported, [types.md, Zero values](types.md#zero-values-and-definite-initialisation) 6), so every Ref is one that `Put` returned, or `Null`. *Why not a check-time guarantee:* an ownership rule could make a Ref a resource that `Free` takes, but a resource is not copied, and a graph or a linked list holds several Refs to one value, which is the point of the store; and tying a Ref to one store by the type system needs a type per store (a brand or a lifetime), which the checker does not have. Generations give a typed failure where a check-time rule cannot apply. A generation is a Go `int`, advanced by one per `Free` of the slot: it does not wrap before 2^63 frees of one slot.
3. **Limits and storage.** A Ref is meaningful only for the store that returned it: a Ref of another store of the same `T` can name a live value there, and that is not detected (slotmap and generational-arena have the same limit). `Free` clears the removed value through a private host storage cell (`arena.stored[T]`, bound to `v1.ArenaValue[T]`). The cell has no public accessor: `Get` checks the generation before reading, and `Put` initializes the cell before publishing a Ref. Occupancy lives in the generation's parity, as slotmap's version does: `Put` makes a slot's generation odd, `Free` makes it even, and every Ref carries an odd generation (`Null`'s is 0). So one compare of generations tells a live slot, with no extra field, and a Ref from another store never reads cleared free storage, since no Ref's generation equals a free slot's (`FreeSlotNamesNothing`). This is physical host zero storage, not an Ergo zero value of `T`; no `zero[T]()` can bypass construction authority or definite initialization. `TestStdArenaReleasesStorage` inspects the actual freed storage of a pointerful value and exercises the wrong-store/free-slot case without a timed GC or finalizer probe. `Get` takes a borrow, as the writes do: under the value rule ([types.md, Bindings](types.md#bindings) 2) a store passed by value from a `let mut` root is copied whole when `T` may hold a slice or a Map, which would make a read cost the store.
4. **Lowering.** `Of[T]` is a Go struct of a `[]slot[T]`, the head of the free list and the count of values; a slot holds the value as `T` itself, its generation and the next free slot; `Ref[T]` is a struct of two `int`s. No value passes through an interface (inspected in the lowered Go of `examples/lru`; no test pins the shape, since no one-edit mutant boxes a value and keeps the laws green). Every operation is an Ergo body in `std/arena`: a bounds and generation check and a slice access. `Update` with a function literal writes one field as Go writes it, with no copy of `T`. The operations check through a private `live(slots, r)` on the slice they then index, so Go proves that index in range; `Put` and `Free` write the slot through one borrow (`reuse`, `vacate`), so each indexes the store once. Counted in the lowered Go of `examples/lru` (measured, S-arena round 3): no allocation per operation on the hit, update and miss paths (`testing.AllocsPerRun`); one bounds check left in the arena's hot code (`Put`'s index of the free slot), where the hand Go control has four in its arena and cache (`-d=ssa/check_bce`); `Get` and `Update` with a literal inline into the cache's generic code. Go's inliner refuses `Put` and `Free` in a generic shape body (cost 98 and 128 over its budget of 80), so Ergo inlines them at the cache's call sites (OPT4a, OPT5): the churn loop of `examples/lru` counts 825 user-space instructions per operation against 835 for the hand generic Go control (median of 9 interleaved rounds, measured after the OPT5 merge; noise about ±10, so parity, not a lead). The hand-written Go control is a generational arena in Go (slotmap's shape), whose node access checks the generation and returns a pointer into the slot; the command that compares it, the pointer list and `examples/lru` at its base is in `wf10/s-arena/bench-pending.md`. No speed result is claimed.
5. **Laws.** `GetAfterPut`, `FreeThenStale`, `ReuseNeverAliases` (a Put after a Free reuses the slot without growing `Cap`, and its Ref differs from every earlier Ref), `FreeSlotNamesNothing` (a Ref of another store never names a free slot) and `NullIsStale`; `ModelAgrees` is the seeded property: up to 63 operations drawn from a seed, over every Ref `Put` ever returned, agree step by step with a `Map` from Ref to value, each failing with `Stale` exactly when the model has no value. `TestStdArenaLawsRun` (lower) restates and runs them.

*Prior art:* Rust's `slotmap` and `generational-arena` crates (a slot vector, a free list, a key of index and version; `slotmap`'s `Key::null()` is `Null`); `thunderdome` and the entity ids of ECS libraries (Bevy's `Entity` is an index and a generation); Go has no generational arena in its std, and Go programs use pointers or bare indexes into a slice, which a stale index reads silently; Zig's `std.heap.MemoryPool` and Odin's handle maps; Gleam, Elixir and ReScript have no arena in their std (their values are immutable, and the BEAM's references and ETS tables serve identity). Effect has no arena: its `HashMap` keyed by a minted id is the nearest intent, and it detects no use after removal.

## Text spans

1. `text.Span` is two `int`s, `Start` and `End`: the bytes of a text from `Start` up to `End`, `End` excluded. It holds no pointer, so a record or a slice that holds spans in place of strings is never scanned by Go's collector, and a store of a span has no write barrier. A span holds no text: the text is held once, by the value that owns the spans, and every read takes the text and the span together.
2. **Reads.** `In(s, sp)` is `0 <= Start <= End <= len(s)`. `Whole(s)` is the span of all of `s`, `Width(sp)` its byte count. `Cut(s, sp)` is the piece, Go's `s[i:j]`: it shares the bytes of `s` and allocates nothing; a span that is not In `s` is a defect there, as an index out of range is. `Get(s, sp)` is the checked read, `None` outside. `Sub(s, sp, from, to)` is a span inside `sp`, offsets relative to `sp.Start`, or `None`. `Equal(s, sp, lit)` compares the piece in place. `Find(s, sp, sub)` is the span of the first `sub` inside `sp`, or `None`. `Fields(s)` is the spans of the runs between ASCII white space.
3. **Spans.** `text.Spans` is a text and spans of it: one string for n pieces. Its fields are unexported, so `Collect(src, at)`, which gives `None` when a span of `at` is not In `src`, is the only way to make one; every `Spans` holds only spans of its own text, and `At(d, i)` reads with no second range check of the span. `Source`, `Count` and `SpanAt` read it.
4. **Why a span cannot dangle.** A span holds no pointer, so it keeps nothing alive and it cannot point at freed memory (the shape of `arena.Ref`; [types.md, Borrows](types.md#borrows) 3). A string is an immutable value, so the text lives as long as a holder; a `Cut` result shares it and Go's collector keeps it alive through that string. A span used with another text fails `In`, or, where it is in range of both, gives the wrong piece: a wrong value, never a wrong address. `Spans` closes that for stored spans.
5. **No privilege (STD-NOPRIV).** `text` is Ergo over std `strings`: no `extern` of its own and no compiler rule keyed on its name. `TestStdCopyAgrees` checks its sources as a user package of another module: no diagnostic, the same exported names and laws. Its speed comes from general units: NOSCAN1's pointer-first layout reads declarations, not names.
6. **Not built yet.** The fact forms (`gives In(s, it)`, `given In(s, sp)`, the `where` of `Spans`) wait for facts ([facts.md](facts.md)); until then `Get`, `Sub` and `Find` are checked and give `Option`. The window over a stream (`Window`, `Stale`, `Fill`) waits for the stream byte type (SPAN1b). A packed 8-byte span is added only if a receipt counts a gain.
7. **Laws.** `CutOfWholeIsTheText`, `GetIsCut`, `SubThenCutIsCutThenSlice`, `EqualAgreesWithCut`, `FindIsTheFirstInside`, `FieldsAreGoFields` (the pieces of `Fields` are Go's `strings.Fields`), `CollectRefusesPastTheEnd` (a span one byte past its text is refused, whatever other text would hold it) and `CollectChecksEverySpan`; `ModelAgrees` is the seeded property: up to 63 operations over the words of a text (cut, sub-span, find, store in a `Spans` and read back) agree step by step with a model that keeps each word as its own string.

*Prior art:* Zig's compiler (a token is a tag and a start offset; the text is read from the source when needed); Go's `go/token.Pos` (one integer into a file set); rustc's `Span`; tree-sitter's byte ranges; simdjson's tape of offsets. Erlang, Elixir and Gleam sub-binaries reference the parent binary and keep it alive, a known leak that `binary:copy` fixes; Java removed substring sharing (7u6) for the same leak; JavaScript engines (ReScript's host) slice strings that keep the parent alive, unseen by the program. A span makes what stays alive visible in the types, with no lifetime parameter.

## Text width

Std `text` also says how many columns of a terminal a text takes, and wraps, cuts, pads and tabulates by it (wf10 SQ2). It is Ergo over std `strings`, with no `extern` of its own, like the spans.

1. **Cells.** `Cells(s)` is the columns `s` takes, as Bun's `Bun.stringWidth` counts them (Bun 1.4.4). A rune takes 0, 1 or 2 by the recorded tables of `width_table.ergo` (East Asian Wide and Fullwidth and emoji presentation take 2; combining marks, format characters and controls 0). A few grapheme rules join runes: a ZWJ after a pictograph makes it 2 and joins the next pictograph at no cost (a family emoji is 2), and the sequence then takes in spacing marks; U+FE0F after a narrow emoji makes it 2; a skin tone after an emoji that takes it adds 0; two regional indicators are one flag of 2; U+20E3 after a rune makes it 2. An escape sequence takes 0: CSI (`ESC [` … a final byte), OSC (`ESC ]` … BEL or `ESC \\`), `ESC` and one rune, and the C1 forms U+009B and U+009D. The name is the unit: `Width` stays a span's byte count.
2. **Pad, Truncate, Wrap, Columns.** `Pad(s, w)` is `s` with spaces to `w` columns. `Truncate(s, w, tail)` is `s` when it fits, else the longest prefix that leaves room for `tail`, then `tail` (`tail` alone when it is wider than `w`). `Wrap(s, w)` is the lines of `s`: its own lines filled with whole words joined by one space; a word wider than `w` is cut where it fills a line, and a cluster wider than `w` takes a line of its own. A cut falls between clusters, never inside one (a flag, a keycap or a ZWJ sequence stays whole). `Columns(rows, sep)` pads each cell but a row's last to the widest of its column and joins a row's cells with `sep`.
3. **The reference.** The property `WidthIsTheTable` (eval `TestTextCellsIsBun`) holds `Cells` to `Bun.stringWidth` on 5318 recorded rows: generated texts of whole clusters of every kind above (ASCII words, CJK, combining marks, emoji, skin tones, ZWJ sequences, flags, U+FE0F, keycaps, controls, SGR, CSI and OSC 8 escapes) and each end of every table range alone. `testdata/textwidth/record.ts` records the tables and the rows once; no Bun runs at test time. The tables are Bun's, probed one code point at a time, so a later Bun with a newer Unicode is a new recording.
4. **Laws.** `CellsAddOverClusters` (the cells of two texts are the sum when the first ends a cluster), `StylingTakesNoCells` (an SGR or OSC 8 escape and its reset add none: the text side of ansi's `Cells(Paint(s, t)) == Cells(t)`), `PadIsWidth`, `TruncateFits` (a cut text fits and ends on a cluster), `WrapFits` (every line takes at most `w` for any `w` of 2 or more), `WrapLosesNoRune` (only spaces and newlines go) and `ColumnsAlign` (every line of a table takes the same cells up to its last cell). They run in process (stdlib `TestStdTextWidthLaws`), over texts made of the clusters `clusterPieces` lists, with Go's `strings` functions as the evaluator's natives (ORACLE O20).
5. **Speed.** `Cells` allocates nothing: printable ASCII is one compare a byte, any other rune a binary search of the tables (at most 10 steps). The floor is go-runewidth's two-stage lookup and an ASCII scan 8 bytes at a time; the receipt is the batch run's.

*Prior art:* Bun's `stringWidth` and `wrapAnsi` (the reference); Node's `string-width` and `wrap-ansi`; Go's `go-runewidth` and `x/text/width` (East Asian Width only, no grapheme rules) and `text/tabwriter` (columns by runes); Rust's `unicode-width`; Python's `wcwidth`. Gleam, Elixir and ReScript have none in their std: Elixir's `String.length` counts graphemes, not columns. Effect has none.

## Dense stores

Std `dense` (wf10 DN1, DN2) holds rows in slices and addresses them by four-byte ids. The facts that remove the bounds compare are DN3's.

1. **Ids.** `dense.ID[K]` is the id of a row of a store of `K`: a record of one `uint32`, so four bytes and no pointer. Only `dense` makes one (its field is unexported): every `ID[K]` came from a `Put` or a `Next` of some store of `K`, so an id is never forged and never of another row type. Ids compare with `==`, in the order the rows were put; `Index(id)` is the row's position. `dense.Opt[K]` is an id or none in the same four bytes, holding the id's number plus one with none 0 (the largest `uint32` is one past the last id, so the sum fits), so memory Go zeroes holds none and a fill with none is a clear: `OptOf(id)`, `NoID[K]()`, `Or(o, id)` and `Unpack(o)` (an `Option[ID[K]]` for a `match`). A row's link to another row (a first child, a next sibling) is an `Opt`, where `Option[ID[K]]` would take eight bytes. Signed ids cost 5 to 11% more than unsigned in the DOD0 benchmarks and unsigned cost nothing (the DOD0 receipt, decision 1).
2. **Stores.** Rows are never removed, except that a `Counted` set frees the id of a value no holder holds; for rows that are removed while others keep handles, use [arenas](#arenas).
   - `Table[T]` appends rows: `New[T]()`, `t.Put(v)` returns the new row's id, `At(t, id)` and `t.Set(id, v)` read and replace a row, `Get(t, id)` is `None` for an id past the end (an id decoded from outside), `Has(t, id)` and `Len(t)`. `Each(t)` gives each row with its id and `IDs(t)` each id, in put order, each a `Seq` that stops when its yield returns false; `Rows(t)` is the rows as a slice, for `for i, row in dense.Rows(t)`, with `IDOf(t, i)` the id of position `i`.
   - `Count[K]` and `Col[K, V]` are a table by hand, one column per field (struct of arrays): `c.Next()` makes the next id, and the owner `Push`es one value on each column per `Next`, so the columns share the count's ids. `Of(col, id)`, `col.SetOf(id, v)`, `In(col, id)`, `Total(c)`, and `IDAt(c, i)`, the id of position `i`. Rows whose fields all live in columns take ids from a `Count`: a `Table` of a type with no fields still keeps a slice, and Go grows a slice of zero-size elements on every append.
   - `Side[K, V]` holds data that few rows have, with a default: `NewSide[K, V](zero)`, `s.SideSet(id, v)`, `SideOf(s, id)` (the default for an id never set, past the end too). It is where Go keeps a map keyed by a pointer; it is as long as the largest id set.
   - `Pool[E]` holds many short lists in one slice: `p.PutList(xs)` returns a `Run[E]`, `List(p, r)` is its items (a window, no copy), `RunLen(r)` and `Items(p)`. A list is put whole and never changes. `PutList` is one bulk append (`slices.AppendAll`), and an empty list does no work.
   - `Stack[T]` gathers lists (SL2): `NewStack[T]()`, `s.StackPush(v)`, `Depth(s)`, `Mark(s)` (the place of the next push), `Since(s, m)` (the rows pushed after mark `m`, a window), `s.Cut(m)` (back to depth `m`; the rows below are unchanged) and `PutSince(&pool, &s, m)` (the rows since `m` into the pool as one list, then the cut). It keeps its top in an integer, so a cut keeps its room and a push after it allocates nothing until the stack is deeper than it ever was. `Push` and `Len` are the column's and the table's names in the package, so the stack's are `StackPush` and `Depth`.
    - A capacity at creation: `Reserve(n)` checks a table reservation without allocating, returns `Room`, and fails `Capacity` when `n` is negative or greater than the number of IDs (law `ReserveRejectsOutOfRange`). `WithCap[T](room)` allocates an empty table with that room. `ColWithCap[K, V](n)`, `PoolWithCap[E](n)` and `StackWithCap[T](n)` size the other empty stores; a negative `n` is a defect for those constructors.
   - `Interner[T comparable]` gives each distinct value one id: `NewInterner[T]()`, `s.Intern(v)` (the id an equal value was given before, or a new one), `Value(s, id)`, `Lookup(s, v)` (`None` for a value never interned) and `Interned(s)`. Ids compare where values would.
   - `Strings` interns text, its ids `ID[Str]`: `NewStrings()`, `s.InternText(text)`, `Text(s, id)`, `FindText(s, text)` and `Texts(s)`. Settled texts lie in one string, addressed by two `uint32` offsets per id, and `Text` is a part of that string, not a copy; texts interned since the last settling wait in a short list that is joined onto the string when it holds as many bytes. The lookup is a `[]uint32` of ids, open addressed by a 32-bit FNV-1a hash. So the collector scans the one string and the short list, not a pointer per text as in a Go `map[string]uint32`.
   - `Counted[T comparable]` is a set of values in use, each with an id and a count of holders (Ghostty's RefCountedSet): `NewCounted[T]()`, `c.Add(v)` (the value's id, a freed id, or a new one, with no holder yet), `c.Use(id)`, `c.Release(id)` (at 0 the value leaves the set and its id is free for the next new value; a Release with no holder is a defect), `Member(c, id)`, `Uses(c, id)`, `Live(c)` and `Slots(c)`.
   - `Sparse[K]` is a set of ids with constant-time `Insert`, `Contains`, `Remove` and `Clear`, whose members are a dense list, `Members(s)` (Briggs and Torczon; Go's `ssa.sparseSet`): `NewSparse[K]()` and `Size(s)`. A `Remove` moves the last member into the removed one's place.
3. **Limits.** An id is meaningful only for the store that made it. An id of another store of the same row type that is in range reads that store's row, never memory outside it; out of range, `At`, `Of`, `Set` and `SetOf` are a defect, as an index past the end of a slice is, and `Get`, `Has`, `In` and `SideOf` answer. A store holds at most 4294967295 rows; one more is a defect. A table's room never passes one row per id (`WithCap` rejects a larger reservation, and `Put` cuts a larger growth back), so `Put` checks for a full store only when it grows the table (SL2b: the check on every `Put` cost 1.13 instr/B on toktree with table ids, source path). The package is Ergo source with no Go body and no `import go` (STD-NOPRIV), so a copy of it in a user module checks and behaves the same (`TestDenseCopyAgrees`).
4. **Lowering.** `Table[T]` is a Go struct of one `[]T`, an id a struct of one `uint32`. `Put`, `At` and `Rows` lower to an `append`, a slice index and a field read, with no call but conversions, `len`, `cap` and the prelude's `Append`, and in `Put` one `WithCapacity` that a table of rows of no size (a type with no fields) runs once (`TestDenseLowersFlat`, the one promised fact). Go grows a slice of rows of no size on every append, as its capacity stays its length; `Put` sees that growth, which no other row type has, and takes room for every id at once, at no memory (SL2b). The growth guard reads the room of such a table after 4096 Puts: room for every id, taken once at its third Put, so no later Put calls Go's growslice (`TestDenseFlatTableRegrowsOnce`). `Put` appends in amortized constant space: a growth guard counts the bytes it allocates at n and 2n rows (`TestDensePutGrowsLinearly`). An `Each` loop calls its yield per row unless the loop is fused; that cost is not measured yet. `TestDenseStringsCounts` counts the bytes and allocations of interning n words twice each in `Strings` and in a hand-Go `map[string]uint32`, and holds `Strings`' bytes at 2n to at most three times n; it pins no bound against the map. Measured 2026-11-12 (DN2): at 4096 words `Strings` 823544 bytes in 8516 allocations, the map 550488 bytes in 16230; at 8192, 1592264 bytes in 16735 against 1120664 in 32655 (the words' own strings are in both counts). A chained `x.Append(a).Append(b)` copies `x` (the lowering clips the outer receiver), so `Strings` appends its offsets one statement each; the guard found it.
5. **Laws.** `PutThenAt`, `PutKeepsOthers`, `IDsAreDense` (Each, IDs, Rows and IDOf agree on put order and ids 0 to Len-1), `TableEachStops`, `TableIDsStops`, `GetAgreesWithHas` (on ids past the end too), `SetThenAt`, `OptRoundTrips`, `NextThenOf`, `SideDefaults`, `ListRoundTrips` and `ListsDoNotOverlap`; `FlatRowsKeepIDs`, `IDAtIsNext`, `EmptyRunsKeepTheirPlace`, `CutRestores`, `PutSinceIsPutList` and `WithCapIsNew` (SL2, beside `Stack` in dense.ergo); `InternTwiceSameID`, `DistinctValuesDistinctIDs` and `ValueOfIntern` (each over an `Interner[int]` and a `Strings`), and the model-based `ReleaseThenReuse` (a seeded run of `Add`, `Use` and `Release` against a `Map` of each live value's id and count: a live value keeps its id, a new value never takes a live id, an id is reused only after its count fell to 0) and `SparseAgreesWithSet` (a seeded run of `Insert`, `Remove` and `Clear` against a `Map`); all run by `TestStdDenseLawsRun`. `TestDenseAgreesWithModel` is the seeded property: generated command sequences over a `Table`, a `Count` with a `Col`, a `Side`, a `Pool` and a `Stack` (every other seed made with a capacity) print one line per command, which must equal a model of plain Go slices and a map; a failing seed prints its Ergo source, both outputs and the replay command (`ERGO_DENSE_SEED=<n>`). It builds Go, because `Put` and the other writes borrow their store and the check-time evaluator does not run a borrow yet. Each operation has a planted mutant in the std mutant ledger (`dense-*`).

*Prior art:* Zig's `MultiArrayList` (struct of arrays) and the index arenas of Zig's, rustc's (`IndexVec`, `newtype_index!` with a niche for none) and Go's own compilers (`cmd/compile`'s `ssa.ID`); Rust's `la-arena` and `id-arena` crates (an id typed by its arena's element, no generation). Go programs use `[]T` with `int32` indexes by hand. Gleam, Elixir and ReScript have no such module: their values are immutable, and a dense id is a plain integer into a tuple or an ETS table. Effect has none; its `HashMap` keyed by a minted id is the nearest intent.

## UI layout

`ui/layout` is the layout kernel of the ui hosts (H2L; v1-gui plan §2.4): every painted host lays out with it, so a view has one layout on every host. It is Ergo source over `[N]T` and `arena`, with no host call.

1. **The tree.** `New()` is an empty `Tree`; `Add(&t, style)` makes a node and returns its `Id`, `Append(&t, parent, child)` makes `child` the last child of `parent` (taking it from the parent it had), `Remove(&t, id)` takes a node out with its descendants, and `SetStyle` and `StyleOf` write and read a node's `Style`. An `Id` holds an `arena.Ref`, built only by `Add`: an Id kept after `Remove` fails `arena.Stale` ([Arenas](#arenas) 2). `Layout(&t, root, w, h)` lays out the tree under `root` in a w×h window, the root taking exactly that size; `BoxOf(&t, id)` is a node's border box (`Box{X, Y, W, H}`) in DIPs from the root's top left.
2. **The subset of flexbox.** A box lays its children out in a `Row` or a `Column` (`Direction`), with `Gap` between them, `Padding` inside it and each child's `Margin` around it (edges are `[4]float32`: top, right, bottom, left), sized border-box. A child's `Width`, `Height` and `Basis` are a `Length`: `Auto`, `Px{Value}` or `Percent{Value}` of the container (automatic while the container's size is unknown). It grows by `Grow` and shrinks by `Shrink` times its inner base size (CSS Flexbox 9.7), never below its content's minimum. Children align across the line by the container's `Items` or their own `Self` (`Start`, `Center`, `End`, `Stretch`) and are placed along it by `Justify` (`Start`, `Center`, `End`, `SpaceBetween`, `SpaceAround`, `SpaceEvenly`). `Position` is `Flow` or `Absolute{Left, Top}`, an offset from the parent's padding box. `Default()` is CSS's flex column: automatic sizes, `Shrink` 1, `Items` `Stretch`. Lines do not wrap, there is no grid, no minimum or maximum size, no border and no automatic margin: each is a later unit.
3. **MyGo is the oracle.** The kernel follows MyGo's `flexLayout` (`ui/layout.go` at afb7d5f) step by step, in float32 as MyGo computes. `TestLayoutMatchesMyGo` (hosts/mygo) lays out 300 generated trees of up to 14 boxes with fixed-size leaves, then each again after three style edits, with the kernel (through the oracle program `hosts/mygo/testdata/layoutoracle`) and with MyGo under `ui.Tester`, and holds every box equal within 0.01 DIP, as far as the window shows it (MyGo's `Find` gives the visible part of a box). A failing seed prints the oracle's input, both boxes and the replay command.
4. **Relayout along dirty paths.** A node keeps the size it was laid out at, its max-content and min-content widths and its height at the last width it was measured at. `SetStyle`, `Append` and `Remove` forget those of the node and of each node up to the root; `Layout` lays a box out again only when it was forgotten or its size changed, and otherwise keeps its children's boxes. `Work(t)` counts the boxes the last `Layout` computed. `TestLayoutRelaysOutAlongThePath` (hosts/mygo) is the growth guard: in trees of fanout 3 and depth 2, 4 and 6, a change of one leaf computes exactly the boxes on its path to the root, and a layout with no change computes none. The edits of `TestLayoutMatchesMyGo` lay out on the boxes kept from before, so a change that does not reach the boxes it moves shows there.
5. **Measured text.** `Content` is `Boxes` or `Text{Value, Size}`. A text node is a leaf as wide and as high as the operation type `Measure` says: `Widths(text, size)` is its max-content and min-content widths (`Intrinsic{Max, Min}`) and `Height(text, size, width)` its height broken into lines no wider than width. `Layout` needs `Measure`, which the host provides; `Monospace` is MyGo's stub engine's measure (half an em a rune, lines 1.25 em apart, breaking at spaces), for tests. The tree keeps what `Measure` said keyed by (text, size, width), up to 4096 of each kind before it lets them go, so a text is measured once at a width; a tree is therefore laid out under one `Measure`. `Measured(t)` counts the calls. *Fonts:* a size stands for the font until the font unit (H5d) adds a family and a weight to the key.
6. **Laws.** `Measure.MinFitsMax`, `Measure.WiderIsNoTaller` and `Measure.AtMaxOneLinePerParagraph` hold of every provider; `TextTakesItsMeasure` (a text of several words stretched in a column is the column's width and its measured height there, one of half that width its measured height at half, and a text in a row fits its content between its min-content and max-content widths) and `MeasuresOnceAtAWidth` (two equal texts at one width, laid out twice, ask `Measure` once) and `HeightFollowsTheWidth` (a text laid out again at another width takes its height there, not the height kept from before) hold of the kernel under `Monospace`. `TestStdLayoutLawsRun` (lower) restates and runs them.
7. **Lowering.** The tree is a slice of node records indexed by int, with links by index (first, last, next, previous child, parent), and an `arena.Of[int]` from Ids to indexes; the items of a box laying out sit on one stack in the tree, above those of the boxes laying out around it; a pop moves the stack's top down and keeps the slice, because a reslice `t.items[:n]` of a value slice lowers to a copy, so a layout allocates only when the stack grows past its largest size. A store into an item is computed before the store, since measuring a child may grow that slice (Go evaluates the slice of `t.items[k].cross = f()` before `f`). Counted (H2L, a 4400-box tree of rows of ten growing leaves, instructions by `perf_event_open`, five runs): a first layout costs 1,366 to 2,241 instructions a box (median 1,889) and runs no collection; a relayout after one leaf's change is below the noise of a process run (about 2 million instructions). MyGo's Tester, on the same tree, takes 89,735 to 94,076 instructions a box for a first frame (build, layout and paint) and 36,933 for a frame with nothing changed; its layout alone cannot be counted from outside the package, so no ratio is claimed. Timed runs: `wf10/corpus-wave/LY/bench-pending.md`.

*Prior art:* MyGo's flexbox, Yoga (React Native) and Taffy (Rust) cache a node's measures and lay out again only along dirty paths, as this kernel does, and take text sizes from a measure callback; Taffy's `compute_layout` takes the measure function as a parameter, Yoga sets one per node. Gleam's Lustre, ReScript's React bindings and Elixir's Phoenix LiveView leave layout to the browser's CSS engine; Elm's elm-ui computes its own layout model and lowers it to CSS. None of them lowers a layout kernel of its own to native code.

## PubSub and send strategies

STORES SB2. Std `pubsub` fans each published value out to every subscriber; std `queue` gives a producer the three answers to a full queue. Both are Ergo over `queue` and `sync` with no Go body of their own, except `Offer` and `Cap` on the queue's runtime body.

1. **Send strategies.** The strategy is chosen at each send, not fixed in the queue: `q.Push(v)` waits for room (back-pressure); `q.Offer(v)` never waits and fails `queue.Full` when there is no room, so a producer that catches `Full` and does nothing drops the new value and keeps the oldest (Effect's dropping); `q.Slide(v)` takes the value at the front out and adds `v` in one step of the queue, so `v` enters exactly once and the queue keeps the newest (Effect's sliding); it gives the evicted value back as `Some`, so a resource evicted is owned by the caller, which must bind it, and its scope releases it (resources R42), as for `Poll`. One queue can take all three ([runtime.md, Queues](runtime.md#queues) 11). `Slide` on a queue of capacity 0 is a defect. Laws: `DroppingKeepsTheOldest`, `SlidingKeepsTheNewest`.
2. **PubSub.** `pubsub.New[T](capacity)` (capacity 1 or more); `p.Subscribe()` gives a `Subscription[T]`, a resource (it includes `Closer`): it receives every value published after `Subscribe` returned, and its owner's scope ends it. `p.Publish(v)` pushes `v` into each live subscription's queue of `capacity`, so a subscriber that does not read holds the publisher back (Effect's bounded strategy); a subscription that closes while `Publish` waits for it is skipped. `p.End()` ends the hub: a later `Publish` fails `queue.Closed`, and each subscriber takes what it has, then `Closed`. `s.Take()`, `s.Values()` (a `Seq`), `s.Len()` and `p.Subscribers()` read it. One publisher's values reach each subscriber in the order it published them; two publishers at once may interleave differently for two subscribers. A subscriber that must not hold the publisher back reads into a queue of its own with `Offer` or `Slide`. Laws: `EverySubscriberSeesEveryValueAfterItJoined`, `SlowSubscriberBoundsThePublisher`, `ClosedSubscriberIsSkipped`.
3. **Why a package of its own.** `sync` imports `task`, which imports `queue`, so `queue` cannot hold a `sync.Ref`; `pubsub` imports both, under Effect's module name.
4. *Not taken from Effect* (Effect 4 `PubSub.ts`, `Queue.ts`): the strategy as a kind of queue or hub (`bounded`, `dropping`, `sliding`, `unbounded` constructors) — the producer chooses per send; `subscribe` as a scoped `Effect` (W14) — a subscription is a resource whose release is its scope's end (resources R1); `publishUnsafe`, `offerUnsafe` and the `Effect`-returning twins (W14); the replay buffer and `takeUpTo`/`takeBetween` — no user yet; an unbounded hub — every Ergo queue has a capacity, so memory stays bounded by construction.

*Prior art:* Go's fan-out of one channel to many with a goroutine per subscriber, and `select` with `default` for a dropping send; Elixir's `Registry` with `dispatch` and Phoenix.PubSub (processes and mailboxes, no back-pressure: a slow subscriber's mailbox grows), GenStage's `BroadcastDispatcher` (demand-driven, the slowest consumer bounds the producer, as here); Gleam's `gleam_otp` has no pubsub (a list of subjects and `process.send`); ReScript uses the host's event emitters. Lowering paths: the BEAM sends a message per subscriber process; Ergo pushes into each subscriber's runtime queue, one mutex operation per subscriber.

## Batching

Std batch, cache and pool. A provider implements `FindMany`, and `context` wraps it: `Users = batch.Resolve(UsersDB, batch.Window(2ms), batch.Max(100))`. Go has no scheduler tick to batch on, so the window is explicit. Duplicate keys share one result. A stopped waiter leaves the batch; the batch is cancelled when no waiter is left. "One query" holds only when every call arrives inside the window. *Prior art:* DataLoader states its scheduling policy; Haxl, ZIO Query and Effect `RequestResolver` batch per round.

## HTTP

Std http and rpc. A handler is an ordinary `func(http.Request) http.Response`. A `catch` without `try` must cover every error, so a handler cannot leave a decode, handler or encode failure without a status. Routes use Go 1.22 `ServeMux` patterns: `"POST /orders": placeOrder`. Tests call `api.Serve(req)` with no server. Declared difference: an unmatched method answers 405, as `ServeMux` does. Open trade: handlers lose codec identity, so `ergo inspect` derives endpoint metadata from signatures and literal statuses in `catch` arms. *Prior art:* Effect `HttpApi` declares endpoints; Go `net/http` handlers; Phoenix controllers.

## RPC

Std rpc. Std `rpc` speaks Effect RPC's HTTP protocol (json and ndjson), so a stock Effect `RpcClient` calls an Ergo server; `ergo gen rpc DIR.OPTYPE` writes a contract's server (`Handle`) and client (`Client`, which provides the operation type). The package, the wire and the int rule are in [rpc.md](rpc.md).

## api

API-FIRST AF1. Std `api` is what every generated transport of an Api shares ([api.md](api.md#std-api-and-the-law-agrees)): `Output { Out, Err, Code }`, what a command line prints and ends with; `ExitOf(o)`, nothing for code 0, else the `os.Exit` of o's code with o's `Err` as its message (a code outside 1 to 125 is 1), `Exit(o)`, which prints o's `Out` on the `Console` first, and `ExitAs(program, o)`, `Exit` with `program: ` before the error line (AF2); `Shell` (`Bash`, `Zsh`, `Fish`), `Entry { Name, Doc }` and `Completion(program, shell, commands)`, the shell's completion script of the commands, each with its first doc line (AF2); `Kebab(name)`, an operation's name as a command or tool name (`RunOf` is `run-of`); `Words(line)` (a line split as a shell splits a simple command, failing `Unclosed`) and `Line(args)`, its inverse, and `Session(lines, run)`, which runs each line of a script through a command line's `Run` and is the `Step`s that ran, stopping after the first whose status is not 0 (AF3); the operation type `Transport[C wire, O wire] { Carry(c C, answer own func(c C) O) O }`, a way to carry a call to an answer and its outcome back; and `Agrees(t, c, o)`, which holds that t hands its answer c, once, and gives back o, both compared by their JSON text. Each generated transport package (`<pkg>rpc`, `<pkg>cli`, `<pkg>mcp`) provides `Wire` and states `law Agrees` with it. Laws: [Std laws](#std-laws), rows `api` (`AgreesSeesTheLoss` holds `Agrees` to see a transport that changes the call; `CompletionNamesEachCommand`, AF2; `WordsReadLine` and `SessionIsEachLine`, AF3). *Prior art:* Effect's `RpcGroup` projected through `RpcServer` and `HttpApi`: one contract, many protocols, each protocol trusted by its own tests; tRPC's adapters share one router. Ergo states a transport's fidelity as one law each transport package runs.

## Code

SEMTOOLS SEM1. Std `code` answers questions about a workspace's code from its fact tables: the declarations of its packages and the sites that name each one, as the checker resolved them ([code.md](code.md)). `Reader` is the contract: `Describe`, `Symbols`, `Symbol` and `References`, which fail `NoSymbol` or `Ambiguous`. Its providers are `Index` (the tables indexed once by handle, position and short name; `IndexOf`) and `Scan` (every question a scan of every row; `ScanOf`), the reference that the top-level law `IndexIsScan` holds `Index` to. `Generated(seed)` draws tables for the law runs, and `NewIndex` and `NewScan` build a provider from it. `ergo code` is `Reader`'s command line, generated by `ergo gen cli` and linked into the binary (docs/tooling.md, Linked Ergo packages). `Planner` plans a `Change` (`Replace`, `Insert`, `Delete`, `Fixes`, `Script`, `Rename`) over the `Checker` seam and stores it; `Writer.Apply` writes a stored plan through the `Files` seam by the protocol of `models/apply` (exchange, verify, journal); `Model` and `Disk` are their memory providers, and the product ones wait for README Gap 17 (SEM4a; [code.md, Plans and apply](code.md#plans-and-apply)).
