Reverse by a crash view whose signals go to a machine of its own.

*Prior art:* Foldkit's `Runtime.makeApplication({ crash: { view, report } })` (the model of this item); Elm has no crash view, since its `update` cannot fail and a runtime exception ends the page; Lustre's `lustre.start` returns an `Error` for a failed start only; React's error boundary (`componentDidCatch`, `getDerivedStateFromError`) shows a fallback for a render error and keeps the tree above it; Phoenix LiveView remounts a crashed LiveView process, which is the reload here, and Erlang's supervisor restarts a child from its initial state.

21. **Frame statistics** (H6b). A pass's work is counted in work units, not time, so a count is the same on every run and every host. `ui.Stats` holds one pass's counts per phase: the step (`Signals`, the signals the session sent the actor since the pass before; 0 for a bare `Render`), the view (`Written`, the nodes the view wrote, with those a stopped group dropped; `Rows`, the row functions `Rows` ran; `Skipped`, the `Same`s kept without running; `Stopped`, the groups that stopped), the compose (`Committed`, the nodes the pass committed), the patch (`Inserted`, `Moved`, `Removed`, `Set`, the ops by kind), the paint (`Painted`, the paint ops of the canvases the patch writes) and the loads (`Fetches`, `Cancels`, the pass's jobs). Layout is the host's (H2L), which reports its own. The readings are `PassStats(f)` (a frame's last pass), `SessionStats(s)` (a session's, with its signals) and `StatsOf(h)` (the test host's, which is what an agent's driver reads), and `StoppedAt(s)`, the call sites of the groups the last pass stopped, in order: a boundary has no node, so the tree cannot show its outcome. The phase entry points are `Handle`, `Render`, `Commit`, `Jobs` and `Arrive`, which were already exported; the paint ops travel in the patch (`PaintOf`), so no phase needs a new entry. Two counters are the composer's own (the nodes written and the groups stopped), an integer addition each, on every pass; every other count is read from what the pass holds, only when asked. A view asks for its slow passes with `OnSlow(f, budget, on)`: after each pass an event of the loop caused (an input, a fetch's result, a frame), the session sends `on(Slow{Counter, Count, Bound})` to the actor for each counter of the `Budget` over its bound (0 is no bound; `Ops` is the patch's ops of every kind), in the order of the budget's fields (`SlowsOf(stats, budget)`). `Counter` is a union, `SignalsCounter`, `WrittenCounter`, `CommittedCounter`, `OpsCounter`, `PaintedCounter` or `FetchesCounter`, so a program's `match` on a warning's counter is exhaustive, and `CounterText(c)` is its `Budget` field name (FK13-g4; the law `SlowNamesItsCounter`, in stats.ergo, holds `SlowsOf` and `CounterText` against an exhaustive reading of each counter over drawn stats and budgets). A pass of a commit the loop did not cause (a work's reply, the `Slow` signals themselves, `Committed`) is not judged, so a slow warning is never judged on its own account. The `Slow` signals the session sends count in the `Signals` of the pass that shows them, as an input's signals do (UC-SIGNALS0; law `SlowAboveTheBudget`). Their pass comes before the session takes any other event (UC-SIGNALS-FLAKE): their `Committed` and a fetch's result are posted by tasks of their own, in an order the scheduler picks, so an event that finds them not yet drawn first runs their pass, and `Step` returns both passes' patches in order. A pass's counts never depend on that order (law `PostedOrderKeepsTheCounts`, which takes the posted events in a drawn order). The laws, on a session: `StatsCountEveryPhase` (drawn passes of an app with texts, a canvas that comes and goes, a keyed `Each` whose order may flip, a list, a group that may stop and a load whose key may change: each pass's stats are the model's, and the patch's counts are the patch's) and `SlowAboveTheBudget` (one `Slow` per input pass over a budget of written nodes, with its count and bound, none for a pass whose view asked for nothing, and none for the passes that show the log). *Decided (corpus wave H6b, 2026-11-13):* the plan's `ui.Driver.Stats()` is `StatsOf(h)`, since the test host is the driver and its readings are functions (`RowRuns(h)`, `CrashOf(h)`); a boundary outcome is its call site, without its failure's type, which waits for derived text forms; a slow pass is judged once, by the loop, not by a timer. Reverse by a `Stats` field per new counter, or a boundary outcome with its type once a type has a text.

**Counts of a step** (NS3, amends H6b). `PassCounts(f)` is a pass's `StepCounts{Inserted, Moved, Removed, Set, Ran, Kept, GroupsRan, GroupsKept, Visits}`: the patch's ops by kind, the nodes the pass wrote and committed, the nodes under the Sames it kept without running, the Sames that ran and that were kept, and the nodes `Commit` visited (one counter add per visit; `Kept` walks the kept subtrees only when asked). On the test host, `Counts(h)` is the last step's: the passes since the last input (`Tap`, `TypeText`, `PressKey`, `PointAt`, `PointOver`, `ScrollTo`, `Navigate`, `GoBackOn`), that input's and those of each `Settle`, `Pump` and `Advance` after it, summed, so a receipt reads the work its input caused, whatever passes settling it ran (FK11-g9). `PlayTrace(h, script)` plays as `Play` does and is its trace, a line per act, `step 3 TapOn "tick": 1 set; ran 3 nodes, kept 801; groups ran 0, kept 200; visits 3` (`CountsLine`); no time is printed, since a time is not a count. The laws: `CountsSumToPatch` (generated programs with a kept Same: the ops by kind are the patch's, the run and kept nodes are the tree below the root, the visits are the nodes not kept, and some pass keeps a node) and `CountsAreTheStep` (a press is one pass with its Set, each later Settle adds its one pass, a key that sends nothing starts an empty step, and the trace line says so). *Decided (corpus wave NS3, 2026-11-15):* a step begins at an input, so `Settle` and `Advance` add to the step they settle; the trace is a function's result, printed by the caller. Reverse by counts per pass only.

**Looks** (UG1). A node's box paints a `Look{Fill, Border{Width, Color}, Corners{TopLeft, TopRight, BottomRight, BottomLeft}, Shadows [4]Shadow, Opacity}` under its children: a resolved, complete value, with no optional field (a transparent colour or a zero width paints nothing), no inheritance from a parent and no merge in std; a caller that builds a look from parts merges them before the pass, as StyleX merges classes at build time. A `Shadow{X, Y, Blur, Spread, Color, Inset}` is CSS's `box-shadow`; `Opacity` multiplies the alpha of the node and its subtree. `Looks{Rest, Hover, Focus, Active, Disabled}` are a node's looks in each interaction state, and a node holds them by handle: `Style.Look` is a `LookId`, which `LookFor(f, looks)` interns in the frame (the same Looks give the same id for the frame's life), and the next `Commit` sends each new one to the host first, one `Define{Id, Looks}` op, so the tree, the diff, `Same` and a patch carry one word per node, the host keeps the table (`MirrorLooks(m)`, `LooksOf(h, node)` on the test host) and a host switches a node's state by an index with no pass. `Plain()`'s look is `BareId()` (`Bare()`, which paints nothing), a `Pressable` given no style holds `PressId()` (`PressLook()`, a light gray fill with corners of 6, the desktop host's button before looks; `PressableStyled(f, label, on, style)` takes a style of its own), and the frame and every mirror start with those two (`DefaultLooks(node)`). A `Mirror` fails `BadPatch` on a `Define` that is not the next id and on a node that names a look it does not hold (the law `MirrorRejectsUnheldLooks`). The screenshot prints, after a node whose looks are not its kind's default, its rest look in braces and each other state's that differs. *Decided (UG1, owner R2):* `ui.Paint` already names a canvas's painter, so the spec's `Paint`, `Paints` and `PaintId` are `Look`, `Looks` and `LookId`; the laws keep the spec's names. Hosts paint the look of the state std decides (UG3). The laws: `PaintIsWhatWasGiven` (the host holds the Looks each node was given, on every container that takes a Style, and the screenshot changes with any part of any state), `PaintIsNotInherited` (a node given no look under a painted parent holds its kind's default, at any depth), and the counted guard `PaintTableGrowsWithDistinctPaints` (n nodes over k distinct Looks give a frame and a host table of k beyond the defaults, at n and at 2n).

**Ink** (OpenTUI gap 1). A `Look` also holds `Ink{Color, Bold, Italic, Underline}`, how the node draws its own text: only a `Text` and a `Readout` use it (`TextStyled(f, value, style)` and `ReadoutStyled(f, r, reserve, style)` give one a Style; `Text` and `ReadoutText` are `Plain()`), and it is not inherited, as no part of a look is. A transparent `Color` is the host's own text colour; `PlainInk()` is that colour, regular, not underlined, and `Bare()` has it (`Inked(k)` is `Bare()` with ink `k`). Like the rest of a look it is per state, so a focused text can change ink with no pass. The screenshot prints ` ink <colour> bold italic underline` for an ink other than `PlainInk()`, each part when present (the law `InkShowsInTheScreenshot`, over a Text and a Readout, which also holds that a Text under an inked Box shows no ink). The desktop draws the colour, bold as the run drawn twice a dip apart, an underline a dip high on a whole pixel row under each line (a probe of the agreement), and italic as its one face sheared by 0.2 of the height above the baseline, a synthetic oblique (the window counts its slanted runs, which the agreement checks, and `TestTextSlants` reads the lean off the pixels); a terminal host maps `Ink` to its foreground colour and its bold, italic and underline attributes.

**Caret, selection and lines** (OpenTUI gap 2). An `InputNode` carries `Lines`, `Caret` and `Anchor`. `Lines` is how many lines the field shows: 1 for `Input`, `Field` and the labelled and hinted forms, which hold no newline (a typed newline is dropped), and n for `InputLines(f, value, placeholder, n, onText)` and `FieldLines(f, draft, placeholder, n)`, whose text keeps its newlines, as a prompt's does. `Caret` and `Anchor` are byte offsets into the value at rune starts; the selection lies between them. They are the model's, not the host's, unlike a DOM input's: a host sends `Edit{Id, Text, Caret, Anchor}` for each edit or caret move in a focused field, a `Type` is an `Edit` with both at the end, and the next pass shows them for as long as the field's value is the text they were set for (a value the program changes puts them at the end). A move of the caret alone changes the view and sends no signal. The edits a key makes are std functions every host shares (`ui/edit.ergo`): `InsertText` (typing, over the selection), `DeleteBack`, `CaretTo`, `Left`, `Right`, `LineStart`, `LineEnd`, `LineUp`, `LineDown`, `SelectAll`, and `KeyEdit(e, key, lines)`, which maps the arrows (Shift extends the selection), Home, End, Backspace, Enter in a field of more than one line, and Ctrl or Meta with A. The test host's `EditText(h, id, text, caret, anchor)` sends an Edit, and its screenshot names `lines n`, `caret c` and `selected a..b caret c` when they are not the defaults. The laws are `CaretStaysInTheText` (over drawn texts with multi-byte runes and newlines and drawn edits, the caret and the anchor stay at rune starts inside the text, typing leaves the caret after what it typed, and Backspace undoes a typed rune) and `CaretIsTheModels` (a Field and an Input show the caret an Edit gives them and keep it across a pass; a Type and a value the program changes put it at the end; a one-line field drops a typed newline). The desktop draws the caret and the selection where the model puts them, a field of n lines n lines high, and edits through `KeyEdit` and `InsertText`.

**Hover and focus** (UG3). The frame decides, for both hosts, which node the pointer is over, which node holds the focus and whether its focus look shows. A host sends each pointer event to the node under the pointer, or to the frame's root when it is over none (`PointNowhere(h)` on the test host); when the node changes, each `Area` that held the node it left and does not hold the new one gets a `PointerLeave` (the innermost first), then each `Area` that holds the new node and did not hold the old one a `PointerEnter` (the outermost first; two more `Phase`s, named so because `Enter` is a machine's decision), before the event's own signal. These are the DOM's pointerleave and pointerenter: a move onto a node inside the same Area crosses nothing, and entering an inner Area does not leave the outer one (OT-PHASES). `LookStateIn(f, id)` (`SessionLookState`, `LookStateAt(h, id)`, and the look itself `LookShownAt(h, id)`) is `Disabled` for a node under a disabled `Fieldset` and a `Semantic` whose access is disabled, else `Active` while a pointer's press holds it or a node under it, else `FocusVisible` when it holds the focus a key gave it, else `Hover` when the pointer is over it or a node under it, else `Rest`; `LookNow(looks, state)` is that state's look. A hover change runs no pass: the host repaints from its look table. The focus look follows the browser's `:focus-visible`: Tab, Shift+Tab, a roving group's keys and a changed `Focus` request show it, a pointer's press hides it, and a key's press of the focused node keeps it (`Activate(h, id)` on the test host; `Tap` is a pointer's press and leaves the pointer over the node; `ClickNowhere(h)` is a click on the window's background). `FocusScope(f) { }` traps the focus: when it comes it takes the focus to its first tab stop and remembers where the focus was, Tab and Shift+Tab go round its own stops, and when it leaves the tree with the focus inside, the focus returns there. `Roving(f) { }` is one tab stop for its focusables, the one the focus was last on (its first before any); `ArrowRight`/`ArrowDown` and `ArrowLeft`/`ArrowUp` move round inside it and `Home` and `End` to its ends, when no key function takes the key. `Focus(f, key)` asks for the focus on the first focusable at or under the zone or `Semantic` that declares `key`; the commit moves the focus only when the request differs from the last pass's, so a view that asks every pass does not take the focus back from Tab. The MyGo desktop paints each node's look of its state and sends every pointer move, so hostagree's `states` app checks each labelled node's look state against the test host's. The laws (laws_focus.ergo): the counted guard `HoverCostsNoPass` (pointer moves onto nodes and off them run no pass, and the node under the pointer shows its hover look, every other its rest look), `PointerCrossesOnce` (the Leave and Enter signals against a model of the node under the pointer), `PointerCrossesAreas` (over nested Areas and the Texts inside them, the crossings against a reference of the Areas that hold the node under the pointer), `FocusFollowsTheRing` (Tab, Shift+Tab, arrows, Home, End and clicks over texts, Pressables, disabled ones and roving groups against a reference ring, with the focus look shown after keys only), `FocusStaysInScope`, `FocusReturnsOnEnd` (to the opener, or where a click outside put it) and `FocusGoesWhereAsked`. *Decided (UG3, 2026-11-17):* hover, focus and focus-visible live in the frame, not in a host, so both hosts paint the same state from the same events; a focus scope and a roving group are a Box with a flag, not new node kinds, so the diff, `Same` and the patch need nothing new.

**Layers** (UG4). `Layer(f, Layered{Z, Anchor, Place, Scrim, Dismiss}, style) { }` writes a subtree above the flow (a `LayerNode`): a host lays it out at its own size and places it with `PlaceLayer(anchor, w, h, view, place)`, a pure function every host with boxes calls. `Place{Side, Align, Gap}` puts the layer on a `Side` of its anchor (`SideTop`, `SideBottom`, `SideLeft`, `SideRight`), `Gap` away, aligned along that side by `Align` (`AlignStretch` as `AlignStart`); when the side has no room in the view and the other side has, the layer flips there (when neither has, it takes the side with more room), then it shifts along both axes to stay in the view, as Floating UI's flip then shift do. The anchor is the first node, in tree order, that declares the `Anchor` key as a zone's key or a `Semantic`'s `Access.Key` (`AnchorIn(m, key)` on a mirror); with no anchor, the layer is centred in the view. Layers stack by kind, `Z = PopoverZ | ModalZ | ToastZ | TooltipZ` in that order, and among one kind the later in tree order is above (`StackOrder(zs)`, `Layers(f)`, `LayersOf(h)`); a host paints them after the flow in that order and hit-tests the top first. Dismissal is the frame's, so every host dismisses alike: Escape, with no modifier, is the dismiss signal of the top layer that has one (the page's key functions do not get it); a pointer's Down on a node outside that layer sends its dismiss signal before the event's own (a click outside a menu closes it and still presses what it pressed, as light dismiss does); a layer inside another is inside it. A layer with `Scrim` shields the rest: an event on a node outside the top scrim layer does nothing but that dismissal (`Shielded(f, id)`); the desktop paints the scrim over the window and a press on it goes to the root, and the test host's `Tap`, `TypeText` and pointer acts on a shielded node land on the root too. The desktop places each layer against its anchor's box in the window, again for a layer anchored in a layer that moved, and hostagree's `layers` app checks each layer's box against `PlaceLayer` and what a press inside, outside, on the scrim and Escape send. The laws (laws_layer.ergo): `LayerStaysInViewAndTouches` (drawn views, anchors, sizes, sides, alignments and gaps: in the view, touching the anchor's edge when a side has room, at the alignment's place when it is in the view, centred with no anchor) and `LayersDismissFromTheTop` (drawn layers of drawn kinds, nested or not, with and without scrims and dismiss signals, and drawn Escapes and clicks, against a reference model, with the stack's order). *Decided (UG4, 2026-11-18):* placement is a pure function in std, not a host's, so the hosts place alike and a law covers it; dismissal and the scrim's shield are the frame's for the same reason; a layer is written where its trigger is (its body under its caller), not portalled, so a nested layer is inside its parent.

**Fingers** (MB4). A `PointerEvent` names its `Pointer{Id, Kind}`, as Pointer Events' `pointerId` and `pointerType`: `Kind` is `Mouse`, `Finger` or `Pen`, and a host gives each finger on a screen its own `Id` from its touch to its lift, so two fingers are two pointers (`MousePointer` is a desktop's one mouse, Id 0). A finger does not hover: its events leave the frame's hover where it was; the test host's `TapWith(h, id, pointer)` is `Tap` by that pointer, so a finger's tap there leaves the hover too. Std ui owns a finger's scroll as it owns hit testing: `TouchStep(touch, input, look)` is a pure machine per container, `Idle → Pressed → Dragging(axis) → Flinging | Settling → Idle`, fed a finger's `FingerDown`, `FingerMove`, `FingerUp`, `FingerCancel` and the host's `TouchFrame`s, each with its time, and giving the container's offsets `X`, `Y` and a `Tapped` or `LongPressed` signal. A finger within `Slop` of its touch is pressed: a lift there before `LongPress` taps, and still for `LongPress` it long-presses once; past the slop the drag locks to the axis that first move went further along and follows the finger on that axis only (an axis with no room stays), `Stretch` of the way past the content's edge; a lift past the edge returns to it at rate `Return` (`Rest` from it, it is there), a lift faster than `MinFling` flings, its speed decaying by e^(-`Friction`·t) per time and not per frame, resting at `MinFling` or at an edge; a finger down during a fling or a return catches it and does not tap. One finger owns a container from its touch to its lift: another finger's inputs change nothing meanwhile. The constants are data, `TouchLook` (`IOSTouch()`, `AndroidTouch()`); `TouchMoving` says a host needs frames, `NewTouch(roomX, roomY)` and `TouchRoom` give the room the content leaves. `Reach(targets, x, y, margin)` is the control a finger hits: the top one under it, enabled or not, else the nearest enabled one within `margin` (`ReachMargin`, MyGo's 14 DIP), the top one of those as near. `Insets{Top, Right, Bottom, Left}` are what the system covers, and a host lays the root out in `InsetRect(view, insets)`. The laws (in touch.ergo, beside `TouchStep`): `FingerLocksOneAxis`, `FlingStops` (inside the content at each frame, at rest within the frames its friction allows, and the same offset at a time under frames three times as far apart), `OverscrollReturns`, `TapIsNotADrag` (a cancelled finger presses nothing), `LongPressNeedsStillness`, `SecondFingerWaits` and `ReachPicksTheNearest`. Not in std ui yet (MOBILE Q3): pinch, pull to refresh, edge back and selection handles. *Decided (MB4, 2026-11-19):* the machine is std ui's and pure, not a host's, by "std ui owns hit testing" (q64), so every host scrolls a finger alike and a law covers it; reverse by a host with native scroll views, which then reports its offset through the same `Scrolled` event.

**Fields on phones** (MB5). A field states what a phone's keyboard shows for it, as HTML's `inputmode`, `enterkeyhint`, `autocapitalize` and `autocorrect` do: `Hints{Keyboard, Return, Capitalize, Autocorrect}`, with `Keyboard = TextKeys | EmailKeys | NumberKeys | PhoneKeys | URLKeys | PasswordKeys`, `ReturnKey = ReturnDone | ReturnNext | ReturnSearch | ReturnSend` and `Capitalize = CapNone | CapWords | CapSentences | CapAll`. `HintedInput` and `HintedField` write them on the `InputNode` (`Hints`); every other field has `PlainHints` (text keys, Done, sentences, corrected). `KeyboardName(k)` is the name a host hands its platform ("text", "email", "numeric", "tel", "url", "password"). A desktop shows its keyboard whatever a field asks. The input method edits the field through the host's text input client, a copy of the field's text with the selection and the composition (the marked range) in UTF-16 units, which the host answers from at once, on its own thread, never waiting for the program's pass: a committed edit (a replacement, a delete, a composition committed, or one ended empty over text it replaced) gives the program the field's committed text as a `Type`, and an open composition gives nothing, so the program never sees half a character. The host numbers the edits it gives, and a paint names the last one the program took: while edits are in flight the copy stays (fast typing while a pass runs), and then a value that is not the text the program was last told (it refused or rewrote it) becomes the copy, the caret at its end. `hosts/mygo` `TestRangeEditsAgree` holds the desktop's client to a model (a rune slice with a selection and a marked range) over seeded input-method edits on texts of one- to four-byte runes, and hostagree's `compose` step holds the state unchanged while a composition is open. Not here yet: the keyboard as an inset (it waits for a phone surface, MB6), and the hints reaching the platform (MyGo's surface at the pin takes no keyboard kind; the window keeps it). *Decided (MB5, 2026-11-19):* the client's copy of the text lives in the host, not in std, because an input method reads back each edit synchronously; reverse by a std edit machine when a second host needs the same client.

**The trace of a test run** (NS3's CLI follow-up). `Play` and `PlayTrace` record each act's line, the one `PlayTrace` returns for it, as a step event of the running task's trace (`rt.TraceStep`, trace kind `step`; [runtime.md, Machines and actors](runtime.md#machines-and-actors) 15.7), and `ergo test FILE --trace` prints them after the run, in the order they ran, under `trace: N steps`. Like a span, a step event is instrumentation: it changes no value the program sees, so `Play` needs no host authority, and its two bindings (`traced`, `traceStep`, in ui's trace.ergo) read the running task. An untraced `Play` pays one check per script and builds no line. `cmd/ergo` `TestTestTrace` is the property: over scripts drawn from a seeded base, the traced run's verdicts and returned lines are the untraced run's, and the printed steps are `PlayTrace`'s lines and then `Play`'s of the same scripts. *Decided (corpus wave NS3 trace, 2026-11-15):* the lines travel on the trace sink `ERGO_TRACE` already binds, not on a stream of their own, so the test harness is unchanged and std ui writes nothing to the host. Reverse by a `--trace` that reads only `PlayTrace`'s results.

*Prior art:* Foldkit's `slow` option warns when an update, a view, a patch or a subscription's dependencies take longer than a threshold of time; React's `<Profiler onRender>` reports `actualDuration` and `baseDuration` in milliseconds; Phoenix LiveView emits `:telemetry` events with durations for `mount` and `handle_event`; Elm and Lustre have no frame statistics. They all measure time, which differs between two runs; this item counts work, which an agent can compare across runs and a test can assert, and leaves time to the profiler (the plan's DV units).

22. **Zones, pointer capture and announcements** (H5g). A pure view cannot read boxes, so the host hit-tests and the composer names what was hit. `Zone(f, key, style) { … }` is a container (`ZoneNode{Key, Style}`) that marks its body for the pointer events of the `Area` around it. The host's `Point{Id, Pointer, Over}` names the Area and, in `Over` (`ZoneBox{Id, Box}`), the topmost zone under the pointer and its box in the Area's box; the composer fills the event's `PointerEvent.Zone` with a `ZoneHit{Path, Box, X, Y}`: the keys of the zones from the Area down to that zone (its own last), its box, and the pointer's offset in it. A zone the tree no longer holds, a node that is not a zone, or a zone outside the Area gives none, and a host's own `Zone` is replaced. Finding the path walks the Area's subtree, so its work is at most that subtree's size. Capture is the host's, as the web's `setPointerCapture` is: after a Down on an Area, every pointer event goes to that Area, in its box's coordinates, wherever the pointer is, until an Up, or until the tree no longer holds the Area. Whether a move is a drag is the composer's (FK12-g1): a move the host sends, as `Moved` or as `Drag`, reaches the program as `Drag` exactly when it goes to the node the last Down went to with no Up since, and as `Moved` (a hover) otherwise, so a move without a press is never a drag and every host gets the same answer (law `CaptureKeepsThePointer`, whose model says which phase each move arrives as). A press (a tap on a `Pressable`, `Check` or `Toggle`, or its key) is a press and a release, so it ends the hold: the test host's `Tap` lets go of the capture and no move after it is a drag until the next Down. The desktop host routes a move to the Area holding the pointer, as the test host does. While an Area holds the pointer near an edge of a `Scroll`, the host scrolls it by `EdgeScroll(offset, height, content, y, edge, step)`, a pure function that moves toward the edge the pointer is within `edge` of (faster nearer it) and stays inside `[0, content - height]`. `Announce(f, text)` writes a live region (`LiveNode{Text}`, role `Status`): the host says each text a pass newly shows in it, politely, and nothing for an empty text or an unchanged one. `TestHost` is a host of these rules: `PointAt` and `PointOver` route as capture says, `Captured(h)` is the Area holding the pointer, `Announced(h)` is what was said, by every pass the host shows, the last pass of `Settle` included (FK11-g5: a text that only `Settle`'s last pass writes is said; `AnnounceSaysEachNewText` changes a third of its texts through a cell the view reads, which no event announces, so only that pass shows them), and the locator `WithZone{Key}` finds a zone. Drag and drop is not std: it is a machine of a program's (`examples/kit/drag`, FK11). The laws: `ZoneHitNamesItsPath` (drawn columns of cards, each a zone, and a zone outside the Area: each drawn hit's path, box and offset, and none for a non-zone, a removed card or the outside zone), `CaptureKeepsThePointer` (drawn events on two Areas and a Text, and a press that removes an Area, against a model of capture), `AnnounceSaysEachNewText`, `EdgeScrollStaysInRange` and `EdgeScrollRunsEachFrame` (the test host's `Advance` scrolls the first `Rows` list inside the Area that holds the pointer by `EdgeScroll` with `EdgeBand` (32) and `EdgeStep` (16) each frame period while it moves, the Area's box taken as the list's viewport, since the test host lays nothing out; the scroll is a host `Scrolled` and stops at the end of the range). *Decided (corpus wave H5g, 2026-11-15):* the host hit-tests and the composer turns a zone into keys, so every host shares the path rule and no host needs keys; capture lives in the host, which alone has the boxes to give a captured event its coordinates; every Area captures on a Down, as Qt's and GTK's implicit grab and the web's touch capture do; a live region is a node, so it travels in the patch as any other. Reverse by a host that sends the path itself, or an Area option that opts out of capture.

*Prior art:* Foldkit reads the DOM in Commands (`getBoundingClientRect`) and keeps drag state in an 889-line `DragAndDrop` component; Elm reads boxes with `Browser.Dom.getElement`, a Task; React and the web use `setPointerCapture` and `aria-live` regions; dnd-kit's `KeyboardSensor` and its auto-scroll near a container's edge are the model for the drag machine and `EdgeScroll`; Qt and GTK grab the pointer implicitly on a press.

23. **Enter and exit transitions** (H5e). `Presence(f, xs, key, dur) func(x, p Progress) { … }` writes a row per `x` of `xs`, keyed by `key`, each under a `TransitionNode{Key, Motion, T}` (`Motion` is `Entering | Present | Exiting`; `T` is in `[0, 1]`, 1 fully in). The rows of its first pass start `Present`; a key that comes later enters from T 0; a key the list loses exits from the T it had, and its row stays in its place, right after the row that was before it, as the subtree it last wrote: the row's body does not run, and when the pass cannot keep that subtree (a cell under it changed, its group stopped) the row goes at once. A row that comes back while it exits enters from where it was, and one that leaves while it enters exits from where it was; one that leaves at T 0 goes at once. Progress moves only on frames: a motion starts at the first `Tick` after the pass that began it and moves `1/dur` per unit of frame time, and a pass of any other event shows the progress of the last frame. While a row is not `Present` the view asks for frames (`Animating`), so the session renders on each `Tick`; once every row is still it asks for none, and a settled screen costs nothing. The laws (`TestUIHostModel`): `TransitionEndsAtLayout` (drawn lists, keys added, removed and reordered, and drawn waits: after frames past every motion, no frame is asked for, every row is `Present` at 1, and the screen with each `TransitionNode` replaced by its children is the screen of the same list written with `Each` and a `Box` per row), `PresenceKeepsTheList` (after each drawn change, the rows not exiting are the list in its order, a gained row enters at 0, a lost row is exiting after the row before it, and frames are asked for exactly while a row moves), `ExitTakesItsDuration` (an exit from the T an entry reached loses its share per frame and ends at 0) and `NoTickWhenRowsSettle` (a settled Presence runs no frame over any wait). Not built: a move animation (a kept row whose place changed) and a shared element between two views, which interpolate the first and last boxes, the host's (H2L). *Decided (corpus wave H5e, 2026-11-15):* progress lives in a cell of the Presence and moves on the frame's time, never on a clock of its own, so a transition is a function of the frames the host sent and a test host replays it exactly; an exiting row replays its last subtree as a `Same` does, so its body need not handle a value the list no longer has. Reverse by a per-row clock, or a body that runs for an exiting row.

*Prior art:* React's `AnimatePresence` (Framer Motion) keeps an exiting child until its exit animation ends, as Vue's `<TransitionGroup>` and Svelte's `transition:` directives do; Elm leaves transitions to programs (elm-animator keeps the timeline in the model); Flutter's `AnimatedList` keeps a removed item in place while it animates out; the web's View Transitions API snapshots first and last boxes for a shared element, which the move animation here waits for.

**Tracks** (TRACKS, RUNE §2). `Track(f, key, prop, target, curve) { … }` is a container (`TrackNode{Key, Prop, Target, Curve, Style}`) whose body the host moves along x or y (`TrackX`, `TrackY`, in DIP) or fades (`TrackOpacity`) to `target`, on its own frames and with no pass: a pass that writes another target retargets the track at the host's clock, from where it is and as fast as it moves, and the frames between passes only sample the curve. A curve is data: `TrackLinear{Over}`, `TrackEase{Over}` (a cubic Hermite from the track's place and speed to rest on the target) or `TrackSpring{Stiffness, Damping}` (mass 1, solved in closed form, under, critically or over damped). The motion is pure: `TrackState{From, Velocity, To, Start, Curve}`, `TrackStill`, `TrackAt(t, at)` (value and velocity; exactly `To` once done), `TrackRetarget(t, to, at)` (a Linear track keeps only its place) and `TrackDone` (a Linear or Ease track after its duration, a spring once its energy is within `TrackRest` of rest, which a damped spring never leaves). A host asks for frames while a track moves; a frame is a `Tick`, which runs no pass unless the view animates. `TestHost` runs tracks on its clock (`TrackedValue(h, key)`, and `Advance` frames while one moves); the desktop host moves and fades the body as it paints. The laws (in track.ergo, beside `Track`): `TrackEqualsAnimate` (a drawn spring retargeted by taps shows at every frame the value an Animate-driven program integrates for itself by RK4, its velocity carried through each retarget, within 0.01 DIP), `TrackCostsNoPass` (the frames after a retarget move the track and add no pass), `TrackRetargetKeepsMotion` and `TrackEndsOnTarget`. Not yet: transitions between `Looks` (TRACKS-b) and `Presence` progress on tracks (TRACKS-c), a scale track, and the receipts that go with them. *Decided (TRACKS, 2026-11-19):* the curve is std ui's data and the host runs it, so a frame costs a sample and a repaint, never a pass; fluid's `motion.Spring` maps onto `TrackSpring`. Reverse by a view that animates with `Animate` as before.

24. **Readouts** (LIVE). `NewReadout(text)` makes a `Readout`, a handle to a text that a host reads each time it paints. `ReadoutText(f, r, reserve)` places it as a `ReadoutNode{Readout, Reserve, Style}`, a leaf laid out by its `reserve` text, so no reading moves its siblings; a reading wider than the box is clipped. The node names the readout by a number, so it still compares with `==`, and a pass that writes it again changes nothing. `Publish(r, text)`, from any task (a signal's handler, a work, a fetch, a timer, an Area's pointer function), stores the text and posts `Published{Readout}` to each session that showed `r`: one event however many Publishes come before the host takes it, and none for an equal text. A session's `Step` of a `Published` runs no pass and gives no op; `Run` then calls `Host.Show` with no ops, and the host paints again each node that reads `r` (`ReadingIn`, `ShownIn`): the desktop marks those nodes and paints with no layout, the cell host draws its frame again, and the test host's `Screen` reads every readout as the `TextNode` of its reading. `Reading(r)` is its text now. A readout is ordinary std code over `sync.Ref` and `atomic` (a package `let` numbers readouts); no compiler rule knows it. Laws: `LiveCostsNoPass` (a counted guard: Publishes from the test's task and from a task of their own run no pass, a new text posts one event, an equal one none, and the screen shows each readout's latest text) and `LiveEqualsRerun` (a screen of readouts equals the screen of the same view with the texts in the actor's state, under reorders and removals).

*Prior art:* rune's `LiveText` (a placeholder reserves the box; an equal string wakes no host; `tx.live_only()` skips view, diff and layout) is the model; Flutter's `ValueNotifier` with a `ValueListenableBuilder` rebuilds one subtree; the web writes `textContent` outside a framework's render.

*Prior art:* Gio (Go) threads `layout.Context` through widget functions and keeps widget state in values the program owns (`widget.Editor`, `widget.Bool`), as `Draft` and `Flag` are kept here; Jetpack Compose keys a node by its call site and passes its composer as a hidden parameter, with a slot table of erased values (`remember`); SwiftUI keys `@State` by structural position; Elm and Gleam's Lustre build a value tree per frame and diff it, with `Html.Keyed` for identity, and keep all state in the model; Inferno and ivi place keyed children by a longest increasing subsequence; Phoenix LiveView (Elixir) sends statics once and dynamics by change, P-ui's later lowering, and its `assign_async` gives an async value per key; Effect's `AsyncResult` and effect-atom's async atoms are the `Async` union. ReScript renders through React's runtime and has no tree of its own.

### UI cell host

The terminal cell host (T1, NS6) is `examples/cells/host`, an Ergo package that other programs require as a module. It steps a `Session` as `TestHost` does and lays the tree out in points with `ui/layout`, under the `Measure` of the grid (`Cells`: a rune is one cell of 8 points, a line one row of 16).

1. **One rounding rule.** A box covers the cells `Span(box)` gives: each edge is rounded to the nearest grid line, and half a cell rounds up. Drawing and hit testing both use it, so the cell a node is drawn in is the cell that hits it (law `HitMatchesDraw`).
2. **Cells from semantics.** A node is drawn from its `Access`: its name, and its state from one table, the marks. A Check shows `[x]` or `[ ]` before its label, a Toggle `(•)` or `( )`, and a Pressable and an Input sit between `[` and `]`. A control under a disabled Fieldset is faint, and the focused control is in reverse video. A Pressable that holds no Text is drawn as its label (an icon button), and a Canvas as its label, faint. A `Semantic` node (NS4) is drawn from the record it declared, by one exhaustive table per field, with no default: its role's ends (`[` `]` around a Button, a Tab, a MenuItem and a TextBox, `<` `>` around a Link, none around the other roles), then its Checked mark, its Pressed mark (`(•)` or `( )`) and its Expanded mark (`▾` or `▸`) before its name; its value, then ` !` when invalid, ` …` when busy and ` ▾` when it has a popup, after it. A selected node, a Heading and an Alert are bold. A Semantic node that declares a press takes the focus and the hit test, as a Pressable does; one that holds a Text shows the Text in place of its name. A live region shows its text; a zone and a transition show their children. A tap on an Area names the topmost zone drawn in the tapped cell (`Point.Over`), by the rounding rule. Law `CellsShowEveryName`: every node that shows and has a name has that name's first cells in the grid, or the name is in the left-out table, and each Semantic node's marks show where it is drawn. `HitMatchesDraw` also taps each zone's cell and requires its Area's signal.
3. **Left out, never imitated.** The host does not draw the following, and does not stand in for them with characters:

   | left out | what shows instead |
   | --- | --- |
   | opacity (a color's alpha) | the cell is drawn or not |
   | shadows and blur | nothing |
   | radii and borders | a box shows no frame |
   | images (pixels) | a Canvas's label |
   | a canvas's paint list beyond its label | the label |
   | sub-cell sizes and positions | the cells `Span` gives |
   | an Area's label, a list's label | their children, their rows |
   | a Pressable's label or a Semantic node's name when it holds a Text | the Text |
   | an Input's placeholder while it holds a value | the value |
   | a transition's motion and progress | the row, whole, while it enters or exits |
   | a live region's announcement | its text, not spoken |
   | a heading's level | every heading is bold |
   | a node's description | nothing |

4. **A patch is applied whole.** A host applies every op of one pass to its tree before it draws, and no input is read between two ops of one patch. The cell host writes a pass as one frame inside the synchronized-update marks (`CSI ? 2026 h` … `CSI ? 2026 l`). It holds only the cells that changed: a run after one cursor move, and each pen change once. Laws: `PatchIsOneFrame` (on a fake terminal's op log, each write is one whole frame, there are no more frames than patches and the host's own redraws, and replaying them gives the host's grid), `DiffReplaysToGrid`, `FrameWritesTheChanges` and `PatchWritesItsChange`. The last two count the cells written per patch, as a guard.

*Prior art:* ratatui and Bubble Tea draw a frame into a buffer and write the difference from the last. Textual and Ink lay a tree out with flexbox and round to cells. Notcurses and Ghostty's renderer use mode 2026 so that a frame shows whole. Gleam, Elixir and ReScript have no terminal host in their standard libraries: Gleam's `shore` and Elixir's `ratatouille` are libraries over a cell buffer, as this host is.

## ansi

`ansi` is a terminal's escape codes (UC2): styles and colours, the cursor's moves and the screen's clears, and `Strip`. A style is painted only where colour is on, which a capability decides, so a program whose output is piped, or a person who set `NO_COLOR`, gets plain text with no change to the program. The codes are ECMA-48's as xterm reads them. Prior art: Go's standard library has none (fatih/color and lipgloss read `NO_COLOR`); Effect's cli has a colour flag; Bun has `Bun.color` and `Bun.stripANSI`; Rust's anstyle and anstream split the style from the stream that decides colour, as `Paint` and `Colors` do here.

| Declaration | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `type Color = DefaultColor{} \| Basic{N int} \| Indexed{N int} \| RGB{R int, G int, B int}`; `Black` … `White` | A colour: the terminal's own, basic 0 to 15 (8 to 15 bright), indexed 0 to 255, or RGB, each number held to its range where it is written. Its variants carry no pointer (a std union lowers flat), so a `Style` is flat. |
| `type Style { Fg Color, Bg Color, Bold bool, Dim bool, Italic bool, Underline bool, Reverse bool, Strike bool }`; `let Plain` | How a text is painted; `Plain` has no colour and no attribute. |
| `type Colors { On() bool }`; `Always`, `Never`, `Detected`; `func Detect() Detected needs os.Process \| io.Stdout`; `func Decide(noColor Option[string], term Option[string], terminal bool) Detected` | Whether colour is on. `Detect` is off when `NO_COLOR` is set and not empty (no-color.org), when `TERM` is `dumb`, or when standard output is not a terminal; `Decide` is the same decision for values a program read itself. |
| `func Paint(s Style, text string) string needs Colors`; `func WritePaint(b *strings.Builder, s Style, text string) needs Colors` | `text` in `s`: `SGR(s)`, `text` and `Reset` with colour on and `s` not `Plain`, else `text`. `WritePaint` appends the same to the caller's builder. |
| `func SGR(s Style) string`; `let Reset` | `s`'s one SGR sequence (attributes 1, 2, 3, 4, 7, 9; colours 30-37, 90-97, `38;5;n`, `38;2;r;g;b` and 40 more for a background), `""` for `Plain`; and the reset. A caller that writes `SGR` decides colour itself. |
| `func Up(n int) string`, `Down`, `Forward`, `Back`; `func MoveTo(row int, col int) string`; `ClearScreen`, `ClearLine`, `ClearToEnd`, `Home`, `HideCursor`, `ShowCursor`, `AltScreen`, `MainScreen` | The cursor's moves (n under 1 is 1; rows and columns from 1) and the screen's sequences. |
| `func Strip(s string) string` | `s` with every escape sequence taken out: CSI (to its first byte in 0x40-0x7e), OSC (to BEL or ESC `\\`), ESC and one byte, and a sequence `s` ends inside. |

Laws (beside their subjects in ansi.ergo, run by lower `TestStdAnsiLawsRun`): `StripUndoesPaint` (Strip of a painted text is the text), `NeverPaints` (colour off paints nothing), `PaintIsSGRAndReset`, `SGRIsTheTable` and `StripIsTheTable` (recorded xterm sequences), `DetectIsTheEnvironment`, and `Colors.OnIsStable`. The survey's width law (`Width(Paint(s, t)) == Width(t)`) waits for SQ2's `text.Width`.

Speed: `WritePaint` appends fixed bytes and decimal numbers into the caller's builder with no allocation of its own (the floor); `Paint` adds one builder. `Style` is flat (no pointer), compared with `==`.
## Host packages

Std host packages. `os`, `fs`, `net` and `sql` are the only standard-library code that calls Go. They are reviewed. Every blocking call has a cancel hook, or is recorded as an uninterruptible host wait that `ergo inspect` lists (G8). Each package states the Go targets it supports. File handles are resources ([resources.md](resources.md)). Through host packages and `import go`, every effect appears in a footprint ([rows.md](rows.md#footprints)).

A host package has five parts: an operation type, the capability a program names in its rows; a record `Host { live hostOnly }`, the host's provider, whose unexported field of a zero-field type gives the package the construction authority and keeps `Host` zero size; one constructor `func NewHost() Host needs Ambient`; for `fs`, `exec` and `os`, `Host { root string }` instead, `""` for `NewHost`'s and the case's folder for the test's own, `func NewTestHost() Host needs TestAmbient` (item 5); the provider's functions, with Ergo bodies; and unexported externs that bind the Go bodies in `runtime/v1` (`host.go`).

**Who chooses the host (host-authority H1).** Only code whose row holds the prelude's `Ambient` makes a host provider, and only the entry provides `Ambient`:
1. Outside its package, `fs.Host` as a value, `fs.Host{}`, `let h fs.Host`, a record update of it, and a type that holds it at zero are S114 "fs.Host has an unexported field, so only package fs builds it; use fs.NewTestHost or fs.NewHost" (types.md, Zero values 6). A generator or codec does not build it (`check.Constructible` is false).
2. A call of `fs.NewHost()` charges `Ambient`. Where no row holds it, the call is S102: in a function whose written row does not list it, in a `test`, in a package-level `let` and in a named context ("context Live requires Ambient while it is built"). Every function writes the need ([rows.md](rows.md#capability-rows) 6), so it reaches its callers through their written rows, up to `main`. A local context charges its `with` ([rows.md](rows.md#composition-laws) 9).
3. The entry provides `Console` and `Ambient`: a `main` that lists `Ambient` gets it from the host, and any other capability in `main`'s row is S109 "the host provides only Console and Ambient". `Ambient`'s one operation is unexported, so a program type does not implement it. No context binds `Ambient`, in any package, the prelude included: an entry `Ambient: v` is S106 "no context binds Ambient: its one provider is the program entry", whatever `v` is (a parameter of type `Ambient` too). So `Ambient` has one source, the entry (fu-ambient-ctor r7).
4. Delegation stays open: a function that takes a host provider or a capability value as a parameter uses it with no row (GLOSSARY, Interface value). The caller that made it holds `Ambient`.
5. A test cannot get `Ambient`, so it binds its own provider: tests are hermetic. A test that needs the live host states it (TH1, [rows.md](rows.md#tests-ambient)): a named context whose header says `needs TestAmbient`, the test's ambient, which only the test runner provides, may make `fs.NewTestHost()`, `exec.NewTestHost()` and `os.NewTestHost()`. Each is the package's `Host` confined to a fresh folder per case, removed after the case: a path out of it is the package's `Denied` (the path's links resolved, except its last element for `Lstat`, `Remove`, `Rename`, `IsLink`, `Readlink` and `Symlink`, which act on a link itself, as Go's do), a command runs in it with `HOME` and `TMPDIR` there (on Linux in the folder's sandbox, where only the folder is writable, TH1b; elsewhere the paths given to exec are confined and the program's own path choices are not), and `os`'s `Host` has no arguments, the folder as its working directory, and only `HOME`, `TMPDIR`, `PWD` (the folder) and the host's `PATH` set. `TestAmbient` counts as the authority in items 6 and 7, as `Ambient` does.
6. The prelude's `LiveScheduler` reaches the host's clock, so it follows the rule too (std-align S2b, host-authority H2): `type LiveScheduler { live liveOnly }`, and its one constructor is the prelude's `func NewLiveScheduler() LiveScheduler needs Ambient`. A program that sleeps on the host binds `Scheduler: NewLiveScheduler()` in `main`; a test or a law binds `time.NewManual(t0)`. `TestStdHostProvidersHaveAuthority` finds every std defined type whose function reaches a host extern and fails on one that another package can build with no authority, or that an exported name of std can hand out (fu-ambient-ctor r5). Let *held* be the provider `T` and the records another package needs to build it (`check.Authorities`). Every exported function, let and named context of every std package whose type mentions a held type lists `Ambient`; a let and a named context have no row, so they mention none. A type mentions what it is made of, at any depth (`check.Mentions`): every field of a record, hidden or not; type arguments; union variants; slice, `Map` key and element; a function's parameters, results, failure row and needs row; a type parameter's constraint; an operation type's operations and, for a sealed one, each type of its package that implements it; a machine's state and signal. The one exemption: a function with a parameter whose type is a held type itself, as in `func Clone(h Host) Host`, since its caller holds the authority. The rule over-reports on purpose: four rounds of a precise rule (what another package can obtain through results, failures, callbacks and needs rows) each missed a channel. `check.Mentions` is one switch over every kind of type that `TestMentionsWalkNamesEveryKind` keeps total; it lists no exception. A signature does not show a value that hides behind a type it does not name: an open operation type the provider implements (`func Open() Files { Host{…} }`, `let Disk = context { Files: Host{…} }`), a closure that captures it, or a call's argument (`try Read(Host{…})`). So the **mint rule** asks where a value is built (fu-ambient-ctor r6): in every std package, each place that builds a value of a held type (`check.Mints`: a record literal, a record update, the zero value of an omitted field or of a `let` with no value, a bare record, a conversion, a constant, a value a test or law generates) is in a function, or a closure in one, whose written row lists `Ambient` or that has a parameter of a held type. A package-level `let`, a named context, a type declaration (a machine's initial state, an operation type's law), a `test` and a `law` have no row, so a build there is always reported. A call is no build: the function it calls is asked where it builds. That holds because `Ambient` has one source (item 3), which the guard checks too: no std type implements `Ambient` and no std context binds it. An extern has no Ergo body to ask, so an extern whose signature mentions a held type lists `Ambient` in its written row (or takes a held parameter), and the result of a call of a generic extern, at its type arguments, is a build at the call (`zero[Host]()`). `check.Mints` decides each kind of expression in one switch that `TestMintFormNamesEveryKind` keeps total. "Another package can build it" is the checker's fact `check.Buildable(t, from)`: from literals, empty literals, zero values, conversions and function values, so a record with an exported field `Caps []Cap` fails, as an empty slice holds no `Cap` (fu-constructible). A sealed operation type is built when a type of its package that implements it is, so a host provider may be one (fu-hostguard2). A program package has no host provider of its own: it binds no Go that reaches the host (S108, S109), and it reaches the host only through a std provider, whose authority is std's (`TestProgramPackagesHaveNoHostProvider`).
7. **Who calls a host extern (fu-extern-callers).** Every std function, exported or not, whose body reaches a host extern lists `Ambient` in its written row or takes a parameter of a held type (item 6), as Go passes the handle. A function reaches when its body names a host extern or a function of its package that reaches, a fixed point; a function that holds the authority stops the chain, since its caller supplies the provider. A borrow of the provider is no authority. A closure is judged in the function around it, unless the closure holds the authority itself (a held parameter, or a written row that lists `Ambient`). An exported host extern follows the rule at its declaration, since another package calls it directly. A *host extern* is an extern whose runtime/v1 Go body its declaration marks `//ergo:host` in its doc comment (`v1.Body.Host`; fu4c), or whose Go package outside runtime/v1 is not on the pure allowlist (`runtime/rt.Sleep`). An impure body that is not marked reaches no host state: it makes or changes a slice, a Map, a queue or a task of the program, reads the running task's context, or makes the seeded stream or the manual timeline. `v1.BodyOf` keeps the mark honest: it refuses an unmarked body that reaches a host member (every member of `os`, `os/exec`, `os/signal`, `syscall` and `net`; the clock, timer and zone members of `time`; the global draws of `math/rand/v2`), directly or through a function or variable of the package, and a body marked both pure and host. A call through a function or interface value, or into `runtime/rt`, is not followed, so there the mark is the claim. An impure extern that a host provider's function reaches and that is not marked fails. So `func Peek(path string) string fails NotFound | Denied | Failed { try hostReadFile(path) }` in `fs` fails the guard (batch-10 r7 On base). `TestStdExternCallersHoldAuthority` runs the rule over every std package and over probe rows; `TestExternCallerRuleAgreesWithOracle` checks it against a model over generated call chains of depth 1 to 3.

So `main` reads `func main() needs Console | Ambient { with context { fs.Files: fs.NewHost(), os.Process: os.NewHost() } { run() } }`. The context in `cmd/<name>/main.ergo` is the one place that chooses the host, by convention: a library may wrap the choice when its own row lists `Ambient` (`func RunLive(f func() needs Console | fs.Files) needs Console | Ambient`). A test binds its own provider in the same place (`TestHostAuthority`, `TestHostAuthorityDomain`, lower `TestHostAuthorityRuns`).

### os

Std `os` is the host process (wave std-1, unit G2).

```go
import os

func run() fails os.Exit needs Console | os.Process {
	match os.Process.Getenv("TOPIC") {
	case Some[string] as t:
		Console.Println(t.Value)
	case None:
		return os.Exit{Code: 3, Message: "[NOT_FOUND] no TOPIC"}
	}
}

func main() fails os.Exit needs Console | Ambient {
	with context { os.Process: os.NewHost() } { try run() }
}
```

| Declaration | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `type Process { Args() []string, Getenv(key string) Option[string], Environ() Map[string, string], Getwd() string fails NoWorkdir, law GetenvIsStable(key string), law EnvironAgreesWithGetenv(key string), law GetwdIsStable(), law GetwdIsAbsolute() }` | The capability. |
| `type Host { live hostOnly }`; `func NewHost() Host needs Ambient`; `func Args(_ Host) []string`; `func Getenv(_ Host, key string) Option[string]`; `func Getwd(_ Host) string fails NoWorkdir` | The host's provider and its one constructor ([Host packages](#host-packages)). |
| `type Code int where (1 <= it && it <= 125) || (129 <= it && it <= 192)`; `type Exit { Code Code, Message string }` | The failure status and the failure that ends `main` with that status. `ParseCode(n)` checks a computed int, failing `Unsatisfied`. |
| `type NoWorkdir { Message string }` | The failure of `Getwd` when the host cannot name the working directory. |
| `type Self { Pid() int, HomeDir() string fails NoDir, CacheDir() string fails NoDir, ConfigDir() string fails NoDir, Executable() string fails NoDir, law PidIsPositive(), law DirsAreAbsolute() }`; `type NoDir { Message string }` | The process's facts about itself beyond `Process`. `Host` implements both capabilities. The host's answers are Go's `Getpid`, `UserHomeDir`, `UserCacheDir`, `UserConfigDir` and `Executable` (made absolute); a directory Go gives relative is `NoDir` (law `DirsAreAbsolute`). A test's `Host` answers with the case's folder as home, and `.cache` and `.config` under it. |
| `type Platform { OS string, Arch string }`; `func Current() Platform` | The GOOS and GOARCH the program was built for (CAP-PLATFORM). It is a fact of the built program, so it needs no capability. |

1. `Args` is the arguments after the program name, Go's `os.Args[1:]`.
2. `Getenv` is `None` when `key` is unset and `Some("")` when it is set to the empty string. The law `GetenvIsStable` states `Getenv(key) == Getenv(key)`: two reads of one key agree, for any provider.
3. `os.Exit` is a failure in the row, not a call. When it is `main`'s only reason, the entry writes `Message` and a line feed to standard error, verbatim (nothing when `Message` is empty), and exits with `Code`. Its field has the ordinary refined type `os.Code`, whose predicate is `(1 <= it && it <= 125) || (129 <= it && it <= 192)`: a program's own failure, or 128 plus a signal's number for an end by a stop (`signal.StatusOf`; CAP-INTERRUPT) ([types.md](types.md#refinements)), and whose declaration names its Go type, `extern go ergo.local/prototype/runtime/v1.Code type Code int where …`, the `v1.Code` of `v1.Exit`'s field (Types 6): constants are checked there, and a computed int enters through `os.ParseCode`. It never skips cleanup: every scope's exit actions run before the entry writes it. Go's `os.Exit` skips deferred calls; Ergo has no call that does.
4. `Process.Environ` returns the environment as a map, one value per name, with the same values as `Getenv` (law `EnvironAgreesWithGetenv`). The Go bodies are `v1.OSArgs`, `v1.OSHasEnv`, `v1.OSGetenv`, `v1.OSEnviron` and `v1.OSGetwd`, each marked `//ergo:wait none`, with no release route. They read the host process, so none is pure. `os.Exit` is the `runtime/v1` struct `v1.Exit`, and `os.NoWorkdir` is `v1.NoWorkdir` (Types 4).
5. A program implements `os.Process`: it has no unexported operation. `os.NewHost()` and a program's own type with `Args`, `Getenv`, `Environ` and `Getwd` both convert to it, in `context { os.Process: … }` and at every other assignability site. It lowers as a program interface, not as a `runtime/v1` type. A test binds a memory provider where `main` binds `os.NewHost()`: `with context { os.Process: FakeEnv{…} } { run() }` (`TestOSProcessRuns`; `TestStdProtocolOpen`). A runtime protocol such as `queue.Queue` declares a private operation and is refused for the same reason as a private operation of a user package (`TestSealingIsTheVisibilityRule`).
6. `Getwd` is the working directory, an absolute path, Go's `os.Getwd` (gap-os). It fails `NoWorkdir` with Go's text when the host cannot name it: the directory was removed, or a directory above it cannot be read. It fails with `NoWorkdir` when the working directory has been removed, on every host: Go's `os.Getwd` fails there on Linux but gives the old path on darwin, so `v1.OSGetwd` also checks that the path names the same file as `.` (`os.SameFile`). Go returns `(string, error)`; Ergo puts the one failure in the row. A program's own provider returns a fixed directory and keeps the operation's row, since a provider's function has the operation's fallibility (S106 "differs in fallibility"): `func Getwd(_ FakeEnv) string fails os.NoWorkdir { "/work" }`. The law `GetwdIsStable` states that two reads agree, in value and in failure: `Process` has no operation that changes the directory. The law `GetwdIsAbsolute` states that a directory it gives is absolute (`filepath.IsAbs`), so a message that names a file under it names one place. *Prior art:* Node's `process.cwd()` throws when the directory is gone; Elixir's `File.cwd/0` returns `{:error, reason}` and `File.cwd!/0` raises; Gleam's `simplifile.current_directory` returns a `Result`; Go's `os.Getwd` returns `(string, error)`.

*Prior art:* Roc's basic-cli platform gives `Arg` and `Env` as platform effects, and its `main` can fail with `Exit code message`, which the platform writes and exits with; `os.Exit` takes that shape and adds the 1..125 check. Effect Platform reads the environment through a `ConfigProvider` layer and leaves argv to `@effect/cli`. Gleam's `argv` and `envoy` packages, Elixir's `System.argv/0` and `System.get_env/1`, and ReScript's Node bindings read the process directly, with no capability; Elixir's `System.get_env/1` returns `nil` for an unset key, and Gleam's `envoy.get` returns `Result(String, Nil)`. Elixir's `System.halt/1` ends the VM at once with any status; Ergo's `os.Exit` is a failure, so cleanup runs first and the status is checked.

### fs

Std `fs` is the host file system (wave std-1, unit G4; writes, batch 9 unit G4b). `Files` reads it and `Writes` changes it: two capabilities, so code that only reads shows only `fs.Files` in its row.

```go
import fs
import strings

func title(path string) string needs fs.Files {
	let text = fs.Files.ReadFile(path) catch {
	case fs.NotFound as n:
		return "no file \(n.Path)"
	case fs.Denied, fs.Failed:
		return "unreadable"
	}
	strings.TrimSpace(text)
}

func main() needs Console | Ambient {
	with context { fs.Files: fs.NewHost() } { Console.Println(title("doc.md")) }
}
```

| Declaration | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `type Files { ReadFile(path string) string, ReadDir(path string) []string, Stat(path string) Info, law ReadIsRepeatable(path string) }`, each operation `fails NotFound \| Denied \| Failed` | The capability. |
| `type Writes { WriteFile(path string, text string), MkdirAll(path string) fails Denied \| Failed, WriteFileMode(path string, text string, mode Mode), MkdirAllMode(path string, mode Mode) fails Denied \| Failed, AppendFile(path string, text string), Chmod(path string, mode Mode), Rename(from string, to string) fails NotFound \| Denied \| Failed \| CrossDevice, Remove(path string), law MkdirAllIsIdempotent(path string), law RemoveThenNotFound(path string), law RenameMoves(from string, to string, text string), law CrossDeviceMovesNothing(from string, to string, text string) }`, each listed operation with no row written `fails NotFound \| Denied \| Failed` | The capability to write. |
| `type Access = NoAccess{} \| Execute{} \| ReadOnly{} \| ReadExecute{} \| ReadWrite{} \| ReadWriteExecute{}`; `type Mode { Owner Access, Group Access, Other Access }`; `Private()`, `PrivateDir()`, `Shared()`, `SharedDir()`; `Bits(a Access) int`, `Perm(m Mode) int` | A file mode (11). |
| `type Host { live hostOnly }`; `func NewHost() Host needs Ambient`; `func ReadFile(_ Host, …)`, `func ReadDir(_ Host, …)`, `func Stat(_ Host, …)`, and one function of `Host` per operation of `Writes` | The host's provider of both, and its one constructor ([Host packages](#host-packages)). |
| `type Mapped { runtimeOnly(), Text() Text, Close() }`; `func MapFile(_ Host, path string) Mapped fails NotFound \| Denied \| Failed`; `func MapText(text string) Mapped`; `type Text string`; `func Clone(t Text) string`; law `MapTextIsText` | The whole file as `ReadFile` gives it, through a read-only mapping instead of a copy, as a resource whose `Close` unmaps it; its text is a borrow of it (4; [types.md](types.md#borrows), Borrows 8). `MapFile` is a function of `Host` only, not an operation of `Files`, and `Mapped`'s one constructor besides `MapText`, a Mapped over a text in memory for tests. |
| `type FileLock { fd int }`; `func LockFile(_ Host, path string, wait duration) Option[FileLock] fails NotFound \| Denied \| Failed`; `func UnlockFile(_ Host, l FileLock)` | An exclusive kernel lock on a file, or `None` when another holder keeps it for all of `wait` (4b). Functions of `Host` only. |
| `func Exchange(_ Host, a string, b string) fails NotFound \| Denied \| Failed` | The files at `a` and `b` trade places in one host call, or `Failed` where the host has no atomic exchange (4c). A function of `Host` only. |
| `func Link(_ Host, from string, to string) fails NotFound \| Denied \| Failed \| CrossDevice`; `func SameFile(_ Host, a string, b string) bool fails NotFound \| Denied \| Failed`; `type CrossDevice { From string, To string }` | A hard link, never a copy, and whether two paths name one file by the host's identity of a file (4d). Functions of `Host` only. |
| `type Info { Name string, Size int, Dir bool, Perm int }` | What `Stat` gives; `Perm` is the permission bits, as `Perm(m)` gives them (17). |
| std `fstest`: `type Memory { tree sync.Ref[…] }`; `func NewMemory() Memory`; one function of `Memory` per operation of `Files` and `Writes`; laws `CopiesShare`, `AppendConcatenates`, `ModeIsKept` | The memory provider of both, for tests (17 to 19). |
| `type NotFound { Path string }`; `type Denied { Path string }`; `type Failed { Path string, Reason string }` | The failures. |
| `type Root { ReadFile(path string) string, ReadDir(path string) []string, Stat(path string) Info, Name() string, law LeavingEscapes(path string) }`, each read `fails NotFound \| Denied \| Failed \| Escapes`; `type Escapes { Path string }` | Confined reads below one directory (20). |
| `type HostRoot { …the operations of Root…, hostRooted() }`; `extern … func OpenRoot(dir string) HostRoot fails NotFound \| Denied \| Failed needs Ambient` | The host's Root, sealed, and its one constructor (21). |
| std `fstest`: `type Tree`; `type Node = File{Path, Text} \| Dir{Path} \| Link{Path, Target}`; `func NewTree(nodes []Node) Tree`; `type Recorder`; `func NewRecorder(inner fs.Root) Recorder`; `func Paths(r Recorder) []string` | The memory Root with symbolic links, and a Root that keeps every path it is asked for (22). |
| std `fs`: `func NewTestHost() Host needs TestAmbient`; std `fstest`: `type Confined { Folder() string, ReadFile, ReadDir, WriteFile }` with laws `OutsideIsDenied`, `InsideIsWritten`, `EachCaseStartsEmpty`; `type Folder`; `func NewFolder() Folder needs TestAmbient` | The host file system of a test, confined to the case's folder, and the operation type whose laws state the confinement ([Host packages](#host-packages) 5; [rows.md](rows.md#tests-ambient)). |

1. `ReadFile` is the whole text of the file at `path`. It returns `string`, not bytes: the language has no conversion from `[]byte` to `string` yet. A path is the host's; a relative path is relative to the working directory.
2. `ReadDir` is the names of the entries of the directory at `path`, sorted by name (Go's `os.ReadDir` sorts them). `Stat` follows symbolic links, as Go's `os.Stat` does (not `os.Lstat`). A link to a directory has `Dir: true`, and a link whose target is missing is `NotFound`. `Info.Name` is the last element of the path, so for a link it is the link's own name. `Info.Size` is the size in bytes of the target.
3. The read operations have a closed row of three failures. `NotFound` is a path that does not exist (Go's `fs.ErrNotExist`), `Denied` a path the process may not read (`fs.ErrPermission`), and `Failed` every other read failure, with the host's `Reason` without the operation and the path, such as `is a directory` for a directory read as a file. A `catch` that lists the three is exhaustive for a read; other operations have their own rows. An uncaught failure is written with its generated text and status 1, such as `error: fs.NotFound{Path:missing.md}`.
4. The Go bodies are `v1.FSReadFile`, `v1.FSReadDir` and `v1.FSStat`, each marked `//ergo:wait none`, with no release route. They read the host, so none is pure. None of the three keeps a file open after it returns, so they expose no handle and need no resource (the mapping of `MapFile` below is one). The four records are the `runtime/v1` structs `v1.NotFound`, `v1.Denied`, `v1.Failed` and `v1.Info` (Types 4). Each record's declaration names its Go type (Types 4), so a record of the same name in another package names its own Go type. **MapFile** (wf10 1BRC) is the body `v1.FSMapFile`: it maps a regular file `PROT_READ`, `MAP_SHARED`, closes the descriptor and returns a `*v1.Mapped` whose text is the mapping as a `string` (`unsafe.String`), so a file of any size costs no read and no heap, and its bytes are read from the page cache (64 MiB read whole: 12.8 GB/s mapped against 1.5 GB/s for `FSReadFile`, measured, `BenchmarkFSMapFile`). **Mapped** (wf10 MAPPED) is a resource: its `Close` (or the release of its owner) unmaps the mapping, once. Its invariant: the mapping is read-only, and no text is read after `Close`, so the string never changes while anything reads it, as Go requires of a string. The checker keeps that second half: `Text()` gives an `fs.Text`, a borrow of the Mapped that never escapes and whose every use is a use of the Mapped (types.md, Borrows 8). `TestFSMappedKeepsItsInvariant` checks the protection in `/proc/self/maps`, the text against `FSReadFile`'s, a read after a collection, and that `Close` unmaps (ledger mutants `onebrc-mapfile-writable`, `onebrc-mapfile-unmapped`, `mapped-close-keeps-mapping`). `MapText` (`v1.FSMapText`, pure) is a Mapped over a text in memory whose `Close` releases nothing; `Clone` (`v1.FSClone`, pure) copies a text into a string that outlives its Mapped; law `MapTextIsText` (run in place, `ergotests -std`) says the clone of `MapText(s)`'s text is `s`. An empty file is `""`, a directory fails as `ReadFile` fails, and a file that is not regular (a pipe, a device) is read as `ReadFile` reads it. **A truncated mapping is a defect.** A file that another process truncates while it is mapped faults on the next read of a page past the new end (SIGBUS), which Go reports as a fatal fault. `v1.FSMapFile` registers each live mapping with `runtime/rt` (`MapRegister`); the mapping task, and every task that starts while a mapping is live (`rt.Run`), asks Go to panic on the fault instead (`debug.SetPanicOnFault`), and the task boundary records the panic as a defect whose value is `rt.MappedFault{Path, Offset, Cause}`, which names the file and the byte (`TestMappedTruncationIsADefect`; mutants `mapped-fault-crashes`, `mapped-fault-unnamed`). A task that starts while no mapping is live keeps Go's crash. *Prior art:* Go's `golang.org/x/exp/mmap` returns a `ReaderAt` and unmaps on `Close`; Go's `debug.SetPanicOnFault` docs name memory-mapped files as its use; Rust's `memmap2` makes the map `unsafe` to construct for this truncation hazard; Java's `MappedByteBuffer` (Wuerthinger's 1BRC entry) is never unmapped until collected. Gleam, ReScript and Elixir read files whole; Erlang's `file:read_file` makes one binary.
4b. **LockFile** (SEMWS WG1, the registry of `work`) is the body `v1.FSLockFile`: it opens the file (made with mode 0666 less the umask when missing, close-on-exec) and takes `flock(LOCK_EX|LOCK_NB)`, again after pauses of 1 ms doubling to 32 ms until `wait` has passed; then it closes the file and answers `None`. The lock is on the open file description, so two `LockFile` calls exclude each other in one process as in two, and the host gives back the lock of a process that ends, however it ends: a dead holder strands nothing. A `FileLock` holds a number the body never gives again, not the descriptor, so a second `UnlockFile` of one lock closes nothing, even when the descriptor's number is reused (`TestFSLockFileExcludes`). The wait is a host wait, as a slow read's is: its `runtimeBindings` row neither waits on the scheduler nor releases. The lock is advisory, and on a network file system `flock` may not exclude. The general form, a scoped lock resource, waits for local resource ownership. *Prior art:* Go's `syscall.Flock` and the `cmd/go/internal/lockedfile` package (an exclusive lock per open file, polled for a timeout by its callers); Rust's `fs4`/`File::try_lock` (Rust 1.89); Cargo locks its package cache the same way. Gleam and Elixir have no file lock in their standard library (Erlang's `file` module has none; Hex and Mix lock by a lock file they create exclusively); ReScript leaves it to Node, whose `fs` has none either.

4c. **Exchange** (SEMTOOLS SEM4b, the product `code.Files`) is the body `v1.FSExchange`: the files at `a` and `b` trade places in one host call, Linux's `renameat2(AT_FDCWD, a, AT_FDCWD, b, RENAME_EXCHANGE)` or macOS's `renameatx_np(AT_FDCWD, a, AT_FDCWD, b, RENAME_SWAP)`, so a reader of either path sees the old file or the new one and never no file (`TestFSExchangeIsOneStep`). Both must be there: a missing one is `NotFound` naming it, and nothing moves. A kernel without `renameat2` or a file system without the flag (`ENOSYS`, `EINVAL`; on macOS `ENOTSUP`, `EINVAL`), and every host other than Linux on amd64 and arm64 and macOS, is `Failed` with reason `no atomic exchange on this host`: the exchange is never done by renames a reader could see half done. Go's `syscall` does not bind `renameatx_np`, so the body traps it by number with `syscall.Syscall6` (SYS_RENAMEATX_NP, 488), which on darwin is a direct trap in Go 1.27 (`syscall/asm_darwin_arm64.s`, `asm_darwin_amd64.s`), not libc; Apple promises only libc, so the body rests on xnu's system call numbers: no assembly and no linkname, so the runtime is Go files only and every build path compiles it (EXCHANGE-NOASM; SEM4c had a libc trampoline in assembly, which the direct build path refuses). `Exchange` is a function of `Host` only, as `LockFile` is, not an operation of `Writes`. *Prior art:* Go has no exchange (`golang.org/x/sys/unix.Renameat2` binds it); Rust's std has none (the `renameat2` crate); Gleam, Elixir (Erlang's `file:rename/2`) and ReScript (Node's `fs.rename`) offer only rename.

4d. **Link and SameFile** (G6, the hard-link store of dray). `Link(from, to)` is the body `v1.FSLink`, Go's `os.Link`: `to` becomes a second name of the file at `from`, so a write through either is read through the other and `SameFile(from, to)` holds. A file at `to` is `Failed` ("file exists"): `Link` never replaces one, so a caller links at a new name beside `to` and renames it over `to`. A missing `from` is `NotFound` naming `from`, else a missing directory of `to` names `to`. Two file systems (`EXDEV`) are `CrossDevice`, never a copy, so a caller tells "place it another way" from a failure. `SameFile(a, b)` is the body `v1.FSSameFile`: Go's `os.Stat` of each (after symbolic links) and `os.SameFile`, the device and the inode on Unix, so a hard link and its source are one file and a byte-equal copy is not; a no-op check of an installed link is two stats (`TestFSLinkIsOneFile`; ledger mutants `g6-link-copies`, `g6-samefile-by-name`, `g6-crossdevice-failed`). `Info` gains no device or inode: an identity is only compared, so `SameFile` is the whole need and no number of the host leaks into a program. A reflink (a copy-on-write clone) has no portable form (Linux's `FICLONE` ioctl, macOS's `clonefile`, none on others, and Go's std binds neither), so std has none: it stays a module's trusted Go. Both are functions of `Host` only, as `Exchange` is. *Prior art:* Go's `os.Link` and `os.SameFile`; Rust's `std::fs::hard_link` and `same-file` crate (std has no SameFile); Elixir's `File.ln/2` (Erlang's `file:make_link/2`), with identity only through `File.stat`'s `inode` and `major_device`; Gleam's `simplifile` binds `create_link`; ReScript leaves both to Node (`fs.link`, `fs.stat`'s `dev`/`ino`).
5. A program implements `fs.Files`: it has no unexported operation, as `os.Process` has none ([os](#os) 5). A test binds `fstest.NewMemory()` (17), or a provider of its own, such as one that fails a write on purpose, in the same place as `main` binds `fs.NewHost()`: `with context { fs.Files: memory.Files{…} } { run() }` (lower `TestFSFilesMemory`).
6. **Authority.** The externs are unexported, and only package `fs` builds an `fs.Host`: other code gets one from `fs.NewHost()`, which needs `Ambient`, or as a parameter ([Host packages](#host-packages), host-authority H1). So code that reads the disk with no `fs.Files` in its row, by `fs.ReadFile(h, p)`, by passing `h` as an `fs.Files` value, or by its own `with context { fs.Files: h }`, either has `Ambient` in its row or was given `h` by code that has it. `fs.ReadFile(fs.Host, p)` is S114; the same call on `fs.NewHost()` in a function whose row does not hold `Ambient` is S102. The same holds for `os.Host`.
7. The law `ReadIsRepeatable` states that two reads of one path agree: both give the same text, or both fail with the same failure (its kind and its fields). A first read that fails is followed by a second read whose failure is compared with the first; a second read that succeeds, or that fails after a first that succeeds, refutes it (batch-12 M2: a provider whose reads alternate NotFound and a text breaks it). It holds for a world with no writer: `Files` has none, so it holds for `fs.Host` on a quiet disk and for any memory provider. A law runner cannot build `fs.Host` by a generator (`check.Constructible` is false): it builds it through `fs.NewHost()` under `Ambient`, or it skips the host provider and says so.
8. **Writes.** `WriteFile(path, text)` makes the file at `path` hold `text`. The body `v1.FSWriteFile` opens a new file `path.tmpN` (N random) in the directory of `path`, with mode 0666, so the umask applies as for Go's `os.WriteFile`. It writes the text, syncs the file, gives it the mode of the file at `path` when there is one, closes it, and renames it over `path`. So a reader sees the old text or the new text, never a part, and the rename never crosses a device. A file that was at `path` keeps its permission bits; a second hard link of it keeps the old text. On any failure the new file is removed and the old file is whole. A crash between the write and the rename can leave a `path.tmpN` file. A symbolic link at `path` is followed, as Go's `os.WriteFile` and Effect's `FileSystem.writeFile` follow it: the file that the link names gets the text and keeps its mode, and the link stays a link. The new file is made beside the file that the link names, so the rename replaces that file, not the link. A link to a missing name makes that file; a link to a directory is `Failed` "is a directory", as a directory is, and the link stays; more than 40 links in a chain is `Failed` "too many levels of symbolic links". A `..` in a link's text is resolved by the host after the link's directory, as for an open, not removed by text (runtime/v1 `TestFSWriteFileFollowsLinks`). A directory at `path` is `Failed` "is a directory"; a missing directory on the way is `NotFound`; a directory the process may not write is `Denied`. The directory is not synced after the rename, so the rename itself can be lost in a power failure.
9. `MkdirAll(path)` makes the directory and each missing parent with mode 0777 less the umask (`v1.FSMkdirAll`, Go's `os.MkdirAll`).
