A directory that is there already is no failure, which the law `MkdirAllIsIdempotent` states. Its row has no `NotFound`: a missing path, such as the empty path, is `Failed` with the host's reason. A file on the way is `Failed` "not a directory".
10. A provider of `Files` and `Writes` over one store keeps one more contract: after `WriteFile(path, text)` succeeds, and with no other writer, `ReadFile(path)` is `text`. No law states it yet, because a member law sees only the operations of its own type. `fs.Host` keeps it (lower `TestFSWritesRuns`). `fs.Host` provides both capabilities. So `main` binds `context { fs.Files: fs.NewHost(), fs.Writes: fs.NewHost() }`; `Writes` has no private operation, as `Files` has none, so a test binds a memory provider of it. The bodies are each marked `//ergo:wait none`, with no release route.
11. **Modes** (std-fs-safe, decision O8). A file mode is the record `Mode { Owner, Group, Other }`, each an `Access`: `NoAccess` (bits 0), `Execute` (1), `ReadOnly` (4), `ReadExecute` (5), `ReadWrite` (6) or `ReadWriteExecute` (7). A write without a read is not a variant: no program needs it. So a mode reads as what it allows, and a program needs no octal literal. `Perm(m)` is the Unix bits, `Bits(m.Owner)*64 + Bits(m.Group)*8 + Bits(m.Other)`, the `int` the bodies take. Go's conventions for secrets are functions: `Private()` is 0o600 and `PrivateDir()` 0o700; `Shared()` is 0o644 and `SharedDir()` 0o755. They are functions, not package `let`s, because a std package `let` has no Go name in a program's build today (Follow-up of std-fs-safe).
12. **Exact modes.** `WriteFileMode(path, text, mode)` is `WriteFile`, but the file ends with exactly `mode`, whatever the umask, also when a file with another mode was at `path`. The body `v1.FSWriteFileMode` opens the new file `path.tmpN` with `mode` (the umask can only narrow it), gives it exactly `mode` with `fchmod`, and only then writes the text: from its creation on, no user outside `mode` can open the file, so a key written with `Private()` has no readable window (runtime/v1 `TestFSWriteFileModeIsExact`, under the umasks 0, 002, 022, 077 and 777). `MkdirAllMode(path, mode)` is `MkdirAll`, but each directory it makes is made with `mode` and then given exactly `mode`; a directory that is there keeps its mode (`TestFSMkdirAllModeIsExact`). `Chmod(path, mode)` is Go's `os.Chmod`, which follows a symbolic link. Go's `os.WriteFile(name, data, perm)` and `os.MkdirAll(path, perm)` apply the umask to `perm`; Ergo's exact mode is never wider than asked and never narrower.
13. **Append.** `AppendFile(path, text)` opens the file with `O_APPEND` (and `O_CREATE`, mode 0666 less the umask, for a missing file), writes `text` in one write and closes it, so the text lands at the end and two appends do not tear each other's text on a local file system (`TestFSAppendFileIsOneWrite`). A file that was there keeps its mode. It does not sync: an append costs one write, as Go's and Effect's `writeFile` with the flag `a` do. It is O(len(text)), where a read and a `WriteFile` of the whole file is O(size).
14. **Rename and Remove.** `Rename(from, to)` is Go's `os.Rename`: it replaces a file at `to`. The host's "no such file" does not say which name is missing: the body names `from` when nothing is at `from`, and `to` otherwise (a missing directory on the way to `to`); every other failure names `from`. A move to another file system (Go's `EXDEV`) is `CrossDevice{From, To}`, as `Link`'s is (G6): `Rename` never copies, and moves nothing (FS-RENAME-CROSSDEVICE; the law `CrossDeviceMovesNothing`). `Remove(path)` is Go's `os.Remove`: a file, a symbolic link (not what it names) or an empty directory; a directory with entries is `Failed` "directory not empty". The laws `RemoveThenNotFound` and `RenameMoves` state what a provider of `Writes` alone shows; lower `TestFSMemoryAgreesWithHost` runs them, and `ReadIsRepeatable` and `MkdirAllIsIdempotent`, on `fs.Host` and on `fstest.Memory` alike (18), and `TestStdHostLawsHold` runs them on the host's edge cases (symbolic links, a file no one may read, `..`).
15. **Contracts over Files and Writes.** A member law sees only its own type's operations, so two contracts of a provider of both are written in `Writes`' doc comment and run by `TestFSWritesModel` (runtime/v1), not by a law: `AppendConcatenates` (after `WriteFile(path, a)` and `AppendFile(path, b)`, `ReadFile(path)` is `a + b`) and `ModeIsKept` (after `WriteFileMode(path, text, m)`, the file has mode `m`, and a later `WriteFile` or `AppendFile` keeps it). `TestFSWritesModel` runs 100 generated sequences of 30 steps of `WriteFile`, `WriteFileMode`, `AppendFile`, `Chmod`, `Rename`, `Remove`, `ReadFile` and `Stat` over four names, each under a umask it draws, against a map of name to text and mode, and checks every name after every step; a failing seed prints its steps.
16. **A breaking change.** Adding operations to `Writes` breaks a program's own provider of it, which must add them (decision O8: Effect's `FileSystem` is one service; a second capability would split one authority in two). The lower test `TestFSSafeRuns` runs every new operation through `fs.Host`.
17. **Memory** (std-fs-memory). `fstest.NewMemory()` is a provider of `Files` and `Writes` over a tree in memory, in the std package `fstest` after Go's `testing/fstest`: it is not in `fs`, so `fs.ReadFile(h, p)` and the other direct calls of an `fs.Host` keep one function of their name each. A test reads and writes no disk: `let m = fstest.NewMemory()` and `with context { fs.Files: m, fs.Writes: m } { run() }`, then `m.ReadFile(p)` to look. It has no `Ambient` in its row: it reaches nothing of the host. `fstest.NewVolumes(mounts)` is a `Memory` whose directories at `mounts` are each a file system of its own: a path is on the volume of the longest mount at or above it, and a `Rename` between two volumes is `CrossDevice` and moves nothing (the law `VolumesAreCrossDevice`; `TestFSMemoryAgreesWithHost` runs `CrossDeviceMovesNothing` on one, since neither `fs.Host` in a test folder nor `NewMemory` has a second file system). Its tree is in a `sync.Ref` (Sync and STM), so every copy of a `Memory` is the same tree (law `fstest.CopiesShare`), and each operation is atomic, so tasks may share one. "." and "/" are its two roots; a path is cleaned (`filepath.Clean`) before it is looked up, so "./a" is "a" and ".." is resolved by its text; the empty path names nothing; a failure names the path as given. `ReadDir` is sorted by name, `Stat` gives the last element, the size (0 for a directory), `Dir` and `Perm`, a missing directory on the way is `NotFound` (`MkdirAll`'s row has none: there it is `Failed` "no such file or directory" for the empty path only, since `MkdirAll` makes each missing parent), and a file on the way is `Failed` "not a directory". A new file has mode `Shared()` and a new directory `SharedDir()`, as the host gives under umask 022; `WriteFile` and `AppendFile` keep the mode of a file that is there (law `fstest.ModeIsKept`), and `AppendFile` adds at the end (law `fstest.AppendConcatenates`). An append puts its text in front of the file's parts, a list no write changes (a union, not a slice, so a file entry holds no slice and a read's result leaves the `Ref` with no copy), and the next read or `Stat` joins the parts once and keeps the join, so a log of n appends and a read copy O(n) bytes, as a host file's appends do, where adding each text to the whole file would copy the file again each time (FU-FSTEST-APPEND; eval `TestFSMemoryAppendCopiesLinearBytes` counts the value bytes the evaluator builds at n and 2n appends, never time). `Rename` moves a directory with everything in it and has the host's failures: an existing directory at `to` is `Failed` "file exists" (Go's `os.Rename` refuses it before the host), a directory onto a file "not a directory", a directory into itself "invalid argument", a root "device or resource busy". `Remove` of a directory with entries is "directory not empty", of a root "invalid argument".
18. **Memory agrees with Host.** Lower `TestFSMemoryAgreesWithHost` is a model-based differential test: 150 seeded sequences of 60 operations, every operation of both capabilities and the laws of `fs`, over paths of one to three of three names (some written "./p", the empty path and "."), run on `fs.Host` in a new directory under umask 022 and on `fstest.NewMemory()`. On Linux every result agrees: the value, the failure, its path and its reason; the sequences reach each outcome of each operation that the host gives there (a list in the test), and each law and each Writes contract (`WriteThenRead`, `AppendConcatenates`, `ModeIsKept`) holds on both, in at least 20 cases that are not vacuous. On another host only the laws are held, since its reasons and the order of rename's checks are its own. `ERGO_FS_SEED=n` replays one sequence, and a failure prints its operations and both outputs. Mutants it kills: an unsorted `ReadDir`, a `WriteFile` into a missing directory, a `Rename` over a directory, copies that do not share, a `Remove` of a directory with entries, an `AppendFile` that replaces, a `MkdirAllMode` that drops the mode, a `Rename` that leaves a directory's entries, a `Stat` that drops the mode.
19. **What Memory does not do.** It has no symbolic links. It keeps each mode and never checks one, as Go's `testing/fstest.MapFS` does, so it never fails `Denied`: a test of a refused or failing write wraps it in a provider of its own that fails the operations it chooses and passes on the rest. A path with a doubled separator or a final "/" is cleaned, where Go's `os.MkdirAll` gives "file exists" for some of them. A provider cannot use a capability yet, so a file-backed provider still reads every file when it starts.
20. **Root** (G1f). `fs.Root` reads one directory tree and nothing outside it: a path is relative to the root, with "/" between its elements. Each operation resolves the path element by element from the root, as Go's `os.Root` does: a link inside the root is followed with its target spliced in where it was, ".." removes the element before it, and any step past the root is `Escapes`, whatever comes after it: an absolute path, a ".." with nothing before it, a link whose target is absolute or leads past the root (also one that comes back in, such as `../root/a`). The empty path is `Failed` "empty path"; more than eight links in one resolution are `Failed` "too many levels of symbolic links". `Stat` follows links, and its `Name` is `filepath.Base` of the path as given. The law `LeavingEscapes` states the part no tree can change: a path that starts with ".." or "/" fails `Escapes` for every operation, so a provider that answers `NotFound` there looked outside. Confinement is not a lexical check: a cleaned path inside the root can name a link that leads out, and only the resolution can refuse it. `Name` is the directory as `OpenRoot` was given it (Go's `os.Root.Name`); `fstest.Tree` is `"."` and a `Recorder` `"recorder"`. `Name` also keeps the type apart from `Files` (wf10 defects D4): without it, the reads of `Files` have Root's form with fewer failures, so by [interface assignability](types.md#interface-assignability) every `Files` provider was a Root. `fs.Host` then passed where a Root is asked for (`http.Files` served a file outside its tree through a link), and the law runner ran `LeavingEscapes` on every program's `Files` test provider and refuted it.
21. **HostRoot.** `fs.OpenRoot(dir)` needs `Ambient`, and opens `dir` once with `os.OpenRoot`. The acquiring frame closes the handle on every exit, after children started later have drained; callers must not retain or return it beyond that frame.
    `fs.OpenTestRoot(fs.TestRootFolder(), dir)` needs `TestAmbient` and opens `dir` relative to the case folder (`"."` opens the folder itself). `TestRootFolder()` gives a sealed value naming the case folder, not a path the caller supplies. The test constructor resolves below that folder's descriptor. An absolute `dir`, a `..` past the case folder, or a link leading out fails `Denied` with `dir` as written. Other acquisition failures map as for `OpenRoot`. The case closes these descriptors before it removes its folder, including when its context fails to build; callers must not retain the handles beyond the case.
    Both constructors return the runtime's `*v1.HostRoot`, sealed by its private `hostRooted` operation, and register cleanup in the same acquisition step. Renaming or replacing `dir` does not change the opened directory. Each operation resolves below its descriptor (`openat` with no following of links, each link read by Go and spliced in), so a link replaced during resolution cannot lead out: there is no canonicalize-then-read. Go's `errPathEscapes` ("path escapes from parent") maps to `Escapes` for operations after acquisition; every other failure maps as `fs.Host`'s does. Its Go methods take the caller's task (each marked `//ergo:wait none` and taking the hidden `*rt.Task`), so `*v1.HostRoot` is also the program's lowered `fs.Root` interface with no adapter. Runtime `TestFSRootKeepsOpenedDirectory` checks all operations after a rename and replacement; `TestFSRootClosesAtScopeEnd` checks closure on normal, failure, defect and stop exits.
22. **Tree and Recorder.** `fstest.NewTree(nodes)` is a read-only Root in memory: files, directories (each directory above a node is made too) and links, which it resolves by the walk of rule 20 written out after Go's `doInRoot`. It never fails `Denied`, a file has mode `Shared()` and a directory `SharedDir()`, and a directory's `Size` is 0. Nothing is outside a Tree. `fstest.NewRecorder(inner)` passes every operation to `inner` and keeps each path it is asked for, in order (`Paths`), so a test sees what a reader of a Root asks. The HTTP property uses it; http's law uses a private recorder in its law-only file, so a production http import has no fstest dependency (`TestHTTPHasNoTestProviderDependency`).
23. **Tree agrees with HostRoot.** Lower `TestFSRootTreeAgreesWithHost` runs 120 seeded trees (links inside, out by a relative and by an absolute target, past the root and back in, through ".", "..", chains and loops) with 40 operations each on generated paths, on `fs.OpenRoot` over the tree written to disk beside a file outside the root, and on `fstest.NewTree` of the same nodes. On Linux every line agrees (value, failure, its path and its reason); on every host no output holds the outside file's text and `LeavingEscapes` holds on both; the trees reach each outcome of a list in the test, including an `Escapes` of a path with no ".." and no leading "/", which only a link gives. `ERGO_FS_SEED=n` replays one tree. Mutants it kills (lower mutant ledger, `root-*`): a host read of `filepath.Join(dir, path)`, a host `Escapes` mapped to `Failed`, a Tree that follows an absolute target, a Tree whose ".." past the root is `NotFound`.
24. **Trees and links** (CAP-FS2, the self-host driver's census). `Files` gains `Lstat(path)`, `Stat` of the path itself: a symbolic link is described, not followed, and is never `Dir` (law `LstatDirIsStatDir`); a provider with no links, `fstest.Memory` and `embed.FS`, gives its `Stat`. `Writes` gains `Mkdir(path, mode)`, one directory with exactly `mode`, made only when nothing is at `path`: anything there, a link included, fails the new `Exists { Path }` (law `MkdirThenExists`; the body `v1.FSMkdir` is `os.Mkdir` then `os.Chmod`). Every other tree operation is a function over `Files` and `Writes` (decision O8: one authority; a function needs no operation of a provider, so a program's own provider has it too): `Walk(path)` is `filepath.WalkDir`'s order (path first as given, then each directory's sorted entries, depth first, `Join(parent, name)`), reading with `Lstat` so a link is listed and not followed; `RemoveAll(path)` is `os.RemoveAll`'s contract (nothing there, no directory above it and the empty path are no failure; a path ending in `.` is `Failed "invalid argument"`), removing first and reading a directory only when the removal fails, so it never goes through a link; `MkdirTemp(dir, prefix)` is `Join(dir, prefix + n)` for the least `n` from 1 whose `Mkdir` does not fail `Exists`, with mode `PrivateDir()`: not random as Go's, so every provider makes the same tree, and `Mkdir`'s exclusive make keeps two callers apart; `Glob(root, pattern)` is the paths of `Walk(root)` that `filepath.Match` accepts. `Scratch` is a temporary directory as a resource (`NewScratch(files, writes, dir, prefix)`), removed with what is in it when its owner's scope ends (resources R5); it keeps its providers, so its release needs none. The links are functions of `Host` only, as `Link` is (G6): `IsLink`, `Readlink` (the target as written), `Symlink(h, target, path)` and `Resolve` (Go's `EvalSymlinks`; a test's `Host` gives the path below its folder, or `Denied` when it leads out). Lower `TestFSMemoryAgreesWithHost` runs `Lstat`, `Mkdir`, `Walk`, `RemoveAll` and `MkdirTemp` in its sequences on both providers; `TestFSTreesKeepLinks` walks and removes trees with links on the test host (mutants `fs2-walk-follows`, `fs2-removeall-reads-first`), and runtime `TestFSSymlinkReadsBack` holds `Readlink` and `IsLink` (`fs2-readlink-cleans`, `fs2-islink-follows`). `fstest`'s laws `WalkVisitsEachOnce`, `RemoveAllTouchesOnlyPath`, `MkdirTempIsNew` and `ScratchIsRemoved` state the functions over a `Memory`. **A breaking change** as rule 16's: a program's own provider of `Files` adds `Lstat` and of `Writes` adds `Mkdir`. *Prior art:* Go's `filepath.WalkDir`, `os.RemoveAll`, `os.MkdirTemp`, `os.Lstat`; Effect Platform's `FileSystem` has `remove(path, { recursive })`, `makeTempDirectory`, `makeTempDirectoryScoped` (a scoped resource, as `Scratch` is), `readLink`, `symlink` and `realPath` in its one service; Gleam's `simplifile` has `delete` (recursive), `get_files` and `is_symlink`; Elixir's `File.rm_rf/1`, `Path.wildcard/2` and `File.read_link/1`; ReScript binds Node's `fs.rm({ recursive })`, `fs.mkdtemp` and `fs.readlink`.
25. **Bounded maps** (CAP-FILE, the self-host driver's census: `internal/source` opens a source file once). `MapBounded(h, path, limit)` maps the regular file at `path` of at most `limit` bytes. It opens the file once, read-only and non-blocking (a pipe put at `path` never blocks the open), and checks the open descriptor, not the path: any file that is not regular is `NotRegular{Path}` and a file over `limit` is `TooLarge{Path, Size, Limit}`, both before a byte is read, so a file swapped in after a `Stat` of the path is never read. `MapFile` instead reads a non-regular file into memory and has no limit. The `Mapped` keeps the identity of the file it read and the absolute path the host gave for its descriptor (`/proc/self/fd` on Linux, `F_GETPATH` on macOS, else the path with its links resolved): `m.Size()` is its length and `m.PhysicalPath()` that path (`""` for a `Mapped` of `MapFile` or `MapText`). `SameAs(h, m, path)` is whether `path`, its links followed as `SameFile` reads one, leads to that file: false once another file is renamed over it, while `m`'s text stays what was read. Both are functions of `Host` alone, as `MapFile` is (G6); a store in memory has no descriptors. Package `fs/mapped` states their laws as the operation type `Maps` (`..Files`, `..Writes`, `MapBounded`, `SameAs`), with its one provider `mapped.Host` over an `fs.Host`, run under `OnTestHost` in a folder of the case's own: `BoundedIsTheText`, `OverLimitIsTooLarge`, `DirIsNotRegular`, `ReplacedIsNotSame`.


*Prior art:* Gleam's `simplifile.read(path) -> Result(String, FileError)` gives a closed enum of POSIX reasons (`Enoent`, `Eacces`, …) plus `Unknown`; Effect Platform's `FileSystem` service gives `readFileString` and a `SystemError` whose `reason` is `NotFound`, `PermissionDenied` and others, bound by `NodeContext.layer`; Elixir's `File.read/1` returns `{:ok, binary} | {:error, posix}`; Go's `os.ReadFile` and `os.ReadDir` with `errors.Is(err, fs.ErrNotExist)`; ReScript binds Node's `readFileSync` as an `external` that throws. `NotFound | Denied | Failed` is their common core: the two reasons a program acts on, and one that carries the host's text. Like Effect and Roc, and unlike Gleam, Elixir and ReScript, the file system is a capability in the row, so a test swaps it in a context. Like Roc's platform and unlike Effect's layer, which any module may provide locally, Ergo keeps the disk out of reach of code that neither holds `Ambient` nor was given a provider (rule 6): Pony's `AmbientAuth` and Austral's `RootCapability` are the model.

*Prior art (writes):* Gleam's `simplifile.write(to:, contents:)`, Elixir's `File.write/3` (Erlang `file:write_file`), ReScript's binding of Node's `writeFileSync` and Go's `os.WriteFile(name, data, 0666)` all truncate and write the file in place, with mode 0666 less the umask for a new file: a reader can see a part, and a failure can leave a part. Go's `renameio` and Rust's `tempfile::NamedTempFile::persist` write a file in the same directory and rename it over the target, as `fs.Host` does; `renameio` also keeps the old mode only when asked. `MkdirAll` is Go's `os.MkdirAll`, Elixir's `File.mkdir_p/1` and Gleam's `simplifile.create_directory_all`. Effect Platform's `FileSystem` has `writeFileString` and `makeDirectory({ recursive: true })` in the one service; Ergo splits writes from reads, so a reader's row does not hold the authority to write.

*Prior art (modes, appends, renames):* Effect Platform's `FileSystem` has `writeFile(path, data, { mode, flag: "a" })`, `chmod`, `rename` and `remove` in its one service, with the mode as a number; Gleam's `simplifile` has `append`, `rename`, `delete` and `set_permissions_octal`, and also `set_permissions(FilePermissions(user:, group:, other:))` over sets of `Read | Write | Execute`, the shape `Mode` takes; Elixir's `File.write(path, data, [:append])`, `File.chmod/2` (an integer), `File.rename/2` and `File.rm/1`; Go's `os.OpenFile(O_APPEND)`, `os.Chmod`, `os.Rename` and `os.Remove`, and `renameio.WriteFile(name, data, perm)`, which applies the umask unless asked to ignore it. ReScript binds Node's `fs`, whose modes are numbers.

### io

Std `io` is the host process's standard streams (std-align S1b; wave std-1 G3 and G3b). It follows Effect's `Stdio` and `Terminal` and Go's `os.Stdout`, `os.Stderr` and `os.Stdin`. `Stdout`, `Stderr` and `Stdin` are three capabilities, so code that only writes errors shows only `io.Stderr` in its row.

```go
import io

func echo() needs io.Stdout | io.Stderr | io.Stdin {
	io.Stderr.Eprintln("reading")
	for line in io.Stdin.Lines() {
		io.Stdout.Println(line)
	}
}

func main() needs Ambient {
	with context { io.Stdout: io.NewHost(), io.Stderr: io.NewErrors(), io.Stdin: io.NewStdin() } { echo() }
}
```

| Declaration | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `type Stdout { Print(text string), Println(line string), IsTerminal() bool }` | Standard output. |
| `type Stderr { Eprint(text string), Eprintln(line string), IsTerminal() bool }` | Standard error. |
| `type Stdin { ReadAll() string fails ReadFailed, Lines() Seq[string], IsTerminal() bool }` | Standard input. |
| `type ReadFailed { Reason string }` | The failure of a read. |
| `type Host { live hostOnly }`; `func NewHost() Host needs Ambient` | The host's `Stdout` provider. |
| `type In { live hostOnly }`; `func NewStdin() In needs Ambient` | The host's `Stdin` provider. |
| `type Errors { live hostOnly }`; `func NewErrors() Errors needs Ambient` | The host's `Stderr` provider. |
| `type Recorder`; `func NewRecorder() Recorder`; `Print`, `Println`, `Eprint`, `Eprintln`, `IsTerminal` (false), `Text() string` | A `Stdout` and `Stderr` provider for tests that keeps what is written to both, in order. |
| `type Input { Text string }`; `func NewInput(text string) Input`; `ReadAll`, `Lines`, `IsTerminal` (false) | A `Stdin` provider for tests. `NewInput` is `Stdin`'s builder: a generated record that holds a `Stdin` reads an `Input` ([runtime.md](runtime.md), Tests 6, Capabilities). |
| `law PrintsConcatenate(a string, b string)`, `law LinesSplitInput(s string)` | The laws of the recording providers ([Std laws](#std-laws)). |
| `type Source { Read() Option[string] fails ReadFailed }` | An input in chunks, in order (rule 10). |
| `let DefaultChunk = 65536` | The chunk size when a size is not positive. |
| `type Memory`; `func CutAt(text string, cuts []int) Memory`; `func Chunked(text string, size int) Memory`; `Read` | A `Source` over a text in memory: cut at offsets, or into chunks of one size. |
| `func File(host fs.Host, path string, size int) Memory fails fs.NotFound \| fs.Denied \| fs.Failed` | A host file, mapped read-only (`fs.MapFile`) and copied out (`fs.Clone`), as `Chunked` chunks. |
| `func Read(_ In) Option[string] fails ReadFailed` | `In` is also the host's `Source`: standard input through the shared reader. |
| `law ChunksJoinToInput(text string, cuts []int, size int)` | The law of the memory provider ([Std laws](#std-laws)). |

1. `Print` and `Eprint` write their text as it is; `Println` and `Eprintln` write the line and a line feed in one write. Each write is kept whole, and writes of `Console` and of `io.Stdout` go through one buffer of the process, so they appear in program order; when standard output reaches the host is rule 10.
2. `Lines` gives each line without its line feed. A last line with no line feed is a line, an empty input has no lines, and a carriage return stays in its line, so the lines joined by line feeds, plus the final line feed if any, are the input (`LinesSplitInput`). A line has no length limit. A `Seq` has no failure row, so a read failure inside `Lines` is a defect; `ReadAll` returns it as `ReadFailed`. A `break` stops the read; the rest of the input stays unread. Every `Stdin` operation of `Host` reads through one buffered reader of the process, so the next `Lines` or `ReadAll` starts at the line after the break (`TestIOStdinRest`, `TestIOStdinShared`).
3. A read of standard input is an interruption point of the reading task (batch 12 C3; [decisions.md](../decisions.md), 2026-11-08, O15). A stop of the task that waits in `Lines` or `ReadAll` of `Host` ends the wait and raises the interruption; no input is lost. One read of the host's input runs at a time, on a goroutine of its own, and the line it lands after the stop stays for the next `Lines` or `ReadAll`. A `ReadAll` that a stop ends puts back what it had read. A masked task's read runs to its end. So rule 2's "the next `Lines` starts at the line after the break" holds after a stop too (`TestIOStdinStopModel`, runtime/v1; lower `TestStdinStopRuns`). The task is the iterating task for `Lines` (Hidden arguments, rule 5), and each body is marked `//ergo:wait caller`. A read of a file is still an uninterruptible host wait.
4. `IsTerminal` of each stream reports whether that stream is a terminal: whether its terminal state can be read (`isatty`; the `TCGETS` or `TIOCGETA` ioctl), so `/dev/null`, a character device, is not one. Each host stream has its own provider, so each answers for itself: `prog < file` has a terminal on standard output and a file on standard input. So `Host` is not the `Stdin` provider; `io.Stdin: io.NewStdin()` is a check error at the binding (CU2).
5. The Go bodies are `v1.IOPrint`, `v1.IOEprint`, `v1.IOIsTerminal`, `v1.IOStdinIsTerminal`, `v1.IOStderrIsTerminal`, `v1.IOReadAll`, `v1.IOLines` and `v1.IORead` (host.go). Every read of standard input, `term`'s keys included, goes through one byte buffer of the process, so a key read after a line read loses no byte. `ReadFailed` is the `runtime/v1` struct `v1.ReadFailed` (Types 4); its name is not `Failed` because `v1.Failed` is `fs.Failed`. `ReadAll` builds its text in one `strings.Builder` that each read lands in directly and hands it over with no copy (IO1). The Builder grows by doubling, and to the size of a regular file once, as `os.ReadFile` does, so the text of a file costs one allocation of its size, and a pipe's at most about 2.5 times it, where Go's `io.ReadAll` and a `string` conversion cost twice (`TestIOReadAllBytes`). A read takes at most 64 KiB.
6. The capabilities are open: a program implements them, and a test binds `io.NewRecorder()` or `io.Input{Text: …}` in the same place as `main` binds the host. `Stdout`, `Stderr` and `Stdin` are exempt from the law rule, each with its reason: the first two have no read, and a read of the host's input takes what it reads.
7. A `Recorder` keeps its writes in a `sync.Ref[[]string]` ([Sync and STM](#sync-and-stm) 1), so every copy of it keeps the same writes, and tasks may write to one `Recorder` at once: each write is kept whole, in the order the writes took the `Ref` (lower `TestIORecorderBehavesAsBefore`). No std code keeps state through a `Map` or slice that two bindings share.
8. `Stderr`'s operations are `Eprint` and `Eprintln`, names that `Stdout` does not have, so a `Stdout` provider is not a `Stderr` provider and the reverse: `io.Stderr: io.NewHost()` and `io.Stdout: io.NewErrors()` are check errors at the binding (`TestIOStreamsAreDistinct`). A program binds `Errors` to `io.Stderr`. The names follow Rust's `eprint!` and `eprintln!` and Gleam's `print_error`.
9. A std package may import another std package: `io` imports `fs`, `slices`, `strings` and `sync`. The std importer checks each package once, and the std has no import cycle (`TestStdImportsAreAcyclic`).
10. **Source** (corpus-wave SJ0). A `Source` gives an input chunk by chunk, so a reader that must not hold its whole input (std json's `Stream`) asks for the next chunk when it has used the one before; its purpose is Go's `io.Reader` and Effect's `Stream` of byte chunks: the reader, not the producer, decides when more is read. `Read` gives a chunk that is not empty, or `None` at the end, and `None` again after it. A chunk is a `string`: Ergo's string holds bytes and a chunk never changes after `Read` gives it, so a reader may keep it and no borrow is needed (Ergo has no `[]byte` to `string` conversion yet). A chunk may end anywhere, inside a UTF-8 sequence or a token; the only promise is `ChunksJoinToInput`: the chunks of a `Memory` are not empty, end at the cut offsets `CutAt` keeps (inside the text, growing, in the given order; the others are passed over) or at `Chunked`'s size, and join to the text. `Memory` keeps its place in a `sync.Ref`, as std codec's `Replay` does, so `Source` needs no provider cell and stays open: any module implements it. The host providers: `In`, the host's `Stdin` provider, is also its `Source`, and reads standard input through the one shared reader of rule 2 (`v1.IORead`: what the reader holds or the next host read lands, up to `DefaultChunk` bytes, with no wait to fill it; a test binds a `Memory` where `main` binds `io.NewStdin()`; a stop ends the wait as rule 3 says; `TestIOStdinShared`, `TestIOStdinStopModel`, lower `TestIOStdinRest`); `File` maps a host file read-only, so its chunks are slices of the page cache and a reader that drops them reads a file larger than memory in a flat heap; an http body, which std http gives whole (`http.Buffered`'s `Text`), is `Chunked` over that text. `v1.IORead` is a host-tier body, as `v1.IOReadAll` is; it is the only one SJ0 adds (owner answer, 2026-11-12), and std json's readers over a `Source` add none. Effect's `Stream` takes an `Effect` per pull and a `Scope` for the file handle (W14, W11); here a pull is a call, and no handle stays open.
11. **Standard output is buffered when it is not a terminal** (SB8; owner, 2026-11-12, std-better Q2 "Buffered when piped"). On a terminal each write of `Console` and of `Host` reaches the host as it comes. Anywhere else, a pipe, a file or `/dev/null`, the writes are kept in one 4 KiB buffer of the process (`v1.writeStdout`, stdout.go) and reach the host in one write when the next would pass 4 KiB (a write of 4 KiB or more goes alone, after what is kept), and at each flush point: before every write to standard error (`Errors`, main's report of a failure, a defect or an interruption, `LogFailures`, the trace file's error), before a read of standard input that waits on the host (`Lines`, `ReadAll`, `term`'s keys; a line the process has already read waits on nothing), before a child process starts (`exec.Start`; `exec.Run` waits on its child, which flushes), when any task reaches a wait of the runtime (`rt.Task.Done`: a sleep, a queue's `Pop` or `Push` that waits, a join, an actor's ask, a child's exit, the network, a timeout's race), and when main ends, however it ends. So the merged stream `prog 2>&1` is in program order, a prompt shows before the read that waits for its answer, a child's output follows what was printed before it started, and a long-running program (a server's log under a supervisor, `prog | tee`) shows what it wrote as soon as it waits, with no timer; only a loop that never waits holds up to 4 KiB until it does. A `Pop` that finds a value, or a line already read, waits on nothing and flushes nothing, and a flush point with nothing held is one atomic load (receipt: `sb8q`'s loop of a push, a pop and a line every 16th step, `scripts/opcount`: 533 instructions a step before, 511 after, within the spread). Lost: the buffer of a process that a signal kills with no exit (`SIGKILL`), or that a Go panic outside every task ends. A program whose `main` needs `go.Foreign` may write the host's stdout through Go's own `os.Stdout`, which the buffer cannot see, so its entry calls `v1.StdoutDirect` and its standard output stays direct; so does a test build, whose harness reports through Go (lower `TestEntryKeepsStdoutDirect`). runtime/v1 `TestStdoutFlushModel` runs generated sequences of writes, standard-error writes, standard-input reads (one whose second line is already read), child processes (one that writes to the inherited stdout), sleeps, a `Pop` that waits and a `Push` and `Pop` that do not, ended by a success, a failure or a defect, against a model of the merged stream and of the writes that reached the host, on a terminal and off it. Receipt (SB8/receipts: 20,000 lines through `Console` and `Host` into a pipe, then a read of standard input, counted with `strace -c`): 20,001 write calls before, 82 after, the same 327,787 bytes. Go's `os.Stdout` is unbuffered, so a Go program that wants this wraps it in a `bufio.Writer` and flushes by hand at each of these points; Rust's `stdout` is line-buffered always, and C's stdio buffers standard output fully when it is not a terminal, as here, but writes standard error beside it with no flush.

*Prior art:* Effect's `Stdio` gives `stdout` and `stderr` sinks and a `stdin` stream in one service, and `Terminal` gives `readLine` and `display`; Go writes `os.Stdout` directly and reads lines with `bufio.Scanner`, which has a 64 KiB line limit; Gleam's `gleam/io` has `print`, `println` and `print_error`, and `stdin` reading is a separate package; Elixir's `IO.puts(:stderr, …)` and `IO.stream(:stdio, :line)` keep the line feed; Roc's basic-cli gives `Stdout`, `Stderr` and `Stdin` as platform effects. Ergo takes Roc's three capabilities and Effect's guarantee that a test replaces each one.

### term

Std `term` is the terminal of standard input (CU2): a mode for the length of a block, one key at a time, and the window's size. It is what a prompt needs (`prompt`). Effect's `Terminal` service has `readInput` (a stream of key events) and `display`; its purpose is to read keys from a person. Ergo takes that purpose: a key is a union, matched exhaustively, and a mode is a resource with a block form, so no program can leave the terminal raw. It does not take the `Terminal` service tag (W4), the `Effect` value per read (W14) or the `Scope` value a raw mode needs in Effect (W11).

```go
import term

func secret() string fails term.NotTerminal | io.ReadFailed needs term.Terminal {
	term.Raw() {
		let mut text = ""
		for _ in 0..1000 {
			match try term.Terminal.ReadKey() {
			case term.Char as c: text = text + c.Text
			case term.Return: return text
			case _:
			}
		}
		text
	}
}
```

| Declaration | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `type Terminal { SetMode(m Mode) fails NotTerminal, Restore(), ReadKey() Key fails io.ReadFailed, Size() Window fails NotTerminal }` | The terminal of standard input. |
| `func Raw[T, E, R](body func() T fails E needs R) T fails E \| NotTerminal needs R \| Terminal` | `body` with the terminal in `RawMode`; the state before is restored on every exit of `body`. |
| `func NoEcho[T, E, R](…)` | The same, with `NoEchoMode`. |
| `type Mode = RawMode{} \| NoEchoMode{}` | A mode. Raw reads each key as it comes, with no echo, no line editing and no signal keys; output processing stays on. NoEcho keeps line editing and hides what is typed. |
| `type Key = Char{Text string} \| Return{} \| Backspace{} \| Tab{} \| Escape{} \| Up{} \| Down{} \| Left{} \| Right{} \| Home{} \| End{} \| Insert{} \| Delete{} \| PageUp{} \| PageDown{} \| F{N int} \| Chord{Mods Mods, Name string} \| Paste{Text string} \| Wheel{Up bool, Col int, Row int} \| Resized{Window Window} \| Interrupt{} \| EndOfInput{} \| Other{Bytes string}` | A key, or what else the key stream carries (UC1). `Interrupt` is Ctrl-C; `EndOfInput` is Ctrl-D or the end of the input. `F` is F1 to F12. A key with a modifier is a `Chord` of its name; `Paste` is a bracketed paste; `Wheel` a wheel turn at a cell; `Resized` the window's new size. |
| `type Mods { Shift bool, Ctrl bool, Alt bool, Meta bool }`; `func KeyName(k Key) Option[string]` | A chord's modifiers, and a key's name as `ui.KeyPress` names it (`"a"`, `"Enter"`, `"ArrowUp"`, `"F5"`). |
| `type Window { Cols int, Rows int }`; `type NotTerminal {}` | The size, and the failure when the stream is not a terminal. |
| `type Host`; `func NewHost() Host needs Ambient` | The host's provider. |
| `type Script`; `func NewScript(keys []Key, window Window) Script`; `Modes() []Mode` | A provider for tests: it plays `keys`, then `EndOfInput`, and keeps the modes set and not restored; its `Size` is `window` until it plays a `Resized`, then that one's window. |
| `func Cut(b string, end bool) int`; `func Decode(token string) Key` | The length of the first key's bytes at the front of `b` (-1: more may come), and the key of one key's bytes. |
| `type Decoder`; `func NewDecoder() Decoder`; `func Feed(d *Decoder, chunk string) []Key`; `func Flush(d *Decoder) []Key` | The keys of a terminal's bytes as they come in chunks (a pty, a recording), and of what is held at the end. |

1. **A mode is a resource.** `Raw` and `NoEcho` call `SetMode`, then register `Restore` with `scope.OnExit`, so the restore runs on every exit of the block: a value, a failure, a defect and a stop. The host keeps a stack: each `SetMode` saves the state before it, and `Restore` sets back the latest saved state. A program calls the block forms; `SetMode` and `Restore` are the provider's halves.
2. **Keys.** `ReadKey` reads through `io`'s one byte buffer of standard input: it peeks at the buffer until it starts with a whole key (`Cut`), takes that key's bytes alone and names them (`Decode`), so the bytes after the key stay for the next read, a line read's included. `Cut` and `Decode` are the only splitter and namer, in Ergo (keys.ergo); the runtime only peeks and drops (UC1). A key is one byte, one UTF-8 rune, an escape sequence (CSI: ESC `[`, parameters, a final byte; SS3: ESC `O` and a byte), a bracketed paste (ESC `[200~` … ESC `[201~`), or ESC and a key (Alt). An ESC with nothing after it in the bytes read is `Escape`, since a terminal writes a key in one write. A carriage return and a line feed are `Return`; delete and Ctrl-H are `Backspace`; Ctrl and a letter (bytes 1 to 26, but Tab, Return, Ctrl-C, Ctrl-D and Ctrl-H) is that letter's `Chord`. xterm's modifier parameter (`ESC [1;5A`, `ESC [3;5~`: one more than a bit set of Shift 1, Alt 2, Ctrl 4, Meta 8) and kitty's keyboard protocol (`ESC [ code ; m u`, alternates after `:` ignored) make a `Chord`; an SGR mouse report of button 64 or 65 (`ESC [<64;x;yM`, modifier bits ignored) is `Wheel`; any other sequence is `Other`. A change of the window's size while `ReadKey` waits (SIGWINCH) is `Resized` with the size then. A stop of the reading task ends the wait; the bytes stay for the next read.
3. **Laws.** `Terminal` is law-exempt: no law over its operations can read the host's state. `RawRestores`, `NestedRestoresInOrder`, `ScriptPlaysKeys`, `DecodeNamesControls` and `ScriptResizes` hold the scope and the decoding over `Script`; `DecodeIsTheTable` holds the key table (xterm, kitty and Linux console sequences) whole and fed in drawn chunks, and `CutTakesOneKey` that a cut never runs past its bytes. runtime/v1 `TestIOStdinStopModel` holds that a peek and a drop take one item and leave the rest. Lower `TestTermModesRestore` is the model of the host: a built program on a pseudo-terminal runs generated sequences of raw, no-echo, read, leave and stop; at each read the terminal's state is the model's, and after the program the state is the state before it.
4. **Go bodies.** `v1.TermEnterRaw`, `v1.TermEnterNoEcho`, `v1.TermLeave`, `v1.TermPeek`, `v1.TermDrop` and `v1.TermGetSize` (term.go). The ioctls go through one `unsafe` function, `ioctl` in term_unix.go, with its invariant in its comment: the FFI edge of the 2026-11-02 rule, since Go's standard library has no safe form.

*Prior art:* Go's `golang.org/x/term` (`MakeRaw`, `Restore`, `GetSize`) does the same ioctls and leaves the restore to the caller's `defer`; Elixir's `IO.gets` reads a line, and OTP 26 adds a raw shell mode (`shell:start_interactive({noshell, raw})`) that the caller sets back; Gleam's standard library has no terminal module (`gleam_erlang` neither); Roc basic-cli's `Tty` has `enable_raw_mode!` and `disable_raw_mode!` with no pairing. Each lowers to its host's call with no scope; Ergo's block form lowers to one `OnExit` registration on the frame scope.

### prompt

Std `prompt` asks a person at a terminal for a line of text, a secret, one choice, several choices or a yes (CU3). Effect's cli `Prompt` (`text`, `password`, `select`, `multiSelect`, `confirm`) is the reference for intent: ask a person for a typed answer, and let a program that runs with no person see that it cannot. Ergo takes that purpose. It does not take Effect's `Prompt` values run by a runtime (W14), the `Terminal` service tag (W4) or the tagged `QuitException` (W5). Each prompt is a machine declaration over `term.Key` with a pure frame, and the function that runs it has its failures in its row.

| Declaration | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `func Line(message string, fallback string) string` | A line of text; Return on an empty line answers `fallback`. |
| `func Secret(message string) string` | A line of text whose frame shows one `*` per character. |
| `func Select(message string, options []string) int` | One option's index. |
| `func MultiSelect(message string, options []string) []int` | The marked options' indexes, ascending; a space marks or unmarks. |
| `func Confirm(message string, fallback bool) bool` | y or n; Return answers `fallback`. |
| `type NotInteractive {}`, `type Cancelled {}` | Every function above `fails NotInteractive \| Cancelled \| io.ReadFailed needs term.Terminal \| io.Stderr`. |
| `type Typer`, `Picker`, `Marker`, `Asker` (machines); `TypingStep`, `PickingStep`, `MarkingStep`, `AskingStep`; `TypingFrame`, `PickingFrame`, `MarkingFrame`, `AskingFrame` | The machines, their steps and their frames. Each machine's input is its question (`Text`, `Choice`, `Question`), and its output is `Option` of the answer: `None` is a cancel. |
| `func Ask[S, O, F, R, W](m Machine[S, term.Key, Option[O], F, R, W], frame func(s S) string) O` | Runs a prompt machine: raw mode (`term.Raw`), one key at a time, each new state's frame drawn over the last on `io.Stderr` (`Redraw`). |
| `func KeyOf(code int) term.Key` | A key of a code, for laws and properties over generated keys. |

1. **Not interactive is a failure.** On a standard input that is not a terminal, the host's `term.Raw` fails `term.NotTerminal`, and `Ask` fails `NotInteractive`. A caller decides what a run with no person does. Escape, Ctrl-C and Ctrl-D fail `Cancelled`.
2. **Frames are pure.** A frame is a function of the state. `Ask` writes the first frame, then for each new state a carriage return, a cursor-up per line of the last frame, an erase to the end of the screen and the new frame. Frames go to standard error, so standard output stays the program's answer.
3. **Laws and models.** `SelectStaysInRange`, `SecretFrameHidesText`, `SecretFramesHideTyping`, `MarkedAnswerIsSorted` and `ReturnAnswersTheDefault` are in [Std laws](#std-laws). Lower `TestPromptLaws` runs them. It also holds each machine to a reference model of its own over generated keys, and holds `Ask` on a `term.Script`: the mode is restored, a secret's drawn text equals that of as many x's, a terminal that is not one fails `NotInteractive`, and Escape fails `Cancelled`.
4. A std package may declare a machine: it lowers as a user package's machine does (lower `std.go`, `naming.go`).

*Prior art:* Charm's `huh` (Go) runs each field as a Bubble Tea model, a state machine with a view; Elixir's `Owl.IO` (`select`, `multiselect`, `confirm`) reads a line per answer; Gleam's `survey` reads lines with `erlang:get_line`; ReScript uses host libraries (Node's `readline`). Each lowers to its host's reads. Ergo's prompt lowers to a Go loop over one step function, with no runtime value per key.

### flag

Std `flag` reads a command line (std-align S1d). It follows Go's `flag` for the syntax, the names and the help layout, and Effect's `@effect/cli` for typed commands, required flags, choices, positional arguments and the closed union of failures. Two rules follow `@effect/cli` and not Go (owner, 2026-11-06, Q7): a flag may follow a positional argument until a bare `--`, and help goes to standard output with status 0. It is Ergo over `strings`, `strconv` and `time`, with no runtime body.

```go
import flag
import io
import os

let Json = flag.Bool("json", false, "print JSON")
let Sort = flag.Choice("sort", "date", []string{"date", "name"}, "the order")
let Files = flag.Remaining("files", "the files")

func run(args []string) fails os.Exit needs Console | io.Stdout {
	let set = flag.Set{Name: "list", Usage: "", Flags: []flag.Flag{Json, Sort}, Args: []flag.ArgSpec{Files}}
	let v = flag.Parse(set, args) catch {
	case err:
		try flag.Exit(err, flag.UsageText(set))
		return
	}
	Console.Println("\(Json.Get(v)) \(Sort.Get(v)) \(len(flag.Rest(v)))")
}
```

| Declaration | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `type StringFlag`, `IntFlag`, `BoolFlag`, `ChoiceFlag`, `DurationFlag` (unexported fields); `type Flag = StringFlag \| IntFlag \| BoolFlag \| ChoiceFlag \| DurationFlag` | Flag handles; only package `flag` builds one. |
| `String(name, def, usage string) StringFlag`, `Int(name string, def int, usage string) IntFlag`, `Bool(name string, def bool, usage string) BoolFlag`, `Duration(name string, def duration, usage string) DurationFlag` | Go's constructors, in Go's argument order. A `Duration` value is read by `time.ParseDuration` (std-duration); `Defaults` names its kind `duration` and writes a default that is not `0ns` with `time.DurationText`, as Go's `PrintDefaults` does. |
| `RequiredString(name, usage string) StringFlag`, `RequiredInt(name, usage string) IntFlag`, `Choice(name, def string, choices []string, usage string) ChoiceFlag` | Effect's additions: a flag with no default, and a flag whose value is one of `choices`. |
| `type ArgSpec` (unexported fields); `Arg(name, usage string)`, `OptionalArg(name, usage string)`, `Remaining(name, usage string)` | Positional arguments: one that must be given, one that may be absent, and the rest (last). |
| `type Set { Name string, Usage string, Flags []Flag, Args []ArgSpec }` | A command line. |
| `func Parse(s Set, args []string) Values fails Help \| Unknown \| NeedsValue \| Invalid \| Missing \| Extra` | Reads `args`. |
| `Get(f StringFlag, v Values) string`, `Get(f IntFlag, v Values) int`, `Get(f BoolFlag, v Values) bool`, `Get(f ChoiceFlag, v Values) string`, `Get(f DurationFlag, v Values) duration`, `Get(a ArgSpec, v Values) Option[string]`, `Rest(v Values) []string` | Typed reads, called as `f.Get(v)`. |
| `UsageText(s Set) string`, `Defaults(flags []Flag) string` | The help, and Go's `PrintDefaults` text. |
| `type Command[C, E] { Name string, Usage string, Set Set, Build func(v Values) C fails E \| Help \| … \| UnknownCommand, Commands []Command[C, E] }`; `ParseCommand[C, E](name, usage string, commands []Command[C, E], args []string) C fails E \| Help \| … \| UnknownCommand`; `CommandUsage[C, E](name, usage string, commands []Command[C, E]) string` | A command tree that parses into the program's own `C`. A leaf declares `Commands: []Command[C, E]{}` and uses `Set` and `Build`. A group dispatches to `Commands`; `Group[C, E](name, usage, children)` builds one without a dummy `Build`. A group requires a child. A child named `""` is its level's own leaf (gap-group): `tool auth` runs it and `tool auth whoami` runs the child `whoami`, as Cobra's `Run` on a parent and `@effect/cli`'s handler on a command `withSubcommands` do. Flags belong to leaves, not inherited global sets. `Build` may fail with the program's own failures `E`, which reach the caller unchanged at every depth. A command whose `Build` fails nothing of its own is written `Command[C]` ([types.md, Generics](types.md#generics) 7). |
| `WithAlias(f, short)` for each handle kind | Declares one ASCII letter or digit as the short name. It replaces any earlier alias. `-n=5`, `-n 5` and `--number=5` read the same handle. Short names are not clustered. An invalid alias or a spelling declared twice in one set is a declaration defect. |
| `OptionalString(name, usage) OptionalStringFlag`, `OptionalInt(name, usage) OptionalIntFlag` | `Get` returns `Option[string]` or `Option[int]`: absence is `None`, while an explicitly given empty text or zero is `Some`. The last occurrence wins. |
| `f.Repeated()` for each scalar handle kind | A typed handle whose `Get` is a list of all occurrences, in input order. Absence gives an empty list; the scalar's default and required marker are not used. It supports strings, ints, switches, durations and choices. Like overloaded `Get`, it is called by the method spelling, so the receiver type selects the overload. |
| `Root[C, E] { Name string, Usage string, Commands []Command[C, E], Version Option[string] }`; `ParseRoot(root, args)`; `RootUsage(root)` | A declared root. `Some(version)` enables `--version` in the first argument and adds it to root help. It yields `Version{Text: version}` in the failure row, handled beside `Help`; it does not change a leaf's `Build` row. `None` leaves the built-in undeclared. |
| `RenderCommand(path []string, v Values) []string` | Prefixes a leaf's canonical `Render` with its command path. The caller keeps that path with its parsed command data; a generic `Build` need not have an inverse. |
| `type Help { Text string }`, `Unknown { Name string }`, `NeedsValue { Name string }`, `Invalid { Name string, Text string, Reason string }`, `Missing { Name string }`, `Extra { Text string }`, `UnknownCommand { Name string, Known []string, Group []string }`; `type Failure = Help \| … \| UnknownCommand` | The failures. |
| `Describe(f Failure) string`; `Exit(f Failure, usage string) fails os.Exit needs io.Stdout` | A failure's text, and its end: `Help` prints its text to standard output and returns (status 0); any other failure fails `os.Exit{Code: 2}` with its text and `usage`, which the entry writes to standard error. |
| `Render(v Values) []string`, `Equal(a Values, b Values) bool` | A command line that parses to `v` again, and the equality of two results: the same flags and argument specs, and the same value for each read (`Equal` reads each value with its handle, not through `Render`). |

1. **Syntax** (Go's `flag.FlagSet.Parse`). An argument is a flag when it starts with `-` and is longer than `-`. `-name` and `--name` are one flag. A value follows `=` or, for a flag that is not a switch, is the next argument, whatever it is (`-s --` gives `s` the value `--`). A switch takes only `-name` or `-name=value`, with `strconv.ParseBool`'s values. `---x`, `-=x` and `--=x` are `Invalid` with `Name` empty ("bad flag syntax"). `-` alone and the empty string are positional. An `Int` value is read by `strconv.ParseInt(s, 0, 64)`, so `0x10`, `0o17` and `1_000` are read as Go reads them. A scalar or optional handle keeps its last occurrence; a `Repeated` handle keeps every occurrence.
2. **Position** (owner, Q7). Flags and positional arguments may interleave; a bare `--` ends the flags, and every argument after it is positional. A value that starts with `-` is given as `--name=value`. Go stops at the first positional argument; `@effect/cli` does not.
3. **Help.** `-h`, `-help` and `--help` (also with `=value`) fail `Help` with `UsageText(s)` when the set declares no flag of that name, as in Go; a set that declares `h` reads `-h` as its flag. Help wins over every other failure (Q7, as `@effect/cli`'s `Command.runWith` reads its global `--help` from every argument before it reports a parse error): a help word anywhere before `--`, and not the value of a flag that takes one, fails `Help`, whatever comes before or after it (`-zz --help` is help). In `ParseCommand`, when the first argument names no command, a help word anywhere before `--` fails `Help` with `CommandUsage`; after a command name, the command's `Parse` reads it. `Exit` prints `Help`'s text to standard output and returns, so `main` ends with status 0 (Q7; Go prints usage to standard error).
4. **Order of checks.** First help (3). Then the arguments are read in order, and the first failure ends the parse. After the flags, each required flag must be given (`Missing`), then the positional arguments go to `s.Args` in order: an `Arg` takes one or fails `Missing`, an `OptionalArg` takes one if any is left, `Remaining` takes the rest. A positional argument left over fails `Extra`.
5. **Help text.** `UsageText(s)` is `Usage of NAME:` (`Usage:` with no name), `s.Usage` on its own line when it is not empty, `Defaults(s.Flags)`, and an `Arguments:` block in declaration order (`name`, `[name]` or `name...`). `Defaults` is Go's `PrintDefaults` byte for byte for `String`, `Int` and `Bool`: sorted by name, the switch of a one-byte name on one line, a back-quoted word of the usage as the value's name, and a default that is not the zero value; a `Choice`'s value name is its choices joined by `|`, and a required flag ends with ` (required)`. The text of each failure is Go's where Go has one, with one difference: an invalid switch value reads `invalid value "x" for flag -b: parse error`, where Go writes `invalid boolean value`.
6. **Handles.** `Get` reads by canonical handle name, and first checks that the handle is the set's flag of that name: the same kind, cardinality, alias, default, usage and choices. A handle of another set, or of another kind or default under the name of one of the set's flags, is a defect, whether the flag was given or not (`TestFlagForeignHandle`). `Parse` checks that every long or short spelling denotes one handle, before help or input validation. Repeated and optional handles retain the long name in storage; `Render` writes only long names, preserving absence and every repeated value.
7. **Laws** ([Std laws](#std-laws)): `RenderParseRoundTrip`, `IndependentFlagsCommute`, `LastWins`, `DoubleDashEndsFlags`, `ShortAndLongAgree`, `DefaultWhenAbsent`, `UnknownNamesTheFlag`, `HelpNamesEveryFlag`, `HelpWinsInEveryForm` (every help spelling wins, after a flag whose value is any text, `--` too), `EachCommandReaches`, `BuildFailureReaches` (a `Build`'s own failure reaches the caller as it is, with its value) and `FlagCrossesPositional` (Q7). lower `TestFlagLaws` restates them as property tests. lower `TestFlagMatchesGo` checks the parse on 3,500 seeded argument lists (500 of them with a help word put in among other tokens): against Go's `flag` where every flag comes before the first positional argument and no help follows a failure, and against a written model of Go's `parseOne` with the Q7 rules (interleaving, and `@effect/cli`'s help precedence) on every list. `RenderParseRoundTrip` compares every read (each flag's `Get`, an `Arg`, an `OptionalArg` and `Rest`) of the result and of its re-parse, not `Equal`.
8. **Cost.** `Values` holds a `Map` of a union of the value kinds, so each value is boxed until a closed union of records lowers without boxing (X1). The `Set` is a value built at each call; a constant `Set` could lower `Parse` to a `switch` on the flag name.
9. **Trees (CU1).** Dispatch consumes one command name per level and uses the complete path as the leaf's help name. Group help lists all descendant relative paths. Trees have no special compiler rule. **A level's own leaf** (gap-group) is its child named `""`; a level holds at most one (the first counts), and it has no children (a defect otherwise). When the first argument names no child: a help word before `--`, read with the leaf's flags, fails `Help` with the level's `CommandUsage`; else the own leaf parses every argument when none follows, the first is a flag or `--`, or the leaf takes positional arguments; else `UnknownCommand`. So a typo after a group whose leaf takes no positional argument is still `UnknownCommand`, as Cobra reports "unknown command" for a parent whose `Args` is `NoArgs`. `UnknownCommand.Group` is the path of group names from the root to the level (`["auth"]` for `tool auth bogus`, empty at the root), its `Known` leaves out the own leaf, and `Describe` adds " of auth" for a nested level. `CommandUsage` lists the own leaf at its level's path (the program's name at the top) beside the children. `Render` writes every flag and then `--` before the positional arguments, so a rendered own-leaf line never starts with a child's name. `RenderParseTreeRoundTrip` (to a named leaf or to a level's own leaf), `DeclaredShortAndLongAgree`, `AbsentIsNone`, `RepeatedKeepsOrder`, `HelpNamesEveryCommand` (each own leaf included) and `UnknownCommandNamesItsGroup` run in `TestFlagTreeLaws`. *Prior art:* Cobra runs a parent's `Run` when no subcommand matches and its `Args` accept the words; `@effect/cli` runs a parent's handler when no subcommand follows; Elixir's `OptionParser` and Gleam's `glint` route by path, and glint gives every path node its own `run`. `TestFlagTreesAgree` generates trees of depth 1 to 3, where each level may have an own leaf, with or without positional arguments, and compares distinct leaf values, all repeated scalar kinds, parse failures, typed `Build` failures and the root version with an independent Go parser. Replay: `ERGO_FLAG_TREE_SEED=31 go test -run '^TestFlagTreesAgree$' ./internal/sketch/lower`. Measured speed for these additions is not claimed; the hand-Go control is a recursive command-name switch plus Go's scalar readers and append-only occurrence slices. The earlier scalar benchmark above is historical, not a measurement of CU1.

*Prior art:* Go's `flag` supplies scalar readers and help; Cobra adds command trees and shorthand names. Effect `cli` supplies typed parsing, optional flags, repeated occurrences and subcommands. Its intent is to turn untrusted argument text into typed application commands; Ergo uses records, unions, `Option` and a `Build` failure row instead of `Command.make` inference, tagged validation errors or Effect generator/pipe wrappers. Gleam `glint` and `argv` use typed parser values and `Result`, lowered to ordinary Erlang or JavaScript calls; Elixir `OptionParser` uses `aliases` and `:keep` to retain repeats, executing as BEAM library code; ReScript uses host parsers, with ordinary uncurried JavaScript calls. MoonBit uses `Option` and host argument access. Ergo takes the guarantees, not a framework's API shape. Type-derived commands remain S-cli's later part.

### exec

Std `exec` runs programs of the host (std-align S3c, G8). It follows Go's `os/exec` for its names and its process model, and Effect's `ChildProcess` (`unstable/process`) for what a run gives back: the exit status and the output, with a stop of the caller that ends the child.

```go
import exec
import os

func branch() string needs exec.Runner {
	let r = exec.Runner.Run(exec.New("git", []string{"branch", "--show-current"})) catch {
	case exec.NotFound:
		return "no git"
	case exec.Denied, exec.Failed:
		return "git did not start"
	}
	if r.Status != 0 {
		return "not a repository"
	}
	r.Stdout
}

func main() needs Console | Ambient {
	with context { exec.Runner: exec.NewHost().InheritEnv(os.NewHost().Environ()) } { Console.Println(branch()) }
}
```

| Declaration | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `type Command { Name string, Args []string, Env Map[string, string], Dir string, Stdin string }`; `func New(name string, args []string) Command`; `WithEnv(c, key, value)`, `WithEnvFrom(c, env)`, `law WithEnvFromKeepsOwn`, `WithDir(c, dir)`, `WithStdin(c, text)`; `func Key(c Command) string` | One program to run, and the builder of changed copies. |
| `type Result { Status int, Stdout string, Stderr string }` | What a program that ran gives back. |
| `type Runner { Run(c Command) Result fails NotFound \| Denied \| Failed, Env() Map[string, string], law NotFoundNamesTheProgram(name string), law EnvIsExact(values []string), law LookupUsesChildPath() }` | The capability; `Env()` is what it lays under each Command's `Env`. |
| `type Host { root string, base Map[string, string] }`; `func NewHost() Host needs Ambient`; `func InheritEnv(h Host, env Map[string, string]) Host`; `func PathOf(env Map[string, string]) Map[string, string]`; `let TestHostKeys []string`; `func Run(_ Host, c Command) …` | The host's provider and its one constructor ([Host packages](#host-packages)). |
| `type Script { Results Map[string, Result] }`; `func Run(s Script, c Command) …`; `func Start(s Script, c Command, st Streams) …` | The test provider: it runs nothing. |
| `Runner.Start(c Command, s Streams) Running fails NotFound \| Denied \| Failed`, `law StartAgreesWithRun(sh Shell)`, `law StartMergedInterleavesRun(sh Shell)`; `type Shell { Writes []ShellWrite, Status int }`, `type ShellWrite { Text string, Stderr bool }`, `func ShellCommand(sh Shell) Command` | Start a program that runs while its output is read (CU4); the laws draw a `Shell`, a `sh` program that exists on every host, so on a host provider each case runs one. |
| `type Sink = Inherit \| Discard \| ToFile{Path string, Append bool} \| ToCaller \| ToStdout`; `type Streams { Stdout Sink, Stderr Sink }`; `func Interleaves(s string, a string, b string) bool`, `law InterleavesMatchesModel(a string, b string, picks []bool, t string)` | Where each stream of a started program goes; `ToStdout` is standard error's, to where standard output goes (GENT-G5); `Interleaves` is the order a merged stream keeps. |
| `type Running { Lines() Seq[Line], Chunks() Seq[Chunk], Wait() int, Stop() int takes, Close() }`; `type Line { Text string, Stderr bool }`; `type Chunk { Text string, Stderr bool }`; `func Split(text string) []string` | A started program, a resource; its lines; its bytes as read; the line rule. |
| `func WaitFor(p Running, d duration) int fails timeout.Expired needs Scheduler`, `law WaitForEndedGivesStatus(status int, d int64)` | Wait for the end with a deadline; Expired leaves the program running (S-tz). |
| `Command.Grace duration`; `WithGrace(c, d)` | The time between SIGTERM and SIGKILL when a stop, a timeout or a release ends the program; 0 (New's) sends SIGKILL at once. |
| `type NotFound { Name string }`; `type Denied { Name string }`; `type Failed { Name string, Reason string }` | The failures. |

1. `Name` is a path when it holds a `/`, else a program looked up on the `PATH` the program will see (the absolute directories of `c.WithEnvFrom(Runner.Env())`'s `PATH`), never on the `PATH` of the process that runs it (law `LookupUsesChildPath`). `Args` are the arguments after the name. **The environment is a value** (STD-EXEC-ENV): the program sees exactly `Env` over the Runner's `Env()`, one entry per key, sorted by key, and nothing of the environment of the process that runs it (law `EnvIsExact`; no `PWD` is added for a `Dir`). The host's Runner as `NewHost` makes it has an empty `Env()`; `h.InheritEnv(env)` lays `env` under every Command's own, so the code that chooses the provider says what passes on: `exec.NewHost().InheritEnv(os.Process.Environ())` where a user's commands run, `exec.NewTestHost().InheritEnv(exec.PathOf(os.Process.Environ()))` where only `PATH` is needed. `WithEnvFrom(c, env)` does the same for one Command (law `WithEnvFromKeepsOwn`). Reading the environment needs the `os.Process` capability, so a row shows every program that passes environment on. A test's Host adds its `TestHostKeys` (`HOME`, `TMPDIR`, `GIT_CEILING_DIRECTORIES`) over everything. `Dir` is the working directory, `""` for the caller's. `Stdin` is the whole of the standard input; the program reads end of file after it.
2. `WithEnv`, `WithDir` and `WithStdin` return a copy. `WithEnv` copies `Env`, so the command it is given keeps its own entries (the laws `WithEnvOverrides` and `WithEnvKeepsTheOriginal`).
3. A status that is not 0 is a `Result`, not a failure: a CLI matches on it. A program that a signal ended has the status 128 plus the signal's number, as a shell reports it. `Stdout` and `Stderr` hold all the program wrote, read to their end.
4. The failures are a closed row of three, for a program that did not start. `NotFound` is a name not on the `PATH`, a path with no file at it, or the empty name (Go's `exec.ErrNotFound`, `fs.ErrNotExist`). `Denied` is a file the process may not run, a directory included, as Go's `LookPath` reports it (`fs.ErrPermission`). `Failed` is every other reason, with the host's text, such as a `Dir` that does not exist (`dir /x: no such file or directory`). An uncaught one is written with its generated text and status 1, such as `error: exec.NotFound{Name:git}`. The law `NotFoundNamesTheProgram` states that `NotFound` names the program.
5. **The stop protocol.** The Go body is `v1.ExecRun(t *rt.Task, name, args, env, dir, stdin)`. It is marked `//ergo:wait caller`: it waits on the caller's task; the stop of that task is its cancel hook ([Host packages](#host-packages)):
   - The program starts in a process group of its own (`Setpgid`), which holds it and each process it starts that does not leave the group.
   - `Run` returns only after the program is reaped and its output is read.
   - A stop of the calling task sends `SIGKILL` to the group, waits until the program is reaped, and ends the call with an interruption. So the caller's exit actions run after the program is gone. Inside a mask the wait is uninterruptible, and the program runs to its end.
   - After the program exits, `Run` sends `SIGKILL` to what is left of its group.
   - After either kill, `Run` waits until the group is empty: `SIGKILL` only marks a process, the kernel ends it later, and the host's reaper then reaps it. A process keeps the group's id until it is reaped, so `kill(-group, 0)` fails with `ESRCH` exactly when no process of the group is left, zombies included. The wait is at most 500 ms (for a slow reaper, or a process in an uninterruptible wait, which has `SIGKILL` pending and runs no code of its own again); an empty group costs no wait. So a call leaves no process of the group, not even a zombie (measured: without the wait, a killed grandchild was still in the run state 22 µs after `Run` returned). A process left in the group that holds stdout or stderr open delays the return by at most 500 ms (as Go's `WaitDelay` does), and `Run` then returns the status and the output read so far. A program that must outlive the call leaves the group itself (`setsid`).
   - The body signals only the group of the program it started, whose id is the program's pid, and only while it holds it. `Run` waits for the program's end without reaping it (`waitid` with `WEXITED|WNOWAIT` on Linux; a kqueue `EVFILT_PROC`/`NOTE_EXIT` on Darwin and the BSDs, where `waitid` also returns on a stop, golang/go#19314). An unreaped program, alive or a zombie, keeps its pid and so the group's id, so no other group can have that id at either kill. `Run` reaps the program after the kill, and then waits for the group's end with `kill(-group, 0)`, which sends no signal. `Run` reads the output from pipes it owns, so it can wait for the output's end before the reap. `TestExecStopModel` checks, at each kill, that the program is still unreaped in its group (a test hook).
   `TestExecStopModel` runs 120 generated programs (output, grandchildren that close or hold the output, exits by status and by signal, a block) with the caller stopped before the call, while the program runs, inside a mask, or never, against a model of the states `Running`, `Exited(status)` and `Killed`: the result, the output, the reap of the program and the end of every process in its group. Lower `TestExecRuns` stops a task once its program has started, and the program never reaches the line after its 20 s sleep; the check is that order, not a wall-clock bound.
6. A program implements `exec.Runner`: it has no private operation. `exec.Script` is the std's test provider: `Run` gives the `Result` that `Results` holds at `Key(c)`, or `NotFound` (the law `ScriptReplaysRecorded`). `Key(c)` is the name and each argument with a NUL byte before each argument, made by one `strings.Join` (`exec` imports `strings` and `slices`); no program's name or argument holds a NUL byte, so two commands have one key exactly when their names and arguments are equal. The law records the arguments `arg, arg` and the one argument `arg + "," + arg` side by side, so a key whose separator a program can write makes it false. The environment, the directory and the standard input are not in the key. A test binds `with context { exec.Runner: exec.Script{Results: …} } { run() }` in the place where `main` binds `exec.NewHost()`.
7. The six records are the `runtime/v1` structs `v1.ExecNotFound`, `v1.ExecDenied`, `v1.ExecFailed`, `v1.ExecResult`, `v1.ExecLine` and `v1.ExecChunk`, which their declarations name (Types 4), so `exec.NotFound` and `fs.NotFound` are two Go types.
8. Speed: process start dominates a run. `BenchmarkExecRunTrue` holds `Run` of `true` to Go's `exec.CommandContext(ctx, "true").Run()`, and `BenchmarkExecRunMiB` holds a run that writes 1 MiB to a hand `bytes.Buffer` read and its `String()`. The body copies each stream once into its string, as the Go control does; no `unsafe` (std-align section 4: no measured gain over process start).
9. **Start** (CU4). `Runner.Start(c, s)` starts `c` and returns once it has started; the caller owns the `Running` (a resource: [resources](resources.md) R1, R10). Each stream goes to its sink: `Inherit` is this process's stream, `Discard` the null device, `ToFile{Path, Append}` a file the program writes itself (created when missing, emptied first unless `Append`; the bytes reach the file as the program writes them), `ToCaller` the caller's `Lines`, and `ToStdout`, a sink of standard error only, where standard output goes: the same pipe, file or stream (the shell's `2>&1`, Go's `CombinedOutput`), so the bytes of the two keep the order the program wrote them in, and with a `Stdout` of `ToCaller` they come as one stream, no `Chunk` or `Line` marked `Stderr` (GENT-G5: two `ToCaller` streams order each stream, not the two between them). A `Stdout` of `ToStdout` fails `Failed` (`stdout: ToStdout is a sink of standard error, …`), from every provider. A file sink that cannot be opened fails `Failed` with the reason `stdout PATH: REASON`, and nothing starts; a stopped caller starts nothing.
10. **Lines and Wait.** `Lines()` gives each line of the `ToCaller` streams as it comes, without its line feed (`Split` states the rule: a last line with no line feed is a line), stdout's and stderr's in the order they come, each stream in its own order, until both end. At most 64 lines wait for the caller: then the program waits in its write, so the caller's reading paces it. A `break` leaves the rest for the next `Lines`. One merged `Seq` and not two: a caller that read stdout to its end while stderr filled would wait forever. `Wait()` waits for the program's end and gives its status (128 plus the signal for a program a signal ended); it drops the lines not yet read, so it cannot wait on a full buffer, and `Lines` after it gives none. A stop of the caller ends either wait with the interruption and leaves the program to its owner.

**Chunks (CU4c).** `Chunks()` gives the bytes of the `ToCaller` streams as each read returns them, with no wait for a line feed: a `Chunk`'s `Text` holds any bytes, line feeds included, and a last fragment with no line feed comes as it was written, so `x` and `x\n` differ. After each stream's last bytes comes one `Chunk` with empty `Text`, its end. Where a stream is cut is not a promise; each stream's bytes and their order are. At most 64 chunks (each at most 32 KiB) wait for the caller, so the caller's reading paces the program. `Lines` reads the same chunks (one reader, one path): it keeps the bytes after a stream's last line feed until the next chunk of that stream or its end, and on a `break` or a stop it puts back what it read and did not give, so the next `Lines` or `Chunks` starts there and a caller may mix the two. A tee is the caller's own loop, not a sink:

```go
func tee(r exec.Running) string needs io.Stdout {
	let mut text = ""
	for c in r.Chunks() {
		if !c.Stderr {
			io.Stdout.Print(c.Text)
			text = text + c.Text
		}
	}
	text
}
```

`TestRunningMatchesModel` holds it against a fake process whose reader must pass each read on before it reads again: fragments with and without line feeds, `Lines` with a break and with a stop mid-read, `Chunks` that finds every byte written so far, and a read to the end that gives every byte and one end per stream. `TestExecStartRuns` runs a tee on the host. The Go control is a `Read` loop that sends each read's bytes on a channel; `Lines` is that loop's consumer with `strings.IndexByte`, as `bufio` would split it. No speed claim. Effect's `ChildProcess` gives stdout as a byte `Stream` and decodes lines on top; Elixir's `Port` delivers `{:data, binary}` messages as read and line mode only on request; Gleam's `shellout` buffers to the end; ReScript binds Node's `data` events of `Buffer`s. Ergo takes the byte stream as read and the line view over it, and leaves out a decoder pipeline: `Text` is a byte string, and `Lines` is the one decoder std needs.
11. **The release** (`Close`, run by the owner's scope end on every exit, a stop and a timeout included, masked, R9): a program that still runs gets SIGTERM to its group when `c.Grace` is above 0, then SIGKILL when it has not ended within `Grace`; `Grace` 0 sends SIGKILL at once. Then, as `Run` does after a program's end: the output is read to its end for at most 500 ms, SIGKILL goes to what is left of the group, the program is reaped, the release waits until no process of its group is left, and the sinks' files are closed. Each signal goes to the group while its leader is unreaped. `Run`'s stop follows the same grace rule. A timeout is the same path: `timeout.After(1h) { let r = try Runner.Start(c.WithGrace(15s), s); r.Wait() }` ends the program by `r`'s release when the deadline stops the block. A follower is a `Running` the caller leaves: `for l in r.Lines() { … if done { break } }`, and the scope's end ends `tail -f`. A tee reads `ToCaller` lines and writes each where it must go.
12. `exec.Ended(r, s)` is the `Running` of a program that has already ended with the `Result` `r`: `Stdout` as one chunk and its end, then `Stderr`'s, each when its sink in `s` is `ToCaller` (so `Lines` gives `Split(Stdout)`'s lines, then `Split(Stderr)`'s), and `Status` from `Wait`; a `Stderr` of `ToStdout` with a `Stdout` of `ToCaller` gives `Stdout + Stderr` as one unmarked stream (a `Result` keeps no order between the two, and that is one interleaving); it starts nothing and writes no file and nothing to this process's streams. `Script.Start` is `Ended` of the `Result` recorded for `c`. `Running` is the runtime's value (its declaration names `v1.Running`), so no other package's type implements it; a module's own `Runner`, a stateful fake among them, implements `Start` with `Ended` (STD-NOPRIV, IA2: a module implements `exec.Runner`). The law `StartAgreesWithRun` states that a started program read to its end gives `Run`'s lines and status; `TestExecLaws` runs it against a `Script`. The law `StartMergedInterleavesRun` states that a program started with `Streams{Stdout: ToCaller, Stderr: ToStdout}` gives one unmarked stream that interleaves `Run`'s `Stdout` and `Stderr`, each in its order, and `Run`'s status; `TestExecLaws` runs it against a `Script`, and as written on the test host (`context needs TestAmbient { exec.Runner: exec.NewTestHost() }`), where each case runs the drawn `Shell`, as `StartAgreesWithRun` does; `exec.Interleaves(s, a, b)`, the order check it states, is public, and its law `InterleavesMatchesModel` holds it to a search written apart from it; `TestExecStreams` holds the exact write order on the host (a burst of lines to the two streams in turn, with no wait, arrives in the order written). `runtime/v1` `TestRunningMatchesModel` holds the lifecycle against a model over a fake process (the signals, the status, one reap and no signal after it, each stream's order, no line after `Wait`, a stopped caller's `Lines` and `Wait`); `TestExecStreams` is the acceptance check on the host (a helper writes on command; after each command the file or the caller's lines hold exactly what was written; the release ends the group, by SIGTERM or after the grace); lower `TestExecStartRuns` runs the surface in a built program. The Go type is `*v1.Running`; `Line` is `v1.ExecLine`. *Prior art:* Effect's `ChildProcess` gives stdout as a `Stream` and `"inherit"`, and its scope kills the process; Elixir's `Port` sends lines as messages and `System.cmd(into:)` collects into a `Collectable`; Gleam's `shellout` has no streamed form; Go's `os/exec` gives `StdoutPipe` and an `*os.File` as `Stdout`, which `ToFile` uses so the bytes never pass through Ergo. Ergo takes the scope's kill and the inherited stream, not Effect's `Stream` type (a `Seq` a `for` reads is the language's own iterator) and not a collect-into protocol.

*Prior art:* Go's `exec.CommandContext` kills only the process it started, by `cmd.Cancel`, and `WaitDelay` bounds the wait for output a grandchild holds; Ergo kills the whole group, as a shell's job control does. Effect's `ChildProcess` spawns in a scope that kills the process when the scope closes, and gives `exitCode` as a value, not a failure. Elixir's `System.cmd/3` returns `{output, status}` with a status that is not 0 as data, and takes `env:` and `cd:` options; Gleam's `shellout.command(run:, with:, in:, opt:)` returns `Result(String, #(Int, String))`, which makes a status that is not 0 an error; ReScript binds Node's `child_process.spawnSync`, whose result holds `status`, `signal`, `stdout` and `stderr`. Ergo returns the status as data, as Elixir and Effect do, and keeps the failures for a program that did not start.

**Stop and status (CU4b).** `Running.Stop() int takes` runs the same masked grace, group shutdown and reap as `Close`, then returns the child's actual status using `Wait`'s status rule. It consumes the handle; a later use is S108. `Close` stays the scope-release form.
