A `timeout.After` body is escaping and runs on a child task (ESC1), so it cannot borrow a `Running` the caller owns: `timeout.After(1h) { r.Wait() }` is S108 (resources R31, R33). The wait with a deadline is `exec.WaitFor` (below).

**WaitFor (S-tz).** `func WaitFor(p Running, d duration) int fails timeout.Expired needs Scheduler`, called `p.WaitFor(d)`, waits at most `d` for the program's end and gives its status by `Wait`'s rule, or fails `timeout.Expired` when the deadline passes first. The deadline is the `Scheduler`'s time, as `timeout.After`'s is, so a test's `time.Manual` or `testing.VirtualClock` decides it. `Expired` leaves the program running, unsignalled, with its unread bytes for `Lines` and `Chunks`: the caller still owns `p`, and the idiom of a wait and then a stop reads

```go
let code = p.WaitFor(5s) catch {
case timeout.Expired:
	p.Stop()
}
```

A program that has ended gives its status at once and arms no deadline, whatever `d` is (law `WaitForEndedGivesStatus`). A program that ends as the deadline passes may give `Expired`; `Stop` then gives its status. A stop of the caller ends the wait, as `Wait`'s. `p` is a lend, not a move (`std.ArgClauses`): the body, `v1.ExecWaitFor`, waits on the caller's task when the program has ended and otherwise runs `v1.After` over `p`'s `Wait` on a child task, which `After` stops and joins on every exit before it returns, so the lend ends with the call. This is sound for `Running` and not for every resource: `Wait` waits on channels and its finish runs once, so it is safe on any task, where `timeout.After`'s escaping body must also refuse task-affine resources such as `tasks.Set` (ESC1). The ownership rules stay as they are: no structured lend into `timeout.After`'s child, which would need a per-type fact about task affinity. `TestRunningMatchesModel` (runtime/v1) runs `WaitFor` with a deadline that passes at once, before and after the program's end, against its model: before, `Expired`, no signal, every unread byte kept; after, the status and no deadline armed. Lower `TestExecStartRuns` (`postStopStatus`) waits with `WaitFor(0ns)` on a program that waits on a fifo, catches `Expired`, and stops it to read its TERM trap's 37. The Go control is a `select` on the process's exit and a `time.After`; `WaitFor` adds one child task, as `timeout.After` does. No speed claim. *Prior art:* Effect's `Effect.timeout` around a process's `exitCode` interrupts the wait and leaves the process to its scope, as here; Go's `exec.Cmd` has no wait with a deadline (`CommandContext` kills the process on the context's end; `WaitDelay` bounds only the output); Elixir's `Port` and `receive … after` give the message or the timeout and leave the port open; Gleam's `shellout` has no deadline; ReScript binds Node's `spawnSync`, whose `timeout` kills the child at the deadline. Ergo keeps the program alive on `Expired`, so the caller chooses `Stop`, another `WaitFor`, or the scope's release.

`TestRunningMatchesModel` also drives Stop before output, mid-stream and after exit, with arbitrary TERM-trap statuses and a stopped caller. `TestExecStartRuns` checks Stop's status with a real TERM trap that exits 37. The direct Go control is `Close`'s shutdown followed by the stored exit status; Stop adds no process or extra wait. No speed claim is made. Effect's scoped child process solves the same cleanup need; Ergo's consuming operation combines termination and status observation so a caller need not keep an unowned exit-code handle or signal a raw pid.

**Detached start (STD-NOPRIV, IA2 b).** A daemon's `up` starts a program that outlives its caller's scope and the process itself, and a later `down` (often another process, which reads a pidfile) ends it. That is a capability apart from `Runner`, so it shows in the row of the code that does it: `exec.Spawner` has `Spawn(c Command, s Streams) Detached fails NotFound | Denied | Failed`, `Alive(d Detached) bool` and `Kill(d Detached, grace duration)`. A `Running` stays owned by its scope; no `Running` becomes a `Detached`.

1. `Detached { Pid int, Start int64, Name string }` is plain data (`v1.ExecDetached`): it crosses, has no release, and is stored where a later `Alive` or `Kill` reads it. `Pid` is the program's process id and the id of its session and process group; `Start` is its start time as the host reports it (Linux: `/proc/<pid>/stat` field 22, clock ticks after boot; Darwin: `kinfo_proc`'s `p_starttime` from the sysctl `kern.proc.pid.<pid>`, microseconds after the epoch, read by Go's own `syscall.Syscall6(SYS___SYSCTL, …)` with no cgo), so `Alive` and `Kill` never act on another process that reused the pid.
2. `Host` implements `Runner` and `Spawner`. Its `Spawn` starts `c` as `Start` does (env, dir, stdin), in a new session and process group (`setsid`), and returns once it has started; a stop of the calling task, and the end of this process, do not end it. Its sinks are `Inherit`, `Discard` or `ToFile`; a `ToCaller` sink fails `Failed` (no caller outlives the program to read it) and starts nothing. While this process lives, a goroutine reaps the program, so its end leaves no zombie.
3. `Kill(d, grace)` ends `d`'s process group when `d`'s program is alive: SIGTERM, then SIGKILL when the group has not ended `grace` later (a grace of 0 sends SIGKILL at once), and it returns once no process of the group is left, with `Run`'s bound of 500 ms. A `d` whose program is gone, or whose pid another process now has, gets no signal and is no failure. The host gives no event for the end of a process this process is not the parent of, so the wait for the group's end polls `kill(-group, 0)` with a doubling pause from 50 µs to 10 ms.
4. `exec.Processes` (`NewProcesses(programs)`) is the memory `Spawner` for laws and for tests of code that spawns: `Spawn` records a live entry under the next pid (its `Start`), `NotFound` for a name not in `programs` and `Failed` for a `ToCaller` sink as the host's; `Alive` reads the entry; `Kill` removes it. Its table is in a `sync.Ref`, so copies share it. The laws `KilledIsNotAlive` and `KillIsIdempotent` hold for it (`TestExecLaws`); `runtime/v1` `TestExecSpawnOutlivesItsStarter` holds the host's: a program spawned by a process that then exits is `Alive` from another, a `Detached` with another start time names no process, and `Kill` ends a group that ignores SIGTERM, after which no process of it is left. On a host other than Linux and Darwin the start time is not read yet (`Start` is 0, and `Alive` checks only that the pid exists): a known gap.

*Prior art:* Effect's `ChildProcess` has no detached form: every child is scoped. Node's `child_process.spawn(…, {detached: true})` with `unref()` is the model a ReScript binding takes; Elixir's ports die with their owner and a detached program goes through `:os.cmd("… &")` or a supervisor outside the VM; Gleam's `shellout` has none. Go's `os/exec` with `SysProcAttr.Setsid` and `Process.Release` is the body. Ergo keeps Node's explicit flag as a capability of its own in the row, and adds the start time to the pid so a stored handle cannot reach a reused pid, which a pidfile alone cannot promise.

**LookPath and Replace (CAP-PROC1, the self-host driver's census).**

1. `LookPath(name) string fails NotFound | Denied needs os.Process | fs.Files` is where `name` is on the PATH, as Run finds it (Go's `exec.LookPath`): a name with a "/" is that path when a file there has an execute bit (else `Denied`, or `NotFound` when nothing is there); any other name is `filepath.Join(dir, name)` for the first absolute directory of PATH holding a file, not a directory, with an execute bit. Relative directories of PATH, the empty one included, are skipped, as Go refuses a program found there (`exec.ErrDot`). It is a function over `os.Process` and `fs.Files` (decision O8's tiebreak: an operation joins a type only when no function over its operations can do it), so a test answers it from a Process of its own and an `fstest.Memory`. It reads the mode's bits where Go asks the kernel (`eaccess`), so a file whose execute bit is not the caller's class is found and then refused by `Run` as `Denied`. Laws `LookPathFindsTheFirst` and `LookPathSkipsRelative` (`TestExecLaws`; std mutants `proc1-lookpath-relative`, `proc1-lookpath-noexec`).
2. `Replace(h Host, c Command) fails NotFound | Denied | Failed` makes this process the program (Go's `syscall.Exec`, the body `exec.ExecReplace`). It resolves the program, arguments, environment and working directory as `Run` does: a bare name is looked up on the command's effective `PATH`, the environment is exactly the command's entries over `h.Env()`, and `Dir` adds no `PWD`. The process keeps its id, its standard streams and its process group, so a shell that waits for it waits for the program, and a terminal's interrupt reaches the program. Standard output is flushed first; no scope exit action runs and no resource is released, as for a process a signal ends. It returns only with a failure, and the process is then as it was (its working directory restored). A command with standard input text is `Failed` (the program reads this process's input), and so is a test's `Host`. It is a function of `Host` only: a `Script` replaces nothing. `TestExecReplaceMatchesRun` checks argument and environment parity; `TestExecReplaceBecomesTheProgram` checks the output, directory and exit status.

`Run`, `Start`, `Spawn`, and `Replace` fail with `Failed` before launch if an effective environment key is empty, contains `=` or NUL, or its value contains NUL. Other bytes, including line feeds, are data, not separators.

*Prior art:* Go's `exec.LookPath` and `syscall.Exec`; Effect's `ChildProcess` has neither (a Command names its program and the platform looks it up); Elixir's `System.find_executable/1`, with no exec (the BEAM cannot be replaced); Gleam has neither in its std; ReScript leaves both to Node, which has no exec.

### signal

Std `signal` is the stops the host sends a process from outside (CAP-SIGNAL and CAP-INTERRUPT, the self-host driver's census): Ctrl-C at a terminal, a service manager's terminate, a closed terminal. Its model is Go's `os/signal` for SIGINT, SIGTERM and SIGHUP; its guarantee is Effect's interruption of the main fiber on SIGINT: a stop becomes structured cancellation, so the work it ends runs its exit actions.

| Item | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `type Stop = Interrupt \| Terminate \| Hangup`; `type Stopped { Kind Stop }` | A stop the host sent, as a closed union a `match` covers; the failure of `UntilStop` when a stop ended its body. |
| `type Stops { Next() Stop, Poll() Option[Stop] }` | The capability: `Next` waits for the next stop and takes it, an interruption point of the calling task; `Poll` takes a stop already delivered and never waits. |
| `type Watched { … }`; `func Watch(kinds []Stop) Watched needs Ambient`; `func Unwatch(w Watched)` | The host's provider: from `Watch` until `Unwatch`, a stop of `kinds` no longer ends the process but is delivered to `Next` ([Host packages](#host-packages)). |
| `type Script { … }`; `func NewScript(raised []Stop) Script`; `func Raise(s Script, k Stop)` | The provider a test or a law raises stops on, in order. |
| `func UntilStop[T own, E, R](s Stops, body own func() T fails E needs R) T fails E \| Stopped needs R` | Runs `body` until it ends or a stop comes from `s`, whichever is first. |
| `func StatusOf(k Stop) os.Code` | The exit status of a program a stop of kind `k` ended, as a shell reports it. |

1. Both providers deliver through a `queue.Queue`, so `Next` waits as `Pop` does: a stopped task never hangs in it. A stop that comes while eight wait untaken is dropped, as the host coalesces stops of one kind.
2. `Watch` needs `Ambient`: only code that may choose the host's behaviour takes its stops. `Watched`'s fields give the package construction authority, so only `Watch` makes one (`TestStdHostProvidersHaveAuthority`). Pair it with `scope.OnExit() { signal.Unwatch(w) }`.
3. When a stop comes first, `UntilStop` stops `body`'s task, whose exit actions run before `UntilStop` returns, and fails `Stopped{Kind}`. A stop that comes as `body` ends may be taken and dropped, as a loser of `tasks.Race` may take a value.
4. `StatusOf(k)` is 128 plus the signal's number: 130 for `Interrupt`, 143 for `Terminate`, 129 for `Hangup`, the same on every Unix host, and never a status of a program's own failure (1 to 125; [os](#os) 3). A `main` ends with it as `signal.UntilStop(stops, run) catch { case signal.Stopped as s: return os.Exit{Code: signal.StatusOf(s.Kind), Message: ""} }`.
5. Laws: `RaisedInOrder`, `PollTakesOnce`, `QuietRunsBody`, `StopEndsBody` and `StatusIsTheShells` ([Std laws](#std-laws)). `Stops` states no member law: it has no operation that makes a stop, so the laws state `Next` and `Poll` on `Script`.

*Prior art:* Go's `signal.Notify` with `signal.Stop`, and `signal.NotifyContext` for the cancellation; Effect's runtime interrupts the main fiber on SIGINT (`runMain`); Elixir's `:os.set_signal/2` hands a signal to the `:erl_signal_server` event manager; Gleam and ReScript leave signals to their hosts (Erlang, Node's `process.on("SIGINT")`).

### sql

Std `sql` runs statements against a relational database (wf10 TX1). Its model is Go's `database/sql`: one handle over a pool of connections, a transaction held on one connection. Its guarantee is Effect's `SqlClient.withTransaction`: a block runs in a transaction that commits when the block ends normally and rolls back when it fails or is stopped, and a transaction inside another is a savepoint.

```go
import sql

func move(from string, to string, n int64) fails sql.Unique | sql.Serialization | sql.Deadlock | sql.Timeout | sql.Closed | sql.Other needs sql.Tx {
	let _ = try sql.Tx.Exec("UPDATE account SET n = n - $1 WHERE id = $2", []sql.Value{sql.Int{N: n}, sql.Text{S: from}})
	let _ = try sql.Tx.Exec("UPDATE account SET n = n + $1 WHERE id = $2", []sql.Value{sql.Int{N: n}, sql.Text{S: to}})
}

func transfer() fails sql.Unique | sql.Serialization | sql.Deadlock | sql.Timeout | sql.Closed | sql.Other needs sql.DB {
	try sql.Transaction() { try move("a", "b", 10) }
}
```

1. **Operation types.** `DB` is a database: `Exec(stmt, args) Result`, `Query(stmt, args) []Row` and `Begin() Conn`. `Conn` is one connection in a transaction: `Exec`, `Query`, `Commit`, `Rollback` (which never fails), `Savepoint(name)`, `Release(name)` and `RollbackTo(name)`. `Tx` has `Exec`, `Query` and the unexported operation `bound`. A provider (the host's, or `sqltest.Script`) implements `DB` and `Conn`; only package `sql` implements `Tx`, by the visibility rule (grammar.md, Visibility), so a program cannot fake a transaction boundary. A statement's arguments and a row's columns are `Value = Null | Int | Float | Bool | Text | Bytes | Stamp`; `Row { Columns, Values }` is read with `IntAt`, `FloatAt`, `BoolAt`, `TextAt`, `TimeAt` and `IsNull`, each failing `Mismatch` on another type or a missing column. `Rows(stmt, args, parse)` and `One(stmt, args, parse)` parse a query's rows with a function the program gives; `One` of no row is `NoRows` (law `RowsReadEachRow`, over a `Script`).
2. **Transaction.** `Transaction[T, E, R](body func() T fails E needs R | Tx | DB) T fails E | Unique | Serialization | Deadlock | Timeout | Closed | Other needs R | DB` calls `DB.Begin`, registers a frame-scope exit action (`scope.OnExit`, masked, run on every exit) that rolls back unless the commit was reached, runs the body with `Tx` bound to the connection and `DB` rebound to it, then commits. So every statement of the body runs in the transaction, one through `DB` included, and a failure or a stop of the task rolls back (laws `CommitsOnce`, `RollsBackOnFailure`, `RollsBackOnInterrupt`). The body's failure, or a statement's, is the Transaction's. A failed `COMMIT` is the Transaction's failure and ends the transaction: no `ROLLBACK` follows it (`CommitFailureEndsIt`).
3. **Nesting.** Inside a Transaction, `DB.Begin` marks a savepoint: `SAVEPOINT ergo_savepoint`, and a savepoint inside a savepoint adds its name to the outer one's (`ergo_savepoint_ergo_savepoint`), so names never collide on one connection. A nested block that ends normally releases its savepoint (`NestedCommitReleases`); one that fails goes back to it and releases it, and the outer transaction goes on (`NestedFailureKeepsTheOuter`). Effect names its savepoints by depth (`effect_sql_<n>`); Ergo gets the same uniqueness from the nesting of names, with no depth in fiber state.
4. **Outside a transaction.** A statement through `DB` needs no `Tx`: it runs on the provider's pool, alone. `Tx` is bound only by `Transaction`, so a call of a function whose row needs `sql.Tx` where no Transaction encloses it is S102 "this call requires sql.Tx, which is not in the capability row …" (lower `TestSQLTxOnlyInsideTransaction`, the plan's `StatementOutsideNeedsNoTx`).
5. **Failures.** `Unique{Constraint}`, `Serialization`, `Deadlock`, `Timeout`, `NoRows`, `Closed` and `Other{Code, Message}`, named by what a program does about them: a retry loop catches `Serialization | Deadlock`, an insert catches `Unique`. `type Failure` is their union, a value a `Dialect` gives. A provider reads a driver's error as `HostError{Code, Constraint, Message}` and its `Dialect.Classify` gives the failure; `Standard` reads the SQLSTATE codes 23505, 40001, 40P01 and 57014 that PostgreSQL and CockroachDB report, and any other code as `Other` (laws `StandardReadsSQLState`, `StandardKeepsTheRest`).
6. **sqltest.** `sqltest.NewScript(rules)` is a `DB` and a `Conn` that runs nothing: it logs each statement's text and `BEGIN`, `COMMIT`, `ROLLBACK`, `SAVEPOINT name`, `RELEASE SAVEPOINT name` and `ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT name`, in a `sync.Ref` its copies share, and answers each line from the first `Rule` that names it: `Answer{Statement, Rows, Affected}` or `Refuse{Statement, With}`. `Begin` gives the Script itself as the connection, so one log holds the whole run. The transaction laws are stated over its log and run in lower `TestSQLLaws`; the live leg against PostgreSQL waits for LIVE2.
7. **The host.** `sql.Open(driver, dsn, dialect) Host … needs Ambient` is a pool of Go's `database/sql` (the leaf `runtime/v1/sql`), pinged once; `Host` implements `DB`, and its `Begin` is a `BeginTx` under the Go context of the calling task, so a stop of that task cancels the transaction and `database/sql` rolls it back, and the exit action's `Rollback` after it ignores `ErrTxDone`. Savepoints are the SQL statements `SAVEPOINT`, `RELEASE SAVEPOINT` and `ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT` on the transaction's connection. A driver's error reads its SQLSTATE through a method `SQLState() string` and its constraint through a field `ConstraintName` or `Constraint` (pgx, lib/pq), and an ended pool, connection or transaction is `Closed`. The program links its driver; a name no linked driver registered is `Other` ("sql: unknown driver"). Std links no driver today, so a program reaches a database once LIVE2 brings one. `runtime/v1/sql` `TestHostSendsTheTransaction` and `TestHostReadsValuesAndErrors` hold the host body against a fake `database/sql/driver`.

The Go control is `tx, err := db.BeginTx(ctx, nil)`, `defer tx.Rollback()`, the statements on `tx`, then `tx.Commit()`. No speed claim.

*Prior art:* Effect's `SqlClient.withTransaction` keeps the transaction's connection in a fiber reference, so a statement written anywhere joins the transaction it runs in, and nothing in a signature says a function needs one; it runs the body under `uninterruptibleMask`, rolls back on any failure or interruption, makes a nested call a savepoint named by depth, and turns a failed rollback into a defect. Ergo takes the guarantee and the savepoints and moves the connection into the row: `needs Tx` is the signature fact Effect cannot write, and `DB` rebound inside the block gives Effect's "a statement anywhere joins" for code written against `DB`. It leaves out Effect's tagged-template statements (`sql\`SELECT …\``), since Ergo has no template literals and arguments are values. Elixir's Ecto `Repo.transaction(fn -> … end)` keeps the connection in the process dictionary, rolls back on a raise or `Repo.rollback/1`, and nests by joining the outer transaction (no savepoint unless the adapter is asked); Gleam's `pog.transaction(db, fn(conn) { … })` passes the connection to the callback explicitly and rolls back on an `Error` result, with no nesting; ReScript binds a Node driver's `BEGIN`/`COMMIT` as promises with no scope. Ergo's capability in the row is Gleam's explicit connection without its threading through every call.

## Gen

PROPERTY-TESTING P1 (2026-11-22). `gen` is test data drawn from a tape, in plain Ergo: no compiler rule names it, so a user library could be it (STD HAS NO PRIVILEGES). A generator is any function that `needs gen.Draws`; a property is a body `func() bool fails gen.Rejected needs gen.Draws`; `gen.Check` is its runner. Imported, not prelude. Since P1b, `Draws` declares the harness `test Property` ([runtime](runtime.md#tests), Tests: harnesses). A test whose body draws runs as a property: `gen.Property(run gen.Run, body func() fails gen.Failed needs gen.Draws) fails gen.Failed` runs the body over `Defaults()`'s cases, seeded by the run's `--seed` mixed with the test's name (FNV-1a of the name, then splitmix), so two tests with the same body draw different cases. A failing case is shrunk. The test fails with a `gen.Failed`, whose Message is its whole report: the failed assert, `gen.Report` of the run (its seed is the run's `--seed`), `\tat FILE:LINE` and `\treplay: ergo test SOURCE --run NAME --seed SEED`, SOURCE the path `ergo test` was given (`gen.Run.Source`), so the command runs as printed from any working directory. A body that needs `gen.Rejected` (`gen.Assume`) is not admitted yet; that comes with P1c, which also deletes the Go `testgen` tape and `v1.ShrinkTape`.

| Declaration | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `type Draws { mut Draw(n uint64) uint64, CaseSize() int, mut Begin(label string), mut End(), mut Show(label string, text string) }` | The draw source. `Draw(n)` is in `0..n-1` (any 64 bits for 0); `Begin`/`End` mark a span; `Show` names a value for the report. Law `DrawBelowBound`. |
| `Below(n)`, `Size()`, `Bool()`, `IntIn(lo, hi)`, `OneOf[T](xs)`, `ListOf[T](min, max, g)`, `Named[T](label, show, g)` | Generators, plain functions over `Draws`. |
| `type Rejected {}`; `func Assume(c bool) fails Rejected` | A case the body rejects does not count. |
| `type Tape { Size int, Draws []uint64 }`, `type Shown { Label, Text string }`, `type Case { Number int, Tape Tape, Shown []Shown }` | A case's draws, its named values. |
| `type Config { Seed uint64, Cases, Shrinks, MaxDraws, MaxRejected int }`; `func Defaults() Config` | Seed 1, 100 cases, 500 replays a shrink, 8192 draws a case, 1000 rejected runs. |
| `type Outcome = Held{Cases, Rejected int} \| Refuted{Seed uint64, Of int, Found Case, Shrunk Case, Shrinks int} \| GaveUp{Cases, Rejected int}` | What a run came to, as data. |
| `func Check(cfg Config, body …) Outcome`, `func Replay(t Tape, body …) Ran`, `func Shrink(cfg Config, t Tape, body …) (Tape, int)` | The runner, one replay, the shrinker. |
| `func Report(o Outcome) string`, `func ReplayText(t Tape) string` | The outcome as text: empty when it held. |

1. **Zero is simplest.** Every generator maps a zero draw to its simplest value, and a smaller draw to a value no less simple: `IntIn` ranks the range by distance from its value nearest 0 (0, 1, -1, 2, -2, …, the side above first at a tie, then on along the longer side); `Bool` is false at 0; `OneOf` the first element; `ListOf` draws, past `min`, one stop draw before each element (`Draw(size+1)`, 0 ends the list), so the empty tape gives `min` simplest elements. Laws `ZeroIsSimplest`, `IntInRanksByDistance`.
2. **Cases.** Case k (from 1) draws from splitmix64 seeded by `cfg.Seed` and k, at size (k-1)/4, as `ergo test`'s cases (runtime.md, Tests 6). A rejected run, or one past `MaxDraws` draws (cut: every draw past the bound is 0), draws another case and counts apart; more than `MaxRejected` give `GaveUp`, which is no pass. Law `RejectedIsNotACase`.
3. **Replay.** A run records the draws it read, each below its bound; a replay reads them back (past the end a draw is 0; a value at or above the bound is taken modulo it), so a replay of a recorded tape reads the same draws. Law `ReplayGivesTheSameDraws`.
4. **Shrink.** From the failing case's tape, repeated until a round keeps nothing or the budget of replays ends: delete each span; delete a chunk of 8, 4, 2 or 1 draws; zero a chunk of 8, 4 or 2; lower each draw to 0, then by a binary search and single decrements. A candidate is kept when its run fails, counts, and reads a tape smaller in shortlex order than the best: so the result still fails, is never larger, and the shrink ends (laws `ShrunkStillFails`, `ShrinkingEnds`). At a fixpoint no single span deletion or decrement fails smaller (`ShrinkIsLocallyMinimal`); for a predicate monotone in one drawn integer the result is the boundary a search of every value finds (`ShrinkReachesTheBoundary`); a list property shrinks by whole elements (`SpanDeletesAnElement`). No global minimum is promised for other predicates.
5. **Report.** `refuted (case K of N, seed S, size Z; T shrinks):`, then a line `label = text` per named draw of the shrunk case, `shrunk from:` and the found case's named draws when the tapes differ, and `replay: gen.Replay(gen.Tape{Size: Z, Draws: []uint64{…}}, body)`. A value's text is its `show` function's, since only basic values have a text form ([types.md](types.md) 9; derived text forms). Law `ReportNamesSeedDrawsReplay`.
6. **A property today.** `test T { assert gen.Check(gen.Defaults(), func() bool fails gen.Rejected needs gen.Draws { … }).Report() == "" }`: a failure prints the report in the assert's line diff (`examples/sorted-insert`).

*Prior art:* Hypothesis and Go rapid shrink the choice sequence, not the value (as `v1.ShrinkTape` does for parameters); Hegel draws inside the test body and prints each draw; QuickCheck's `sized`. Not taken: a `Gen[T]` value type with `map`/`flatMap`, a workaround for a language without rows (owner, 2026-11-08): a function with a `needs Draws` row is that value.

## Testing

The prelude services. `testing.VirtualClock` provides `time.Clock` and `Scheduler` and jumps to the next timer when every task in the test is blocked (batch 12, C1; built). `testing.Script[S]` is a provider of any capability, derived from its operations, with answers the test sets and a record of calls (L5b; built). `T.Idle()` reports the tasks that keep a test from reaching idle (not built). The test harness is an ordinary `context`, so production code needs no change to run on virtual time.

| Declaration | Meaning | Go body |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `type VirtualClock { line time.Timeline }`; `func NewVirtualClock(sec int64) VirtualClock` | A test `time.Clock` and `Scheduler` whose time is `sec` Unix seconds at the start. | `NewVirtualTimeline(t *rt.Task, sec, nsec int64) *Timeline` (time.go), over `rt.NewAuto` (rt idle.go) |
| `Now(c VirtualClock) time.Time`, `Sleep(c VirtualClock, d duration)` | The clock's time; a sleep on it. | `Timeline.Read`, `Timeline.Wait` |
| `law SleepAdvancesExactly(n int64)`, `law IdleOnlyAdvance(n int64, k int)` | The laws ([Std laws](#std-laws)). | in place (`ergotests -std`) |
| `type Script[S] derive Provider(Of[S](), Of[Op](), Of[NoArgs]())`, made as `testing.Script[S]{}` | A test provider of the capability `S`, derived from `S`'s operations: one field per operation, of type `Op[A, F]` (rules 7 to 12). | a generated Go struct per instance, one `*v1.Op[A, F]` field per operation, and an adapter per operation type it converts to (lower script.go) |
| `type Op[A, F] { Answer(f F), Calls() []A, Hold(), Release(f F), Held() []A, Unread() []A }`; `type NoArgs {}` | One operation of a Script: the answer the test set, the arguments of each call so far, the calls held until a Release, and the calls no `Calls` read. | `*v1.Op[A, F]`: `NewOp`, `Answer`, `Calls`, `Hold`, `Release`, `Held`, `Unread`, `Call`; the defect `NoAnswer` (script.go) |
| `func Unanswered() string` | The report a test that ended now would fail with: each Script operation with a held call no Release answered or a Release no call took (rule 10). | `ScriptUnanswered` (script.go) |
| `law ScriptRecordsEachCall(ks []string, n int)`, `law ScriptAnswersByTheLastAnswer(k string, a int, b int)`, `law HeldCallWaitsForRelease(k string, a int)`, `law ReleaseAnswersOldestFirst(k string, j string, a int)`, `law ScriptNamesTheHeldCall(k string)` | The laws ([Std laws](#std-laws)). | `TestStdLawsRun` (lower) |
| `func Point(name string)`, `func At(name string, f own func())` | A named point of a library, and a test's arming of it (rule 15). | `HookPoint(t *rt.Task, name string)`, `HookAt(name string, f func(*rt.Task))` (hook.go); a run build lowers neither (lower hook.go) |
| `func World() Context[fs.Files \| fs.Writes \| time.Clock \| Scheduler \| Random]` | A test's world with no host: a fresh `fstest.Memory` for both file capabilities, one `VirtualClock` for both time capabilities, `rand.Seeded(1)` (rule 14). | none (Ergo) |
| `law WorldSharesEachPair(text string, n int64)`, `law WorldsAreFresh(text string)` | The laws ([Std laws](#std-laws)). | `TestStdLawsRun` (lower) |

1. **Domain.** The clock's domain is the task that calls `NewVirtualClock`, from that call on, and every task started below a task of the domain. A task that made an earlier VirtualClock leaves that clock's domain. Tasks started before the call are outside it: the clock does not wait for them.
2. **When the time moves.** The clock counts the live tasks of its domain and those parked in a wait it sees: a `Sleep` on the clock, a queue's `Push`, `Pop` or `Values` step, a `Task`'s `Wait` and `Stop` (and a frame's drain of its tasks), and `timeout.After`. When the counts are equal and a sleeper is armed, the time moves to the earliest deadline, and every task asleep until that deadline wakes, in the order they slept. It never moves while a task of the domain can run.
3. **A host wait keeps the time still.** A wait on anything else, such as a read of the host's input or a file, a sleep on the host or on a `time.Manual`, or a lock, is not parked. So the clock does not move past it: a test that feeds standard input sees no timeout fire while the read waits.
4. **No lost window.** A waiter parks in the same step that checks its event, under the clock's lock, and the waker clears the parked flag of the task it wakes before the event is visible (the queue's signal, the end of a task, a timer, a stop). The Quint model of the batch-12 C1 unit checks the protocol (`time moves only when all are idle`, `no wake-up before its deadline`, `no lost wake-up`); `TestVirtualClockModel` (runtime/v1) runs generated programs of 1 to 5 tasks against a discrete-event model, 300 seeds.
5. **Time.** `Now` is `time.Unix` of the clock's reading: the Time has no monotonic reading, and the clock's wall time never falls. The clock is the one interface every machine timer reads ([runtime.md, Machines and actors](runtime.md#machines-and-actors) 9.6, one clock): `Scheduler.Sleep` and `time.Clock.Now`. `machines.RunTimed` reads both, and a live runner sleeps on them, so a machine's timers and a test's sleeps run on one virtual time with no change.
6. **Laws.** `SleepAdvancesExactly`: a lone `Sleep(d)` moves the clock by exactly `d`. `IdleOnlyAdvance`: a task that pushes while its parent sleeps reads the start time, and the parent wakes at its deadline.
7. **Script.** `testing.Script[S]` is a provider of the capability `S` that a test writes no code for (stage 5, L5b). `S` is a named operation type with operations and written type arguments (`Script[Store[Job]]`). A type parameter, a type that is not a capability, a sealed std type, and a capability with an operation that returns a `Task` or takes a function are S108 at the type argument: "testing.Script[T]: T is a type parameter; …". The record of calls keeps each argument past the call, so a Script's operation never takes an argument that a call only lends (one rule, q39; L8 C4): a function (a non-escaping function value, types.md, Function values 2), a borrow `*T` ("its operation Fill takes the borrow b *Box, …"; types.md, Borrows 1), and a value that holds a resource, taken or lent ("its operation Use takes the resource c Conn, …"; [resources.md](resources.md#lending)), since the record would be a second owner. The rule is decided at the type argument, a `Close` declared later included. A Script field of a generic record names a concrete capability (`Script[Use[T]]` is S108: "Use[T] is not a capability"), so an instance of the record, `Rig[Conn]`, adds no Script to decide (checked: `TestScriptDiagnostics`; the generic refusal, `TestGenericCapabilityDiagnostics`).
8. **Derived record.** The checker derives the record of `Script[S]` from `S`'s declared operations, as it derives a codec (no name list, no reflection): one field per operation, in `S`'s order and with its name, of type `Op[A, F]`. `A` is the operation's arguments: the one parameter's type, a tuple of several (`Put(k string, v int)` records `(string, int)`), `NoArgs` for none. `F` is the operation's signature as a function type, its rows included (`Get(k string) int fails Absent` gives `func(k string) int fails Absent`). The fields are `S`'s package's, so an operation hidden from a package is a field hidden from it.
9. **Making one.** `testing.Script[S]{}` makes one with no answers and no calls; a literal that sets a field is S108 ("a testing.Script[S] is made with no fields, …"). Every copy of a Script shares its operations (each `Op` is a synchronised handle, runtime.md, Tasks rule 6), so the test keeps its Script and binds a copy in a context: `with context { Photos: s } { run() }`.
10. **Providing S.** A `Script[S]` implements every operation type whose operations `S` has with `S`'s signatures (types.md, Interface assignability 1), so it converts to `S` wherever a value of `S` is expected, a context entry included. A call of an operation records its arguments (`s.Get.Calls()` is a copy, oldest first), then calls the answer that `s.Get.Answer(f)` set last, with the caller's task; the answer's failure is the operation's failure. A call of an operation with no answer is the defect `testing.Script[main.Photos]: Get has no answer`, except an operation with no result and no failure, whose call is only recorded. `s.Get.Calls()` marks the calls it returns read; `s.Get.Unread()` is the calls since, oldest first.

    **Held calls** (SC1; Foldkit's `Command.resolve` and `expectExact`). After `s.Get.Hold()`, every call of `Get` is recorded and then waits, held, on the caller's task, whatever the answer, until `s.Get.Release(f)` answers it: each Release answers the oldest held call with `f`, and the call returns what `f` returns, its failure included. A Release with no call held is kept, and the next call that would be held takes it and does not wait, so a test may release before the code calls. `s.Get.Held()` is the arguments of the held calls no Release answered, oldest first. `s.Get.Answer(f)` ends the hold: a later call gets `f`, and a call held already stays held. A stop of a held caller ends its wait (the interruption, as a queue's `Pop`), not the hold: the call stays in `Held()` until a Release takes it, so the test accounts for every call it held. A held caller parks in a VirtualClock's domain, so the clock moves past it (rule 2). **A test that ends with a held call or an untaken release fails**: when a test's or a law case's body returns, a held call no Release answered fails the case with `testing.Script[main.Photos]: Get has a held call that no Release answered` (`2 held calls` for two), and a Release no call took with `testing.Script[main.Photos]: Get was released with no held call` (`released 2 times` for two), since it is a script that expected a call the code never made; the clauses of every operation are joined by `; `. `testing.Unanswered()` is that report now, `""` when there is none.
11. **Lowering.** `Op[A, F]` is the runtime's `*v1.Op[A, F]`: a mutex, the calls and the answer. `Script[S]` is a generated Go struct with one field per operation; its conversion to `S` is an adapter whose method for each operation takes the lock once to append the arguments and read the answer, then calls the answer outside the lock (an answer may call the Script again). Hand Go control: a fake struct with a mutex, a slice of calls and a func field per method, as Go's `mockgen` writes; the same work per call (inferred, not measured).
12. **Laws.** `ScriptRecordsEachCall`: a Script records the arguments of every call of each operation in order, and an operation with no result and no failure needs no answer. `ScriptAnswersByTheLastAnswer`: each call gets the answer set last before it, its failure included, and a call with no arguments records `NoArgs`. `HeldCallWaitsForRelease`: after a Hold a call waits, whatever the answer, until a Release answers it with the released function, and a later Answer ends the hold. `ReleaseAnswersOldestFirst`: each Release answers the oldest held call, and a Release with no call held answers the next call at once. `ScriptNamesTheHeldCall`: while a call is held, `Unanswered` names the Script and the operation, and is `""` once a Release answers it; `Calls` marks the calls read. The end-of-test failure's text is checked by `TestScriptHeldCallFailsTheTest` (lower).
13. **Machine laws are searched, not sampled** (X1; [types.md, Laws](types.md#laws) 13). A reachability law of a machine whose starts and signals can be listed and whose state has a key is decided by a breadth-first search of the machine's graph (runtime/explore), within `ergo test --states N` states (1,000,000 by default; a count of states, never a time); `ergo test --law NAME` runs that law alone, and `--skip-law NAME` all but it (X1b). The report says "proved" only when the whole graph was seen; a search the budget stops passes with "not proved" and the run length it checked. A refuted `never reaches` prints its shortest run as an Ergo `test` that fails by itself, and a replay command. So a protocol design written as an Ergo machine, with each invariant a variant its step enters and each mutant a second machine whose law must be refuted, is checked by `ergo test` in a small scope (the folders of `models/`, such as `models/durable` and the sync replica's `models/replica`). An invariant can also be a pure predicate on the state (X2): `law Safe always safe` is decided as `never reaches` a state where `safe` is false, and `law CanDrop sometimes dropped` as `reaches` one where `dropped` holds; a sometimes law prints its witness as an Ergo `test` that passes by itself, and with no witness in a search the budget stopped it fails as "not found" ([types.md, Laws](types.md#laws) 13).
14. **Stages** (SC2; docs/domain.md, Stories and scenes). A scene is a test that plays steps on the running program and reads what it shows; `testing.Stage[A, V]` is that program as an operation type: `Do(a A) fails Refused` gives it an act and returns once the act is taken, `Advance(d)` moves its clock by `d` and returns once each task the move woke waits again, `Settle()` returns once every task of the program waits, and `Show() V` is what the test reads. A `Step[A]` is `Do{Act}`, `Advance{By}` or `Settle`, and `testing.Play(st, steps)` plays them in order and is the list of what `st` shows after each step, one show per step; a refused act stops the play with `Refused`. Its oracle is the fold: a law compares `Play`'s shows with a pure reference over the same steps. `actor.NewStage(a, clock)` is the stage of an actor started with the `time.Manual` `clock` as its Scheduler: an act is a `Send` (an ended actor is `Refused`), `Advance` is the clock's, `Settle` is `clock.Advance(0s)`, which returns once every other task of the clock's domain waits (the runner, its timers and its work), and `Show` is the state. So a scene on it is deterministic: the steps, the clock and the Scripts give the world. The laws `Stage.SettleIsIdempotent` and `Stage.ShowIsStable` hold of every stage; `ActorStageAgreesWithFold` (actor) plays seeded adds, saves (work that sends a signal back), moves of the clock (a timer that re-arms) and settles on an actor's stage, and holds each settled show to the fold. Effect: `TestClock.adjust` with `TestServices`; Go: `testing/synctest`'s `Wait`; Elm and Lustre program tests that send messages and inspect the model.
15. **Named points** (RT-HOOK). A library marks a place where another task's event decides what it reads with `testing.Point("pkg.name")`, such as std ui's `"ui.versioned"` between a pass's state read and the version read after it, and `"ui.settle.waits"` where `Settle` waits for what a fetch posts. A test or law arms it with `testing.At(name, f)`: the next task that reaches the point runs `f` there, once, on its own task, before it goes on, so the test lands an event (an actor's commit, a server's answer) at that exact place on every run, where it would otherwise race for it. Arming a point twice runs both functions, in order, at the next reach; a point no test armed does nothing, at the cost of one atomic load and a branch. The points are the process's. **A point never reached stops the run.** A law that waits for what a point's function releases must not wait forever for a point no task will reach. So once a point is armed, the run watches the clock domain of the task that armed it, with the domains its tasks made since (runtime/rt stall.go). When every member of those domains waits on a wait its clock sees, and no clock of them can move, the point is still armed, and the run stops with the defect `testing.At: point "name" was never reached: every task waits, and none can reach it` (`TestUnreachedPointStopsTheRun`). A task that arms a point outside every clock's domain gets no watch. Only a test build has points: in a run build a call of `Point` or `At` lowers to nothing but the evaluation of each argument that is not a literal, so a program's binary calls no hook code (`TestHooksStayOutOfRunBuilds`, on the lowered Go). The laws `VersionedNeverRunsAhead` and `OffClockAnswerIsWaitedFor` of std ui use them. *Decided (orchestrator, 2026-11-15):* one named interleaving a law states, as a narrow form of a controlled scheduler. *Prior art:* Go's `testing/synctest` makes "every other goroutine is durably blocked" a point a test waits for; Loom (Rust) and the explored schedules below choose every interleaving; a named point picks the one a law names, as FoundationDB's `BUGGIFY` sites mark places in library code that its simulation tests steer.
16. **World** (TEST-AMBIENT). `testing.World()` is a Context value (rows.md, Context values) that a test installs with `with testing.World() { … }`. Each call makes new providers: a `fstest.NewMemory()` bound for `fs.Files` and `fs.Writes`, a `NewVirtualClock(0)` for `time.Clock` and `Scheduler` (the calling task joins its domain, rule 1), and `rand.Seeded(1)` for `Random`. So a write through `fs.Writes` reads back through `fs.Files`, a `Sleep` moves `Now`, and nothing leaks from one test to the next (`WorldSharesEachPair`, `WorldsAreFresh`). A test reaches the providers through the capabilities (`fs.Writes.WriteFile` sets a fixture) and replaces one with an inner `with context { … }`. `ergo test` binds nothing a test does not name: World is opt-in, as every context is ("Public effect contracts are explicit"), and it is plain Ergo a user could write. Effect's `TestContext` (TestClock, a test Random) and Go's `testing/fstest.MapFS` are the prior art.
17. **Skip** (TSKIP). A run-time skip is the statement `skip "reason"` ([grammar.md](grammar.md#keywords), a contextual keyword), not a `testing` function: its rules are [runtime.md, Tests](runtime.md#tests) 4. A program's `examples/` test that skips lists the line in its `expected-skips`, as for every skip.
18. **Allocations** (MX-P3 F6). `testing.Allocs` counts heap allocations: `PerRun(runs, f)` is the integer mean of the heap allocations of one call of `f` over `runs` calls after one warm-up call, with GOMAXPROCS 1, as Go's `testing.AllocsPerRun` counts them (runtime/v1 allocs.go; it does not import Go's `testing`). `runs` below 1 is a defect. The counters are the host's, so its one provider, `testing.NewAllocCounter()`, needs `TestAmbient` (rows.md, Test's ambient): a test or a law states "allocates nothing" under `let counted = context needs TestAmbient { testing.Allocs: testing.NewAllocCounter() }` with `assert testing.Allocs.PerRun(100, func() { step() }) == 0`. The count is the process's, so a task that allocates while it counts counts too: the counted step starts no task. Law: `EmptyCallIsZero` (`TestAllocsLaws`).

### Trace conformance

`explore.Conform(g, trace, opts, invariants)` (MODELCHECK T2; `std/explore/conform.ergo`) decides whether a log of the running program is a behaviour of a model: a passing model says the design is right, conformance says the code follows the design. `ergo conform DIR --machine M --trace FILE [--hidden N] [--stutter] [--limit N] [--json]` runs it on a machine of a package, in process at check time.

| Declaration | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `func ReadTrace(text string) []Obs fails TraceError` | JSON Lines, one observation per line: `{"signal": "Save", "args": {"who": "a"}, "state": {"memory": 1}}`. Every key optional, unknown keys ignored; a line that is not an object, a `signal` that is not a string, `args` that is not an object, is a TraceError at that line. |
| `type Options { Hidden int, Stutter bool, Limit int }` | Up to `Hidden` unobserved steps before each observation; with `Stutter`, a line with no signal may leave the state unchanged; at most `Limit` candidates (100,000 when not positive). |
| `type Invariant[S] { Name string, Holds func(s S) bool }` | A named property checked on the behaviours that explain the trace. `ergo conform` passes the machine's `always` and `never reaches` laws. |
| `type Conformance = Conforms{Run, Report} \| Mismatch{Line, Observed, Before, Allowed, Nearest, Differ, Report} \| Broken{Line, Invariant, Run, Report} \| OverLimit{Line, Candidates, Report} \| TraceError{Line, Why}` | The verdict. `ergo conform` exits 0, 20, 23, 22 and 21 for them, in that order: everystate's 0, 20, 21 (bad trace) and 22 (limit reached), and 23 for Broken, which everystate does not have. |
| `func FitOf[T wire](v T, obs json.Value) Fit` | `Fits`, `Differs` or `Bad{Why}`: the observed members laid over `v`'s wire form, decoded as `T`, give `v` again (by key). |

1. **Typed matching.** An observation is read with the model's own codecs: the observed fields are laid over a candidate's wire form and the result is decoded as the state (or signal) type. A value of the wrong kind is a TraceError naming the field, and so is a member the type does not have or a signal name the model has no variant of; a well-typed value that differs is a non-match. A field left out is any value.
2. **The set of candidates.** Conform keeps the states consistent with the trace so far, deduplicated by the search's key (rule 13). Before each line it closes the set under up to `Hidden` steps by any signal; the line's step goes from each candidate by each signal the line allows (all of them when it names none) to a state that fits it. A `Mismatch` names the first line no candidate explains, the candidates before it cut to the observed fields, the steps the model allowed there, and the nearest next state with the fields that differ. `Broken` is a property every candidate breaks, so every behaviour that explains the trace so far breaks it. More than `Limit` candidates is `OverLimit`: no verdict, never a pass.
3. **What one trace proves.** That this trace is a behaviour of the model, nothing about other runs. Hidden steps and partial states weaken it, so the report prints the hidden steps the matching behaviour took, the peak count of candidates, the signals (by variant) the trace never took and the state's fields it never observed.
4. **Laws.** `ProjectionsConform`: a real run of a generated machine, with signal names, fields and state fields left out and up to `h` whole steps dropped, conforms with `Hidden >= h`. `ConformAgreesWithReference`: a projected run with one part changed conforms exactly when a naive reference that enumerates runs one by one finds one that explains it. `TraceErrorsNameTheLine`: each kind of malformed line is a TraceError at its line. `LimitNeverConforms`: a verdict stands on at most `Limit` candidates. Runner `TestStdExploreLawsRun`; the CLI's status and report, `TestConformCommand` (cmd/ergo).
5. **Not yet.** Machines with a start that takes inputs, signals that cannot be listed, generic state types, liveness on a finished trace (E3's `Leads`) and an online mode. `ergo conform` runs at check time, where the interpreted search costs about 48 times the Go search's steps (MODELCHECK E1); a compiled front end (orrery, S3) runs `Conform` as Go.

*Prior art:* everystate's `conform` (untyped JSON against TLA+ values; its exit codes 0, 20, 21 bad trace, 22 limit reached are kept, and Broken is 23); TLC's trace validation (Kuppe et al.) checks a log as a constrained behaviour of the spec; Quint's `run` replays a trace step by step.

### net

`net.NewTestHost()` makes a `Host` under [`TestLoopback`](rows.md#test-loopback). Its `Listen` and `Dial` require an explicit port and a literal loopback address; they fail `net.Denied{Addr}` before touching a socket for any other address. The `Network` operation rows include `Denied` as well as `ListenFailed` or `DialFailed`.

Std `net` makes stream connections (NET1). It follows Go's `net` for its names (`Listen`, `Dial`, `Accept`, `"host:port"` addresses, port 0 for a free port) and Effect platform's `Socket` and `SocketServer` for what a provider promises. `net.Network` is the TCP capability. `NewHost()` needs `Ambient` and gives the unrestricted host provider, so `main` binds `context { net.Network: net.NewHost() }`. `nettest.Pipe` (`NewPipe()`) is the memory provider for tests: its listeners and connections stay in the process and need no `Ambient`.

```go
import net

func echo(l net.Listener) fails net.Failed {
	let c = try l.Accept()
	try c.Write(try net.ReadAll(c))
}
```

1. **Resources.** A `Listener` and a `Conn` are resources (`..Closer`): their owner's scope closes them. A `Listener`'s `Close` frees its address, after which a dial to it fails, and resets each connection dialed and not accepted. A `Conn`'s `Close` ends the peer's reads after the bytes it has left; a `Close` that leaves bytes unread resets the peer, whose next read fails, as TCP's does.
2. **Bytes.** `Read()` gives the next bytes the peer wrote, at most 64 KiB, waiting until there is one, and `""` once the peer closed and every byte was read (Erlang's `gen_tcp:recv(S, 0)`). `ReadAll(c)` reads to the end. `Write(data)` writes every byte, waiting while the peer has not read enough; a pipe holds 64 KiB a direction. A write to a peer that closed fails `Failed`, at once in memory and on the host at the latest at the next write.
3. **Order.** A listener accepts its connections in the order of the dials. A dial needs no accept to return: the connection waits in the listener's backlog, and the dialer may write at once.
4. **Interruption.** A blocked `Accept`, `Read`, `Write` or `Dial` is an interruption point: a stop of the caller's task ends the wait (runtime.md, I1). On the host the wait is a system call: the stop moves the call's deadline into the past, as Go's `net/http` ends a blocked read, and the deadline is cleared again, so the connection stays usable by whoever releases it. Inside a mask the wait is uninterruptible (I2). The cost is one goroutine per blocking host call.
5. **Laws.** `Network`'s laws `WriteThenReadRoundTrips`, `CloseEndsReads`, `DialAfterCloseFails`, `AcceptsInDialOrder` and `ListenTakenFails` run against `nettest.Pipe` (lower `TestNetLaws`). `TestNetHostAgrees` (runtime/v1/net) holds the host on loopback equal to the pipe: for seeded scenarios of dials, accepts, writes of random bytes, reads and closes, both observe the same bytes, ends, failures and accept order. `TestNetStopEndsWaits` holds rule 4 for both.
6. **The runtime.** The bodies are the leaf `runtime/v1/net` (binsize BS1): only a program that names one links Go's `net`. Its `Listener` is also std `http`'s: `http.Listen` binds one and `http.Serve` serves it, so the two packages share one transport type. `http.Serve`, and so `rpc.Serve`, serves a `nettest.Pipe`'s listener as it serves the host's (NET1b): the leaf gives Go's `net/http` the pipe's connections as Go `net.Conn`s whose deadlines end a wait as a stop does (Go's own `net.Pipe` keeps them the same way), so a test serves and dials a server with no socket. `TestHTTPServePipeMatchesLoopback` (runtime/v1/http) sends the same seeded raw requests (one to three on a connection, sometimes a malformed one last) to a loopback server and a pipe server and holds the response bytes equal, Date aside.

### Explored schedules

`ergo test FILE --explore MODE` runs the tests under many schedules of the controlled scheduler ([runtime.md, Explored schedules](runtime.md#explored-schedules)). The test binary is built once with the `ergoexplore` tag; each schedule runs in a worker process of it, and the search on the `ergo` side picks the task at each scheduling point (`runtime/explore/sched`).

| Mode | Flags | What it does | Verdict |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Bounded search | `--explore dfs [--preempt B] [--schedules S]` | Depth-first over schedules with partial-order reduction on the dependency table below, at most `B` preemptions (default 2) and `S` schedules (default 10,000). The search under a bound keeps no sleep sets (item 2) | `ok explore dfs: proved for at most B preemptions (…)` when the search ended; `no failure in S schedules; not proved` when the budget stopped it |
| PCT | `--explore pct [--depth D] [--schedules S] [--seed N]` | `n` tasks and `k` steps come from a first, cooperative run; each schedule gives the tasks random priorities and `D - 1` change points (default depth 3, 100 schedules) | `no failure in S schedules (pct, depth D)` |
| Random walk | `--explore random [--schedules S] [--seed N]` | A uniform pick at each point | `no failure in S schedules (random)` |
| Replay | `--schedule TOKEN` | Runs the one schedule a failure printed | the run's own report |

1. **Dependency table.** Two steps of one task are dependent. A push, pop, close or write is dependent with every step on its handle; a read with every change of its handle; reads commute. A task's start is local: dependent only with a barrier. A spawn, join, stop, sleep, await, end and a resume after a wake are barriers, dependent with every step, since their effect is not one handle's. `TestDependencyTableTotal` keeps the table equal to `rt.OpKind`.
2. **The search is sound for its bound.** `TestDPORFindsEveryOutcome` (runtime/explore/sched) checks it against a naive enumeration of every schedule, on generated programs of 2 to 4 tasks on queues, inboxes and a `Ref`: the same set of outcomes, deadlocks included, with no bound and with bounds 0 to 2, and no more schedules. A race adds a choice at its earlier step (Flanagan and Godefroid's rule with vector clocks); under a bound the choice goes to the start of that task's stretch too, and no sleep set is kept (Coons et al.'s bounded POR).
3. **Workers.** A worker runs schedules back to back while each ends with every task ended. A run that stops, deadlocks, leaves its schedule or escapes the scheduler ends its worker, whose process group is reaped; the next run starts a new one. So a worker never holds the stacks of an abandoned run, and a search starts at most one worker more than the runs it abandoned (`TestPoolReplacesAbandonedWorkers`, `TestWorkerRetainsNoTask`). Each worker runs under the test memory limit.
4. **A failure.** The report prints the failing run's steps in words, the failing test again as a test that starts with `explore.Replay(schedule)` and the window of the schedule that the test ran, and `replay: ergo test FILE [--run NAME] --schedule 'TOKEN'` (the token is quoted: its stars are glob characters to a shell). A step names a task by its spawn path, and a handle by its name in the program: a task as `task 0.1`, a queue, a `Ref` or an actor (and its inbox) as the Ergo call that made it at its source position, relative to the folder of the program's files: `step 9: task 0.1: read on sync.NewRef at memory/fetch.ergo:39:14`. A second handle made by one call in the run, as in a loop, is `… (2)`. A handle the runtime made for itself (a scope, the source of a wake) keeps the number the run gave it, `#5`. A task's own start, spawn and end name no handle. The printed test needs `import explore`; under `ergo test FILE --explore` it fails by itself. A failure in a law or a property prints no test, only the command.
5. **One test or law.** `--run NAME` selects one test or law of the package by name, as it does for a plain `ergo test` (one flag, read by the test binary): the search explores that test's schedules only, the failing test is that test, and the replay command keeps `--run NAME`. A name the package does not have is a usage error (exit 2).
6. **Counters, no clock.** Each verdict prints the schedules, the scheduling points over all of them, the longest schedule, the runs a sleep set blocked and the workers started. No mode reads a clock; no test counts how often a random mode finds a failure.

| Declaration | Meaning | Go body |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `func Replay(schedule []string)` (package `explore`) | The next points of the explored run follow `schedule`, paths relative to the caller (`"0"` the caller, `"0.k"` the k-th task it starts after the call). A point whose task is not enabled stops the run with a defect. Under a plain `ergo test` it does nothing. | `ExploreReplay` (runtime/v1 explore_on.go; explore_off.go is empty) |

*Prior art (explored schedules):* Loom explores schedules depth first with DPOR and a preemption bound (`LOOM_MAX_PREEMPTIONS`); Concuerror uses source sets and sleep sets on the BEAM and replays from its linear trace; Coyote and P sample with PCT and replay a `.schedule` file; shuttle prints a schedule string for `replay`. Each needs the program to use its types or a rewritten binary; Ergo's seam is its runtime, so the test is unchanged. Gleam and Elixir reach Concuerror through the BEAM; ReScript has no scheduler to control.

*Prior art:* Go 1.25 `testing/synctest` moves a bubble's clock when every goroutine in the bubble is durably blocked, which the Go runtime knows; Ergo's runtime learns it from its own waits. Tokio `time::pause()` auto-advances when the runtime is idle; Trio `MockClock(autojump_threshold=0)`. Effect's `TestClock` moves only on `TestClock.adjust` (Ergo's `time.Manual`). Gleam, ReScript and Elixir lower no virtual clock: on the BEAM and in JavaScript it is a library over the host scheduler. *Effect workarounds replaced:* `TestServices` and `TestContext` layers become an ordinary `context`; `Effect.fork` before `adjust` becomes no step at all, since the clock moves on idle.

*Prior art (Script):* the purpose, in each, is a fake that follows the contract, answers what the test says and records what the code did. Elixir's Mox defines a mock module per behaviour (`defmock`) and checks it against the behaviour's callbacks; `expect(Mock, :fun, fn … end)` sets an answer and `verify!` checks that each one ran. Go's `mockgen` writes a struct per interface ahead of the build, and testify's `mock.Called` records calls through reflection on a string name. Gleam and ReScript have no derivation: a test writes a record of functions by hand (Ergo's `tape` and `blind` before L5b). Effect has no derived test service: a test writes a `Layer.succeed(Tag, { … })` by hand. *Not taken:* Mox's global expectations and `verify!` (a Script is a value a test holds and reads, so no registry and no process ownership; a test asserts on `Calls()`); `mockgen`'s generate step (the checker derives the record, so it never drifts from `S`); testify's string names (`s.Get` is a field the checker resolves); Effect's `Layer.succeed` of a hand object (the context entry `S: s` binds it).
